# The One Hundred Sayings of the
### Seraphic Doctor <note>The "Seraphic Doctor" is the traditional title for Saint Bonaventure (c. 1221–1274), a Franciscan friar and philosopher celebrated for his intense devotion and mystical theology.</note> Saint Bonaventure, newly printed with the utmost care. <note>original: "Centiloquiũ Seraphici doctoris sancti Bonauenture summa diligentia nouiter impressum." A *Centiloquium* refers to a literary work consisting of one hundred sections or propositions.</note>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page serves as the introduction to the "Centiloquium" (The Hundred Sayings), a work traditionally attributed to the Franciscan theologian Saint Bonaventure. It begins by interpreting a verse from Proverbs to establish the foundations of sacred study and the fourfold method of biblical interpretation.</meta>

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure
## ¶ Here begins the Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure:

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial 'E' featuring detailed floral patterns and foliage within a rectangular frame.</image-desc>
**“Behold, I have described it to you in three ways, in thoughts and knowledge, so that I might show you the certainty and the words of truth, to answer from these things those who sent you.”** (Proverbs 22:20-21). The wise man, instructing in these words those who intend to be promoted to the office of preaching, suggests four things that the studious disciple must observe.

Namely, first: **what one should study.** This refers to the internal tradition when he says, *“Behold, I have described it”*—that is, sacred doctrine—in three ways. He says "three ways" because I have informed the **rational** powers for the discernment of good and evil; 

¶ I have animated the **irascible** power <note>original: *irascibilem*; in medieval psychology, this refers to the part of the soul associated with courage, vigor, and the rejection of evil.</note> for the rejection of evil; 

¶ and I have excited the **concupiscible** power <note>original: *concupiscibilem*; the part of the soul associated with desire and the love of the good.</note> for the love of the good. 

Likewise, the doctrine of the sacred canon is described in three ways because, besides the **literal sense**, it is handed down to be explained in three modes: namely, *morally* (or tropologically), *allegorically*, and *anagogically*. 

*Tropology, or morality,* is when through that which was done, it is given to be understood what **ought to be done.** 

*Allegory is* when through that which was done, something else is indicated—namely, what **ought to be believed.** 

*Anagogy is* like a “leading upward,” where through a single deed one must understand that which is **to be desired**: namely, the eternal happiness of the blessed.

¶ Second: **what should be reflected upon.** This concerns the lesson heard, when he adds, *“in thoughts and knowledge.”* This means you should think upon the things you have heard, and frequently compel the intellect toward rumination <note>The medieval practice of *ruminatio* involved "chewing" over a text slowly to fully digest its spiritual meaning.</note> so that you may know how to grasp the things heard and ruminated upon through cognition.

¶ Third: **what should be proposed in preaching.** For these are moral precepts which, under the law of nature, scripture, and grace... <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

<summary>This opening page uses Proverbs 22 to introduce the "three ways" of sacred doctrine, detailing the three parts of the human soul and the classic fourfold method of interpreting scripture (literal, moral, allegorical, and anagogical).</summary>
<keywords>Bonaventure, biblical interpretation, tropology, allegory, anagogy, preaching, moral philosophy, medieval psychology</keywords>

It transitions from the theological justification for the work into the author’s personal preface, where he explains his humble intent to provide a "Centiloquium" (one hundred sayings) for beginners in theology. He then begins the table of contents for the first section of the work, focusing on the nature of sin.</meta>

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

...remained **firm and immovable**, and the Gospels especially came forth from the mouth of the Most High. This is why he says: *“so that I might show you the firmness and the words of truth.”* 

¶ Fourthly, he suggests **by what means one must respond** when questioned: specifically, through those things which have been perceived by instruction, gathered by reason, and conceived through preaching. This is why he adds: *“to answer those examiners who sent you”*—meaning those who have been sent to examine you. 

Therefore, as a support for little ones <note>“Little ones” (*parvuli*) refers to novice students or those new to the study of the faith.</note>, I, an unpolished writer, having been asked by those equally unpolished, have compiled an unpolished treatise for the unpolished. In this work, the simplicity of the simple can be educated, at least in a basic way, regarding the general principles of theology. Thus, through God’s preceding grace and the diligence of the senses, one may be prepared to move from infant’s milk to solid food. 

I have collected these things from the sayings of worthy men, plucking them from here and there and gathering them into the narrow confines of one small book. Since it contains one hundred sections, it may be called the **Centiloquium** <note>original: *Centiloquium*; from the Latin *centum* (hundred) and *loquium* (discourse/saying).</note>. 

To make its contents more easily accessible to the seeker, the one hundred section titles are listed first, which are divided into four parts. Indeed, this little work deals with four general topics:
1. First, **evil** under the aspect of guilt.
2. Second, **evil** under the aspect of punishment.
3. Third, **good** under the aspect of grace.
4. Fourth, **good** under the aspect of glory.

¶ The first part contains thirty-four sections: 
1. On the **evil of guilt** in general. 
2. On **temptation**. 
3. On the **definition of sin**. 
4. On **original sin**. 
5. On **actual sin**. 
6. On **venial and mortal** sin. 
7. On **ignorance**. 
8. On **omission and negligence**. 
9. On **commission** <note>Acts of “commission” are sins involving an action, as opposed to “omission,” which involves failing to act.</note>. 
10. On **error**, wicked judgment, and the showing of favoritism <note>original: *personarum acceptione*; the practice of judging based on a person’s status rather than the facts.</note>. 
11. On the **sin of consent**, sins against **conscience**, and moral **perplexity**. 
12. On **lying**. 
13. On evil **flattery**...

¶ **First**, on the wounding of natural powers <note>original: *vulneratione naturalium*; the theological idea that original sin damaged human faculties like reason and will.</note>; **second**, on the stripping away of grace-given gifts; **third**, on bodily punishments; **fourth**, on places of punishment; **fifth**, on the opening of the books <note>A reference to the biblical imagery of the Last Judgment, where the deeds of all are revealed.</note>; **sixth**, on the fire of the conflagration; **seventh**, on the conflagrations of the lower regions.

**The third part** has fifty-seven sections: 
¶ **First**, on that which is "to enjoy" and "to use" <note>original: *frui et uti*; a fundamental distinction in medieval thought where God is the object of enjoyment for His own sake, while the world is used as a means to reach Him.</note>; **second**, what is to be believed, understood, and expressed regarding the triune God; **third**, how the God who is three and one manifests Himself in the effects of creation; **fourth**, on the attributes appropriated to the Father; **fifth**, on the attributes appropriated to the Son; **sixth**, on the attributes appropriated to the Holy Spirit; **seventh**, on the four created things and the measures of durations; **eighth**, on the truth to be expressed regarding the production of the world, with the exclusion of error; **ninth**, on the threefold difference of the creature; **tenth**, on the production of the cosmic machine <note>original: *machine mundialis*; a common medieval metaphor for the intricately designed and functioning universe.</note>; **eleventh**, on the usual composition of the bodily machine; **twelfth**, on the sufficiency of the hea... <note>The text likely continues as "heavens" (*celorum*) on the following page.</note>

# On the evil of guilt.
# Chapter One.

Evil comes to be considered with regard to its definition. Defining it, Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), whose definitions of evil as a "privation" or lack of good shaped all subsequent medieval philosophy.</note> says: "Evil is the privation of mode, species, and order." <note>original: *Malum est privatio modi speciei et ordinis*</note> 

For the understanding of this, it must be noted that any creature, however small, must be compared to God according to a threefold kind of cause: namely, the **efficient cause** <note>The agent that brings something into being; here, God as Creator.</note> and thus it has **mode**; the **formal cause** <note>The design or essence that makes a thing what it is.</note> and thus it has **species**; and the **final cause** <note>The purpose or goal for which a thing exists.</note> and thus it has **order**.

A rational creature, however, does not possess mode, species, and order only insofar as it is a certain nature like other creatures, but also insofar as it possesses free will. Now, our will is good when it possesses mode, species, and order. It possesses these three things when, in its operation, it continues with the divine power as its moving principle; when it is conformed to Him as the directing rule; and when it is united to Him as the resting end <note>The final goal that brings peace or "quiet."</note>. 

In the first [the moving principle] is mode; in the second [the directing rule] is species; and in the third [the resting end], order is observed, according to the threefold kind of cause. For whoever sins mortally is necessarily disconnected from God as the moving principle, because God never moves [a person] toward sin. He also disagrees with God as the directing rule...

<summary>This page concludes the table of contents and begins the treatise with a definition of evil, drawing on Augustine to describe it as a lack of "mode, species, and order" within the human will.</summary>
<keywords>Augustine, definition of evil, privation, efficient cause, formal cause, final cause, free will, mortal sin, divine rule</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "mode, species, and order" from the previous page, explaining how evil disrupts these three categories of existence. It moves into a systematic classification of different types of evil and their origins in the human will.</meta>

# ¶ The Lentiloquium <term>Literally "The Slow-Speech," a title for a concise but profound theological treatise</term> of Saint Bonaventure

**It is distanced** and disordered from God as the ultimate goal <term>Latin: *finis*, the purpose or "end" for which a thing exists</term> when a person mistakenly makes a created thing their ultimate goal. And thus, the **evil of guilt** <term>original: *malum culpe*; the moral wrongdoing for which one is responsible</term> deprives and corrupts these three <note>referring to mode, species, and order mentioned on the previous page</note> inseparably and equally. However, it does not destroy them totally in a rational creature, insofar as that creature acts voluntarily and freely above all other creatures.

¶ **The evil of punishment** <term>original: *malum pene*; the suffering or consequences that follow guilt</term> truly corrupts these three aforementioned things just as it occurs naturally in other creatures.

¶ **Likewise**, evil is considered according to its classification. For evil is **distinguished** by a threefold difference: namely, the evil of guilt or transgression; the evil of struggle or temptation; and the evil of punishment or penalty.

¶ **The evil of guilt** is the same as general injustice, or the lack of due honesty, or a voluntary passion <note>In this context, a "passion" refers to an uncontrolled emotion or desire that pulls the will away from what is right.</note>.

¶ **The evil of punishment** is the withholding of due delight, accompanied by the sensation of its opposite, or an involuntary passion.

¶ **The evil of struggle** holds the middle ground between these two.

¶ **For** to the devil, there is guilt in instigating the struggle. **To the just person**, there is punishment in resisting it.

¶ **Concerning the subdivision**

¶ For the **evil of guilt is subdivided** into that which is evil according to itself and in itself, which can in no way be done well—such as hating God and blaspheming God.

¶ **Into** that which is evil in itself or in its general category—such as killing—which [nevertheless] can be done well for the sake of justice <note>For example, a judge or executioner carrying out a legal sentence.</note>.

¶ **Into** evil arising from circumstance—such as giving alms for the sake of vainglory.

¶ **Likewise**, it is considered according to its introduction <note>meaning its origin or how it enters the world</note>; and thus evil originates from the good, that is, from the will of free choice—not as an **efficient cause**, but as a **deficient cause** <note>A famous distinction from Saint Augustine: God (the efficient cause) creates good things, but evil happens when a creature "fails" or "falls short" (the deficient cause) of its purpose.</note>.

¶ It is **substantiated** by the good; for evil is not some essence or nature, but a corruption of the good, which cannot exist except in a good thing that is being corrupted—that is, in the free will.

¶ It is **opposed** to moral good. **To understand this:**

¶ **Note** that evil corrupts and destroys justice—that is, the rectitude <term>uprightness or "straightness"</term> of the will.

¶ For **three** things are observed regarding the rectitude of the soul, namely—

<summary>This page classifies evil into three categories (guilt, punishment, and struggle) and explains that evil has no independent existence, but is a "deficiency" or corruption within the good of the human will.</summary>
<keywords>evil of guilt, evil of punishment, evil of struggle, free will, deficient cause, Saint Bonaventure, rectitude of the soul, Scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how evil affects the human soul, specifically focusing on the "rectifiable power" of the will. It transitions from the general nature of evil to a specific examination of temptation, drawing on several Church Fathers for definitions and classifications.</meta>

<header>¶ Part One.</header>

...power capable of being set right. This power is not destroyed by a right-ordering habit <term>original: *habitus*; a stable disposition or quality of the soul</term>. Evil simply deprives and corrupts this, namely <term>original: *scilicet*</term> grace and virtue. ¶ There is a **middle ability**, and evil diminishes this when a person is rendered less able toward good because of sin, yet it does not take it away entirely. ¶ For evil is not opposed to that ability by reason of its nature, but by reason of grace and virtue, or the goal <term>original: *finis*; the ultimate purpose or "end" of a thing</term> for which it exists.

### ¶ On temptation. ¶ Chapter 2.

The evil of temptation or struggle is identified through the definition of **Hugh of Saint Victor** <note>A prominent 12th-century mystic and theologian</note>. To tempt is to test craftily and, as it were, to try someone with certain alluring attempts. **Anselm** <note>Anselm of Canterbury, famous for his ontological argument</note> says: every temptation is a testing. **Cassiodorus** states: **Temptation** is the imitation of an apparent good for the purpose of deceiving. It is also **defined** in another way: **Temptation** is a striking <term>or an urging</term> from intention or purpose to reveal the good or evil of a person. ¶ It is **multiplied** by division. For temptation can be considered according to change. In this way, it is divided into that which is from the enemy <note>referring to the devil</note>, from the flesh, and from the world. ¶ In accordance with the saying: the world, the flesh, and the demons stir up various battles. ¶ **Likewise**, it is divided into those temptations which are from the enemy and those from the flesh: the first generally, the second specifically. ¶ It is also divided according to the instrument [used], and thus there is that general division of **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great</note>: There is a certain temptation through vices, and another through scourges <term>original: *flagella*; meaning trials, afflictions, or divine corrections</term>. ¶ **Specifically**, there is the temptation mentioned regarding the Psalm: "Their soul loathed all manner of food" <note>Psalm 107:18; used here to describe a spiritual aversion to what is good</note>. ¶ There are **many types** of temptation, of which: ¶ **First** is error or ignorance of the way of truth. ¶ **Second** is the difficulty of overcoming desires <term>original: *concupiscentia*; strong worldly or carnal longing</term>. ¶ **Third** is weariness toward the good. ¶ **Fourth** is the storm of the world. ¶ [Temptation is also categorized] **according** to its general effect, and...

<summary>This page defines temptation through the lenses of major theologians like Hugh of Saint Victor and Gregory the Great, categorizing it by its source (world, flesh, devil) and its nature (error, desire, weariness).</summary>
<keywords>temptation, grace, virtue, Hugh of Saint Victor, Anselm, Cassiodorus, Gregory the Great, concupiscence, trials, spiritual struggle</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of temptation within the "Centiloquium," following the general definitions of Hugh of Saint Victor and Anselm on the previous page. It moves into specific categories of temptation (human vs. diabolical) and provides various schemas from Church Fathers like Augustine, Gregory, and Bernard to help the reader identify the source and nature of spiritual trials.</meta>

<header>¶ The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure</header>

Thus it is in 1 Corinthians 12 <note>The text cites chapter 12, though the verse "No temptation has overtaken you..." is famously from 1 Corinthians 10:13</note>: "No temptation has overtaken you except what is common to mankind." One temptation is diabolical, another is human. The **Diabolical** has an evil outcome because it ends in mortal sin; the human kind does not have a dangerous outcome because there is either a venial sin <term>original: *peccatum veniale*; a lesser, pardonable sin</term> or no guilt at all. **Specifically**, and thus it is in **Augustine's *On Christian Doctrine***: The **Devil** tempts either by troubling the flesh, or by wounding natural qualities, or by stripping away spiritual graces <term>original: *gratuita*; gifts given freely by God, such as grace or faith</term>. **As for the degree:** and thus, generally, there is one temptation that is external and another that is internal. **Specifically: and so it is stated by Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great</note>: **Temptation** is completed in three ways, namely <term>original: *scilicet*</term> through suggestion, through delight, and through consent. **As for the manner of tempting.** And thus it is stated generally and implicitly in the commentary on the psalm **"He who dwells in the aid [of the Most High]"** <note>Psalm 91</note>: Some temptation is light and hidden, some is grave and hidden. Some is light and manifest, and some is grave and manifest. **Generally and explicitly, as in Bernard’s seven members** <note>Bernard of Clairvaux</note>: the first is bothersome, which insistently persists; the second is doubtful, which wraps the soul in a cloud of doubt; the third is sudden, which arrives before the judgment of reason; the fourth is hidden, which escapes the order of deliberation; the fifth is violent, which transcends our strength; the sixth is fraudulent, which seduces the soul; the seventh is complex, which hinders the soul through various vices. **If considered specifically and implicitly, it is as Augustine says:** the devil tempts as a lion and attacks as a dragon <note>original: *temptat ut leo, tempnat ut draco*; representing the choice between open force and hidden guile</note>. **If specifically and explicitly: thus it is stated by Gregory in his commentary on Job**: The **Devil** captures by oppressing; he circumvents by **lying in wait**; he terrifies by threatening; he flatters by persuading; he breaks by causing despair; and he deceives by making promises. **It is also diversified by intention. For God tempts so that he may instruct; the devil, so that he may deceive; the world, so that it may allure; man, so that he may prove <note>original: *comiciat*; likely a variant for *comprobet* or *committat* in the sense of testing or engaging</note>; and the flesh, so that it may infect.**

<summary>This page categorizes various types of spiritual temptation, distinguishing between human and diabolical sources and outlining the stages of temptation (suggestion, delight, and consent). It draws on the authority of Augustine, Gregory, and Bernard to provide a taxonomy of how the soul is tested by God, the devil, the world, and the flesh.</summary>
<keywords>temptation, diabolical temptation, human temptation, venial sin, mortal sin, Augustine, Gregory the Great, Bernard of Clairvaux, spiritual warfare, intention</keywords>

<header>Part One</header>

<margin>On the definition of sin</margin>
<margin>Chapter I</margin>

Having considered the evil of guilt in general and the evil of the struggle that impels one toward guilt, we must now descend to the consideration of sin in specific detail. Concerning this, we ought to consider three things: namely, the manner of its division, the manner of its definition, and the manner of its punishment. Sin can be considered formally, insofar as it denotes a privation <term>original: *privatio*; the absence or lack of something that should naturally be present</term>, and materially, insofar as it involves some underlying action. It has, therefore, formal definitions insofar as it denotes a privation in this manner: 

¶ For insofar as it denotes a turning away from the good, it is described by three definitions that express nearly the same idea. The **First Definition**, in the book *The City of God* <note>By Saint Augustine, a foundational work of Christian philosophy</note>, is this: sin is the absence of good where it ought to be. The **Second**, in the book *On True Religion*: ¶ Sin is the desire not for evil natures, but the desertion of better ones. The **Third**, in the book *On the Nature of the Good*: Sin is the corruption of form, measure, and order. 

**Or**, it denotes a turning away from the rectitude <term>original: *rectitudo*; the "straightness" or moral uprightness</term> of nature, and thus three definitions expressing the same idea are found. The first is by Damascene <note>John of Damascus, an 8th-century Syrian monk and scholar</note>: ¶ Sin is a turning from that which is according to nature into that which is contrary to nature. The **Second** is by Anselm <note>Anselm of Canterbury</note>: Sin is the lack of required justice. The **Third** is by Augustine: Sin is a certain falling away from the light of truth, not destroying nature but darkening it. 

**Or**, it denotes a turning away from the fullness and perfection of every good, and thus it is defined in three ways. ¶ First, by Augustine in the book *On Free Will*: ¶ Evil is a turning away from the unchangeable good <note>original: *bono incommutabili*; referring to God as the source of all goodness that does not change</note>. The **Second**, by the same author in the book *On True Religion*: ¶ Sin is to be without the unchangeable goods which are meant to be enjoyed. The **Third** is also taken from him: Sin is the darkness of the intellect. 

¶ Sin also has "material" definitions, insofar as it involves...

<summary>This page initiates a systematic study of sin, categorizing it through the lens of privation—the lack of a required good—drawing on the definitions provided by Augustine, Anselm, and John of Damascus.</summary>
<keywords>definition of sin, privation, rectitude, Augustine, Anselm, John of Damascus, City of God, On True Religion, nature of the good, unchangeable good</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of definitions for sin, shifting from sin as a "lack" to sin as an offense against God, virtue, and the proper use of the world. It concludes the introductory definitions and begins Chapter IV on the classification of sins, specifically Original Sin.</meta>

...nature by **action**. According to this, it can be compared in three ways: either to **God**, whom it offends. **And** thus it is defined in three ways: **First**, by Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), whose writings shaped much of Western Christian thought on sin and grace.</note> writing against the Manicheans: sin is to err regarding the precepts of truth or in truth itself. **Second**, by Ambrose in the book *On Paradise*: sin is the transgression of the divine law and disobedience to heavenly mandates or commands. **Third**, by Augustine: sin is a word, deed, or desire contrary to the law of God.

**Or** it is compared to the **virtue** which it excludes. And thus a threefold definition of sin is given. The **first** is by Basil <note>Basil the Great, a 4th-century Greek bishop and theologian.</note>: sin is a disposition of the soul held in opposition to virtue. The **second** is by Augustine in the book *On Two Souls*: **Sin** is the will to retain or pursue what justice forbids. The **third** is by Augustine in the book *On Christian Religion*: sin is to desire what Christ despised or to despise what Christ desired.

**Or** it is compared to the **good** toward which it aims. **And** thus it is defined in three ways. **First**, by Augustine in the book *The City of God*: sin is the evil use of a good thing. **Second**, by the same author in the book *On the Trinity*: sin is to use what should be enjoyed and to enjoy what should be used. <note>original: *uti fruendis & frui utendis*. Augustine taught that we should "enjoy" God as our final end, but only "use" worldly things as tools to reach Him. Sin flips this order.</note> **Similarly** in the book *Eighty-Three Different Questions*. **Third**, in the book *On Free Will*: sin is to cling to changeable things while the unchangeable good is scorned.

**Two** other definitions of sin are also found. One is this: sin is an act arising from a defect of good. The **second** is this: sin is the desert of eternal punishment.

**Having** seen the definition of sin, it follows to look at its **division**. Sin is **divided** into original and actual; venial <note>Minor sins that do not break one's relationship with God.</note> and mortal <note>Grave sins that "kill" the life of grace in the soul.</note>; and sins of omission <term>original: *delictum*; failing to do what is required</term> and commission <term>original: *commissum*; doing what is forbidden</term>. **And** these divisions are determined according to their formal cause. **Also**, sin is divided into those of heart, mouth, and deed <note>Commonly known as thought, word, and deed.</note>. **Partly into** the sin of pride or vainglory; **envy**, anger, sloth, avarice, gluttony, and lust. **Into** the concupiscence <note>Strong, disordered desire or lust.</note> of the flesh, the concupiscence...

<meta>This page continues the classification of sins, moving from general definitions based on God, virtue, and use, to specific categories such as the senses, internal disposition, and target of the sin. It then transitions into the first major section of the work, focusing on the definition and transmission of Original Sin.</meta>

...lust of the eyes, and the pride of life. <note>original: *concupiscentia oculorum & superbia vitae*. These categories are drawn from 1 John 2:16, describing worldly temptations.</note> **In** sin, there is the thought, the delight, the consent, the action, and the habit. And these are understood according to the material cause. **Likewise**, one sin is committed out of weakness, another out of ignorance, and another out of certain malice. **Likewise**, one sin comes from an evilly humbling fear, and another from an evilly inflaming love. **And** these depend on the conditions of the sinner. **Likewise**, one sin is against God, another against oneself, and another against one's neighbor. **And** these are understood according to the circumstances of the one against whom the sin is committed.

## ¶ Part One

**¶ We** follow the **first** division.
**¶ First**, regarding original sin, four things must be considered: the definition, the corruption, the cure, and the naming. **¶ Original** sin is **defined** by Anselm <note>Anselm of Canterbury (1033–1109), a philosopher and theologian who defined original sin as the "privation of justice."</note> as follows: **¶ Original** sin is the lack or nakedness of the justice that is owed, caused by the disobedience of Adam, which is accompanied by the lack of the vision of God. **¶ This** is the formal definition, indicating the essence, cause, and penalty of this sin. **¶ Likewise**, it is defined another way: original sin is a vice born in human nature from the quality of the seed. **¶ This** is the material definition, showing the effect and origin of original sin. **¶ Third**, it is defined by Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), who emphasized the role of desire in original sin.</note> as follows: **¶ Original** sin is concupiscence <term>concupiscence: an intense worldly desire or longing, particularly physical or carnal, that persists in human nature after the Fall.</term> or the tendency toward it, which makes the child "desirable" [to sin] and the adult "desiring." **¶ The** human race is **corrupted** through original sin. For although the soul is not passed down by descent <note>original: *ex traduce*. This refers to the theological debate of Traducianism—whether the soul is inherited from parents (like the body) or created individually by God. The author here suggests that while the soul itself isn't "inherited," the guilt of Adam still reaches it through the flesh.</note> from the original source, nevertheless, guilt passes from the soul of Adam to the souls of his descendants through the medium of the flesh. It is generated through concupiscence and sown through lust; drawing infection with it, it infects and vitiates the soul united to it—not only with penalty, but also with guilt. And so, a person generated through intercourse is born a "child of wrath" <note>A reference to the biblical book of Ephesians 2:3, describing the state of humanity separated from God's grace by sin.</note> because they are deprived of the rectitude of divine justice. Due to this absence, they incur, regarding the soul: weakness, ignorance, malice, and concupiscence; and regarding the body: penalties and labors.

<summary>This page categorizes sins by their internal process and target, then begins a detailed study of Original Sin, providing definitions from Anselm and Augustine. It explains how the "infection" of sin is transmitted from Adam to all humans through the flesh, resulting in both spiritual and physical consequences.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of original sin from the previous page, specifically concluding the definition of its penalties before moving into its cure through baptism and the various theological names used to describe this condition.</meta>

# ¶ The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

...pains, defects, multiple diseases, and besides these the penalty of death and turning to ash; the penalty of the lack of the vision of God and the loss of heavenly glory, both in children <note>original: *puulis*, referring to infants who die before baptism.</note> and in adults. **¶ Original sin**, however, is **cured** through baptism in this way: regarding the guilt, it is removed; yet it remains regarding the physical penalty. **¶ Thus** it is cured regarding the liability of eternal punishment, but remains regarding the act and movement of **concupiscence** <term>concupiscence: the tendency of human nature toward earthly and carnal desires rather than toward God</term>. **¶ Thus** it is cured in the present parent so that, nonetheless, it is transmitted from the cured parent into the generated offspring. **¶ Thus** the stain of original guilt is removed while the consequence remains, with which it is necessary for us to fight as long as we live in this life.

**¶ It is named**, moreover, by various terms, both on the side of guilt and on the side of penalty. For when compared to the soul, it is called **infirmity**, because it renders the soul powerless to resist impulses. **¶ It is called foulness** because, through it, the soul is defiled by reason of its connection to the body. **¶ It is called proneness** because of the inclination of the appetite toward evil. **¶ It is called corruption** because it leads to nothingness, namely to sin. **¶ It is called vice** because it diminishes natural virtues. **¶ It is called the languor of nature** because of the reduction of natural good and delight.

**¶ When compared**, however, to the body, it is called the **law of the flesh**, because it is a penalty naturally inflicted by the law of justice. **¶ It is called the law of the members** because it provides the aptitude for performing evil works. **¶ It is called a tyrant** because of its almost violent dominion over desires. **¶ It is called fomes** <term>fomes: literally "tinder" or "kindling"; the internal spark or fuel that ignites sinful desire</term> because it is the material cause of preserving sin in the flesh. **Ambrose** <note>St. Ambrose (c. 339–397), Bishop of Milan and a Doctor of the Church.</note>, defining *fomes*, says this: **fomes** is a certain morbid affection which moves one toward illicit desire regarding the functions of reproduction and nutrition. **¶ Compared** to illicit pleasurable acts, it is called **concupiscence**, as con- <note>original: *con-*, the catchword for the next page's first word, *concupiscentia*.</note>

<meta>This page continues the list of criteria for measuring the gravity of sin, moving from the eleventh to the twelfth reason, before beginning Chapter 6 on the distinction between venial and mortal sins.</meta>

<header>Part One.</header>

...by ingratitude: and thus the sin <note>original: *peccatum*</note> is said to be greater in one who has received more gifts of grace. Fifth, by dangerousness <note>original: *periculositate*</note>: and thus a sin of ignorance is said to be graver because it is more dangerous. Sixth, by inseparability: and thus the sin of greed <note>original: *cupiditatis*</note> is said to be greater: because while other vices grow old with a man, avarice alone grows young <note>original: *iuuenescit*; a common medieval trope that greed increases as one ages while other physical passions fade</note>. Seventh, by importunity <note>original: *importunitate*</note>: and thus pride is said to be the greatest sin: because it is even generated from good works. Eighth, by proneness: and thus lust <note>original: *luxuria*</note> is called graver, toward which men are more prone to incline. Ninth, by the assault of a greater grace: and thus the sin against the Holy Spirit <note>original: *spiritum sanctum*</note> is said to be graver. Tenth, by reason of a greater offense: and thus the sin of idolatry is said to be greater. Eleventh, by reason of a greater ease of resisting: and thus the sin of Adam was most grave. Twelfth, by reason of greater wickedness and desire <note>original: *libidinis*</note>, and thus a sin of any kind can become greater than another.

### ¶ On venial and mortal sin. ¶ Chapter 6.

It follows to look at the division of sin into venial <term>venial: from the Latin *venia* (pardon); a lesser sin that does not result in a complete separation from God</term> and mortal <term>mortal: a grave sin that "kills" the life of grace in the soul</term>. ¶ Toward the clarity of which, three things must be briefly noted: namely, the order of divine justice, which venial sin (which is contracted) and mortal sin (which is incurred) oppose. ¶ The order of divine justice, however, to which the Lord has bound men, is that the unchangeable good <note>original: *bonum incommutabile*; referring to God</note> be preferred to the changeable good; that the honorable good <note>original: *bonum honestum*; something desired for its own sake</note> be preferred to the useful good; and the will of God be preferred to one's own will; and the judgment of right reason be preferred and preside over human sensuality <term>sensuality: the faculty of the soul relating to physical senses and desires</term>. ¶ Actual sin, indeed, is the actual disorder <note>original: *inordinatio*</note> of the will. ¶ This disorder, however, if it is so small that it does not destroy this order, although it disturbs it in some way, then the sin is venial. Regarding this, we must treat briefly its definition, distinction, and cure. ¶ Defin[ition...]

<header>The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure</header>

...is defined, or it is defined thus: venial sin <note>original: *peccatum veniale*</note> is a desire or a love of pleasure in a created thing <note>original: *creatura*; anything that is not God</note> that stops short of God. ¶ Venial sin is distinguished in three ways. For one kind is venial by stealth <note>original: *subreptione*; an impulsive or sudden movement of the mind</note>, and it is venial because it cannot be avoided at that moment, and because it is committed apart from reason; this is always venial. Another kind is venial from thought without consent: and it is venial because one does not fully consent—as in a thought about the pleasure of a woman without consenting to that pleasure; yet such a venial sin becomes mortal through taking pleasure in it and giving consent. **A certain** kind is venial with reason and consent, and it is venial because it is not a disorder against a commandment—such as [minor] drunkenness or a joking lie; and though this is venial because of the pleasure which is short of God, it can nonetheless become mortal because of excessive desire and contempt. However, it should not be understood that any venial sin becomes mortal by its very essence, but rather that there is a transition from the venial to the mortal through taking pleasure and giving consent, or through lust and contempt. ¶ Venial sin is **cured** through acts in which there is some humility and devotion; these acts, when shaped by grace <note>original: *gratia informati*</note>, are repugnant to venial sin. And thus the acts of curing and deleting venial sins assist and invigorate "sanctifying grace" <term>sanctifying grace: original *gratiam gratum facientem*; the theological term for the divine life in the soul that makes a person pleasing to God</term>. Remedies of this kind are contained in these verses: *I confess, I strike, I am sprinkled, I am contrite, I pray. I am signed, I eat, I give, through which I put aside venial things; **Anointing**, light penance, and the suffering shared with the needy.* <note>original Latin mnemonic: *Confiteor tondo / respergor / conteror / oro. Signor / edo / dono / per que venialia pono Unctio / pena leuis / et passio que fit egenis.*</note> For these remedies are: general confession, the striking of the breast, the sprinkling of holy water, contrition of heart, the Lord’s Prayer, the signing [with the cross] by a bishop, the receiving of the Eucharist, the forgiving of an offense, extreme unction, light penance, and brotherly suffering <note>original: *passio fraterna*; shared compassion or enduring hardships with others</note>. If, however, there is such a great disorder of the will that it destroys the order mentioned above, then the sin is mortal...

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of sin begun on the previous page. While the preceding text defined venial (minor) sin, this section shifts to the definition, punishment, and remedies for mortal (grave) sin.</meta>

# ¶ First part
b.ij.

[mortal] sin <note>The text begins mid-word with "-catum," completing "peccatum" from the previous page.</note>, which can be considered in terms of its definition, its punishment, and its remission. It is defined by **Ambrose** <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 340–397), the Bishop of Milan and a Doctor of the Church.</note> thus: mortal sin is a transgression of divine law and a disobedience of heavenly commands. ¶ The penalty for mortal sin is eternal damnation. For it is called "mortal" because through it the life of grace is lost in the present and the life of glory is lost in the future. And thus the "death of guilt" is contracted and the "death of Gehenna" <note>original: *mors gehenne*; Gehenna is a historical term for Hell, derived from a valley near Jerusalem.</note> is inflicted. It is just, however, that because of a mortal sin in which someone finally perseveres, they are tormented everlastingly. Wherefore, according to **Gregory** <note>Saint Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), known for his writings on morality and the afterlife.</note>: because the sinner sins in his own eternity <note>A scholastic argument that since the sinner would have sinned forever if he lived forever, he is punished forever.</note>, he is to be tormented eternally. According to **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430), whose theology shaped Western views on sin and grace.</note>, however, because he sins against an eternal and infinite good, he shall be punished eternally. ¶ Mortal sin is **cured** or remitted through a sevenfold remedy, namely: through baptism, martyrdom, the conversion of sinners, the forgiveness of injuries, the weeping of penance, almsgiving, and through the worthy reception of the Eucharist. Note, however, that baptism and penance are effective not only against the penalty of sin, but also against the guilt; the others are effective only against the penalty <term>guilt vs. penalty: original *culpa* vs *poena*; theology distinguishes between the stain of the sin (guilt) and the debt of punishment owed for it.</term>. Whence the verse:

->*There are seven ways by which you can erase mortal [sins]:*<-
->*The font, alms, martyrdom, the conversion of brothers,*<-
->¶ *Forgiving the one who sins against you, weeping for guilt,*<-
->¶ *And the worthy reception of the blood and flesh of Christ.*<-

Ecclesiasticus 3: Almsgiving resists sins. **Ambrose** <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 340–397), Bishop of Milan.</note>: There is no sin so grave that it cannot be purged by abstinence and extinguished by almsgiving; just as water extinguishes fire, so almsgiving extinguishes sin.

### ¶ Concerning ignorance. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; ¶ Chapter 10.

**IT** follows next to look at the division of sin into transgression <term>original: *delictum*</term> and omission. The breach of commandments is transgression; this is the violation of affirmative commands <note>Commands that require a specific action, such as "Honor thy father."</note>. The breach of prohibitions is omission; this is [the violation] of negative commands <note>Commands that forbid an action, such as "Thou shalt not kill."</note>. The different forms of transgression are **ignorance**, **omission**, and **negligence**. Transgression <term>delictum</term> is, as it were, "abandonment" <term>derelictum</term>, in which there is a desertion of the good.
The good is twofold. Namely, that which is to be known, the desertion of which is called **ignorance**. And that which is to be done, for which two things are required: namely, that a good act is performed—the desertion of which is called **omission**—and that it be done well, that is, with the proper circumstances—the desertion of which is called **negligence**.
Regarding ignorance, a fourfold division is to be observed. The first is this: ignorance is threefold, namely: of **negation**, of **privation**, and of **disposition**.
Ignorance of negation <note>A simple absence of knowledge about things one is not expected to know.</note> is in small children, which is neither a fault nor a punishment until the prescribed time. Ignorance of privation <note>The absence of knowledge that one is required to have.</note> is in adults, which at a determined time...

<header>Part One</header>

...those who ignore what they could know, or have forgotten what they once learned. **Ignorance** of disposition is when someone holds opinions contrary to the faith, which can be called erroneous; and this is found in heretics. The **first** <note>Simple ignorance, or not knowing what one is not required to know.</note> excuses entirely. The second <note>Ignorance of something one ought to know.</note> excuses in part but not in whole. The third <note>Erroneous ignorance or heresy.</note> accuses entirely, because they follow a perverse will.

¶ **The second division** is this. **Ignorance** is either of fact or of law. Ignorance of the law is either regarding natural law—and this does not excuse since it is written in the human heart—or regarding divine law, such as the laws of the prophets, the Gospel, and the apostolic doctrine. This [ignorance] accuses as a great and mortal sin, especially for prelates, bishops, and metropolitans <note>Bishops with authority over a provincial group of dioceses.</note>, who are bound to give an account of faith and hope. It **accuses** as a venial sin <note>A lesser, pardonable sin that does not break one's relationship with God.</note> simple and literate clerics who could have learned but were unwilling. It does not accuse laypeople at all, because it does not pertain to them, provided they do not hold it in contempt. **If** it is ignorance of positive law <term>original: *iuris positiui*; man-made law, such as civil or canon law</term>, such as the law and the decrees, it accuses only judges. **If** it is ignorance of the law pertaining to moral ecclesiastical statutes for discerning sins, it accuses priests and confessors. **If**, however, it is ignorance of fact, it either concerns a matter of indifference, in which case it excuses sufficiently; or it concerns what is necessary for salvation. And in this case, it either cannot be avoided, as in an insane person <term>original: *amente*</term> who from birth lacked the judgment of reason—and this excuses. Or it can be avoided, as in the Jews and pagans <term>original: *paganis*</term> who could have known that Christ suffered for the salvation of mankind—and this does not excuse.

¶ **The third division** of ignorance is this: one kind is simple, as when someone does not know what they are bound to know, yet does something—though not fully—to know it; this excuses to a degree, but not entirely. **Another** kind is gross or supine <term>original: *crassa* or *supina*; referring to lazy or negligent ignorance</term>, as when someone neglects to know what they are bound to know, even when they realize they are bound, yet it is not firmly decided for them that they must know those things; this excuses less, for it does not excuse entirely but only in part. **Another** is "affected" or intentional ignorance <term>original: *affectata*</term>, such as when...

### ¶ On Ignorance. ¶ Chapter 5

**I**t follows next to see the division of the part into offense <term>original: *delictum*; used here to mean a transgression against a positive command</term> and omission. The prevarication <note>A "prevarication" here means a turning aside or a violation of a religious duty.</note> of precepts is an offense; this is the transgression of affirmative commands <note>Commands to do something specific, like "Honor thy father."</note>. The prevarication of prohibitions is a commission; this is the violation of negative commands <note>Commands to avoid something, like "Thou shalt not kill."</note>. The different types of offense are ignorance, omission, and negligence. "Offense" is named as if it were a "dereliction": that is, the desertion of the good. The good is twofold: namely, that which is to be known—the desertion of which is called ignorance—and that which is to be done. Regarding what is to be done, two things are required: namely, that a good act be performed—the desertion of which is called omission—and that it be performed well, that is, with the proper circumstances—the desertion of which is called negligence. Concerning ignorance, a fourfold division is to be noted. The first is this: ignorance is threefold: namely, of negation, of privation, and of disposition. <meta>This page continues the scholastic breakdown of "ignorance" (ignorantia) as it relates to sin and moral responsibility. It specifically addresses how different types of ignorance—whether of the law, of facts, or of specific duties—affect a person's level of guilt or excuse before God.</meta>

<header>¶ Part One</header>

...those who ignore what they are able to know, or have forgotten what they once learned. ¶ **Ignorance of disposition** is when someone holds opinions contrary to the faith, which can be called erroneous ignorance; and this is found in heretics. <note>In medieval theology, "ignorance of disposition" refers to a state where the mind is wrongly "disposed" or inclined toward a false belief, rather than just being unaware of the truth.</note> ¶ The first type excuses entirely; the second excuses in part, but not entirely; the third accuses entirely, because it follows a perverse will. <note>The author is ranking types of ignorance from "simple lack of knowledge" to "willful rejection of truth."</note> ¶ The second division is this: some ignorance is of **fact**, and some is of **law**. 

Ignorance of the **Law** in this context refers to either **natural law**—and this does not excuse, since it is written in the heart of man—or it refers to **divine law**, such as the laws of the prophets, the Gospel, and apostolic doctrine. And this [ignorance of divine law] accuses as a great and mortal sin <term>mortal sin: a grave transgression that separates the soul from God's grace</term> the prelates, especially bishops and metropolitans, who are bound to give an account of faith and hope. However, it accuses as a venial sin <term>venial sin: a lesser sin that weakens but does not destroy the soul's relationship with God</term> those simple and literate clerics who, though they could have learned, were unwilling. ¶ As for the **laity**, it accuses them in nothing, because it does not pertain to them, provided they do not treat [knowledge] with contempt. ¶ If it is ignorance of **positive law**, such as specific laws and decrees, it accuses only the judges. ¶ If it is ignorance of law pertaining to the moral constitutions of the Church for the purpose of discerning sins, it accuses priests and confessors. 

¶ If indeed it is **ignorance of fact**: either it concerns an indifferent matter, and thus it excuses sufficiently; or it concerns something necessary for salvation. And in this case, either it cannot be avoided—as in a person suffering from madness who from birth lacked the judgment of reason—and this excuses; ¶ Or it can be avoided—as in the case of those who could have known that Christ suffered for the salvation of mankind; and this does not excuse. 

¶ The third division of ignorance is this: one kind is **simple**, as when someone does not know what they are bound to know, yet they do something (though not fully) to learn it; this excuses to an extent, but not entirely. ¶ Another is **crass** or **supine** <note>original: *crassa sive supina*; this refers to a lazy or negligent failure to learn something one is responsible for knowing</note>, as when someone neglects to know what they are bound to know, even though they are aware they are bound to it, yet it has not been specifically determined for them what they ought to know; this excuses less, for it does not excuse entirely, but only to an extent. ¶ Another is **affected** or **intentional** <note>original: *affectata sive affectiva*</note>, as when...

<meta>This page concludes the previous discussion on types of ignorance—specifically addressing how much guilt is attached to what we fail to know—and transitions into a new chapter regarding "omission" and "negligence" in the performance of duties.</meta>

...someone neglects to know what they are bound to know: since it is determined that they are bound to know those things, this accuses them rather than excuses them. 

¶ The fourth division is this: some ignorance is **vincible** <note>Vincible ignorance is a lack of knowledge that a person could overcome if they applied reasonable effort.</note> and some is **invincible** <note>Invincible ignorance is a lack of knowledge that a person has no way of overcoming at the time.</note>.

¶ **Vincible ignorance**: either it can be removed with great difficulty, as in the very dull-witted—this excuses to a degree, but not entirely, if it concerns things necessary for salvation. Or, it can be removed with ease, as in the clever; this does not excuse.

¶ **Invincible ignorance**: this is either invincible by nature, as in a child who does not yet use reason, or in the elderly who have lost their discernment, or in one born without their senses. This excuses entirely. ¶ Another type is invincible through one's own fault, such as in one who becomes a madman through their own vice; according to some, this does not excuse entirely.

# The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure
<note>The 'Centiloquium' (meaning 'One Hundred Sayings') is a systematic summary of theology attributed to the Franciscan scholar St. Bonaventure.</note>

### On Omission and Negligence
### Chapter 4

<image-desc>A large, square woodcut decorative initial 'V' with intricate interlaced floral and scroll motifs inside a double-lined border.</image-desc>

Having looked at **ignorance**, which is the abandonment of due knowledge, there follows a section on **omission** and **negligence**, which are called the abandonment of a due action that ought to be done.

¶ **Omission** is the abandonment of a required act within a determined time. And an act is called "required" (or "due") when we are bound to perform it. Regarding omission, we must consider four aspects: ¶ **Condition** ¶ **Distinction** ¶ **Description** ¶ **Absolution**.

It is said that omission is the abandonment of the act itself, not merely a circumstance; it concerns the principal act, not something merely attached to it; and it concerns an **affirmative precept**, not a negative one. <note>An "affirmative precept" is a command to *do* something (like 'keep the Sabbath holy'), whereas a "negative precept" is a command *not* to do something (like 'do not steal').</note>

¶ There is, however, a threefold obligation <term>obligation: the original Latin uses 'tencio', referring to a binding moral or legal duty</term> by which we are bound not to omit our duties:

¶ One is by reason of **divine obligation**, and this is twofold: one toward acting, as in affirmative precepts, such as "Honor your father" and so on. The other is toward *not* acting, as in negative precepts or prohibitions, such as "Thou shalt not kill" and so on.

The first [to act] binds always, but not for every single moment, <note>original: 'semper sed non ad semper'. In medieval logic, this means a law is always in effect, but you are only required to perform the action at appropriate times and places.</note> except according to place and time. The second [not to act] binds always and for every single moment. <note>Meaning one must *never* kill, at any time or in any place.</note>

¶ Another obligation is by reason of **spontaneous presentation**, as in contracts, vows, oaths, promises, and so on.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section on the types of ignorance and their moral weight, now transitioning into how conscience binds an individual through "tension" or moral obligation, and exploring the failures of negligence and sloth in spiritual duties.</meta>

# ¶ First Part

...obligation which conscience creates. ¶ There is a certain obligation by reason of a culpable offense. For because of a **mortal sin** <note>In Catholic theology, a grave sin that destroys the relationship with God.</note>, a person is obligated, or held, to eternal punishment; because of a **venial sin** <note>A lesser sin that does not fully break the relationship with God but requires purification.</note>, to temporal punishment. **Tension** is described in this way: **Tension** <note>original: "tensio"; here meaning a moral "binding" or "strain" of obligation.</note> is the obligation of a divine command, or a prohibition, or of one's own consent, or of a fault, toward acting, not acting, or enduring.

Toward acting in regard to affirmative commands; toward not acting in regard to negative commands and prohibitions; toward enduring in regard to a fault. ¶ There is, however, a threefold release from the aforementioned threefold tension: namely, impossibility, dispensation, and **penance** <note>original: "pnia," an abbreviation for "poenitentia."</note>.

**Negligence** is the abandonment of a required circumstance while performing a good work. Concerning this, three things must be briefly seen: its origin, its naming, and its accompaniment. Negligence is said to arise from torpor <note>A state of spiritual sluggishness or apathy.</note>, regarding which **Gregory** <note>St. Gregory the Great, a 6th-century Pope and influential theologian.</note> says: "Two things are to be feared in good works: namely, **sloth** <note>original: "desidia."</note> and **fraud** <note>original: "fraus."</note>." 

¶ Sloth is caused by a lesser love of God. ¶ Fraud is caused by an excessive love of oneself. Likewise, sloth is born through torpor; fraud is born through private affection. This fraud is defined thus: He commits fraud in the work of God who, loving himself inordinately, hastens toward the goods of transitory reward <note>The author suggests that doing a "good deed" just to get a temporary reward, like praise or money, is a form of spiritual fraud.</note>. 

¶ This fraud is committed in three ways: either for the sake of human favor, or for the "breeze" of popular acclaim, or when any exterior thing is desired. For in order that a good work be done well, a good **end** <note>The "end" refers to the ultimate purpose or motivation behind an action.</note> is required; he who omits this commits fraud. 

¶ Other circumstances are also required, such as place, time, and manner; he who omits these certainly neglects them. It is named with a twofold term: namely, **negligence** and **sloth**. If these are taken in a broad sense, they are common to any omission of both a required act and its circumstances. If, however, they are taken strictly for the omission of a circumstance, then sloth more concerns the sin...

<summary>This page defines the nature of moral obligation (tension) and the spiritual failures of negligence, sloth, and fraud. It emphasizes that a good deed is only truly "good" if it is performed with the right motive and under the correct circumstances.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual failures, moving from the definitions of negligence and sloth into the category of "commission"—the active performance of acts that violate divine or natural law. It builds on the previous page's exploration of moral "tension" by detailing how the will fails through sluggishness or direct transgression.</meta>

<header>¶ The Brief Word of Saint Bonaventure</header>

the meaning of sloth <note>original: "desidia," implying a state of idleness or sitting back.</note> as a kind of "ceasing." **Negligence**, however, more closely regards its proper meaning, namely the omission of a required circumstance; it is, as it were, "non-diligence," because diligence diligently pursues all necessary circumstances. **There are**, however, companions of negligence: torpor and laziness <note>original: "pigritia."</note>. Although these are the same, "laziness" is named from the fact that the difficulty of labor is shrinking <note>original: "pigeat"; the root suggests a sense of reluctance or being irked by effort.</note>. **Torpor**, however, regards the desire for rest. **Torpor** or laziness is **twofold**. First, from a natural infirmity, such as in those of a phlegmatic <note>In medieval medicine, the "phlegmatic" temperament was thought to be naturally slow or cool.</note> temperament; this is a penalty and not a fault. **Secondly**, there is the torpor of an infirmity acquired from a preceding fault, as in the drunk and the gluttonous; according to some, such a state does not excuse the person, and it is lustful. It is called "deliberate torpor," and that torpor is the will refusing to do a difficult good thing simply because it is difficult. Such torpor or laziness, since it is a sin, is rebuked in the Scriptures.

### **Concerning Commission**

<margin>Chapter 1</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' with leafy/floral ornamentation inside a rectangular border.</image-desc>
**I**t follows next to consider "commission" <note>original: "commisso"; in theology, a "sin of commission" is an active deed that breaks a law, whereas a "sin of omission" is a failure to do a required good deed.</note>. Concerning this, it is to be briefly seen and added that it is a transgression of the divine law which prohibits certain things through negative precepts <note>For example, "Thou shalt not kill" is a negative precept.</note>. Moreover, an act can be committed against nature. **And** this occurs in two ways: namely, against the nature shared by humans and beasts, such as when a male acts in place of a female. **And** this is the sin of sodomy. **Or** it is against a specific nature, such as in the angelic or human nature, and this is the "natural judgment" <note>original: "naturale iudicativum"; the innate ability to distinguish right from wrong.</note> which dictates that evil is to be avoided and good is to be done. **This** nature dictates that we ought to obey God, worship Him, and love Him. **It also dictates** that we ought to do to our neighbor <note>original: "proximo."</note> what we wish to be done to us by the same, **and** what we do not wish to be done to us, we ought not to inflict on another. Thus, in a broad sense, every sin that is committed is "against nature." **It can** also be committed against the law. **And** this varies in two ways: for the law prohibits something in regard to...

<header># The First Part</header>

time. And in this way, something is prohibited because it is evil, and such are the prohibitions of the **Decalogue** <note>The Ten Commandments.</note>. It [the law] also prohibits something not simply [for all time] but for a time; and such are the prohibitions of the **ceremonial laws**; and in this case, the thing is evil because it is prohibited <note>A classic legal distinction: some things are "evil in themselves" (malum in se), while others are only "evil because they are forbidden" (malum prohibitum) for the sake of discipline or ritual.</note>. Something can also be committed against **custom**; and this is what is committed against morals. Strictly speaking, however, unwritten moral practice is called "custom" (*consuetudo*), while a written moral practice is named a "constitution" (*constitutio*). 

Note that to act against a custom that preserves **honor** <note>original: "honesti," referring to moral integrity or that which is intrinsically worthy of respect.</note> is a sin; to act against a custom that destroys honor is holy; to act against an indifferent custom is not an evil. 

¶ Furthermore, the **Honorable** is that which draws us by its own power and allures us by its own dignity. 

¶ Also note that the law regards the Honorable; custom or constitution regards the **useful**, which is the preservation of the Honorable.

### **¶ Concerning error, bad judgment, and respect of persons. Chapter 10**

There follows the division of **sin** into sin of the **heart**, of the **mouth**, and of the **deed** <note>original: "cordis, oris, et operis"—the three traditional ways one commits a sin in Catholic theology.</note>. And first, something concerning the **sin of the heart**, which is briefly defined thus: 

¶ The sin of the heart is a transgression of the divine law through the disordered state of the mind's internal movement. 

¶ Its categories are the sin of **thought**, of **delight**, and of **consent**. 

¶ Regarding the sin of delight, it was seen above when actual sin was treated. 

¶ To the sin of thought belong **error**, **bad judgment**, and **respect of persons** <note>original: "acceptio personarum," meaning partiality or showing favoritism.</note>. 

¶ But the sin of consent leads back to the sin of conscience. 

¶ Regarding the sin of thought, we must first consider thought itself, then bad judgment, and finally the respect of persons. 

¶ Of thoughts, one kind is purely **speculative**; and this is not a sin. Another is **practical** or **affective**. 

¶ The first is within the mind's view and is involuntary as far as itself is concerned <note>The author suggests that a stray thought passing through the mind is not a sin unless the will engages with it.</note>. The second is in the [mind's] gaze with affection; it is voluntary, and therefore it can be a sin. 

¶ A certain kind of **error** belongs to the infirm, such as in those suffering from fever, to whom sweet things seem bitter. A certain [other kind]...

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Chapter 10, following the introduction of sins categorized by heart, mouth, and deed. Having established that internal sins start in the heart, Bonaventure here analyzes the first of these: error and the failure of human judgment.</meta>

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

is a result of original weakness: by which reason is corrupted in judgments and frequently goes astray. This error, because it is not voluntary <note>That is, it is an unintentional byproduct of human fallen nature rather than a choice.</note>, is not a sin but rather the penalty of sin. ¶ There is another error of a perverse will, which consists in holding false things as true. This error is either against the faith or good morals, and that is always a sin. If it is not against the faith or good morals, it either occurs in those things that are beyond human judgment—such as judging the intentions of men or their hearts—and this is always a sin: because then one usurps what belongs to God alone. 

¶ Or it occurs in those things that are subject to human judgment: such as erring in matters of logic, natural sciences, or mathematics, and then it is not a sin <note>Bonaventure distinguishes between moral/theological errors (sinful) and intellectual or academic mistakes (not sinful).</note>. 

¶ **Bad judgment** admits of a definition and a distinction. It is defined thus: **Bad judgment** is a rash sentence or a suspicion of evil concerning the hidden things of others, proceeding from error. 

¶ **Error** is holding unknown things as known. A **Sentence** is the act of asserting or defining that something is so. **Suspicion** is holding an opinion with doubt, or rashly passing judgment on things that do not pertain to oneself. 

¶ Furthermore, human judgment is distinguished in this way: one judgment is of certainty; another of rashness; another of suspicion. The first, namely of **certainty**, is not a sin because we may lawfully judge regarding manifest <note>original: "manifestis," meaning things that are obvious, visible, or clearly proven.</note> things. 

¶ The second, the **judgment of rashness**, is threefold: namely, of definition, of rebuke, and of assertion. 

Of **definition**, when someone judges wrongly. To despair of someone’s correction and to rebuke him as a castaway <note>original: "abiectum," someone thrown away or deemed worthless.</note> who now appears evil—when we do not know what he will be in the future—is a rash judgment. 

¶ Of **rebuke** <term>arguitio: a formal challenge or calling someone to account</term>, when someone judges concerning things that do not pertain to him. Romans 14: *Who are you to judge someone else’s servant?* 

¶ Of **assertion**, when someone judges regarding hidden things. The **Gloss** <note>The "Glossa Ordinaria," the standard medieval commentary on the Bible.</note> on the aforementioned passage states: "Regarding those things where it is uncertain with what spirit they are done, no one judges without danger." **Augustine**.

<summary>This page analyzes the nature of human error and judgment, distinguishing between unintentional intellectual mistakes and sinful moral judgments. It specifically warns against "rash judgment," which involves usurping God's role by judging the secret intentions of others.</summary>

<keywords>error, judgment, rash judgment, original sin, logic, intentions of the heart, Saint Augustine, Romans 14, suspicion</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of judgment and error from the previous section. Having established the definition of bad judgment as a "rash sentence," the author now explores different categories of suspicion and the sin of partiality (acceptance of persons), drawing on authorities like the Venerable Bede and Scripture.</meta>

# First part

or Bede <note>The Venerable Bede (c. 672–735), a Northumbrian monk and influential theologian.</note>, on that passage of Matthew 7, "Do not judge," <note>original: "nolite iudicare"</note> says that in this place nothing else is commanded of us except that those deeds which are doubtful as to the intent with which they are done should be interpreted in the better part. The first is pernicious, the second is presumptuous, and the third is superstitious.

¶ The third [type of] judgment, namely of **suspicion**, is threefold:
1.  **Violent suspicion**: when all signs of evil appear and none or few of good.
2.  **Rash suspicion**: when there are few signs of good or evil.
3.  **Light suspicion**: when there are many signs of evil and few of good.

In the **First** case, it is not a sin <note>Presumably because the evidence is so overwhelming that the observer is forced to the conclusion.</note>. In the **second**, it is a grave venial sin. In the **third**, it is a light or small venial sin.

**Note** that a judgment of certainty is neither a venial nor a mortal sin. The judgment of rashness is measured according to that passage in Matthew 7: "In the judgment with which you judge, you shall be judged." The gloss <note>The "Glossa Ordinaria," the standard medieval commentary on the Bible.</note> says: "If you judge someone rashly with a venial condemnation, you will be venially condemned; if with a mortal condemnation, you will be mortally condemned." And understand this without prejudice. A judgment of suspicion, as long as it remains a suspicion, is not a mortal sin unless one arrives at a judgment of assertion <note>That is, when one stops merely doubting and begins to firmly assert the evil of another without proof.</note>.

Regarding the **acceptance of persons** <term>acceptance of persons: "Personarum acceptio," the sin of partiality or showing favoritism based on a person’s status, wealth, or appearance rather than their actual merits or the truth.</term>, one must consider the definition and a threefold difference. It is defined thus: the **acceptance of persons** is an injustice which prefers one person over another for an improper cause.

The differences of the acceptance of persons are three: 
*   One is committed in the **showing of honor**, concerning which James 2 says: "My brothers, do not hold the faith of Jesus Christ with acceptance of persons."
*   Another is in the **execution of judgment**, concerning which Deuteronomy 16 says: "You shall not accept a person in judgment," and so forth.
*   Another is in the **promotion to dignities**, which is prohibited in 1 Kings 16 <note>In modern Bibles, this is 1 Samuel 16.</note> in the election of David, whom the Lord preferred over his brothers, not on account of the beauty of their faces or the height of their stature.

<summary>This page analyzes the moral weight of suspicion, categorizing it by the strength of evidence, and defines the sin of partiality (acceptance of persons) in social, legal, and ecclesiastical contexts.</summary>
<keywords>judgment, suspicion, venial sin, partiality, acceptance of persons, Matthew 7, James 2, King David, Venerable Bede</keywords>
