<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on judgment and suspicion to the nature of internal consent and the role of conscience in determining sin. It follows the systematic breakdown of how a thought becomes a sinful act.</meta>

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure 
<note>original: "Centiloquii sancti Bonauenture." The *Centiloquium* is a "collection of one hundred points," a common medieval format for summarizing theological doctrine.</note>

<margin>Chapter 4</margin>
## ¶ On the sin of consent

**I**t follows to consider the sin of consent, where first it must be shown what consent itself is; second, concerning sin through conscience; and third, concerning the complex differences. Consent is defined thus: Consent is a spontaneous assent of the will, or a free movement of the mind regarding itself. And since nothing is so much in our power as the ability to consent or to dissent, it is for this reason that every sin is consummated <note>Completed or finalized.</note> there. For sin is initiated in thought, it grows in delight, but it is completed <note>The OCR "percitur" is likely a contraction for *perficitur*, meaning "is perfected" or "brought to completion."</note> in consent and the will. To understand how one sins when acting against conscience, the multiplicity <note>The various definitions or layers.</note> of conscience must be considered. For conscience is spoken of in one way as the higher part of reason, which the Greeks call <term>synderesis</term> <note>Synderesis: In medieval theology, this is the innate, infallible spark of moral consciousness that always inclines toward the good.</note>, and among us can be called the "spark of conscience." This is defined by Basil <note>Saint Basil the Great (c. 330–379), a Greek bishop and doctor of the Church.</note> thus: Conscience is the natural judgment of what is to be done or not to be done. However, synderesis and conscience differ in their logic. For synderesis judges what *should* be done, that is, what is worthy of being done. Conscience, however, judges what *must* be done, that is, what is a duty to be done. In another way, conscience is called a natural habit of dictating good or evil, and it is thus defined by Damascene <note>Saint John of Damascus (c. 675–749), a Syrian monk and theologian.</note>: Conscience is the law of our intellect, naturally opposed to sin. And according to this definition, conscience never errs. In a third way, conscience is called an opinion held regarding things to be done or not to be done—that is, a formed opinion regarding duties. Of this, it is said in 1 Corinthians 8: "Some with the conscience of the idol until now [eat...]" <note>original: "quidaʒ cum conscia ydoli usq̃ nūc." Paul is discussing those who eat meat sacrificed to idols while still feeling internally that it is a religious act.</note> and in Romans 14: "Everything that is not from faith is sin," <note>original: "Omne quod non est ex fide peccatum est."</note> which means whatever is against conscience. In this way, conscience is an acquired habit, frequently erroneous in judging that something should be done which should not be done, and vice versa. Now, conscience (spoken of in this third way) can dictate something according to the law of God, beside the law of God, or against the law...

<summary>This page defines 'consent' as the final stage of sin and distinguishes between 'synderesis' (the infallible moral spark) and 'conscience' (the applied judgment which can err).</summary>
<keywords>consent, conscience, synderesis, Saint Basil, John of Damascus, moral theology, will, sin, habit</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on conscience, moving from the definition of synderesis to the practical application of the "dictate of conscience" and how it governs moral action, even when erroneous.</meta>

<header>¶ First Part</header>

...of God. And here the discourse concerns the dictate <note>A "dictate" (Latin: *dictamen*) is the final conclusion of practical reason regarding what should be done here and now.</note> of conscience by way of a precept or prohibition, rather than by way of counsel or persuasion.

¶ In the first way, conscience binds simply and universally because it agrees with the law of God, which conscience shows to be binding. 

¶ In the second way, conscience binds as long as it remains. Hence, a person is bound either to lay it aside or to fulfill what it dictates through action—for example, if conscience dictates that picking up a straw from the ground is necessary for salvation <note>This is a classic example of "scrupulosity," where a person incorrectly believes a trivial act is a moral obligation.</note>. 

¶ In the third way, conscience does not bind a person to doing or not doing something, but rather to laying the conscience aside. Because when such a conscience is erroneous and that error is necessarily repugnant to divine law, as long as it lasts, it places a person outside the state of salvation. For instance, if a harlot <note>original: *leccatrix*</note> says to someone, "I shall die if you do not know me" <note>Here, "to know" is used in the biblical sense of sexual intimacy.</note>, and that man begins to have a conscience of mortal sin if he does not "know" the harlot—he is not in a state of salvation while that conscience persists. For if he does not know her, he sins mortally by despising God while his conscience dictates that this <note>letting her die</note> does not displease the Lord; and if he does know her, he acts against the divine precept <note>The commandment against adultery/fornication.</note>.

¶ Perplexity <note>A state of moral "deadlock" where every choice seems to lead to sin.</note> arises from the error of conscience. There is, however, a threefold perplexity:

¶ The first is of the understanding of the words of Holy Scripture, which seem to contradict themselves. 

¶ The second is of the understanding of what is to be done, concerning which it is said in Romans 8: "For we know not what we should pray for" <note>original: *Quid oremus? nescimus*</note>, etc. 

¶ The third is the inevitability of sinning.

¶ The first perplexity is resolved through distinction and consideration of the reasons for saying both sides. 

¶ The second is resolved through the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, which is the "anointing teaching about all things" <note>Reference to 1 John 2:27.</note> through the merit of prayer, as in James 1: "If any of you lack wisdom," etc. And in 2 Chronicles 20 <note>The OCR reads "1 Paralipomenon 26," but the quote is from 2 Paralipomenon (Chronicles) 20:12.</note>: "When we know not what we ought to do" <note>original: *Cum ignoramus quid agere debeamus*</note>, etc. 

¶ The third is resolved by laying aside the erroneous conscience.

## ¶ Chapter 12.
### ¶ On lying, perjury, and contention.

<vocab>conscience (conscientia), dictate (dictamen), perplexity (perplexitas), scripture (scriptura), sin (peccatum), lie (mendacium), perjury (periurium), contention (contentio)</vocab>

<summary>This page analyzes how conscience binds an individual to action, explaining that even an erroneous conscience must be resolved or followed to avoid the sin of acting against one's own moral judgment. It also introduces the concept of moral "perplexity" and how spiritual wisdom and divine inspiration resolve such dilemmas.</summary>

<keywords>conscience, moral obligation, erroneous conscience, perplexity, Holy Spirit, Saint Bonaventure, medieval ethics, biblical interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of moral theology from the previous section. Having established how the "dictate of conscience" governs our internal choices, Bonaventure now moves to the "sins of the mouth," categorizing how speech can violate truth and goodness.</meta>

<header>St. Bonaventure’s *Lenten Discourse* | First Part</header>

¶ **I**t follows to consider the sin of the mouth, which is varied by many different categories. For the sins of the mouth are **lying**, in which truth is destroyed; and **perjury**, in which truth is despised. ¶ **Contention** <note>original: *contemptio* in the transcription, but defined as *contentio* (strife)</note>, in which truth is attacked—for contention is the attacking of truth with the confidence of shouting. These three are especially against truth. ¶ **The following** are against goodness: **scurrility** <note>buffoonery or coarse, unseemly joking</note>, which is against decency. ¶ **Flattery**, which is against rectitude in the praise of others. ¶ **Boasting**, which is against rectitude in one’s own praise. ¶ **Blasphemy**, which is against rectitude in the reviling of God. ¶ **Cursing**, which is against rectitude in the reviling of one’s neighbor regarding punishment. ¶ **Insult** <note>original: *contumelia*</note>, which is against rectitude in the open reviling of a neighbor regarding a fault. ¶ **Detraction**, which is against rectitude in the hidden reviling of a neighbor regarding a fault. ¶ **Concerning** lying, it is to be viewed according to its definition and its divisions. ¶ **It is defined** by **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), the primary authority for Bonaventure's ethics of speech</note> thus: **Lying** is a false signaling of the voice with the intention of deceiving. ¶ **And note** that since "to lie" (*mentiri*) is to "go against the mind" (*contra mentem ire*), a person can lie by speaking a falsehood from certain knowledge. ¶ **Likewise**, a person can lie by telling the truth: because although what he speaks is true, he feels otherwise in his conscience. ¶ **Likewise**, a person can speak a falsehood without lying or sinning: for instance, when he is mistaken, provided he has applied due diligence. ¶ **Lying is divided** by a three-part division, namely: the dutiful lie, the harmful lie, and the joking lie. ¶ **The dutiful** <note>original: *officiosum*</note> is that which helps. ¶ **The harmful** <note>original: *perniciosum*</note> is that which hurts. ¶ **The joking** <note>original: *iocosum*</note> is that which delights. ¶ **It is also divided** by an eight-fold division as follows: there are eight kinds of lies. ¶ **The first** is that which occurs in the teaching of religion, such as saying Christ was not born of the Virgin Mary. **The second** is that which profits no one and harms someone, such as the lie of detraction, or if someone acts as a false witness in a criminal case. **The third** is that which

<column-break/>

harms someone so that it may profit someone else: for instance, if someone is a false witness in a financial case. **The fourth** is that which is done from the mere lust for lying. **The fifth** is that which is done from a desire to please or to delight with sweet speech, such as the lie of flattery. **The sixth** is that by which someone lies to preserve someone’s money. **The seventh** is that by which someone lies to preserve another’s life. **The eighth** is that by which someone lies to preserve another’s chastity. ¶ In none of these cases should one lie <note>Medieval theologians like Bonaventure, following Augustine, held a "rigorist" view: lying is an intrinsic evil and is never permitted, even to save a life.</note>. ¶ Moreover, **Augustine** gives this rule: the more a lie approaches the first kind <note>religious heresy</note>, the greater the sin; the more it recedes from the first kind, the lesser the sin. ¶ **After** lying, by which truth is destroyed, follows **perjury**, by which truth is despised. ¶ **But** because perjury is a violation of an oath, we must first look at the **oath**, either as to its essence: and thus to swear is to invoke God as a witness to one’s speech; or as to its lawfulness: and thus it is lawful to swear to preserve peace and justice, or to strengthen friendship. ¶ **To establish** the truth: and in this way Paul frequently swore. ¶ **To avoid** false accusation <note>original: *calumpnia*</note>: just as the plaintiff and defendant swear. ¶ **To preserve** loyalty: in this way princes frequently swear to a king. ¶ **Or** as to its form: and thus there is a permitted form, such as swearing by God. And this has varied. ¶ **For** in the Old Testament it was said: "As the Lord lives," that is, by the living God. ¶ **In** the early Church it was said: "God is my witness," that is, I invoke God as witness. ¶ **But now** it is said: "By God." ¶ **And** there is a form not permitted: such as swearing by idols. ¶ **As for** its "companions" <note>This refers to the three qualities required for a valid, non-sinful oath: truth, judgment, and justice.</note>: it is said that an oath ought to have three companions, namely **truth**, which ought to be in the conscience so that one knows it to be so...

<summary>Bonaventure categorizes various "sins of the mouth" such as lying, flattery, and blasphemy, providing a detailed hierarchy of lies based on St. Augustine's teachings. He also begins a legal and theological analysis of oaths, defining when it is lawful to swear and the proper forms for doing so.</summary>
<keywords>sins of speech, lying, perjury, St. Augustine, flattery, detraction, oaths, moral theology, medieval ethics</keywords>

# ¶ St. Bonaventure’s Lenten Discourse

**Judgment**, so that one may swear discreetly and with deliberation. **Justice**, so that what is sworn is lawful and honorable. **Regarding** the differences, there are two: namely, an **assertory oath**, as when one swears regarding the present or the past that something is or was; and a **promissory oath**, when someone swears to do something in the future. **Now** the definition of **perjury** can be seen, which is: perjury is a lie confirmed by an oath. **A distinction** can also be seen. **First**, ¶ **Perjury** is called a false swearing. **Second**, it is a perverse swearing, which occurs when that which is sworn is unlawful. **Third**, it is called the transgression of an oath <note>breaking a promise previously made under oath</note>.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring a scrolling vine and floral pattern within a rectangular frame.</image-desc>

**It follows** to consider flattery, cursing, and detraction, which are committed against the rectitude <note>the moral uprightness or proper standard</note> of praise and blame. **Flattery**, however, is defined thus: **Flattery** is a speech of praise offered to someone with the intention of pleasing. It is **distinguished** thus: **For** there is a certain flattery that attributes a good quality which a person does not actually possess. **Another kind** is that which extols <note>original: "extolitt"</note> a quality by saying <note>original: "dendo," likely "dicendo"</note> it is greater than it actually is. **Another kind** is that which nourishes evil by praising the bad, saying it is good and lawful. **Psalm**: "The sinner is praised in the desires of his soul," etc. <note>Psalm 10:3 (Vulgate 9:24)</note>. **Another kind** is that by which a man wishes to please another man—though through true praise—yet not for the sake of God. **Galatians 1**: "If I yet pleased men, I should not be the servant of Christ," that is, if I wished to please them not for the sake of God. ¶ **Cursing** <note>original: "maledictum"</note> varies in four ways. **There is** the curse of surreption <note>a curse uttered through oversight or a sudden slip of the tongue</note>, of which **Augustine** says: "If we curse with all ease and rashness, it pertains to a minimal sin." **The curse** of playfulness <note>original: "lasciuie," referring here to jesting or frivolous speech rather than sexual immorality</note> is when we curse someone not from the heart; regarding which **Jerome** <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), known for his Bible translation and moral commentaries</note> says: "He who is neglected..."

<summary>This page defines perjury as a lie confirmed by oath and categorizes flattery into four types based on the speaker's intent and the truth of the praise. It also begins a classification of cursing, distinguishing between impulsive slips and jesting.</summary>
<keywords>perjury, oaths, flattery, cursing, moral theology, St. Bonaventure, St. Augustine, St. Jerome, sins of the mouth</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on "sins of the mouth." The previous page concluded by introducing the various ways speech can be misused. This section completes the analysis of cursing and detraction (slander) before moving into Chapter 13, which examines sins committed through physical actions.</meta>

...to cut off the habit of a cursed mouth <note>original: "oris maledicti"</note>; for even if the heart does not intend to curse, one still incurs the uncleanness of the lips and the defilement of the mouth. **There** is also the "curse of hatred," which proceeds from an evil heart out of a desire for vengeance. The fourth type is the "curse of justice," which proceeds from charity—that is, when we curse someone who is a friend to us for the sake of their correction. **Regarding** these two last types, Gregory <note>St. Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), a foundational theologian for medieval moral teaching.</note> says: "Sacred Scripture mentions cursing in two ways: one that it approves and another that it condemns. For it is uttered differently when prompted by a love for justice than when prompted by a love for vengeance."

¶ *Detraction is defined in this way:* **Detraction** is the blackening of another person's reputation. **It** is committed in six ways: 
1. When we deny a good act that we know was done.
2. When we keep silent about a good act so that it is not made known to others. 
3. When we pervert a good act by claiming it was done with a bad intention. 
4. By revealing a hidden evil in a case where it was not previously known. 
5. By exaggerating, saying a fault is greater than it actually is. 
6. By deceitfully inventing an evil act that was never actually committed. 

**The** detractor is held to the **restitution** <note>term: "restitutio" — the legal and moral requirement to give back what was stolen. In this context, the sinner must publicly restore the victim's reputation to be forgiven.</note> of the good name which they took away. **¶ For** the detractor takes away a greater good than a physical thief; a sin is not forgiven unless that which was taken away is restored.

### Concerning the sin of deed ¶ Chapter 13.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'D' featuring foliate and floral scrolls within a square frame.</image-desc>

**Regarding** the sin of deed <note>term: "peccatum operis" — a sin committed through physical action, as opposed to thought or speech.</note>, we must briefly look at its definition and how it is distinguished from an offense of the will, as well as what it adds to the malice of the heart. **A** sin of deed is a transgression of the divine law through the disordered outward movement of the mind. To the category of "sin of deed" is also reduced the **sin of habit** <note>original: "peccatum consuetudinis" — sins that have become reflexive or ingrained through repetition.</note>; for we sin through thought, delight, consent, word, deed, and habit. **¶ Now** a sin of deed is compared to a sin of the internal will insofar as they are joined together, so that they are considered a single sin because there is one act of contempt and one...

c.i. <note>Signature mark indicating the start of the third gathering of pages.</note>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of sin, specifically focusing on the relationship between the internal will and the external act. It concludes the previous chapter’s analysis of how an action adds weight to a sinful intention before transitioning into a general overview of the Seven Capital Vices.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
-> Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure <-

...aversion <note>term: "aversio" — the act of turning away from God, which medieval theologians saw as the essence of sin.</note>: it is said they are one [sin] when speaking formally, since that aversion is the preceding disorder. However, speaking materially, there are several sins because the disordered parts are multiple: namely, the interior and exterior will. But if these two work together as described, so that one person sins by will alone and another by both will and deed, then they are diverse sins, not only materially but also formally. Indeed, the malice of the deed is added to the malice of the will when the evil will does not want the work to proceed, because then the consent is not entirely full. For if the act of the deed follows contempt, then the aversion and the conversion <note>term: "conversio" — in this context, the turning toward a created thing or "creature" in place of the Creator.</note> are completed. If, however, the evil will wishes to proceed to the deed but cannot, then a distinction must be made. For one is judged greater than another in three ways: either by reason of greater contempt, and in such a case the deed does not add to the iniquity of the will; or by reason of greater pleasure and lust, and in this way the deed aggravates the sin, especially regarding the genus of carnal sin, because one sins in the senses. Or, it is by reason of greater damage, and in this way the deed adds to the malice of the will, especially regarding the genus of sin by which one sins against their neighbor. For no matter how much someone might want to commit fornication, they do not find as much pleasure in the will as they do in the deed. And no matter how much someone might want to kill, they do not do as much harm by willing it as they do by actually killing.

### ¶ Concerning the seven capital vices in general. ¶ Chapter 14

**It** follows to see the division of sin into the seven capital vices, which are: Pride, Envy, Anger, Sloth <note>original: "accidia" — a spiritual apathy or "noon-day devil" that makes religious duties feel burdensome.</note>, Greed, Gluttony, and Lust. Concerning these, we must first look at them in general, and then at each one specifically. In general, there are three things to consider regarding the capital vices: namely, the different naming, the connected ordering, and the sufficient assignment <note>The logical way the sins are categorized and attributed to different causes.</note>. **By Blessed Gregory** <note>St. Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), who standardized the list of Seven Deadly Sins.</note> and other doctors they are named as above. **By Isidore** <note>St. Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), whose "Etymologies" was a standard reference for medieval scholars.</note> they are named thus: Gluttony <note>original: "Castrimergia" — a term derived from Greek meaning "stomach-madness" or excessive indulgence.</note>...

<summary>This page analyzes how physical actions aggravate the sin of the will through increased pleasure or harm to others, and introduces the Seven Capital Vices, citing the naming conventions of Gregory and Isidore.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the will relates to the outward act, specifically finishing the word "fornication" from the previous page's final sentence. It transitions into a formal categorization of the Capital Vices (the Seven Deadly Sins), citing the influential views of Saint Isidore of Seville and Pope Gregory the Great.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
## First Part
-> c.ij. <-

...ication <note>The word begins on the previous page as "forni-".</note> / greed <note>original: "philargia" — from the Greek for "love of money."</note> / sorrow / sloth <note>term: "accidia" — spiritual apathy or a lack of care for one's religious duties.</note> / vainglory <note>original: "zenodoxia" — from the Greek for "empty glory."</note> / pride.
¶ Likewise, Isidore <note>Saint Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), a scholar whose encyclopedic works were standard textbooks in the Middle Ages.</note> distinguished between pride and vainglory. But Gregory <note>Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), who established the standard list of Seven Deadly Sins.</note> joined them together because they are the same in reality, though they differ in their definition. Pride is said to occur when someone appears great to themselves within their own soul. ¶ Vainglory is when someone strives to appear great for the sake of praise in the sight of others.
Similarly, Isidore distinguished between sloth and sorrow. But Gregory joined them: because they are the same in reality and differ only in definition. For it is the same movement in both sloth and sorrow, differing only by the reason of their "terms" <note>In scholastic logic, "terms" refers to the starting point or ending point of a movement or change.</note>. For according to the turning away <note>original: "aversionem"</note> and withdrawal from a difficult or burdensome height, it is called sorrow; while according to the approach and turning toward <note>original: "conversionem"</note> what is easy or "light," it is called sloth. For example: because it is burdensome for a certain man to rise for matins <note>Matins: the early morning prayer service of the Divine Office, often held before dawn.</note>, the sorrowful man turns himself away from the task. But because it is easy to rest, the slothful man turns himself toward the ease. ¶ Moreover, the capital vices <note>term: "vicia capitalia" — the Seven Deadly Sins; "capital" comes from "caput" (head), meaning these are the source-sins from which others grow.</note> are ordered according to a connection by which one is produced from another. For the proud man does not wish to be subject to superiors; he wishes to be preferred over equals; he wishes to trample on inferiors. And if he cannot do this, he envies his superiors because they rule, his equals because they prosper, and his inferiors because they are exalted. Therefore, since he achieves nothing by this, he easily conceives anger toward his superiors, peers, and inferiors. Then he falls into sloth, by which the peace of mind is disturbed. From then on, when he has lost his interior consolation, he turns himself toward exterior things through greed. And having thus become immoderately wealthy, he stuffs his belly through gluttony <note>original: "ingluviem"</note>; and finally, he dissolves his flesh into lust <note>original: "luxuriam"</note>. He is thus depraved from the highest peak of reason down to the lowest part of the flesh. Thus it is clear that the first five are spiritual <note>Pride, Envy, Anger, Sloth, Greed.</note>, while the last two are carnal <note>Gluttony and Lust.</note>.

<summary>This page explains the definitions and relationships of the Seven Capital Vices, reconciling the different classification systems of St. Isidore and St. Gregory. It describes the "chain of vice," showing how pride inevitably leads to a descent into carnal sins like gluttony and lust.</summary>
<keywords>Capital Vices, Seven Deadly Sins, Pride, Sloth, Gregory the Great, Isidore of Seville, spiritual sins, carnal sins, moral psychology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the classification of the Seven Deadly Sins (Capital Vices) from the previous section. It moves from a general list to a psychological explanation of why these specific seven are considered "sufficient" to describe all human failings, then begins Chapter 15 to define technical theological terms.</meta>

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure
<note>original: "Centiloquium" — literally "a hundred sayings," a common title for medieval theological summaries.</note>

The sufficiency of the seven capital vices is understood according to their proximity to an apparent good. This proximity, if it regards the spirit in itself, can be fourfold, following the four conditions of a "good" toward which the human appetite is inclined: ¶ For one desires a thing under the reasoning of **dignity**, and thus it is **pride**. ¶ Or by the reasoning of **exclusivity** <note>original: "proprietatis" — the desire to possess something solely for oneself.</note>, and thus it is **envy**. ¶ Or under the reasoning of **liberty** <note>The OCR reads "curitatis," likely a transcription error for "libertatis." In Bonaventure's system, Wrath (ira) is the disordered desire for "liberty" or unhindered action.</note>, and thus it is **wrath**. ¶ Or under the reasoning of **rest and tranquility**, and thus it is **sloth** <note>original: "accidia."</note>. ¶ If, however, the proximity is in respect to a good which regards the flesh, or the spirit within the flesh, it is threefold: ¶ For the carnal spirit desires a good under the reasoning of **sufficiency**, and thus it is **greed** <note>original: "avaritia."</note>; or under the reasoning of **pleasure** <note>original: "demulcentis" — literally "that which soothes or caresses."</note>, and thus it is **lust**; or under the reasoning of **refreshment**, and thus it is **gluttony**. ¶ From this, the number and the sufficiency of the capital vices are made clear.

## ¶ On the difference between a capital vice, the beginning of sin, and the root of evils. ¶ Chapter 15

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'A' featuring ornate foliage patterns within a rectangular frame.</image-desc>Attention must be paid to the difference between a **capital vice**, the **beginning of sin**, and the **root of all evils**. ¶ A vice is called **capital** because, just as the head <note>original: "caput" — the Latin root of "capital."</note> is the principal and complete member and the source of the other members regarding movement and sensation, so a vice which is called "capital" signifies a complete sin from which other vices arise. ¶ However, the **beginning** of sin is properly named as that in which the act is commenced. ¶ The **root**, truly, is that from which nourishment and strength are drawn. ¶ Hence, since sin draws its nourishment from the side of the "desirable thing" and implies a withdrawal from God, the **beginning** of sin is considered from the perspective of turning away <note>term: "aversio" — the act of turning one's back on God's light.</note>. ¶ The **root**, however, is considered from the perspective of turning toward <note>term: "conversio" — the act of turning one's desire toward temporary, created things instead of the Creator.</note>. ¶ And thus, the "turning away" is by reason of **contempt**. The "turning toward," however, is by reason of **cupidity** <note>Greed or illicit desire.</note> and **lust**. ¶ And for this reason, pride is called the beginning of all...

<summary>This page explains the psychological motives behind the Seven Deadly Sins—categorizing them as spiritual or carnal desires—and begins a technical distinction between a sin's source, its beginning, and its root.</summary>
<keywords>Seven Deadly Sins, Saint Bonaventure, Scholasticism, Pride, Envy, Wrath, Sloth, Greed, Lust, Gluttony, Aversion, Conversion, Capital Vice</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical definitions of sin, moving from the categorization of the Seven Deadly Sins to an analysis of their origins—specifically exploring the concepts of the "beginning" and the "root" of all moral failings.</meta>

<header>First Part</header>

¶ **Greed** <note>original: "Cupiditas." While often translated as "avarice" or "greed for money," in this medieval context, it refers to a general disordered desire for any temporal thing.</note> is said to be the **root of all evils**. Furthermore, the "beginning" of every sin is spoken of in a threefold way: 

First, it is named according to the withdrawal of free will from the law of majesty; in this sense, **pride** or **contempt** is called the beginning of sin. For whoever transgresses the precept of the Creator begins from pride and contempt. 

Second, it is named according to the law of truth; in this sense, **error** is called the beginning of sin. As the wise man says: *Every evil person is an ignorant one* <note>A common medieval paraphrase of Aristotle's ethics, suggesting that moral failure starts with an intellectual mistake about what is truly good.</note>. And truly, sin begins from ignorance and error, because one deserts eternal things for the sake of earthly ones. 

Third, it is named according to the law of goodness; in this sense, **folly** <note>original: "insipientia" — the opposite of "sapientia" (wisdom).</note> is called the beginning of sin. Regarding this, **Chrysostom** <note>John Chrysostom, a 4th-century Church Father known for his eloquent preaching.</note>, commenting on the verse from the Psalms, *by reason of my folly* <note>original: "ps. a facie insipientie mee" — referring to Psalm 38:5 (Vulgate numbering).</note>, says that the beginning of every sin is folly. Folly is the opposite of wisdom, in which divine goodness is tasted beforehand. Therefore, he who chooses to take delight in the malice of sin rather than in the sweetness of God is rightly said to begin from folly.

The **root** of sin—or of the evil that concerns sin, which is called the proneness of desire <note>original: "libidinis pronitatem."</note>—can also be understood in three ways. 

1. **Most generally**: When it is said that **greed** or **lust** <note>original: "libido" — used here as a general term for disordered craving.</note> is the root of all evils. 
2. **Generally**: As when it is said that the root of evils is twofold: namely, **love** that is wrongly inflamed and **fear** that wrongly humbles or casts one down. Love regards a perceived good, while fear regards a perceived evil. 
3. **Less generally**: As when it is said that the root of sin is threefold: namely, the **lust of the flesh**, the **lust of the eyes**, and the **pride of life** <note>A reference to 1 John 2:16, a foundational text for medieval moral psychology.</note>. 

Thus, from what has been said, it is clear how a **capital vice** <note>term: "vicious capitalis" — a "head" or "source" sin that gives rise to others, commonly known as the Seven Deadly Sins.</note> is understood. It is also clear that the "beginning" of sin is sometimes called pride, sometimes error, and sometimes folly. It is nonetheless clear that sometimes one root is named, sometimes two, and sometimes three.

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

<note>original: "Centiloquii" — meaning a collection of one hundred points or sayings. This was a popular medieval format for summarizing complex theological topics.</note>

Definitions, distinctions, and degrees: **Pride** is defined thus by **Bernard** <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux, a 12th-century mystic and influential monk.</note>: **Pride** is specifically the appetite for one's own excellence. Likewise, by **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo, the foundational theologian of the Western Church.</note> in the *Book of Innocence*: **Pride** is the love of one's own excellence. Also, by the same author in *The City of God*: **Pride** is the appetite for a perverse height <note>original: "puerse celsitudinis appetitus." Augustine argues that pride is a "perverse" imitation of God, seeking power for oneself rather than from God.</note>.

**Pride**, according to **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great, who formulated the standard list of the Seven Deadly Sins.</note>, has four distinctions. The first is when a person attributes to themselves the good which they have. The second is when, even if they admit it comes from God, they attribute it to their own merits. The third is when they boast of having what they do not have. The fourth is when they believe themselves to be superior to others. Hence the verse:
¶ Himself, for merits, falsely, more than all, he inflates.

According to **Anselm** <note>Anselm of Canterbury, an 11th-century philosopher and theologian.</note> in the *Book of Similitudes*, there are four types of self-exaltation. These are exaltation in opinion, in will, in speech, and in deed. 

**Exaltation in opinion** is when someone considers themselves worthy of a bishopric or some other dignity <term>dignity: a high-ranking office or position of honor</term>, yet they would not actually want to hold the office. 

**Exaltation in will** is when someone would like to have a bishopric, yet they do not consider themselves worthy, or even have a low opinion of themselves. 

**Exaltation in speech** is when they neither consider themselves worthy nor would they want to have the office, yet they speak grandiosely <note>original: "amplosa" (ampullosa) — literally "bottled up" or puffed-up language.</note> about some branch of knowledge so that they might appear more important. 

**Exaltation in deed** is when someone neither considers themselves worthy, nor wants the position, nor speaks big words, yet is exalted in deed, such as when they are called to councils and similar gatherings. 

The degrees of pride according to **Bernard** are twelve, namely: **Curiosity**, **levity of mind**, **inappropriate joy**, **boasting**, **singularity** <note>original: "singularitas" — the desire to appear unique or better by departing from common rules or the behavior of others.</note>, **arrogance**, **presumption**, **defense of sins**, **feigned confession**, **rebellion**, **freedom [to sin]**, and **the habit of sinning**. By these, as if by certain steps, the proud person diligently arrives at the end they intend. 

**Curiosity** is when the proud person diligently inquires after rumors, namely, what is being said about them. 

**Levity of mind** is when someone lightly...

# ¶ First Part

...es better than they are. Consequently, such a person **is moved to delight by words of praise and into sadness because of words of blame.** 

**Unsuitable joy** occurs when one rejoices in the defects and crimes of others. **Boasting** occurs when one recounts the things they possess specifically so that they may be praised. **Singularity** <term>Singularitas: the vice of acting contrary to common custom or rules specifically to stand out and appear unique.</term> is when someone works in a unique way outside of established custom so that they alone might be seen and praised. **Arrogance** is when one boasts of having what they do not actually possess. **Presumption** is when one intrudes upon tasks or responsibilities that do not belong to them. 

The **defense of sins** occurs when someone openly defends and excuses their own sin and error. **Simulated confession** is when one excuses their sin secretly <note>This refers to a "false confession" where the sinner admits the act but manipulates the narrative to avoid blame.</note>. **Rebellion** occurs when one is at odds with others—namely, with superiors, equals, and inferiors. Such a person opposes superiors, oppresses inferiors, and troubles or disturbs their equals. At that point, a "freedom to sin" follows, because fearing neither God nor respecting men, the person sins freely. Becoming accustomed to evil and falling into the depths of sin, the sinner becomes contemptuous.

### On Vain Glory
## Chapter Eighteen

It follows next to consider **Vain Glory**, which is the same as pride in substance, though it differs in its logical definition. Regarding this, four points must be briefly examined: its definition, its opposition, its distinctions, and its "daughters" <note>original: "filie" — In medieval theology, the "daughters" of a vice are the specific smaller sins that grow out of a major one.</note>. 

**Vain Glory** is defined thus: **Vain Glory** is the love of praise for the sake of one's own apparent excellence. **Vain Glory** is understood to be the opposite of **Humility**. 

**Humility**, however, has four distinct acts:
1. The first act is the **will to be subject** to others, and in this way, it is the opposite of pride in its broadest sense.
2. The second act is **to think little of oneself**. According to **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo.</note>: "Humility is the sense of one's own smallness" <note>original: "humilitas est sensus proprie parvitatis"</note>; and so it works...

The third is to wish to be beneath everyone—namely, superiors, inferiors, and equals—and thus it is opposed to pride strictly understood, which is the love of one's own excellence. The fourth is to wish that humble things be said of oneself, and thus it is opposed to vainglory. **Vain Glory** has two formal distinctions: namely, the appetite for favor and for praise. Favor is [the recognition of] someone’s excellence in the heart; praise is [that excellence expressed] by the mouth. It has, moreover, three material distinctions which are set down in Jeremiah, Chapter Nine: "Let not the wise man glory in his wisdom, nor the strong man glory in his strength, nor the rich man glory in his riches. But let him who glories, glory in this: that he understands and knows me," says the Lord. <note>original: "non glorietur sapiens in sapientia sua..." from Jeremiah 9:23–24.</note> Glory is proper to the Most Blessed Trinity; it is not to be sought by the world in wisdom, which is attributed to the Son; nor in strength, which is attributed to the Father; nor in riches—whether temporal or spiritual—which are attributed to the Holy Spirit.

¶ According to Saint Gregory, there are seven daughters of **Vain Glory**: namely, disobedience, boasting, hypocrisy, lack of self-restraint, stubbornness, discord, and the presumption of novelties. <note>Saint Gregory the Great (c. 540–604) famously categorized the "offspring" or "daughters" of the seven capital sins in his work *Moralia in Job*.</note>

¶ **Disobedience** is a will that flees the humbling authority of superiors, refusing to be subject to those to whom obedience is owed. 

¶ **Boasting** is a will that shows itself off for the sake of making its own power apparent. 

**Hypocrisy** is a will that, through the appearance of holiness, feigns a lie in public while acting basely in secret, whether in intention or in deed. 

¶ **Lack of self-restraint** <term>Incontinentia: in this medieval context, it refers to a lack of restraint in seeking validation, often specifically seeking the approval of the wicked or worldly to bolster one's ego.</term> is a will that, through seeking confirmation of itself, desires to be praised by those who are evil. 

**Stubbornness** <term>Pertinacia: a persistent, irrational refusal to change one's mind.</term> is a will that flees the humbling of its own truth, defending its words or deeds irrationally. 

¶ **Discord** is a will that flees the humbling of its own wisdom, disagreeing with what is said by others.

### On Envy
### Chapter Nineteen.

Next, we must consider envy with regard to five points: namely, its various names, its different definitions, its opposition [to virtue], its distinctions, and its "daughters" <note>In medieval moral theology, the "daughters" of a vice are the specific sins that typically spring from it.</note>. **Envy** is sometimes called by the name of "anger," as in **Genesis 4**: "Why are you angry?"—that is, you are twisted by spite <note>original: "quare irasceris id est liuore torqueris." Referring to Cain’s reaction to Abel’s sacrifice.</note>. **Sometimes** it is called by the name of "hatred," as in **Genesis 37**: "His brothers hated him"—that is, they envied him <note>Referring to the brothers of Joseph.</note>. **Sometimes** it is called by the name of "emulation" <note>or "jealousy"</note> in the first book of Kings, chapter two: "Her rival afflicted her" <note>original: "Affligebat eam emula eius." From 1 Samuel 1:6 (historically cited as 1 Kings), referring to the rivalry between Hannah and Peninnah.</note>. **However**, "emulation" has four meanings contained in this verse: **Emulation** is puffed up, it loves, it envies, and it imitates. <note>original: "Emulor inflatur amat inuidet ac imitatur." This mnemonic verse helps students remember that "emulation" can be negative (pride/envy) or positive (love/imitation).</note> **Sometimes** it is called by its proper name. **And** thus John Damascene says that there are four species of <term>tristitia</term> <note>Sadness or sorrow; in this context, a disordered spiritual heaviness.</note>: namely, <term>accidia</term> <note>Sloth or spiritual apathy.</note>, which is a weighing-down sadness; **anguish** <note>original: "Achos," a Latinized Greek term for distress that stifles expression.</note>, which is a sadness that takes away the voice; mercy, which is sadness over another’s misfortune; and envy, which is sadness over another’s good. **Envy is defined** thus by Saint Augustine: **Envy** is the hatred of another’s happiness. According to **Remigius**, envy is pain over another’s good. According to **Damascene**, envy is sadness at the apparent happiness of some enemy. **Also**, in another way, envy is a disagreement from another’s [good]...

This refers to secret gossip or tale-bearing intended to destroy friendships or sow discord.</note> is a will for speaking that sows discord among brothers so that one might persecute a neighbor, grieving on account of that neighbor's good. ¶ **Detraction** is a will that blackens the reputation of neighbors through words in order to destroy it in the opinion of others, grieving at their good. ¶ **Exultation in the adversity of neighbors** is a will that finds delight when a neighbor faces adversity. ¶ **Affliction at the good of a neighbor** is a will that seeks evil through its own desire, feeling cheated by the occurrence of a prosperity contrary to its own wishes.

### Part One

...stirred up to provoke the infliction of punishment. **¶ Wrath**, according to Bonaventure <note>The OCR "bano" is a common medieval abbreviation for "Bonauentura."</note>: **Wrath** is every evil movement toward doing harm. **According** to Augustine, **Wrath** is a sadness arising from the inability to take revenge. **¶ According to [John] Damascene <note>John of Damascus, an 8th-century theologian whose work on the soul and emotions was highly influential in the Middle Ages.</note>, Wrath** is a boiling of the blood around the heart, occurring from the evaporation of bile or from a disturbance. **Also** from the same: **Wrath** is that which is the bold avenger of the mind's desire. **Also** from the same: **Wrath** is the desire for retaliation <note>original: "repunitionis," literally a "punishing back."</note>. **¶ According to the Philosopher <note>In medieval texts, "The Philosopher" almost always refers to Aristotle.</note>, Wrath** is a craving for a contrary pain because of a perceived slight <note>original: "examinationem," here used in the context of an assessment of an insult or injury.</note>, and a craving to inflict pain on one who opposes him, on account of a perceived injury inflicted, which in a certain way "weighs" or tests <note>original: "examinat."</note> the man, in that it deprives him of the proper order regarding the judgment of the soul.

**Wrath** moreover stands in opposition to **meekness** <note>original: "mansuetudinem," the virtue of remaining calm and gentle under provocation.</note> by reason of its beginning, because a meek person never even wishes to begin to be angry. **¶ To** wrath [of the virtuous kind] through zeal <note>original: "zelum." Medieval theology distinguished between "wrath through vice" (sinful) and "wrath through zeal" (righteous anger against sin).</note>, it is related by reason of its matter. **¶ For** there are two types concerning the same matter—namely, the person: **wrath through vice** and **wrath through zeal**; both of which desire punishment. **But** wrath through vice wants the person to be punished so that vengeance is taken upon the person. **Wrath** through zeal, however, desires the punishment of the person so that it may destroy the vice within the person. **Briefly**, wrath through zeal wants the punishment of the person with the proper circumstances; **Wrath** through vice with improper circumstances.

**¶ Toward patience**, however, wrath has an opposition by reason of its end: because patience extinguishes wrath lest it proceed further. **¶ For** he who is patient mitigates the angers that have been stirred up. The **species** or categories of wrath are three according to Damascene: namely, gall, mania, and rancor <note>original: "fel, mania, et cotus." These represent the three stages of anger: the sudden flash (gall), the lasting agitation (mania), and the deep-seated grudge (cotus/rancor).</note>. **Gall**, or the bilious passion, is called a sudden movement of the mind that is impatient of injury. **¶ And** it happens on the part of the body from the commotion of the bile, and on the part of the soul from the...

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

...the corruption of the irascible power <note>The "irascible power" (vis irascibilis) was believed to be the part of the soul responsible for forceful emotions like anger or courage.</note>, and then the bile is moved toward vengeance so that sadness may be expelled. Concerning which **Augustine** says: **Wrath** is the commotion of a passion inflicted [upon one]. This wrath quickly passes. **Mania** is called a lasting wrath that is handed over to the memory. **Lotus** <note>original: "Lotus," likely a variant of "lotos" or "lutea," referring to a smoldering or hidden anger that waits for an opportunity.</note> is a wrath that watches for the right time for vengeance. The daughters of wrath, according to the blessed **Gregory**, are six, namely: **Strife**, **fear of the mind**, **insult**, **clamor**, **indignation**, and **blasphemy**. **Strife** is a bold will for vengeance that proceeds into an injurious act. **Fear of the mind** is a will for vengeance that flees the adversary on account of perceived enmities. ¶ **Insult** is a will taking vengeance through the reproaching of evils. ¶ **Clamor** is a will taking vengeance by raising the voice through scolding so that it reaches the ears of others. **Indignation** is a will taking vengeance through an outward sign of trouble. ¶ **Blasphemy** is a will taking vengeance through an insult brought against God or the saints. According to **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), an influential scholar whose "Etymologies" served as a medieval encyclopedia.</note>, there are three daughters of wrath, namely: **homicide**, ¶ **clamor**, and **indignation**. ¶ They are distinguished in this way: **For** vengeance is carried out by the hand in homicide, by the mouth in clamor, and by a sign in indignation.

By **Richard** <note>Likely Richard of Saint Victor, a prominent 12th-century mystical theologian.</note>: **Sloth** is a torpor of the soul that is negligent in beginning a good work. By **Damascene** <note>John of Damascus.</note>: **Sloth** is a heavy sadness. ¶ **By the Masters**: **Sloth** is a lack of confidence in one’s own strengths and in the help of God for fulfilling difficult tasks or commandments. ¶ It is opposed...

**rancor**, **faint-heartedness**, **despair**, **torpor**, and the **wandering of the mind** toward illicit things. ¶ **M**alice is a will impatient of a burden, plotting evil against the one whom it judges to be inflicting that burden. ¶ **R**ancor is a will embittered by the inability to repel a burden, manifesting the bitterness shut up inside through an external sign. ¶ **F**aint-heartedness is a will excessively fearing a burden when beginning tasks to be done, or excessively distrusting one’s own strengths. ¶ **D**espair is a will shrinking from a burden, distrusting the help available to finish tasks, or distrusting the divine reward for the endurance of the burden found in fulfilling the commandments. ¶ **T**orpor <term>Torpor: a state of spiritual numbness or mental inactivity **sloth**. ¶ For he calls "sadness" the state of the will insofar as it turns away from a heavy duty. ¶ He calls "sloth," however, the state of the will insofar as it turns itself toward undue rest. ### On Avarice.

<margin>Chapter 22.</margin>

Next, we must consider **avarice** <note>greed or the excessive desire for wealth</note> in five respects: namely, its **definition**, **comparison**, **opposition**, **differences**, and **daughters**. **Augustine**, in his work *The Literal Meaning of Genesis* <note>original: "super genesim ad litteram"</note>, defines avarice thus: **Avarice is a desire not only for money, but also for status and knowledge.** The same author in the book *The City of God* says: ¶ **Avarice is the lust for possessing money.** ¶ The same author says elsewhere: **Avarice is an immoderate desire for possessing.** Likewise, **Tully** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman orator whose ethical writings were highly respected by medieval theologians.</note> defines it thus: **Avarice is an immoderate love of possessing.**

Avarice is moreover compared to **idolatry**; for which reason it is often called the "service of idols" in the Scriptures. This is said because a worshiper of God is related to God in a threefold way: namely, by **loving** Him, **honoring** Him, and **trusting** in Him. These three things are found in the greedy person in respect to money. Because of this, avarice is not undeservedly called the "service of idols."

There is also a twofold act of avarice: namely, **to love money** and **to retain it** (or not to give it away). By reason of the first act, avarice is opposed to **charity** <note>original: "caritas," the highest form of Christian love</note>, which loves the unchangeable good. By reason of the second act, it is opposed to **generosity** or **liberality**, which gives what ought to be given and retains what ought to be retained. A twofold...

<summary>This page defines avarice (greed) using the authorities of St. Augustine and Cicero, ultimately characterizing the sin as a form of idolatry where money replaces God.</summary>
<keywords>avarice, greed, St. Augustine, Cicero, idolatry, charity, generosity, Seven Deadly Sins, medieval ethics</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Avarice, transitioning from its general definitions to a technical analysis of how greed functions as a vice of "excess" or "deficiency" compared to the virtue of generosity. It then categorizes specific types of greedy acts and begins listing the "daughters of avarice" according to Gregory the Great.</meta>

### First Part.

¶ Indeed, it is found in the act of **generosity** <note>original: "largitas"</note>, namely to give what should be given and to retain what should be retained. ¶ There is a twofold vicious extreme to this: namely **prodigality** <note>original: "prodigalitas," or wasteful extravagance</note>, which gives what should not be given; and **avarice** <note>greed</note>, which retains what should not be retained. **Generosity** or **thrift** <note>original: "parcitas"</note> (which is the same thing) is a virtue; however, by reason of the acts performed, the "mean" <note>the virtuous middle ground between two extremes, a concept popularized by the philosopher Aristotle</note> will differ. If the opposition is viewed according to the act of giving: **Prodigality** is the excess which gives what should not be given. **Avarice** is the deficiency which gives nothing. **Generosity** is the middle ground. If indeed it is ordered according to the act of retaining: **Avarice** is the excess which retains both what should be retained and what should not be retained. **Prodigality** is the deficiency which retains nothing. **Thrift** indeed is the middle ground.

¶ There are five differences <note>or categories</note> of this vice: namely, **withholding of benefit**, **theft**, **robbery**, **usury**, and **simony**. **Withholding of benefit** is greed retaining a benefit that one is bound to give to the poor in a time of necessity, such as a rich man toward one in need. ¶ **Theft** is greed retaining another's property against the owner's will. ¶ **Robbery** <note>original: "rapina," implying taking by force or pillage</note> is greed seizing another's property by violence. **Usury** is greed acquiring something through a loan beyond the principal <note>original: "praeter sortem"; in the Middle Ages, charging any interest on a loan was considered a sin</note>. **Simony** is greed eagerly seeking to sell or buy spiritual things or things attached to the spiritual <note>named after Simon Magus from the New Testament, this refers to the illicit buying or selling of church offices, sacraments, or sacred objects</note>.

¶ The **daughters of avarice**, according to **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD), an influential theologian who categorized the vices and their "offspring"</note>, are seven: namely, **treachery**, **fraud**, **deceit**, **perjury**, **restlessness of mind**, **violence**, and **hardness of heart** against mercy. **Treachery** <note>original: "proditio"</note> is a desire gaping after gains through the betrayal of those in one's own household. **Fraud** is a desire deceiving a neighbor's interests for the sake of earthly gain. **Deceit** <note>original: "fallacia"</note> is a desire deceiving a neighbor through words for the sake of profit.

<summary>This page defines greed as the failure to find the "middle ground" in giving and keeping, while categorizing various forms of theft and spiritual corruption like usury and simony. It also begins a list of seven secondary sins, or "daughters," that arise from an avaricious heart.</summary>
<keywords>generosity, prodigality, thrift, usury, simony, daughters of avarice, Gregory the Great, Aristotelian mean, theft</keywords>

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure
<note>A "centiloquium" is a theological work organized into one hundred points or "flowers." This text is attributed to the Franciscan scholar Saint Bonaventure (1221–1274).</note>

¶ **Perjury** <note>original: "periurium"</note> is a will that violates the sanctity of an oath. **Restlessness of mind** is a will that never ceases thinking about how it might amass riches. ¶ **Violence** is a will that seizes the goods of others through force. ¶ **Hardness of heart** <note>literally "obduration of mind"</note> against mercy is a will provoked against the request of a needy person out of an excessive passion for retaining money. According to Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), a scholar whose encyclopedic works were standard references in the Middle Ages</note>, there are nine daughters of **avarice**: namely, lying <note>original: "mendacium" – the transcription "mēdicium" is likely a clerical error for lying</note>, fraud, theft, the appetite for shameful gain, false witness, violence, inhumanity, and rapacity.

## ¶ Concerning Gluttony.

<margin>¶ Chapter 22</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' at the start of 'Sequitur', featuring floral and scrolling vine motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>
It follows to look at **gluttony** <note>original: "gula"</note> in four respects: namely, regarding its definition, its opposition, its categories, and its daughters. Gluttony is defined regarding excess in food in this way: gluttony is an immoderate love of eating. Indeed, regarding excess in drink, it is called **drunkenness**, which Seneca <note>The Roman Stoic philosopher Lucius Annaeus Seneca, often quoted by medieval theologians for his moral insights</note> defines thus:

> I define **drunkenness** as nothing other than a voluntary insanity.

Indeed, regarding the excess of both food and drink, it can be defined thus: **Gluttony** is an immoderate love of nourishment. Likewise: Gluttony is an immoderate love of the pleasure that concerns or follows the sense of taste. According to this, gluttony can be taken strictly as immoderation in food; and in this sense, **thrift** <note>original: "parcimonia"</note> is opposed to it. Or it can be taken broadly for immoderation in both food and drink together; and in this sense, **sobriety** <note>original: "sobrietas"</note> is opposed to it, which preserves the proper measure in both. ¶ The categories or conditions of gluttony, according to Isidore, are four: namely, *what*, *how much*, *when*, and *in what manner*. *What* pertains to the thing itself <note>referring to the quality or type of food</note>. *When* is if one desires it before the lawful time. *How much* refers to immoderation <note>quantity</note>. *In what manner* is if [one eats] too greedily <note>The text cuts off mid-word at "ar", likely for "ardenter," meaning passionately or eagerly.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on the sub-vices of Avarice and begins a systematic analysis of Gluttony, defining it as an immoderate love of eating and drinking.</summary>

<header>First Part.</header>
<sig>D.i.</sig>

...eats greedily <note>original: "ardenter appetat" — completing the sentence from the previous page</note>. According to **Saint Gregory** <note>Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), a Pope and Doctor of the Church whose "Moralia in Job" defined the standard list of Seven Deadly Sins</note>, there are five distinctions comprised in this verse: *Too soon, too fastidiously, too sumptuously, too much, too eagerly.* <note>original: "Pre/prope/laute/nimis ardenter/studiose"</note> ¶ **Too soon** <note>original: "Preprope"</note> pertains to the taking of food before the proper time. **Sumptuously** <note>original: "laute"</note> pertains to the substance of the food, namely when it is in itself precious and delicate. **Too much** <note>original: "Nimis"</note> pertains to the quantity of the food. **Eagerly** <note>original: "Ardenter"</note> is considered on the part of the emotions. **Fastidiously** <note>original: "Studiose"</note> on the part of the intellect <note>meaning a preoccupation with the elaborate preparation or choice of food</note>. According to Saint Gregory, he captures these last two distinctions under that which is the fourth mode. According to Gregory, there are five daughters of **gluttony** <note>original: "gula"</note>: namely, inappropriate joy, scurrility, uncleanness, loquacity, and dullness of the mind or of the senses regarding understanding. **Inappropriate joy** <note>original: "inepta leticia"</note> is a will that excites lustful desire in the sense of taste through external games, and is subsequently excited by them. **Scurrility** <note>original: "Scurrilitas" — referring here to buffoonery or coarse joking</note> is a will provoking the lust of taste through carnal words, and is subsequently moved by them. **Uncleanness** <note>original: "immundicia"</note> is a will desiring to continue the lust of taste through the excessive taking of food. **Loquacity** <note>original: "multiloquium" — excessive talking</note> is a will intending to preserve the lust of taste through a multitude of words. **Dullness of mind** <note>original: "ebetudo mentis"</note> is a will to preserve lust, being nearly absorbed by the flesh and finding spiritual things distasteful to understand. According to **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636)</note>, there are two daughters of gluttony: namely, feasting <note>original: "commessatio"</note> and drunkenness, of which one is observed in food and the other in drink.

Also: lust is the inordinate appetite for that delight which is according to sexual intercourse. It is opposed, however...

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of Gluttony into a systematic analysis of Lust and its opposite virtue, Continence. It defines the stages of temptation and categorizes the different types of sexual sins according to medieval canon law and theology.</meta>

# The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure <note>original: "Centiloquii sancti Bonaventure"</note>

Lust [is opposed by] Continence <note>original: "luxuria continentie"</note>, of which there is a threefold distinction: for there is marital, widowed, and virginal continence. **F**urthermore, the temptation of lust is fivefold, according to what is contained in this verse: *sight and speech, touch and kisses, the deed.* <note>original: "visus et alloquium contactus et oscula factum"</note> Indeed, the temptation of lust is completed in this order: **F**irst, it begins in sight, whether outward <note>physical looking</note> or inward, as in thought. Next, one comes to conversation; afterward, it passes to touch; then it breaks forth into a kiss; and finally, the deed is consummated. **T**here are six types <note>original: "differentie"</note> of lust, namely: fornication, adultery, defilement, sacrilege, incest, and the sin against nature. **F**ornication is the lust by which an unattached man <note>original: "solutus"</note> has knowledge of an unattached woman in the natural way. **A**nd "unattached" is understood to mean free from the bond of marriage, blood relation, affinity <note>relation by marriage</note>, holy orders, religious life, or a vow of continence. **A**dultery is the lust by which the marriage bed is violated. **D**efilement <note>original: "stuprum"</note> is the lust by which virginal purity is deflowered. **S**acrilege is here called the injury of a sacred person through the vice of lust. **A**nd it would be better called sacrilegious lust. **T**his is the lust by which a vow of continence is violated—and this in itself—as when someone established in holy orders or a member of a religious order commits fornication with an unattached woman, or an unattached woman with a religious brother or a priest. **I**ncest is intercourse committed against blood relation, spiritual kinship <note>original: "compaternitatem" — the relationship between a child's parents and their godparents</note>, or affinity. **T**he sin against nature is the lust by which the natural use of the union between male and female is cast aside. **T**he daughters of lust, according to **Gregory** <note>Saint Gregory the Great</note>, are eight, namely: **B**lindness of mind, **t**houghtlessness, **i**nconstancy, **h**aste <note>original: "precipitatio"</note>, **s**elf-love, **h**atred of God, **a**ffection for the present world, and **d**read or despair.

# ¶ First Part

...of the world to come. ¶ **Blindness of mind** <note>original: "Cecitas mentis"</note> is a will that feels disgust for and looks down upon higher and spiritual things. ¶ **Recklessness** <note>original: "Inconsideratio"</note> is a will that refuses to look ahead at what must be done in lower matters. ¶ **Inconstancy** is a will broken by the lust of the flesh, unable to maintain its own steady state. ¶ **Precipitation** <note>original: "Precipitatio"</note> is a will that rushes headlong into any action with impulsive force. ¶ **Self-love** is a will soaked in the affection of the flesh, clinging to itself for its own sake above all else. ¶ **Hatred of God** is a will that detests God as if he were an obstacle to one’s own carnal will or pleasure. ¶ **Affection for the present world** <note>original: "Affectus presentis seculi"</note> is a will desiring the present age to last forever for the sake of enjoying the pleasures of the flesh. ¶ **Despair** or the **horror of the world to come** is a will blinded by present pleasure, despairing of eternal joy. ¶ According to <term>Isidore</term> <note>Saint Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), an influential scholar whose encyclopedic works were standard textbooks in the Middle Ages.</note>, there are four daughters of fornication, namely: foul speech, buffoonery, jesting, and foolish talk. ¶ In speech, **foolish talk** is the opposite of wisdom. The other three are opposed to **goodness** in speech. ¶ For as goodness is threefold—namely, usefulness, lawful pleasure, and honesty—**jesting** is the opposite of useful speech; **buffoonery** is the opposite of speech that provides lawful pleasure; and **foul speech** is the opposite of honest speech, etc.

<header>The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure</header>

# The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure
<note>The title *Centiloquium* literally means "a hundred sayings" or "a hundred discourses." This work, attributed to the Franciscan theologian Saint Bonaventure (1221–1274), provides a concise summary of Christian doctrine and ethics.</note>

There is sin against the Father, sin against the Son, and sin against the Holy Spirit. One is said to sin against the Father—to whom **power** is attributed—when one sins out of **weakness**. One is said to sin against the Son—to whom **wisdom** is attributed—when one sins out of **ignorance**. One is said to sin against the Holy Spirit—to whom **goodness** is attributed—when one sins through **deliberation** <note>original: "ex industria"</note> or out of certain malice. ¶ First, we must consider the sin of weakness, which is a turning away of a will that is either wrongly humbled or wrongly inflamed by a weak creature, away from the almighty God. There are two varieties of this sin: **fear** that wrongly humbles and **love** that wrongly inflames. Love is distinguished in three ways: namely, as **gracious**, **lustful**, or **natural**. Gracious love has as its primary end the true Good, namely, the unchanging God. Lustful love has as its end pleasure or delight in a created thing. Natural love has as its end necessity or one’s own utility. ¶ The first is praiseworthy, as it was in Christ; the second is blameworthy, as it is a sin; the third is neither praiseworthy nor blameworthy, as it is natural. Similarly, a threefold fear is distinguished: namely, natural, gracious, and lustful. **Natural fear** is a passion—that is, a "first movement"—and was in Christ as a testimony of his true humanity. **Gracious fear** is divided into servile, initial, and filial fear, about which he speaks elsewhere, namely, in the book *On the Gifts* <note>Likely referring to Bonaventure's *Collationes de septem donis Spiritus Sancti* (Conferences on the Seven Gifts of the Holy Spirit), a series of sermons on the gifts of the Holy Spirit.</note>. **Lustful fear** <note>original: "Timor libidinosus"</note> is divided into **human fear**, by which one chooses to sin so as not to be harmed in the body, and **worldly fear**, by which one chooses to sin so as not to be harmed in one’s possessions. Through lustful fear, a person is wrongly humbled; through gracious fear, rightly; through natural fear, neither praiseworthily nor blameworthily. Likewise, through lustful love, a person is wrongly inflamed; through gracious love, rightly. Through natural fear in...

<summary>This page introduces the Trinitarian classification of sins (weakness, ignorance, and malice) and begins a detailed breakdown of "sins of weakness" driven by different types of love and fear.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Bonaventure, Holy Trinity, sin of weakness, ignorance, malice, types of love, types of fear, Scholastic theology, Centiloquium</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic breakdown of types of sin. Having previously discussed sins of weakness (sins against the Father), the text now shifts to sins of ignorance (sins against the Son, to whom wisdom is attributed), defining the various legal and moral categories of ignorance.</meta>

<header>First Part</header>

...differently. ¶ A sin out of ignorance is a turning away of a will that is ignorant and performing what is evil, away from the all-knowing God. Note, however, that a sin *by means of* ignorance <note>original: "peccatum ignorantia"</note> is one thing, and a sin *of* ignorance <note>original: "peccatum ignorantie"</note> is another. ¶ A sin of ignorance is the omission of required knowledge. ¶ A sin out of ignorance is a sin that is committed ignorantly. There is ignorance of the law <note>original: "iuris"</note> and ignorance of the fact <note>original: "facti"</note>. ¶ Ignorance of fact is twofold: either due diligence was applied, and this excuses [the sinner] entirely <note>original: "a toto"</note>; or due diligence was not applied, and this excuses to a certain extent <note>original: "a tanto"</note> but not entirely. ¶ Certain ignorance of the law is **vincible** <term>vincible ignorance: ignorance that a person could overcome by applying reasonable effort; it is considered a fault</term>, which is a fault. Moreover, this occurs with true consent, such as **affected ignorance** <term>affected ignorance: a deliberate decision to remain ignorant so as not to be restrained by the law</term>. ¶ And this, in one way, provides an excuse, but in another way, it further aggravates the sin. ¶ Or it arises from negligence and laziness, such as from **crass and supine ignorance** <note>original: "ignorantia crassa et supina"; this refers to a lazy or extreme lack of effort to learn what one ought to know</note>; and this, even if it excuses in some way, does not excuse so much as to prevent one from burning in eternal fire. ¶ As Augustine says. Likewise, **invincible** <term>invincible ignorance: ignorance that cannot be overcome by the person even with reasonable effort</term> [ignorance] can be twofold. ¶ For it is either introduced through a preceding fault <note>original: "preambulam culpam"</note>, or without it. ¶ If it is without any fault, it either simply deprives one of the knowledge of the law—as in infants and the insane <note>original: "furiosis"</note>, who entirely lack the use of reason—and this excuses entirely. ¶ Whence Bernard says: that to small infants and those sleeping, nothing they do is imputed [as sin]. ¶ Or it does not entirely take away the use of reason but leaves it "half-full"; then it does not excuse entirely, but to a certain extent, as in those who are not fully insane but have in some way certain **lucid intervals** <note>original: "interualla lucida"</note>, and in children who are in some way capable of [understanding] a command, though not fully. ¶ If, however, the ignorance is introduced through a preceding fault—as in the drunkard and the madman, both of whom cast themselves down through their own fault—in this case, it does not excuse entirely, but as...

<summary>This page analyzes the theology of ignorance, distinguishing between ignorance of law and fact, and determining to what degree such ignorance excuses a person from the guilt of sin. It relies on the authority of Saints Augustine and Bernard to categorize the "insane," infants, and the negligent.</summary>

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

<note>original: "Centiloquii sancti Bonauenture". A *Centiloquium* is a "collection of a hundred sayings," a common title for medieval theological or philosophical handbooks.</note>

The Philosopher <note>This refers to Aristotle, specifically his *Nicomachean Ethics*, Book III, where he discusses whether a drunk person is responsible for their actions.</note> says that a drunk person <note>original: "Ebrius"</note> deserves a double penalty: one for the preceding fault <note>the choice to become intoxicated</note>, and another for the fault that follows <note>the act committed while drunk</note>. However, strictly speaking, it should not be said that a drunkard who performs some evil commits a *new* fault. Rather, the first fault is aggravated by the consequence, as appears to be the case in many instances. This is because some have argued that in addition to the first fault committed in the state of drunkenness, he commits a second, new fault. ¶ A **sin of malice** <note>original: "Peccatum ex industria"</note>, or a **sin against the Holy Spirit**, is the turning away of a malicious will from the most merciful God. ¶ There are six different categories of this: namely, **despair**, **presumption**, **attacking the known truth**, **envy of a brother’s grace**, **obstinacy of mind**, and **final impenitence**. ¶ The logical completeness <note>original: "sufficiencia"</note> of these is understood as follows: This sin attacks the grace of penance, through which the forgiveness of sin is granted by God within the unity of the Church. ¶ It can, therefore, attack penance in itself, and thus there are two species. Because penance causes one to pull back from things already committed (against which is **obstinacy of mind** <term>obstinacy of mind: a stubborn refusal to repent for past sins</term>), and it also causes one to beware of committing future sins (against which is **final impenitence** <term>final impenitence: the settled intention to never repent before death</term>). ¶ If, however, it attacks the grace of penance in relation to God, by whom it is given, there are likewise two species. ¶ For all the ways of the Lord are mercy and truth <note>or justice</note>. **Despair** <term>despair: the belief that one's sins are too great for God's mercy to forgive</term> attacks mercy. **Presumption** <term>presumption: the belief that one can be saved without merit or repentance, attacking God's justice</term> attacks justice. ¶ If, however, it attacks the grace of penance in relation to the unity of the Church in which it is received, there are two types of sin. ¶ For the unity of the Church consists primarily of two things: namely, in faith or truth (against which is **attacking the known truth** <term>impugnatio veritatis agnite: a deliberate rejection of established religious truths</term>), and in grace or charity (against which...
