## ¶ On idolatry and heresy.

### ¶ Chapter Twenty-Six

It **f**ollows that we must examine the division of sin based on whom the sin is committed against: one is sin against God, another against one’s neighbor, and another against oneself. ¶ Sin against God is called **impiety**. ¶ Sin against one’s neighbor is called **iniquity**. ¶ Sin against oneself is called **impurity**. ¶ First, we must look at those sins by which the omnipotence of the divine majesty is dishonored through the superstition of worship; these are **idolatry**, the **Jewish rite**, and the **Saracen rite** <note>original: "Ritus sarracenicus". In medieval theology, Islam and Judaism were often categorized as "superstitions" or "infidelity" because they were viewed as rejecting the established Christian revelation.</note>. ¶ Also included are **apostasy**, **schism**, and **heresy**. 

**Idolatry** is a false and superstitious proclamation of divine majesty attributed to an idol. There are two species of idolatry: one concerns natural things <note>such as worshipping the sun or stars</note> and the other concerns artificial things <note>such as statues or man-made objects</note>, as is shown in the third chapter of the Book of Wisdom <note>original: "sapientie tertio". While the text cites the third chapter, the primary critique of idols in the Book of Wisdom is found in chapters 13–15.</note>. ¶ It is committed in three ways: namely, by the **proclamation of the heart**, such as believing a created thing to be omnipotent (which belongs only to the Creator); ¶ by the **confession of the mouth**, which is not only to believe it but also to express it in words; ¶ and by the **outward performance of work**, as is done through acts of reverence and sacrifices. 

¶ **Apostasy** is threefold: namely, that of **perfidy** <term>perfidy: in this context, the total rejection or "bad faith" of one who abandons the Christian faith entirely</term>, as when someone withdraws from the faith, such as **Julian the Apostate** <note>Roman Emperor Julian (r. 361–363), who famously attempted to revive traditional Roman paganism after the empire had turned toward Christianity.</note>; ¶ that of **disobedience**, when someone withdraws from the commands of the Church, for "the beginning of human pride is to apostatize from God"; ¶ and that of **irregularity**, when someone withdraws from the religious life of the cloister or the clerical state. ¶ Apostasy can be described thus: ¶ **Apostasy** is a rash withdrawal from a state of faith, obedience, or religious life.

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure
<note>The "Centiloquium" (literally "the collection of a hundred sayings") is a systematic theological work traditionally attributed to St. Bonaventure, a 13th-century Franciscan doctor of the church.</note>

¶ **Schism** is an unlawful dissension among those who ought to be united, or an unlawful withdrawal from unity. Every heresy is a schism, but the reverse is not always true <note>original: "non convertitur". In medieval logic, this means the terms are not interchangeable: while all heretics are schismatics because they break away from the Church, not all schismatics are necessarily heretics, as they may maintain correct doctrine but reject the Church's authority.</note>. **Heresy** is a departure from the faith according to the truth. **Also**, schism and heresy differ because heresy adds perverse dogma. **Schism** in the end fabricates some heresy. **After** its withdrawal from the unity of the Church, there follows disobedience to the Church’s mandates. **In the end**, however, there follows an assertion that the Church should not be obeyed. "Heresy" is the same as "division," and thus one is most properly called a "heretic" for a withdrawal from the faith because of a false interpretation of the scriptures. ¶ Another [definition] is specifically excommunication.

**According** to this, "heretic" is a term used in many ways. In one way, according to **Augustine**, a heretic is one who, for the sake of some temporal gain and especially for his own glory and preeminence, gives birth to or follows false and new opinions regarding the faith. He **gives birth** to them like the heresiarchs <note>A "heresiarch" is the founder or leader of a heretical movement.</note> Sabellius and Arius <note>Sabellius (3rd century) taught that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit were merely different "modes" of one person, while Arius (4th century) taught that Jesus was a created being and not co-eternal with God the Father.</note>; he **follows** them like the Sabellians and Arians who are deceived by such people. **According** to Jerome, a person can be called a heretic even if they have not departed from the Church, if they understand Scripture differently than the sense of the Holy Spirit requires. **Thirdly**, anyone is called a heretic who is divided from the sacraments of the Church or the communion of the faithful, such as one who is excommunicated, or a subverter of the Church, or a destroyer of the sacraments; hence a **simoniac** <note>term: "simoniac". One who commits simony, the sin of buying or selling sacred things, such as church offices or sacraments.</note> is [considered] a heretic re... <unclear>reputatur</unclear>

### First Part

...[suspension] sometimes from office, sometimes from a <term>benefice</term><note>A church office that provided an income or "living" to the priest holding it.</note>, and sometimes from both. ¶ An **interdict** is pronounced upon a place, such as a city, village, or castle. ¶ Under a general interdict, these things must still be granted by the church:
**Baptism for children; penance for the dying; and for pilgrims receiving the cross** <note>original: "peregrinis crucem accipientibus." This refers to Crusaders who took a vow and received a cloth cross to wear.</note> out of reverence for the Crucified, penance is not to be denied. ¶ A bishop can confirm baptized children once a month or once a week, and he may call the interdicted people to the church for the purpose of preaching. ¶ **Also, for the dying**, if they repent worthily, the <term>Eucharist</term><note>Holy Communion.</note> can be administered. ¶ **Clerics** who pass away may be buried in the cemetery in silence, without the ringing of bells and with solemnities ceasing, provided they have properly observed the interdict.
**In conventual** <note>monastic or communal</note> churches, two or three together may read the <term>canonical hours</term><note>The daily cycle of prayers and psalms required of clergy and monks.</note> but not sing them, behind closed doors and with those under interdict excluded. This must be done so quietly that they cannot be heard from the outside. From this it is gathered that those under interdict—and even more so those who are excommunicated—must not listen to the hours from outside the church. ¶ **To most religious orders**, however, it has been granted that they may celebrate divine services together behind closed doors with the interdicted excluded; today this privilege is extended to bishops unless it is specifically prohibited to them. **Excommunication** is the stroke of the spiritual sword of the church, a separation from the communion of the sacraments of the faithful. There are two species of excommunication: one which is called "major," which excludes one from the sacraments and from the legal acts of the faithful, as captured in this verse: *Mouth, prayer, greeting, communion, and table are denied.* <note>original: "Os, orare, vale, communio, mensa negatur." This was a mnemonic for the five ways the community was to avoid an excommunicated person: no conversation, no praying together, no greetings, no sacraments, and no eating together.</note> ¶ **The other** is called "minor," which separates one only from the sacraments. ¶ **Furthermore, excommunication** is sometimes inflicted "by law" <note>original: "a iure." This means the penalty is automatic for a specific crime defined in canon law.</note>, and this is "preservative"; although it may seem to exist to preserve the liberty of the church, its primary purpose is to guard against guilt.
¶ **A certain kind** is inflicted "by a judge" <note>original: "a iudice." This is a sentence passed by an ecclesiastical official for a specific offense.</note>, and this is "curative." ¶ For...

### The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

Although it [excommunication] may seem to be primarily for the sake of making amends, it is nonetheless for the purpose of crushing <term>contumacy</term><note>Stubborn resistance to or disregard of the authority of the Church.</note>. ¶ The penalty for one undergoing excommunication is manifold, as contained in this verse:

->**Grace is withdrawn more and more, the barrier having been broken. ¶ He falls from vice into vice; he loses the intercessory prayers of the Church; he is handed over to Satan to be tormented, ¶ him whom the fault of anathema binds from his own sins.**<- 
<note>original Latin: "Gratia subtrahit magis ac magis obice rupto / De vicio cadit in vicium. suffragia pdit / Ecclesie: sathane vexandus traditur ille / Quem ligat a proprijs anathematis vicio culpis"</note>

The circumstances, however, that preserve a person interacting with an excommunicated person so that they are not involved in the bond of excommunication are manifold, as contained in these verses: 

->**Useful, law, humble, matter unknown, necessity.**<-

These do not cause an <term>anathema</term><note>A formal curse by a pope or a council of the Church, excommunicating a person or denouncing a doctrine.</note> so that it might not be harmful. 

**Utility** for the excommunicated person, such as when one seeks what the excommunicated person owes him, or when he interacts for the sake of correction. ¶ **Law** of marriage, which binds husband and wife together. **Humble**, that is, humility, such as servants and children who are not yet <term>emancipated</term><note>Legally freed from the authority of a father or master.</note>; handmaidens, rural laborers, and others bound in service. ¶ **A matter unknown**, such as when it is not known that the person one is interacting with is excommunicated. ¶ **Necessity**, such as pilgrims, the poor, and other travelers.

### First Part

...who catches a cleric in the act of sexual misconduct with his wife, mother, sister, or daughter. ¶ The **eighth** case concerns one who, by the authority of the Church or by the command of a superior, lays hands on a cleric. ¶ The **ninth** concerns elders. ¶ The **tenth** concerns lords. ¶ The **eleventh** concerns heads of households. ¶ The **twelfth** concerns relatives. ¶ The **thirteenth** concerns one who lays hands on a cleric who has been <term>deposed</term><note>Stripped of his clerical status and protections.</note> and handed over to the secular court. ¶ The **fourteenth** is when a cleric transfers himself to a way of life entirely contrary to the clerical state, such as when he becomes a soldier or contracts <term>bigamy</term><note>In medieval law, this often meant marrying twice (even if the first spouse had died) or marrying a widow, both of which were legal "irregularities" for a cleric.</note>.

¶ The **second rule** is this: ¶ Whoever is <term>excommunicated</term><note>The most severe ecclesiastical penalty, which excludes a person from the sacraments and the community of the Church.</note> because of laying violent hands on a cleric must be sent to the <term>Apostolic See</term><note>The papal office in Rome.</note> for absolution. However, this requirement fails <note>That is, an exception is made.</note> in fifteen cases:

¶ The **first** is when the striker is at the point of death. ¶ The **second** case is regarding one who has a just excuse, such as deadly enmity. ¶ The **third** is regarding doorkeepers who, by the necessity of their office, have struck someone, provided it was not an excessive blow. ¶ The **fourth** regarding the sick. ¶ The **fifth** regarding the poor. ¶ The **sixth** regarding those weighed down by old age. ¶ The **seventh** regarding a <term>cloistered</term><note>A monk or nun living within the restricted area of a monastery.</note> person who did not inflict a severe injury. ¶ The **eighth** regarding a woman. ¶ The **ninth** regarding those who are not <term>legally independent</term><note>original Latin: "sui iuris non sunt." This refers to those under the legal authority of another, such as children or dependents.</note>. ¶ The **tenth** regarding those who are of great power and are too delicate to endure the labor of traveling to the Roman Court. ¶ The **eleventh** regarding those who have not inflicted a heavy injury, but rather a light or moderate insult to clerics. ¶ The **twelfth** regarding a youth, whether he asks to be absolved before or after reaching <term>puberty</term><note>The age of legal responsibility in the eyes of the Church.</note>. ¶ The **thirteenth** regarding a servant, whose absence would excessively burden his master, or when the servant strikes a cleric specifically so that he might have an excuse to wander away. ¶ The **fourteenth** regarding those specifically named. ¶ The **fifteenth** regarding all those living in a community.

<meta>This page marks a transition from the previous discussion on canon law regarding physical violence against the clergy to a new section of Saint Bonaventure’s "Centiloquium." # The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

## ¶ On Divination
## ¶ Chapter 21

It follows next to consider those sins by which the height of divine wisdom is dishonored through the superstition of seeking knowledge, namely sorcery and **divination**. ¶ To understand this evil, we must see what divination is, what its various types are, and whether every form of divination is forbidden. ¶ Divination is described as follows: ¶ **Divination** is the superstitious investigation and foreknowledge of future events. ¶ There are five types of it, namely: **mantica**, which is named from a "maim" <note>original: "manco"</note> because of the spiritual defect that prevails within it. ¶ And this has five types of its own:

1. **Necromancy**, which is divination performed upon the corpses of the dead, from "nekros" <note>original: "nigron"</note> which means "dead" and "manteia" meaning "divination."
2. **Geomancy**, which is divination performed in the earth, from "ge" meaning "earth," such as by making circles in the soil.
3. **Hydromancy** is divination performed in water, from "hydros" which is "water," such as using rainwater.
4. **Aeromancy** is divination performed in the air, as in observing signs or circles in the atmosphere.
5. **Pyromancy** is divination performed in fire, from "pyr" which means "fire."

¶ These are the types of mantica. ¶ **Maleficium** (harmful magic) is a craft through which women create certain "bindings" to cause harm or inconvenience to someone, using items such as a cock's comb, a frog, or images of the victim. ¶ **Sortilege** (the casting of lots) is a method of divining through lots; the use of this was found in the Old Testament. ¶ Even the Apostles used lots before they received the fullness of the Holy Spirit <note>Referring to the selection of Matthias to replace Judas in the Book of Acts.</note>. ¶ **Augury** is a method of divining through the chattering of birds or their behaviors, such as their flight patterns. ¶ **Prestigium** (illusion) is that by which the eyes of men are <term>dazzled</term><note>original: "prestringuntur," meaning to be bound, blinded, or fascinated by a trick.</note> through diabolical illusions so that they cannot perceive the reality of things, but see instead fantastic figments, as enchanters do. ¶ Besides these methods, there are other lies—and many more—which are invented daily by the devil. ¶ There is also a form of divination that predicts the future through fate and the constellations; it is called <term>mathematics</term><note>In medieval scholarship, "mathesis" (with the middle syllable pronounced long) specifically referred to forbidden astrology or horoscopes, rather than arithmetic.</note>. ¶ There is also divination performed by the inspection of the air or the stars, observing specific days and hours for performing tasks; this is called **aruspicium**. ¶ There is also that which considers the entrails <note>The text ends mid-sentence: "exta v..." likely referring to "exta victimarum," the entrails of sacrificial animals.</note>

<summary>This page defines and categorizes various forms of divination and superstitious practices, framing them as a dishonor to divine wisdom. It provides a medieval taxonomy of magic, including necromancy, astrology, and illusions.</summary>
<keywords>divination, necromancy, geomancy, sorcery, superstition, astrology, medieval magic, Saint Bonaventure</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of forbidden divinatory practices begun on the previous page, moving from the five elements of "mantica" and harmful "maleficium" to specific roles and the moral arguments against them. It concludes the definitions and begins a theological condemnation of these practices.</meta>

### First Part.

He inspects the intestines of cattle. There is one who receives responses from demons at the altars of idols and is called an **ariosus** <note>original: "ariosus"</note>, a diviner at the altars. There is one who observes dreams; he is called a **Conjecturator** <note>original: "Coniecturator"</note>, an observer of dreams. An **Augur** <note>original: "Augur"</note> interprets the chattering of birds. A **Maleficus** <note>original: "Maleficus"</note> is an evildoer who sacrifices children to demons or performs rituals of "cleansing" for the sake of health. A **Magus** <note>original: "Magus"</note>, otherwise known as an **enchanter**, is a deceptive illusionist who tricks the eyes of those watching.

¶ The **Pytho** <note>original: "Phito"</note> is a ventriloquist who speaks through a malignant spirit; the name is derived from Apollo <note>Apollo was often called the "Pythian" god</note>, the god of divination. An **Aruspex** <note>original: "Auruspex"</note> is an inspector of the stars who also watches the flight of birds. An **Augur** is one who listens to the voices of birds. An **Extispex** <note>original: "Estipex"</note> is one who inspects the "exta," which is to say the intestines of cattle. An **Astrologer** is one who divines by the stars.

A **Genethliacus** <note>original: "Genitaliacus"</note> is one who attempts to predict the character and conduct of men according to the signs under which they are born; among the common people, such a person is called a **mathematician**, and his superstition is called a "constellation" by the Latins. A **horoscopist** <note>original: "horoscopus"</note> is one who observes the birth of men to predict their various and dissimilar fates. A **Sortilegus** <note>original: "Sortilegus"</note> is one who, under the guise of a false religion, professes the knowledge of divination or predicts the future by inspecting various writings. A **Saliator** <note>original: "Saliator"</note> is one who, whenever any part of a person's limbs twitches <note>original: "salierit," from which the term is derived</note>, predicts that something prosperous or troublesome is about to happen.

For indeed, all divination is a deception and a diabolical illusion; therefore, it is cursed by God and forbidden by the Church. ¶ Nor can anyone engage in such voluntary commerce without committing a sin. ¶ For it is either:
*   The sin of **infidelity**, in that one attributes something divine to the devil which belongs only to God.
*   Or the sin of **idolatry**, as seen in the offerings and sacrifices made.
*   Or the sin of **disobedience**, because it is done against the prohibition of God and the Church.

Such a prohibition is holy, because the one who practices these things sins against himself by committing himself to such danger. ¶ He also sins against the **Divine Majesty** by turning to the devil, as if God were not omnipotent.

<summary>This page defines a wide array of medieval and classical divinatory roles—from star-gazers to limb-twitch interpreters—and argues that all such practices are sinful acts of idolatry and disobedience against God.</summary>

<keywords>divination, astrology, augury, idolatry, superstition, Saint Bonaventure, demonology, medieval law, magic, sins of the intellect</keywords>

# ¶ The Short Talk of Saint Bonaventure <note>original: "Lentiloquii sancti Bonauenture"; the title refers to a concise theological treatise by Bonaventure, a 13th-century Franciscan theologian</note>

...the all-knowing and kind God of Christians. He sins against the Church, since he shares the same fate with him who is entirely cut off from the Church and opposes it, etc.

## On Simony, Hypocrisy, and Sacrilege. ¶ Chapter 27

It follows next to consider those sins by which holiness and goodness are cheaply lost through human perversity. I see that this happens in three ways: namely, by the simulation of apparent holiness, as in **hypocrisy**; by the abuse of a free gift, as in **simony** <note>original: "symonia"; the act of buying or selling sacred things or church offices, named after Simon Magus from the New Testament</note>; and by the violation of a sacred place, as in **sacrilege**.

¶ First, concerning hypocrisy, we must look at the definitions and distinctions. Hypocrisy is defined by Rabanus <note>Rabanus Maurus, a 9th-century Carolingian scholar</note> in this way: a hypocrite is one who wishes to appear as what he is not—namely good—acting as if he were a performer of another person <note>original: "persone representator"; this draws on the Greek origin of "hypocrite" meaning an actor in a play</note>. Also, it is defined another way: a hypocrite is one who wishes to appear good when he is not. Also another: hypocrisy is a vice enclosed by the simulation of virtue and cloaked. It can also be defined in this way: hypocrisy is a vice where someone wishes to appear good in an inordinate manner.

¶ According to Richard <note>Richard of Saint Victor, a 12th-century theologian</note>, there are four types <note>original: "species"</note> of hypocrisy: namely, the striving for dignity, for liberty, for authority, and for power.

¶ According to Gregory <note>Pope Gregory the Great</note>, the leopard in the Book of Daniel <note>See Daniel 7:6</note> that has four wings represents the hypocrite, who has four types of hypocrisy.

¶ The first is boasting about an obvious good.

¶ The second is faking a hidden good.

¶ The third is making excuses for an obvious evil.

¶ The fourth is the hiding <note>original: "dissimulatio"</note> of a hidden evil.

**Simony** is the deliberate will to buy or sell something spiritual or something attached to the spiritual.

¶ This, moreover, is contracted in three ways according to Gregory: namely, by a "gift from the hand," which is money.

¶ By a "gift from service," which is service unduly rendered.

¶ By a "gift from the tongue," which is favor.

There are, however, three material distinctions of simony. The first is when a spiritual thing is sold, such as the **sacraments** of the Church.

¶ The second is when something attached to the spiritual is sold, such as a **prebend** <note>original: "prebenda"; a portion of the revenues of a cathedral or collegiate church granted to a clergyman as a stipend</note> which...

<summary>This page defines and categorizes the sins of hypocrisy and simony, drawing on authorities like Rabanus Maurus and Gregory the Great to explain how these vices corrupt the Church. It emphasizes that hypocrisy is a form of spiritual acting, while simony is the illicit trade of spiritual goods for money, service, or favor.</summary>
<keywords>hypocrisy, simony, sacrilege, Bonaventure, Gregory the Great, Rabanus Maurus, Richard of St. Victor, church law, sacraments, prebend</keywords>

<header>First part.</header>

by reason of kinship, or price, or favor, it is given and obtained unworthily. The third type is when a spiritual matter is sold that is annexed to another spiritual matter, such as preaching. **Sacrilege** is the violation of a sacred thing, or the usurpation of the same, as if it were a "harming of the sacred" <note>original: "sacri ledium"; the author provides a popular medieval etymology for *sacrilegium*, combining *sacrum* (sacred) and *laedere* (to harm)</note>. Moreover, sacrilege is committed sometimes by reason of the person, such as when a member of a religious order or a cleric is struck. ¶ Sometimes it is by reason of the place, such as when the immunity of a church or a cemetery is violated. ¶ Sometimes it is by reason of the thing, as when a sacred object or something set aside for sacred use is usurped. Concerning this, a distinction must be made, because sacrilege is committed either by taking a sacred thing from a sacred place; or a sacred thing from a non-sacred place; or a non-sacred thing from a sacred place.

## On robbery, usury, theft, murder, and scandal: chapter thirty-one.

It follows next to examine those sins which are committed against one’s neighbor, of which there are three kinds. For in some of these, the neighbor is harmed in their possessions, which are **robbery**, **theft**, and **usury**. **Robbery** <note>original: "rapina"</note> is the manifest and violent seizure of another’s property. **Theft** <note>original: "furtum"</note> is the hidden seizure of another’s property while the owner is unaware and unwilling. ¶ **Usury** <note>original: "vsura"; the practice of lending money at interest, which was historically condemned by the Church</note> is the seizure of another’s property under the appearance of business while the owner is aware. ¶ In other sins, the neighbor is harmed in their person, as in **murder** and in war. Now, **murder** <note>original: "homicidium"</note> is the killing of a human being done by a human. ¶ Murder is twofold, namely spiritual and corporal. **Spiritual murder** is that by which someone is killed spiritually, which happens in two ways: namely by hating—for "he who hates his brother is a murderer"—and by slandering; for the tongue of the slanderer is a sharp sword. **Physical murder**, however, is that by which someone is physically killed, which happens in two ways: namely by the tongue and by deed. By the tongue it occurs in three ways: namely by commanding, by advising, or by failing to defend.

# The Hundred Meditations of Saint Bonaventure

In action, [it is done] in four ways: namely, by justice, by cause, by necessity, and by will. In certain ways, indeed, a neighbor is harmed in the mind, such as by **scandals**. Moreover, **scandal** is a word or deed that is less than right <note>original: "minus rectum"; meaning something that deviates from the moral path</note> which offers to others an occasion of ruin. "Scandalon" in Greek is *obex* in Latin <note>meaning a barrier, obstacle, or stumbling block</note>. Just as the foot of a traveler is injured by an obstacle placed in the road, so a word or deed that is less than right is an obstacle to those who are weak in morals. 

Sometimes scandal is called the "obstacle" itself—namely, the word or deed that is less than right. Sometimes, however, it refers to the action or the impact of the one who causes the scandal. At other times, it refers to the fall and ruin of the person who is scandalized. Therefore, according to its different meanings, scandal can signify the obstacle, the impact, or the fall. 

There is, however, a two-fold distinction of scandal: namely, **active scandal** <note>original: "actiuum"</note>, by which someone gives another an occasion for spiritual ruin; and **passive scandal** <note>original: "passiuum"</note>, which is the ruin itself caused by the obstacle of another’s bad example, such as when someone commits fornication following the example of another. 

To understand what things ought to be omitted to avoid scandal, two rules must be noted: one is from Saint Bernard, and the other is from Saint Jerome. 

The rule of **Bernard** is this: we sin out of weakness or ignorance, and sometimes even out of certain malice; we must take care not to do anything that results in the scandal of anyone. Concerning such cases, the Lord says to Peter: "But that we may not scandalize them," etc. <note>Matthew 17:27</note>. In the third case, however, when the scandal proceeds from certain malice <note>this refers to "scandal of the Pharisees," where people are offended not by a bad deed, but by their own spite</note>, a good work should not be omitted for that reason, nor should one desist from the work. Whence the Lord says in the fifteenth chapter of Matthew concerning the Pharisees who were scandalized out of malice: "Let them alone" <note>Matthew 15:14</note>. 

And the rule of **Jerome** is as follows: everything that can be done or not done while preserving a **three-fold truth** <note>original: "salva triplice veritate"</note> ought to be omitted for the sake of avoiding scandal. However, the truth of life, of doctrine, and of justice must not be omitted to avoid scandal. The truth of life pertains to every...

<summary>This page defines "scandal" as a stumbling block to others' faith and distinguishes between active and passive scandal. It provides theological guidelines from Bernard of Clairvaux and Jerome on when one should adjust their behavior to avoid offending others versus when to prioritize truth and justice.</summary>

### First Part

The truth of doctrine pertains to preachers and teachers.

**On the sins of lust and gluttony. Chapter 32.**

**I**t follows now to look at the sins by which a person sins against themselves. These are two: the sin of **lust**, which disarranges the generative faculty <note>The biological power of reproduction</note>, and the sin of **gluttony**, which disarranges the nutritive faculty <note>The biological power of nourishment and eating</note>. 

The sins that occur from the disarrangement of the generative power are these: namely, nocturnal emission, lust within one's own marriage, simple fornication, prostitution, abduction, adultery, incest, and the vice against nature <note>A medieval term usually referring to non-procreative sexual acts</note>. 

Sins from the disarrangement of the nutritive power are committed in two ways: by eating (which is called gluttony) and by drinking (which is called drunkenness). These occur in five different ways, contained in this verse:
**Too soon, too sumptuously, too much, too eagerly, too fastidiously.** 
<note>original: "Praepropere laute nimis ardenter studiose." These were the standard five categories of gluttony in medieval theology: eating before the proper time, seeking expensive food, eating to excess, eating with too much passion, or being overly fussy about preparation.</note>

Supposing that marital intercourse can be a sin, it must be seen through a distinction: which marital intercourse is a sin and which is not, and which is a venial <note>minor or pardonable</note> sin and which is a mortal <note>grave</note> sin. 

Note, therefore, that marital intercourse is either lawful, or "frail," or impetuous. 
**Lawful intercourse is three-fold:**
1. That which is done for the sake of generating offspring to be raised for divine worship; this is an act of the virtue called piety. 
2. That which is done for the sake of "rendering the debt" <note>original: "reddendi debitum"; the medieval legal and moral concept that spouses have a mutual obligation to fulfill each other's sexual needs</note>; this is an act of justice. 
3. That which is done for the sake of avoiding fornication; this is an act of caution, of which the Apostle says in 1 Corinthians 7: **"Let each man have his own wife to avoid fornication."**

**Frail intercourse** is that proceeding from human weakness, when someone seeks marital pleasure in a spouse not for the sake of having offspring, nor for rendering the debt, nor for avoiding fornication, but simply for the sake of pleasure in the spouse. Then...

<meta>catchword: ¶ And then</meta>

**The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure**

...distinguish, however: if that pleasure is loved below the level of God, then it is a venial fault <note>A lesser sin that does not fully break one's relationship with God</note>. Or, if it is loved more than God or equally to God, then the sin is mortal. One is "impetuous" who, proceeding from lust alone, exceeds the boundaries of decency and reason. And therefore it is mortal, which happens in five ways: 

¶ First, when it is done for the sake of fulfilling lust and meretricious <note>original: "meretricias"; relating to or characteristic of a prostitute</note> delights. 
¶ Second, when it is done against the natural manner. 
¶ Third, when it occurs during a forbidden time. 
¶ Fourth, when it occurs in a forbidden place. 
¶ Fifth, when a man approaches a pregnant woman near to childbirth, or one suffering from her menstrual flow <note>Medieval theologians often viewed intercourse during these times as a violation of the "natural order" or harmful to the health of the child/mother</note>. 

¶ **Simple fornication** is the lust by which an unmarried man <note>original: "solutus"; literally 'one who is loosed' or free from bonds</note> mingles with an unmarried woman in the natural use of the female; "unmarried" here excludes the bonds of marriage, blood relation, holy orders, religious vows, or life in a religious order. This refers to an unmarried woman who exposes herself to the lust of one or a few. 

¶ **Prostitution** <note>original: "Meretricium"</note> is the wicked commerce with a woman who submits to the lust of many. For a "prostitute" is properly defined as one who exposes herself to the lust of many. 

¶ **Adultery** is the violation of another’s bed. 

¶ **Incest** is the slip of the flesh committed against blood relation, affinity <note>Relationship by marriage rather than blood</note>, or a religious vow. These things have been briefly discussed above.

<meta>This page continues the systematic analysis of the properties of sin. Having previously discussed specific sexual sins like fornication and prostitution, the text now shifts to a metaphysical and psychological breakdown of how the human will moves away from God and toward worldly things.</meta>

### First Part.

...part of the turning. ¶ On the side of turning away from God are **contempt**, **omission**, **ingratitude**, **disobedience**, and **transgression**. ¶ On the side of turning toward a creature <note>In medieval theology, "creature" refers to anything created, such as physical objects or other people, as opposed to the Creator</note> are **lust** / **concupiscence** / **delight** / **consent to sin** / and **evil intention**. Following these are the **stain** <note>original: "macula"; the spiritual blemish or "dirtying" of the soul caused by sin</note> and **guilt** <note>original: "reatus"; the state of being liable for punishment</note>. The circumstances are those things we call the circumstances of a sin.

¶ Contempt, general omission, ingratitude, disobedience, and transgression accompany the turning away from God. For a will turned toward God and conformed to God wills what God wills and does not will what God does not will. And this is owed, firstly, because the will of God is simply superior. ¶ The will of a rational creature is inferior. And therefore it is bound to follow the superior; if it does not do so, it is said to "contempt" or despise. Secondly, it is because it is worthy to do whatever God wills to be done; therefore, when we turn away, we are said to "omit." Thirdly, it is because it is just to offer service in return for benefits received.

¶ Hence, when we turn away from God, we are called **ungrateful**. Fourthly, because He is the Lord and we are servants; therefore we are bound to do whatever we know He wills. When we do not do this, we are called **disobedient** and **transgressors**. We are disobedient because we turn away from the will of God. ¶ We are transgressors because we step across His law. ¶ And note that in the will turning itself away from God, contempt, omission, ingratitude, disobedience, and transgression are the same thing in reality <note>original: "fm rē" / "secundum rem"</note>; however, they are distinguished by their definition <note>original: "fm rationē" / "secundum rationem"</note>. ¶ For **contempt** regards God’s **power**. **Omission** regards His **wisdom**. **Ingratitude** regards His **benefit**. **Disobedience** regards His **precept** <note>A specific command or instruction</note>. **Transgression** regards His **law**. ¶ And thus, according to these, "withdrawal" <note>original: "recessus"</note> occurs in frequent turning away from God. ¶ "Distancing" <note>original: "elongatio"</note>, however, leads to **obduration** <note>original: "obduracionem"; a hardening of the heart where it becomes impossible to repent</note>. ¶ Lust accompanies the turning of the will toward a creature, which is the wickedness of the will...

# ¶ Saint Bonaventure’s *Centiloquium*
<note>The "Centiloquium" or "One Hundred Sayings" is a structured theological treatise traditionally attributed to Bonaventure, though modern scholars often view it as the work of a follower. It summarizes complex scholastic doctrine into manageable points.</note>

...[of the will] preferring a created good and deserting the Uncreated [Good]. This is followed by **concupiscence** <note>The inclination toward sin or worldly desire</note>, which is the first movement coming from the "fomes" <note>original: "fomite"; refers to the "tinder" or fuel of sin inherited from original sin</note>; it tends from sensuality toward illicit pleasure. Then follows **delight** when the lower part of reason takes pleasure along with it. Third is **consent**, when the higher part of reason does not contradict but assents. This triple state was signified in the first temptation by the serpent, the woman, and the man. <note>In medieval allegory, the serpent represents the initial suggestion of sin, the woman represents the "lower reason" (sensuality), and the man represents the "higher reason" (the intellect), which should have known better.</note> Then remains the completion of the deed, or there is still the evil intention. For the intention is toward the deed. And so, on the side of the "turning" <note>conuersionis: turning toward worldly things as opposed to turning toward God</note>, actual sin is preceded by **lust**, **concupiscence**, **delight**, **consent to sin**, and **evil intention**.

Now, **circumstances** are so called because they "stand around" the sin <note>The word "circumstance" literally comes from the Latin *circum* (around) and *stare* (to stand)</note>. A circumstance is a property of a person or a business <term>negotii: the specific act, case, or matter at hand</term> pertaining of itself to praise or blame, either by diminishing or increasing the severity, or by drawing the act into a specific kind of sin.

A property of a **person** is, for example, that a man is beautiful, wise, good, and other things of this kind. And such a property responds to the question made by **Who?**

A property of the **business** <note>the act itself</note> is that which pertains to the deed and the kind of deed, such as: **What** he did, **where** he did it, **why** he did it, **at what time** he did it, and **how** he did it; these are contained in this short verse:

->**Who, what, where, by what means, why, how, when.**<-
<note>original: "Quis quid ubi quibus auxiliis cur quomodo quando." This was a standard mnemonic used by medieval lawyers and theologians to investigate an act.</note>

These can be explained through nine distinctions contained in these verses:

->**Order, place, knowledge, time aggravate sins;**<-
->**Age, condition, number, delay, abundance, cause,**<-
->**And manner; in guilt: high status, slight struggle.**<-
<note>original: "Aggrauat ordo locus peccata scia tps / Etas conditio numerus mora copia causa / Et mod⁹ In culpa status altus lucta pusilla." These verses list factors that make a sin worse, such as the sinner's high social status or the lack of resistance they put up against the temptation (slight struggle).</note>

Of these, five look to the person: **knowledge**, **age**, **condition**, **order**, and **status**. Three look to the crime: namely, **abundance** [of will/pleasure], **cause**, and **slight struggle**. Those which are adjacent [to the act] are **place** and **time**.

<summary>This page outlines the psychological progression of sin—from initial desire to intellectual consent—and introduces the 'circumstances' (who, what, where, etc.) used to judge the gravity of a moral act.</summary>
<keywords>psychology of sin, concupiscence, consent of reason, circumstances of sin, moral theology, scholastic mnemonics, legal inquiry</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the mechanics of sin. It concludes the first part of the treatise by defining the lasting effects of a sinful act—specifically the "stain" and "guilt"—and transitions into the second part, which examines how sin damages human nature and the soul's natural abilities.</meta>

<header>Part One</header>

Regarding sin, truly, those things which follow are the **stain** and **guilt**. For the soul, when it is turned toward a creature <note>In this context, "creature" refers to any created thing or worldly object as opposed to the Creator.</note>, according to **Augustine**, it is darkened, worsened, and destroyed. This darkening, worsening, and destruction are the same as the deformity of the image of God, or the lessening of natural good, or a "stain." This is because from the spiritual contact with lower things—which happens through inordinate love—the image of God within us is sullied and polluted. 

**Guilt** <note>original: "reatus"; a legalistic term referring to the state of being "bound" to a debt or punishment due to a crime.</note>, however, follows sin by reason of the "turning away" <note>original: "auersionis"; the act of turning one's back on God.</note>, because God is so good (as Augustine says) that it is well for no one who departs from Him. Just as there is a stain from the turning toward a creature, so there is guilt from the turning away from God, which is an obligation to punishment. Indeed, sin passes away as an act, but it remains as guilt—that is, actual sin.

## The second part follows: On the wounding of natural powers. Chapter One.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'O'. The interior of the letter contains a portrait of a bearded king, representing Christ as Salvator Mundi, wearing a crown and holding a globus cruciger (an orb with a cross) in his left hand. The background features two stars.</image-desc>

**A**fter it has been seen concerning evil insofar as it holds the character of fault <note>original: "culpe"; the moral blame or the act of sinning itself.</note>, it must be seen concerning evil insofar as it holds the character of punishment. Therefore, it should be noted that evil or sin is punished with a manifold punishment in the present and in the future. In the present, however, it is punished as much in the soul as in the body. As for the soul, it is punished by the **wounding of natural powers** and the **stripping away of gifts of grace.** <note>original: "expoliatione gratuitorum"; "gratuitorum" refers to the "gratuitous" gifts or graces given by God that are not part of human nature itself.</note>

The wounds by which the soul is wounded because of sin are four, according to **Bede.** <note>Venerable Bede (d. 735), an English monk and scholar whose definitions of the "four wounds" of humanity became standard in medieval theology.</note> For he says that four things are inflicted upon us because of the sin of Adam: namely, **weakness**, **ignorance**, **malice**, and **concu[piscence]**...

<summary>This page explains that sin leaves a lasting "stain" on the soul by turning toward worldly things and "guilt" by turning away from God. It begins a new section detailing the four "wounds" of human nature—weakness, ignorance, malice, and concupiscence—inherited from original sin.</summary>

<keywords>stain of sin, guilt, image of God, Augustine, Bede, wounding of nature, original sin, punishment, grace</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion, completing a sentence regarding how concupiscence is composed. It then introduces a major heading for the "Centiloquium" (a collection of one hundred points of doctrine) and details how the soul’s departure from God results in four specific spiritual wounds.</meta>

...[concu]piscence <note>The text here completes the word "concupiscentia" from the previous page.</note> is treated and forged from these. The sufficiency of these is understood because, as **Augustine** says, the soul is in the middle between God and created things. God, however, is almighty, all-knowing, and supremely good, essentially undivided: power is attributed to the Father, wisdom to the Son, and goodness to the Holy Spirit.

# The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

Undividedness belongs to the unity of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit. Therefore, when the soul turns away from God and passes toward created things through love—deserting the **power** through which it can do whatever it is able to do—it incurs **infirmity** <note>original: "infirmitatem"; a state of spiritual weakness or lack of strength.</note>. Deserting the **wisdom** through which it knows whatever it knows, it incurs **ignorance**. Deserting the **goodness** through which it is worth whatever it is worth, it incurs **malice**. Deserting the **undivided substance** <note>Referring to the perfect unity of God.</note>, it incurs **concupiscence** <note>original: "concupiscentia"; in this context, it refers to disordered desire or the "tug" of worldly appetites that distract from spiritual goals.</note>, by which a human is divided within themselves and from themselves to such an extent that the will of sensuality is not subject to the will of reason. For we do not do the good that we want, since the flesh lusts against the spirit. <note>A reference to the internal struggle described by St. Paul in his letters to the Romans and Galatians.</note>

Now, **infirmity** results from the desertion of omnipotence—that is, the loss of the power to resist evil, or a difficulty in performing good works. **Ignorance**, resulting from the desertion of supreme wisdom, is a dullness in recognizing those things which are necessary for salvation. **Malice**, resulting from the desertion of divine goodness, is a proneness or inclination toward evil. **Concupiscence**, resulting from the desertion of the undivided substance, is the corruption that divides the appetites, so that the appetite of sensuality disagrees with the appetite of reason.

Indeed, our **first parent** <note>Referring to Adam.</note> sinned through his **rational** faculty by desiring improper knowledge; and therefore, in his rational power, he was struck down or punished through **ignorance**. He sinned in his **concupiscible** faculty <note>term: "concupiscible appetite"; the part of the soul that naturally desires pleasures and avoids pain.</note> by desiring the forbidden food; and therefore, in the concupiscible part, he was punished by a fall into **malice**, which is called a proneness toward evil. He sinned in his **irascible** faculty <note>term: "irascible appetite"; the part of the soul that deals with obstacles, anger, and the pursuit of difficult goods.</note> by desiring improper power; therefore, he was punished in the irascible part by falling into **infirmity**. From these three, the aforementioned concupiscence is forged. This fourfold punishment, wounding the soul through **original sin**, has been passed down to all who follow.

<summary>This page outlines the four "wounds" of the soul—infirmity, ignorance, malice, and concupiscence—which result from the soul turning away from God's power, wisdom, and goodness. It explains how these wounds relate to the Fall of Adam and are inherited as the effects of original sin.</summary>
<keywords>Original Sin, Four Wounds of the Soul, Concupiscence, Saint Augustine, Saint Bonaventure, Scholastic Theology, Rational Faculty, Irascible Appetite, Concupiscible Appetite</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the first part of the treatise, which discussed the four spiritual wounds (infirmity, ignorance, malice, and concupiscence), into the second part. It begins a detailed analysis of how sin strips the soul of its supernatural gifts and how sin itself can act as its own punishment.</meta>

# Part Two

## ¶ On the stripping of spiritual gifts. Chapter 2.

It follows next to consider the stripping of spiritual gifts: by which the soul is punished because of sin. Now, the **stripping of spiritual gifts** is the withdrawal of sanctifying grace <note>original: "gratiae gratum facientis"; a technical term for the divine grace that makes a soul pleasing to God and capable of salvation.</note>, the deadening of previous merit, the frustration of the redeeming blood <note>Referring to the sacrifice of Christ being made ineffective for that specific soul.</note>, an injury to the promoting angel <note>Likely referring to the Guardian Angel who assists the soul's spiritual progress.</note>, the enmity of the presiding Lord, the deformation of the divine image, the honoring of the demon, slavery to sin, the guilt of eternal punishment, the weakening of free will, the distancing from heavenly benefit, and the provocation of every creature to take vengeance against the enemies of its creator.

And because, as **Augustine** testifies, there is never the shame of a fault without the beauty of justice, nor is a fault committed without punishment being mixed in, therefore, **note** that certain things are sins in such a way that they are also the punishment for sin.

In a specific way, however, "sins and the punishment of sin" are those which have sadness and pain joined to them, such as **envy**, **anger**, and **sloth** <note>original: "accidia"; a state of spiritual apathy or "the noonday devil" that makes religious duties feel burdensome.</note>. Less specifically, those things are called "sins and the punishment of sin" which have joined to them the mere depravation of nature or ignominy; such as those sins because of which a person is said to be handed over to a depraved mind <note>A reference to the biblical idea in Romans 1:28 where God allows those who reject Him to sink further into their own poor judgment.</note>.

Generally, however, "sins and the punishment of sin" are—or can be called—all sins that occur between the first apostasy <note>The initial turning away from God, either by Lucifer or Adam.</note> and the final punishment of Gehenna <note>Hell.</note>. Blessed **Gregory** says that crimes are avenged by crimes. And a sin that is not washed away through penance soon draws the soul to another by its own weight. For although sin and the punishment of sin may be called the same thing, **note** however that every punishment, insofar as it is a punishment, is just and comes from God. Yet no fault <note>or "guilt"</note> is from God; thus a fault is not just, but comes only from the choice of the free will.

<summary>This page defines the "stripping of spiritual gifts" as the loss of divine grace and the various spiritual injuries that follow sin. It also explores the theological concept that sin acts as its own punishment, distinguishing between the injustice of the fault (which comes from man) and the justice of the resulting punishment (which comes from God).</summary>

<keywords>stripping of gifts, sanctifying grace, free will, St. Augustine, St. Gregory, envy, anger, sloth, punishment for sin, spiritual wounds</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the second part of Bonaventure’s "Centiloquium" (The One Hundred Sayings). While the previous page discussed the spiritual loss of grace and how sin acts as its own punishment, this section transitions into a systematic classification of different types of punishment and focuses on the physical hardships inherited from original sin.</meta>

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

¶ Punishment that is truly punishment is inflicted by God; however, that which is a fault or an inclination toward fault is either contracted or committed. ¶ And so, gather briefly that punishment is threefold: namely, acted, contracted, and inflicted. ¶ **Acted punishment** is that which follows from an actual sin. ¶ For whoever commits a mortal fault brings upon himself, or feels, the punishment of loss <note>original: "penaz dampni"; the theological term for the loss of God's presence or grace, as opposed to the "punishment of sense" which involves physical pain.</note>, because he loses the grace of God. ¶ **Inflicted punishment** is that which God inflicts upon a sinner, not by decree but by judging or taking vengeance. ¶ **Contracted punishment** is that which follows from original sin.

### ¶ On physical punishments. Chapter 3.

It follows next to see punishments in regard to the body which men suffer because of sins. ¶ Men suffer natural punishments because of fault, regarding which the blessed **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), a foundational doctor of the Western Church.</note> says that there are seven arrows bothering the nature of men: namely cold, heat, hunger, thirst, labor, disease, and death. ¶ These punishments were passed down from the first parents to their descendants, and therefore they are natural and general, having begun in those first parents themselves. ¶ For the first parents, immediately after their fault, felt the punishment of rebellion and shame in their flesh; and afterward, by divine judgment, the **man** incurred the punishment of labor and anguish, the punishment of hunger and need, and the punishment of death and being turned to ashes <note>original: "incineracionis"; referring to the biblical "dust you are and to dust you shall return."</note>. ¶ Upon the **woman**, however, punishment was doubled: because there was inflicted upon her the penalty of multiple sorrows in conception, the punishment of pain in birth, and the punishment of subjection to her husband in their life together. ¶ Many also suffer **personal punishments**. For many of the sons of Adam suffer a loss of limbs, some an excess of them, some internal corruption, and some external deformity; some are paralytics, some are epileptics <note>original: "epilantici."</note>, and others are afflicted with various infirmities.

<summary>This page categorizes punishments into three types (acted, contracted, and inflicted) and details the physical suffering inherited from Adam and Eve, including the "seven arrows" of human hardship.</summary>
<keywords>Bonaventure, punishment, original sin, Adam and Eve, St. Gregory, mortality, physical infirmity, gendered punishment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the third chapter of the second part of the "Centiloquium," following a list of physical punishments inherited from the Fall. The previous page ended with a mention of the hardships faced by men.</meta>

¶ header: Second Part

...and are oppressed by sicknesses and specific diseases; some are lepers, others are oppressed and possessed by demons. They also suffer temporal punishments, for as **Orosius** <note>Paulus Orosius (c. 375–418 AD), a Christian historian and student of St. Augustine, known for his "History Against the Pagans."</note> testifies: from Adam until our own time, the passing ages have been heavy with wars, troublesome with diseases, sad with famine, terrible with earthquakes, unusual with floods, fearful with eruptions of fire, savage with lightning strikes and plagues of hail, and wretched with parricides and crimes.

### ¶ On the places of punishment ¶ Chapter 4

Next, we must consider the punishment by which one is punished after this life. ¶ For the understanding of this matter, four things must be noted: namely, the distinction of places, the opening of the books <note>A reference to the Day of Judgment, where the "books" of men's lives are opened.</note>, the burning of worldly fires, and the tripling of the inflicted tortures. ¶ Furthermore, there is a threefold place or receptacle after this life for punishing the sins of men according to the three types of sin. For there is the place of **Purgatory** <note>original: "purgatorius"; a state of temporary purification for those who die in God’s grace but still need to atone for "venial" or lesser sins.</note>, and this is owed to **venial sin** <note>original: "peccatum veniale"; a "lesser" sin that does not result in a complete separation from God.</note>. There is **Limbo**, and this is owed to **original sin** <note>original: "peccatum originale"; the state of sin marking all humans as a result of the Fall.</note>. There is **Hell** <note>original: "infernus"</note>, and this is owed to **mortal sin** <note>original: "peccatum mortale"; a serious sin that destroys the relationship with God.</note>. ¶ And they are believed to be positioned in levels beneath the earth. ¶ These places, however, can be distinguished in another way according to a fourfold division. For a place of punishment is assigned to someone in two ways: either according to the **punishment of loss** <note>original: "poena damni"; the spiritual pain of being deprived of the vision of God.</note>, and such is Limbo; or according to the **punishment of sense** <note>original: "poena sensus"; the physical or sensory torment of fire or other pain.</note>, and such is Hell. ¶ Each of these categories, however, is divided into two. ¶ For those who are punished by the punishment of loss are either punished simply and eternally, and these are in the lower part of that place which is called Limbo; or they are punished not eternally but for a time, and these were in the upper part of the same place, which was called **Abraham's Bosom** <note>original: "abrahe sinus"; traditionally understood as the place where the righteous of the Old Testament waited until Christ's resurrection.</note>. ¶ Similarly, those who [suffer the punishment of sense...]

<summary>This page categorizes the various earthly and afterlife punishments for sin, defining Purgatory, Limbo, and Hell. It introduces the distinction between the "punishment of loss" (separation from God) and the "punishment of sense" (physical torment).</summary>
<keywords>purgatory, limbo, hell, mortal sin, venial sin, original sin, Abraham's Bosom, Orosius, punishment of loss, punishment of sense</keywords>

# Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

...the senses are punished, or they are punished simply and eternally, and these are in **Hell** <note>original: "infernus"</note> properly so called. ¶ The location of **Purgatory** <note>original: "purgatorius"</note> is determined according to common law; and thus it is to be believed that it is lower down, not higher up, just as is believed regarding **Limbo** <note>A place for those who, while not condemned to Hell, are excluded from the full vision of God, such as unbaptized infants or righteous people who lived before Christ.</note>. ¶ It is also determined according to divine law, and thus different places are granted to different people, either for their quicker liberation so that they might reveal their needs to others, or for our instruction so that we might realize after this life what great torment remains for sinners. ¶ Or it is determined by the command of some saint, as is read concerning **Saint Patrick** <note>A reference to "St. Patrick's Purgatory," a legendary site on Station Island in Ireland where the saint was said to have seen the punishments of the afterlife to convince skeptics.</note>, for whom the Lord obtained a place of purgation from God in a certain location. ¶ The place of **Hell**, however, is without doubt deep beneath the earth, in which all the damned shall be eternally afflicted—both humans and evil spirits—under the same material fire. This fire will always afflict the damned but never consume them, according to the requirements of their merits, torturing all their senses. ¶ Nor shall fire alone inflame the damned, but the other elements, confused and mixed, shall be turned against the damned; so that there shall be burning fire, freezing water, restless and disturbed air, and the stench of the earth. ¶ Whence **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory I (the Great), a foundational theologian for medieval views on the afterlife.</note> says: In hell there shall be unbearable cold, unquenchable fire, and intolerable stench.

## ¶ On the opening of the books ¶ Chapter 5

Next, we must consider the opening of the books—that is, the opening of **consciences** <note>original: "conscientiarum"</note>—which will take place at the **Judgment**. Regarding this, it must be held that in the Judgment a divine power will be present to each person, by which it will happen that all merits or demerits become known to themselves and to others. To themselves, their own deeds shall become known...

# Second Part

...so that individuals may have ready thoughts that either accuse or even defend them. ¶ Also brought back to the memory of individuals are their thoughts, affections, **works** <note>original: "opaciones," a medieval spelling for "operationes," meaning actions or deeds</note>, and intentions. This occurs to demonstrate **divine justice** <note>original: "divine iustitie"</note> according to their final state, and to demonstrate **mercy** <note>original: "misericordie"</note> according to their former or past state. To demonstrate divine justice in rewarding each person, the good or evil things in which they finally remained will be brought back to memory, because those alone are worthy of eternal retribution.

¶ To demonstrate divine mercy concerning the past, the evils of the good (from which they were mercifully freed) will be remembered, as well as the good deeds of the wicked, which the Lord mercifully worked through them. ¶ Not only to themselves, but also to others, the individual deeds and thoughts of each—both good and evil—will become known; and from then on they will be known and clear to all. And this is for the certain **bestowal** <note>original: "liberationem," here referring to the rendering or delivery of what is earned</note> of merits, which is known through the recognition of all good and evil acts, so that each person may be able to judge the merits of others.

¶ Indeed, the revelation of evil in the good will be for the manifestation of the divine mercy previously shown to them. ¶ The revelation of the good in them will be for the manifestation of justice, by which they are worthily rewarded by God. ¶ But the revelation of good in the wicked will be for the manifestation of the divine mercy shown to them in this world. ¶ Truly, the revelation of evil in them will be for the manifestation of justice, by which they are worthily condemned by the Lord.

¶ Nevertheless, the book will be opened which is the **Book of Life** <note>original: "liber vite"</note>; that is, the **Incarnate Word** <note>original: "verbum incarnatum," referring to Jesus Christ as the Word of God made flesh</note>. By His power all these things will happen. He will not be seen in His form of **Deity** <note>original: "deitas"; the full divine nature</note> except by the good. ¶ In the form of His **Humanity** <note>original: "humanitas"</note>, in which He will pronounce the sentence, He will be seen by both the wicked and the good; although in that same form He will appear terrifying to the condemned and gentle to the righteous.

# The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

¶ Concerning the fire of conflagration <margin>¶ Chapter VI.</margin>
Concerning the fire of conflagration <note>original: "conflagrationis," referring to a massive, world-consuming fire</note>, we must now treat. **On this point, it must briefly be held that a fire shall go before the face of the Judge, by which the face of the earth shall be burned up, so that the figure of this world shall perish in the conflagration of fires: just as was done by the flooding of waters in the Deluge.** **For just as** previously the world was destroyed by water—which is cold and purifying—against the heat and stench of lust with which that age was infected, so the final world must be ended by fire—which is hot and consuming—against the lukewarmness <note>original: "teporem," referring to spiritual indifference</note> of charity and the chill or coldness of greed, which will reign most of all in the old age of the world. **And** because it will then be necessary for men to be judged <note>original: "iudicari," though the OCR "iūgtas" is likely a contraction for "iustitias" or "iudicandos"</note> and punished, the hearts of all sinners will be terrified, who looked upon the Lord of all things with contempt. **And** because the dwelling place must be formed according to its inhabitant, then the whole world will be thrown into turmoil when man is in turmoil, just as it stood while he stood and in some way fell when he fell <note>This refers to the medieval belief that the physical environment reflects the moral state of humanity; the world "fell" into decay because of Adam’s sin</note>. **Because** also then the severity of the Judge is shown, every creature takes up divine zeal so that they may be conformed to their Author, and therefore it will be necessary for the hinges <note>original: "cardines," implying the fundamental pillars or foundations of the earth</note> of the whole world to be moved. **And** because there is nothing more intense, swifter, and more terrible in acting and moving among the other elements than fire racing through from every side. **Hence** it is that fire shall go before the face of the Judge himself, not from one part only but from every part of the world, so that there will be a coursing of elemental, terrestrial, purgatorial, and infernal fire. **And** then by the infernal fire the wicked <note>original: "reprobi," those who are rejected or damned</note> shall be burned; by the purgatorial fire the righteous shall be punished and purged. **By** the terrestrial fire, the things born of the earth shall be consumed, such as plants and animals. **By** the elemental fire, all things will be refined <note>original: "subtiliabunt," to make thin or subtle, a process of purification</note> and disposed toward the face of renewal <note>original: "innouationis," though OCR reads "iuocatiõis"</note>, and even along with this, not only men but also...

<summary>This page describes the "Fire of Conflagration" that precedes the Final Judgment, explaining how fire acts as a purifying agent to counter the sins of greed and spiritual coldness. It categorizes the four types of fire—infernal, purgatorial, terrestrial, and elemental—that will refine the world and its inhabitants.</summary>

## Concerning the torments of hell
### Chapter VII.

It follows to consider now, in the seventh place, the tribulation of the torments of hell. **Regarding the knowledge of this** matter, it must be held that all who actually sin and persist in their sins until the end shall be punished with a threefold penalty in hell, according to the manifold disorder that exists in mortal and actual sin. **In every** mortal sin, indeed, there is a disordered turning away from the highest light and goodness; and for this is owed the deprivation of the vision of God <note>original: "visio Dei," the beatific vision which is considered the ultimate joy of heaven; its absence is the "pain of loss"</note>. **There is** also a disordered turning toward a changeable good <note>The act of choosing temporary, earthly pleasures over eternal divine laws</note>; and for this is owed the penalty of material fire. **There is** also a disorder of the free will against the dictates of right reason; and for this is owed internal remorse <note>original: "remorsus intrinsecus," often described in medieval literature as the "worm of conscience" that gnaws at the soul</note>. **They shall also be punished** with eternal punishment insofar as the disorder in mortal sin is perpetual. **For the sin** that one commits, and of which one never repents, remains in the soul forever and separates one perpetually from eternal life—that is, from God—and it proceeds from a will that would wish to delight in sin forever. **And** although that passing delight is momentary, the disorder nevertheless possesses the character of perpetuity; therefore, the punishment corresponding to the disorder must have no end. Thus, just as man in his "forever" did not set an end to his sin by turning away from it, so God in His "forever" shall not cease from punishing. And just as he sins against the Infinite <note>In medieval theology, sin is seen as an infinite offense because it is committed against an infinite God</note>, so he should have an infinite punishment. **And** because he cannot endure an infinite punishment in its intensity <note>A human soul could not withstand a literal infinite degree of pain at a single moment</note>, he shall at least have a punishment that is infinite in its duration. **And** just as the will after death clings to evil without the possibility of receiving repentance, so God shall inflict affliction without any change of sentence. **They shall be punished** nonetheless with a bitter penalty according...

<summary>This page outlines the medieval theological framework for hell, explaining that punishment is threefold—loss of God, physical fire, and internal remorse—to match the nature of sin. It justifies the eternal duration of these torments by arguing that a sin against an infinite God requires an infinite (endless) response.</summary>
<keywords>hell, mortal sin, divine justice, vision of God, remorse, eternal punishment, scholasticism, afterlife</keywords>

<header>¶ The Lenten Discourses of Saint Bonaventure</header>

<margin>Concerning punishment</margin>
...and since the disorder in sin was a lustful pleasure, for lustful pleasure ought to be punished through afflictive pain. And because sinners delightfully clung to the creature <note>original: "creature," meaning any created thing or person rather than the Creator</note> and defiantly despised the Creator, they shall therefore be cast headlong into the lowest place, furthest from the state of glory: namely, into the deepest hell. There they shall be subjected to the torments of the lowest nature—that is, the dregs of worldly bodies—to be burned in a smoky and sulfurous fire, bound inseparably in that fire, and eternally tortured by it according to the requirements of their merit, yet never substantially consumed. And so it is clear that in the punishments of the damned there shall be variety; and with variety, eternity; and with eternity, bitterness.

### ¶ Part Three: Concerning that which is to be enjoyed and that which is to be used.

<margin>¶ Chapter 1.</margin>
<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'D' with ornate foliage and scrolling patterns inside a square frame.</image-desc>
**Having spoken** of the evil of guilt, which is to be fled, and of the evil of punishment, which is to be feared, we must now consider the good. This is done in two ways. First, in terms of merit in wayfarers <note>original: "viatoribus," those still traveling through this life toward their eternal destination</note>; second, in terms of the reward in those who have attained it <note>original: "comprehensoribus," those who have reached heaven and "grasped" the vision of God</note>. To the merit of the wayfarer belongs the knowledge and discernment of a threefold good: namely, the uncreated good which is God; the created good which is the world; and the conjoined good which is Christ. God is the Creator; the world is the creature; Christ is both Creator and creature. God is to be **enjoyed** <note>original: "fruendum"; in medieval theology, to enjoy something is to love it for its own sake as an ultimate goal</note>; the world is to be **used** <note>original: "utendum"; to use something is to employ it as a tool or means to reach one's ultimate goal</note>. In the happiness of Christ as God, we are to enjoy; in the conformity to Christ as man, we are to use. There are, therefore, things which are to be enjoyed as uncreated; there are those which are to be used as created; there are those which [are to be] enjoyed...

<summary>This page concludes the description of hell's eternal, sensory punishments before transitioning into a theological treatise on the "threefold good." It introduces the critical distinction between things to be "enjoyed" (God) and things to be "used" (the world) to reach Him.</summary>
<keywords>hell, eternal punishment, sulfurous fire, enjoyment (frui), use (uti), wayfarer (viator), Christology, uncreated good, created good</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general introduction of Part Three into the specific theological definitions of "use" and "enjoyment," a distinction famously established by St. Augustine. It concludes by outlining the upcoming structure of the book—God, the World, and Christ—and begins the formal discussion of the Trinity.</meta>

<header>¶ Part Three.</header>

...and they use them, just as angels and humans do. ¶ Things which are to be **enjoyed** <note>original: "fruendum"</note> make us blessed. Things which are to be **used** <note>original: "utendum"</note> for the sake of blessedness assist wayfarers <note>original: "viatores," those still traveling through this life toward heaven</note>. ¶ Those beings who enjoy and use have Christ—both God and man—so that they may delightfully contemplate His divinity and follow His humanity as their model. ¶ The deity of Christ especially makes the angels and the saints (even the "attainers" <note>original: "comprehensores," those who have finished the journey and now "grasp" the vision of God</note>) blessed. His humanity, however, especially gladdens and builds up those who are still wayfarers. ¶ Now, to **enjoy** is to cling to something in love for its own sake. ¶ To **use**, however, is to direct that which has come into use toward obtaining that which is to be enjoyed. ¶ Likewise, we enjoy those known things in which the delighted will finds rest. ¶ We use, however, those things which we refer back to that which is to be enjoyed. ¶ **Augustine** <note>referring to Saint Augustine's influential definitions in "On Christian Doctrine"</note> treats these two concepts differently, saying: ¶ To **use** is to take something into the power of the will. ¶ To **enjoy** is to use with joy—no longer merely in hope, but now in the reality of the thing itself. ¶ We must therefore look at these three things in order: First, concerning the triune and one God. Second, concerning the world composed of a threefold creation. Third, concerning Christ and His threefold benefit: namely, sacred doctrine or scripture, the grace of virtues, and sacramental medicine.

### ¶ What is to be believed concerning the triune God. ¶ Chapter 2.

Concerning the triune and one God, according to the right faith, we must believe, understand, and explain. ¶ To **believe** in the plurality of persons in the unity of nature. This means that in divine matters there is one nature and three persons: namely, Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. Of these, the first <note>the Father</note> is from none; the second <note>the Son</note> is from the Father alone through eternal generation; the third <note>the Holy Spirit</note> is from the first and the second through a **common breathing** <note>original: "spirationem," the technical theological term for the way the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son</note>;

<summary>This page defines the classical theological distinction between "enjoyment" (loving something for itself, i.e., God) and "use" (using created things to reach God), then begins a treatise on the Trinity.</summary>
<keywords>enjoyment (frui), use (uti), Wayfarers (viatores), Attainers (comprehensores), St. Augustine, Trinity, Father, Son, Holy Spirit, Spiration</keywords>
