<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the nature of God, transitioning into the formal scholastic definitions of the Trinity. It marks the beginning of the "Centiloquium" (The Hundred-fold Discourse), specifically addressing how God can be three distinct persons while remaining one single essence.</meta>

# ¶ The Hundred-fold Discourse of Saint Bonaventure

...so that the trinity of persons does not prejudice the unity, nor does the unity of essence prejudice the trinity. **W**e must also understand that in divine things there are two emanations: one by way of nature, by which the Son is generated; the **o**ther by way of will, by which the Holy Spirit is breathed forth <note>original: "aspiratur"</note> and proceeds. **T**hree hypostases <term>hypostases: a Greek theological term for "persons" or "individual realities" that subsist in the divine nature</term>: one of the Father, one of the Son, and one of the Holy Spirit. **F**our relations: namely, paternity, filiation <note>the state of being a son</note>, spiration <note>the act of breathing forth the Spirit</note>, and procession. **F**ive notions <term>notions: the specific characteristics or logical "signs" by which the divine persons are distinguished from one another</term>: namely, the four aforementioned relations, and innascibility <note>original: "innascibilitas," the property of the Father being "un-born" or without origin</note> is the fifth. Thus, innascibility (taken negatively) and paternity (taken positively) are the two notions of the Father. **F**iliation is the notion of the Son; active spiration is the common notion of the Father and the Son; procession is the notion of the Holy Spirit. **T**here are also three personal properties which are expressed by these names: namely, Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.

**W**e must, thirdly, express the mystery of the blessed divinity according to two modes of predication <note>ways of describing or attributing qualities to God</note>: namely, by way of substance and by way of relation. **F**or all categories spoken of God pass into "substance" <note>in scholastic theology, when we say God is "Great," we mean His very being is Greatness; it is not an accidental quality</note>, except for "relation," which—by reason of the subject in which it exists—passes into substance so as not to create any composition <note>a "composition" would imply God is made of parts, which theology denies</note>. **B**ut by reason of the term toward which it is directed, it remains [as a relation] so as to create a distinction. This occurs according to three modes of supposition <term>supposition: a term in medieval logic referring to how a word "stands for" or points to a specific reality in a sentence</term>, namely: by way of substance, notion, and person. Regarding these, such a rule is given: that when the essence is "supposed" <note>meant as the subject of the sentence</note>, the notion or person is not necessarily included; and when the notion is supposed, the person or essence is not included; and when the person is supposed, neither the notion nor the essence is [necessarily] the focus. This follows four ways of signifying substance: namely, the name of essence, of substance, of hypostasis, and of person. **E**xamples of these four are found in created things: "humanity," which signifies essence; "man" <note>original: "ho," likely the start of "homo" (man)</note>...

<summary>This page outlines the technical framework of the Trinity, defining the relationships and "notions" that distinguish the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit while maintaining their essential unity. It uses scholastic logic to explain how language can distinguish between God's single substance and His three distinct persons.</summary>

<keywords>Trinity, Saint Bonaventure, Scholasticism, hypostasis, divine relations, procession, emanation, divine essence, medieval logic</keywords>

# Third part.
f.i.

...namely "what" <note>original: "quid"</note> refers to the substance. In other contexts, "man" signifies the hypostasis <term>hypostasis: a Greek theological term for an individual, subsistent person</term> or the subject <note>original: "suppositum"</note>, while "Peter" identifies the specific person. This is expressed according to five modes of speaking or inquiring, namely through "Who" [quis], "Who" [quae], "Which" [quod], and "What" [quid]. "Who" [quis] is used by reason of the substance; "Who/Which" [quae] by reason of the hypostasis; "Which" [quae] by reason of the notion <note>the identifying characteristics of a divine person</note>; "Which" [quod] by reason of the substance; and "What" [quid] by reason of the essence.

¶ There are also three modes of differing. First, according to a different mode of existing or emanating: this is how one person differs from another person. Second, according to a different mode of relationship: in this way, a person and the essence differ because one person is related to another <note>e.g., the Father is related to the Son</note>, but the essence itself is not related to anything. Third, according to a mode of understanding: just as one substantial property differs from another, such as goodness and wisdom, and so on.

### ¶ How God, three and one, manifests himself in the effects of the creature. Chapter 3.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'D' decorated with scrolling foliate patterns within the letter's frame.</image-desc>

**C**oncerning God, three and one, according to the Catholic faith, it must be held that although he is unchangeable, invisible, and uncircumscribed <note>meaning he cannot be limited by space or boundaries</note>, he nevertheless makes himself manifest through diverse creatures—that is, through his many and varied effects. Thus, in all creatures, God is said to be present according to nature, and this occurs in a threefold way: namely, by **essence**, **presence**, and **power**. This is so that nothing remains outside the immensity of the divine essence; nothing is hidden from the clarity <note>original: "limpiditatem"</note> of the divine presence; and nothing can prevail against the infinity of the divine power. In this way, "to be" belongs to the most blessed Trinity. ¶ God is also said to **dwell** in the just according to grace, which is an effect of the most blessed Trinity that leads us back to God. Grace makes us "have" God, and allows God to be "had" by us; and through this, the Trinity is said to dwell within us. ¶ Finally, God is said to **sit** in the blessed <note>those in heaven</note> according to glory: by reason of which God...

<meta>This page continues the third chapter of Bonaventure’s "Centiloquium," following the discussion of how the Trinity is identified through specific linguistic terms. Having established the internal distinctions of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, the text now explains how these divine persons manifest within the human soul and the created world through their "missions" and "processions."</meta>

# The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

the Triune God rests in the minds of the blessed. In their power, He sits as equity; **In** their cognitive power, He shines as truth; **In** their affective power <note>the part of the soul responsible for feelings, desires, and the will</note>, He burns as charity. **H**e is said to appear sensibly <note>perceptible to the physical senses</note> with a distinct significance: just as the Holy Spirit appeared in the form of a dove over Christ, and in tongues of fire over the apostles. **A**nd because the persons are distinct, they can be indicated by distinct signs: so that the three persons together are said to have appeared as a Trinity of persons to Abraham in the form of three angels. **T**wo persons can also appear together, and any one of them is said to descend individually from the heavens. This occurs with the beginning of an effect that is both spiritual and sensible; although He remains immutable <note>unchanging</note> in Himself, He is said to become present to us from being absent when He begins to dwell in us through a gratuitous gift which we cannot see—as frequently happened in the primitive Church. **H**e is said to send or be sent in the context of eternal production. Indeed, the Father sends the Son when He signifies that the Son proceeds from Him through eternal generation <note>the technical term for the Son's origin from the Father</note>; the Father and the Son send the Holy Spirit when they signify that He proceeds from both through common spiration <term>spiration: from the Latin 'spirare' (to breathe); the theological term for the way the Holy Spirit 'comes forth' from the Father and the Son</term>. **T**he Father is not sent because He proceeds from no one. **T**he Holy Spirit does not send a person, but is sent, because He does not produce a divine person but is Himself produced. **T**he Son, however, is sent and also sends, because He is produced and He also produces <note>the Son is generated by the Father and, with the Father, 'breathes' forth the Holy Spirit</note>. **H**e is said to be incarnated through the commerce <note>interchange or union</note> of the divine nature and the human person <note>original: "humane persone"; usually understood as human nature united to the divine person of the Word</note> in a union; this union was effected only in the person of the Son, although the entire Trinity worked to bring it about.

## On the attributes appropriated to the Father.

Concerning the Triune and One God, according to the Catholic faith, it must be held that although there is one essence equally in the three persons, and they differ...

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the third part of the "Centiloquium," following a discussion on how the Trinity is described through specific terms. The previous page concluded by explaining that while the persons of the Trinity are distinct, they appear in the world through signs—such as the dove or tongues of fire—and dwell within the soul through grace. This page focuses on the specific attributes "appropriated" to the Father and begins the discussion of those attributed to the Son.</meta>

it is fittingly assigned to the Father: that **unity**, **eternity**, the **principle of origin**, and **omnipotence** be attributed to Him. These four are said to be "appropriated" <note>In theology, "appropriation" is the practice of attributing a specific quality to one person of the Trinity (Father, Son, or Holy Spirit) to help humans understand their distinct roles, even though all three persons share that quality equally.</note> to the Father, not because they are exclusive to Him—since they are common to all three persons—but because they lead us to a knowledge of the Father's person. He is the origin of the persons in the Trinity, having no beginning Himself but acting as the beginning for others, and from Him flows all power.

God's power, moreover, is complete: it is "not being able to fail," because He is not made from nothing. It is "not being able to be overcome," because He is not made from anything else. It is "not being able to be in need," because He never lacks any good. Human power, by contrast, fails in the act of sinning. It is overcome in suffering. In physical actions, it involves a state of need. 

Therefore, God is omnipotent; yet, sinful acts are not attributed to Him, such as being able to lie or do evil. Nor are "penal" <note>meaning acts involving suffering or punishment</note> acts attributed, such as being able to fear or feel pain. Nor are material or physical acts, such as being able to run or walk. God's power is also "ordered" <term>ordered power (potentia ordinata): the theological concept that God's power is not arbitrary but works in harmony with His wisdom and justice</term>, such that unbefitting acts are not attributed to Him—such as being able to make someone greater than Himself, or another god equal to Himself, or a creature that is actually infinite. For as Anselm <note>Saint Anselm of Canterbury (1033–1109), a philosopher-theologian known for his logical proofs of God's nature</note> says, "any inconsistency, even the smallest, is impossible for God."

### ¶ On the appropriations of the Son. Chapter 5.

To the Son are appropriated **truth**, **beauty** <note>original: "species"; here meaning form or outward beauty</note>, the **principle of exemplification**, and **omniscience**. These four are said to be appropriated to the Son, not because they are exclusive to Him—since they are common to the three persons—but because they lead to a knowledge of the person of the Son. He comes from the Father as the Word <note>original: "Verbum"; the Son is often described as the Logos or Word of God</note> who is equal to the Father. He is, therefore, supremely beautiful; in Him, all things foreseen and arranged are "exemplified" <note>patterned or modeled; the Son is the blueprint for all creation</note>. From Him, as the first and highest pattern, flows all knowledge. For the wisdom of God clearly knows all things: good and evil; past, present, and future; actual and possible; things incomprehensible to us and infinite.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine attributes. The previous page concluded the list of attributes "appropriated" to the Father—unity, eternity, origin, and omnipotence. It now transitions into how the single wisdom of God is named differently depending on its object (such as foreknowledge or predestination) before moving into Chapter 6, which discusses the attributes specifically associated with the Holy Spirit.</meta>

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure 
<note>original: "Lentiloquii"; the OCR likely misread the initial 'C' for an 'L'. This is the *Centiloquium* or "The Hundred Sayings," a systematic summary of theology traditionally attributed to Bonaventure.</note>

God's wisdom is such <unclear>that</unclear> in itself it is in no way diversified, even though it receives various names. For insofar as the wisdom of God is the knowledge of all possible things, it is called **knowledge** or **cognition**. Insofar as it is the knowledge of all things that are made in the universe, it is called **vision**. Insofar as it is the knowledge of all things that are done well, it is called **approval**. Insofar as it is the knowledge of all things that are in the future, it is called **foreknowledge** or **prevision**. Insofar as it is the knowledge of those things which are to be performed by God himself, it is called **disposition** <note>In this context, "disposition" refers to God’s internal plan or the arrangement of his creative acts.</note>. Insofar as it is the knowledge of those who are to be rewarded, it is called **predestination**. Insofar as it is the knowledge of those who are to be condemned, it is called **reprobation**.

However, these seven names can be reduced to three categories: 
1. The **knowledge of simple cognition**, by which God knows good and evil things that are, were, and will be, as well as those things that are possible but will never actually exist. 
2. The **knowledge of vision**, by which God knows all good and evil things that are, were, and will be. 
3. The **knowledge of approval**, by which God knows only the good things that are, were, and will be.

## ¶ On the attributes appropriated to the Holy Spirit.

<margin>Chapter 6.</margin>
To the Holy Spirit are appropriated: **goodness**, or the **reason for finishing** <note>original: "ratio finiendi"; this refers to the Holy Spirit as the "perfecter" or "completer" of the Trinity's works.</note>, and **will** or **benevolence**. These four are appropriated to the Holy Spirit, not because they are unique to Him—since they are common to all three persons—but because they lead us to a knowledge of the person of the Holy Spirit. He proceeds from the Father and the Son as **love** and as a **gift** that is supremely beneficial and communicative of all our desires, acting as our ultimate end. Every will that is rationally ordered and inclined tends toward this good as its supreme goal. Moreover, the will of God is so upright that it can in no way be distorted. So also...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of God’s attributes, moving from the internal nature of the divine wisdom to the external "signs" of the divine will. -># ¶ Third Part<-

[signi]fies that it can in no way be delayed. ¶ Thus, the single <term>will of God</term> <note>original: "voluntas dei"</note> is nevertheless signified by these five signs which are contained in this verse: 

->*He commands, forbids, permits, counsels, and fulfills.*<-

The will of God is called **providence** <note>original: "prouidentia"</note> when it is regulated by reason: by which all things that happen in the universe are governed beyond reproach. Since He commands, forbids, or counsels nothing except what is right; He does nothing except what is good; and He permits nothing to happen except what is just. 

¶ Furthermore, since the will of God is the highest standard of rightness, no one can be "right" unless they are conformed to it. Yet, one cannot be conformed to it unless that will is made known through certain signs, acting as a rule for the rectitude that must be pursued. 

¶ There are two types of **rectitude** <note>original: "rectitudo"; meaning moral uprightness or being "straight" with God's law</note>: 
1. The rectitude of **necessity**, which consists in doing the good that is commanded. 
2. And in turning away from the evil that is prohibited. 

¶ There is also a rectitude of **perfection**, which involves "supererogation"—doing more than what is strictly required—which is the subject of **divine counsel** <note>In medieval theology, "counsel" refers to the "evangelical counsels" like voluntary poverty or celibacy, which were seen as paths to perfection rather than mandatory commands.</note>. 

God's will is also most effective, such that no one can accomplish anything unless that will is a co-agent; thus **fulfillment** is a sign of His effective will. Likewise, no one can fail or sin unless God justly and deservedly "withdraws" His grace; thus **permission** is a sign of the divine will's justice in such a withdrawal.

# The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure
<note>original: "Centiloquii sancti Bonaventure"; a "centiloquium" is a "collection of one hundred sayings or points," a common medieval format for theological summaries.</note>

...[ma]tter of the **four elements** <note>In medieval science, the world was believed to be composed of four basic elements: earth, water, air, and fire.</note> and time. The adequacy of these is understood in this way: First, certain primary things had to be created within every genus of creature. That is to say, with respect to spiritual and active physical creatures, passive physical creatures, and also with respect to their measures. 

With respect to **spiritual creatures** <note>original: "creaturarum spiritualium"</note>, the **angelic nature** <note>original: "natura angelica"</note> was created first. With respect to **active physical creatures**, the **Empyrean heaven** <note>original: "celum empireum"; the highest, most refined heaven, often associated with light and the dwelling place of God and the angels.</note> was created first. Concerning this, **John of Damascus** <note>original: "dam."; an influential 8th-century theologian.</note> says that it is the container of all physical and spiritual creatures. With respect to **passive creatures**, the matter of the four elements was created first. With respect to measures, **time** <note>original: "tempus"</note> was created first; this is generally understood as the measure of any duration that has a beginning, whether it has an end or not. 

To understand this matter, it must be known that there are three primary measures of things that are measurable or durable: namely, **eternity**, **aeviternity**, and **time** properly so-called. 

**Eternity** <note>original: "eternitas"</note> is the duration of the Divine Persons, which has neither a beginning nor an end. Regarding this, **Boethius** <note>An early 6th-century philosopher whose definitions were standard in medieval schools.</note> says that it is "the total, simultaneous, and perfect possession of an unending life" <note>original: "interminabilis vite tota simul et perfecta possessio"</note>. 

**Aeviternity** <note>original: "eviternitas"</note> is the measure of those things that have a beginning but no end, such as angels, souls, the nature of the heavens, and the matter of the four elements. This measure is sometimes called **aevum** <note>meaning an "age" or "everlasting time"</note> and sometimes "created eternity." But because "eternity" is properly said only of divine things, and *aevum* is sometimes used to mean "time," the word **aeviternity** is formed from both of these terms. 

**Time** <note>original: "tempus"</note> properly so-called is the measure of those things that have both a beginning and an end, such as things that can be born and can decay <note>original: "generabilia atq(ue) corruptibilia"</note>, which vary according to the motion of the **first mover** <note>In the medieval geocentric model, the "first mover" (primum mobile) was the outermost sphere of the universe whose motion drove all other celestial and terrestrial changes.</note> until the completion of the number...

# Third Part

...[cor]ruptible things and human bodies; nevertheless, they act as signs of things and operations through light, heat, and power <note>original: "virtutem"; in this context, the inherent "virtue" or efficacy of a celestial body.</note>, so that they are not certain signs of future contingencies, nor do they influence free will through the force of the constellations, which some have called **fate**. And thus it is gathered from what has been said that God created things in an orderly way with respect to **time**; He arranged them in an orderly way with respect to **position**; and He governs them in an orderly way with respect to **influence**.

¶ In Sacred Scripture, He narrates in an orderly way according to the sufficiency of sacred doctrine: which in its beginning hints at a luminous nature by the name of **heaven**; an opaque nature by the name of **earth**; and a transparent nature by the name of **water**. It hints at the most blessed Trinity: the Father in the name of **God the creator**; the Son in the name of **the beginning** <note>original: "principij"; a common medieval interpretation of Genesis 1:1, associating "the Beginning" with the Word or the Son.</note>; and the Holy Spirit in the name of the **Spirit of God**. It also hints at the attributes assigned to the three Persons: namely, the **omnipotence** of the Father in producing; the **omniscience** of the Son in distinguishing; and the **benevolence** of the Holy Spirit in bestowing influence.

---

### -> ¶ On the Sufficiency of the Heavens ¶ Chapter II. <-

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'D' containing a central rosette motif surrounded by ornate line work.</image-desc>

Special attention must be given to the nature of heaven. "Heaven," according to common understanding, refers to natures that are transparent and containing. However, according to its proper definition, it refers to a nature that is transparent and containing, but subject to no contrary force <note>In medieval physics, "contrariety" (like hot vs. cold) caused decay; the heavens were "incorruptible" because they lacked these opposing forces.</note>. According to the first understanding, the heavens are distinguished by **Rabanus** <note>Rabanus Maurus (c. 780–856), an influential Benedictine scholar whose encyclopedias were standard references in the Middle Ages.</note> as follows: the aerial heaven, the ethereal heaven, the Olympian heaven, the fiery heaven, the starry heaven, the watery heaven, and the **Empyrean heaven**.

The sufficiency <note>In scholastic writing, "sufficiency" (sufficientia) refers to a logical proof that a list is complete and covers all possibilities.</note> of these is understood this way: for the nature of heaven—being transparent and containing—is either a **giver of light** or a **receiver of light**. If it is a giver, it is either uniform and unmoving—and this is the **Empyrean heaven** <term>Empyrean: the highest heaven, believed to be made of pure light and the dwelling place of God.</term>—or it is multiform and moving—and this is the **starry heaven** <note>original: "sydereū"; the sphere containing the fixed stars and constellations.</note>—or it is uniform and moving—and this is the **crystalline heaven** <note>original: "cristallinū"; a transparent sphere often associated with the "waters" mentioned in Genesis.</note>. A fourth distinction is not found: namely, one that is multiform and unmoving.

<summary>This page discusses how God's orderly creation is reflected in the elements and the Trinity, and begins a classification of the various celestial spheres (heavens) based on their movement and light.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, free will, fate, Trinity, cosmology, Rabanus Maurus, Empyrean, starry heaven, crystalline heaven</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the medieval cosmological classification of the "heavens." Having established that God created the world with order in time, position, and influence, the text now details the specific layers of the celestial spheres based on their transparency and luminosity.</meta>

# ¶ The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

If, however, a transparent <term>perspicua</term><note>In medieval science, "perspicuous" refers to a substance's ability to transmit light, similar to our modern concept of transparency.</note> nature is receptive to light, it is receptive either separably or inseparably. If separably, it is either according to the circle of the uppermost surface, and thus it is the **Olympian**—named for its proximity to Mount Olympus; or if it is separable according to the higher part, it is the **ethereal**; or according to the lower part, it is the **aerial**. 

Furthermore, if "heaven" is defined as a transparent nature that contains nothing contrary to itself, it is distinguished by some into eight spheres: the seven planets along with the eighth sphere <note>The eighth sphere in the Ptolemaic system contained the "fixed stars," which moved together as a single unit.</note>. By those who are more learned, it is divided into nine; and by others even further enlightened, into ten. 

The logical classification of these can be understood in this way: the distinction of the heavens is determined either by the primary diversity of their forms, or by the arrangement of the movements and the light-bearing bodies <term>luminaria</term> they contain. 

**If** by the diversity of forms: then three heavens are distinguished. For either a nature of perfect luminosity flourishes in the heaven—and thus it is stable in a state of glory and rest—this is the **empyrean**; or there flourishes in it a nature of perfect transparency and effective coldness—and thus it is the **aqueous** or **crystalline** heaven, which serves to preserve corruptible things and is therefore capable of motion; or there flourishes in it a nature of both perfect transparency and luminosity along with effective heat—and thus it is the **starry** <note>original: "sidereum"</note> heaven, which causes the transformation of lower things and is therefore multiform and capable of motion. 

**If**, however, the heavens are distinguished according to their light-bearing bodies, then the distinction of the spheres must be considered. For either a body <note>original: "cordis"; likely a scribal contraction for "corporis" (body).</note> contains a multitude of stars—and thus it is the starry heaven or the heaven of the fixed stars; or it contains a single unit—and thus the seven planets are distinguished in their own spheres, by which the whole of time is governed, distributed through the seven-day cycle.

### ¶ On the Four Attributes of the Angels and their Condition, Chapter XIII.

## ¶ Part Three

Regarding the purely spiritual nature, such as that of the **angelic**, six things are to be examined in order: namely, the condition of all angels; the transgression <term>preuaricatio</term> <note>original: "preuaricatio"; specifically referring to the "stepping aside" or "falling away" of the rebellious angels.</note> of the wicked demons; the confirmation of the blessed spirits; the definition of certain common names; the division of the hierarchies of different ranks; and the guardianship of the humans committed to them. 

¶ Regarding the condition of the heavenly spirits, it must be held that from the very beginning of their creation, four attributes were assigned to the angels: namely, **simplicity of essence**; **personal distinction** by reason of their naturally implanted intellect; **memory, intelligence, and will**; and **freedom of choice** <term>libertas arbitrij</term> <note>A central concept in medieval theology, referring to the power of the will to choose between good and evil without external compulsion.</note> to select the good and reject the evil. 

¶ These four principal attributes are accompanied by four others: namely, **efficacy** <term>virtuositas</term> <note>original: "virtuositas"; in this context, it refers to the inherent power or capacity of a spirit to act effectively.</note> in operation; **dutifulness** in ministering; **insight** in knowing; and **immutability** in choosing. I say immutability <note>Scholastic theologians argued that once an angel makes a definitive choice (unlike humans who vacillate), that choice is permanent due to the clarity of their intellect.</note> after the choice has been made, whether for good or for evil. 

¶ Indeed, a spiritual and incorporeal substance that is simple and lacking all division of quantity—which is the nature of the angelic—is, by the very fact that it is simple, efficacious in its operations. By the very fact that it is efficacious and personally distinct, the distinction of its office in ministering is appropriate to it. By the very fact that it is simple and efficacious, insight in discernment is appropriate to it. Finally, by the very fact that it is insightful and simple, possessing a God-like <term>deiformem</term> <note>original: "deiformem"; literally "God-formed," referring to an intellect that perceives reality in a way similar to God's own vision.</note> intellect, it possesses stability in its choice after the selection is made, whether that choice was good or evil. 

¶ The sufficiency of these four is understood as follows: an angel can be considered in four ways: namely, in itself, and thus there is **simplicity of essence**; in relation to another angel, and thus there is **personal distinction**; in relation to God, and thus there is **memory, intelligence, and [will]**...

<summary>This page introduces the study of angels, detailing their fundamental spiritual attributes such as simplicity, free will, and their "God-like" intellect which leads to permanent moral choices.</summary>
<keywords>angelology, simplicity of essence, free will, deiform intellect, angelic hierarchy, scholastic theology, immutability</keywords>

...the will, in which the essence of the divine image consists: toward those things which are subject to its governance, and thus it is the liberty of **free choice** <term>liberi arbitrij</term>.

## ¶ On the transgression of the wicked spirits, chapter fourteen.

Regarding the transgression <term>preuaricatione</term> <note>original: "preuaricatione"; here meaning the "stepping out of line" or the moral fall of the angels.</note> of the damned spirits, it must be held: that **Lucifer**, the first among the angels, presuming upon a private good, desired a private excellence, wishing to be raised above the others. Therefore, with all those who clung to him, he lost the highest place—namely, the **empyrean heaven**—descending into the lowest place—namely, the murky air or hell. 

He fell into guilt through free choice, but slid into punishment through divine judgment. Having fallen, he became immutable <note>original: "immutabilis"; in scholastic theology, this means the angelic will is "locked" in its choice once made, unlike the human will which can fluctuate.</note> after his ruin. Therefore, he was immediately hardened in evil, blinded from the truth, disordered in action, and weakened in power. 

The stubborn will of the **devil** possesses a deformity in which there is a continuation [of sin]; a variation of nature; and a cutting off or a lessening. By reason of the first stain, from whose grip he is in no way separated, he has a proneness in which there is a continuation as far as the act of sinning is concerned; however, a variation intervenes regarding the kind of sin. 

The reason <note>Meaning the rational faculty or intellect.</note> in the devil is indeed blinded; yet he sometimes foresees future things, which **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), whose theories on the natural powers of demons were foundational for medieval thinkers.</note> says happens in four ways: namely, by the sharpness of his senses or intellect; by long experience over time; by deceitful cunning—because he sometimes proposes to do what he predicts; and by foreign instruction—when he learns something from the [holy] angels by the permission of God’s just judgment. 

The mysterious operation <term>misterialis operatio</term> <note>original: "misterialis operatio"; referring here to "magic" or hidden demonic workings in the physical world.</note> of the devil is forbidden to humans, both on account of the sin of infidelity, lest [glory] be attributed to the devil...

<summary>This page describes the fall of Lucifer due to pride and the resulting permanent state of the fallen angels' wills and intellects. It also explains, following Augustine, how demons are able to predict the future despite being spiritually blinded.</summary>

<keywords>Lucifer, Fall of Angels, Free Choice, Empyrean Heaven, Saint Augustine, Demonic Knowledge, Occult Operations</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the fallen angels’ state, specifically finishing the description of their darkened intellect before distinguishing between divine miracles and demonic wonders. <header>¶ Part Three</header>

...blinded in respect to that which is of God. This is both on account of the sin of idolatry—since the devil desires something to be sacrificed to himself—and on account of the sin of disobedience, because voluntary commerce with the devil is strictly forbidden by both God and the Church. The power of the devil, having been rightly restricted by the merit of his sin, is used—when permitted—to perform wonders <term>mirabilia</term> <note>Wonders or "marvels" as opposed to true miracles; things that cause amazement but do not break the laws of nature in the same way divine acts do.</note> through sudden transformations of physical creatures. ¶ The miracles of the devil can be distinguished from the *true miracles of God in four ways*: namely, on the part of the principal agent, the minister, the purpose, and the deed itself.

1.  **On the part of the principal agent:** In the miracles of God, the power of infinite potential is at work. In the miracles of the devil, it is only the power of a created being.
2.  **On the part of the minister:** The miracles of God are performed through "public justice" <note>original: "publicam iusticiam"; in this context, it refers to the legitimate authority and order established by God.</note>, namely through justifying faith which is preached publicly, or signs of public justice, as seen in sinners who have sometimes worked miracles through faith. The miracles of the devil are performed through private contracts and certain secrets, for this is how the inventors of the magical arts made pacts with demons.
3.  **On the part of the purpose:** The miracles of God are performed for the benefit of humanity and the glory of God. The miracles of demons and sorcerers <term>magorum</term> are for deception and fraud.
4.  **On the part of the deed:** The miracles of God are excellent, such as raising the dead, giving sight to the blind, and the like. The miracles of the devil are base <note>original: "vilia"; meaning cheap, common, or lowly.</note>, such as producing flies, toads, and serpents.

### ¶ On the confirmation of the good spirits. Chapter fifteen

It follows next to consider the confirmation of the blessed spirits. Regarding them, it must be held that while others were apostatizing <note>original: "apostazantibus"; those who abandoned their station and rebelled against God.</note>, these spirits adhered immovably to the Lord. Thus, while the others were irrevocably hardened through their malice, these were perfectly confirmed in their will through grace and glory.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the state of the angels. While the previous page focused on the fall and limitations of the demons, this page describes the spiritual perfection and dual nature—contemplative and active—of the faithful angels who remained with God. It also transitions into a systematic definition of angelic structures.</meta>

<header>The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure</header>

In a similar way, in terms of time rather than by their inherent nature, they received through merit the grace and glory of turning toward and clinging to God. Because of this, they were made stable and blessed in their **will**. In their **reason**, they are perfectly enlightened according to "morning knowledge" and "evening knowledge." <note>These terms, popularized by St. Augustine, describe how angels perceive reality. "Morning knowledge" is seeing things as they exist in God's mind; "evening knowledge" is seeing things as they exist in their own created nature.</note> Truly, the divine light, by illuminating the angel, makes that angel a light. Now, the knowledge of God in Himself is called "day," because it is pure light, serving as both the knowledge and the reason for knowing. However, the angelic knowledge, having been divinely illuminated, involves knowing the creature—which is considered darkness in comparison to God. Therefore, that knowledge is called "evening." Insofar as it is known in itself, it is called "morning" when the angelic mind rises from that knowledge to the praise of God, referring that knowledge back to the knowledge it has in Christ <note>The original uses the shorthand "Xbo" for "Christo."</note> and by His power. 

And in their **virtue** <note>Meaning their inherent power or strength.</note>, they are perfectly strengthened, whether in acting or in carrying out tasks, and this is true whether they have assumed a physical body or not. <note>Medieval theologians believed angels, though pure spirits, could "assume" bodies made of condensed air to become visible to humans.</note> In their operation, they are perfectly ordered, whether in contemplation or administration. For the angelic power is twofold: **contemplative**, by which they contemplate God, and **business-like** or **administrative**, by which they perform tasks concerning the salvation of men. They cannot be disordered by ascending to the contemplation of God, nor by descending to serve humanity; for wherever they are sent, they always run and serve within God.

<header>### Third Part</header>

...the angelic order arises. ¶ **From several orders**—at least three—the angelic hierarchy is established. ¶ **Angel** is defined by **Damascene** <note>John Damascene (c. 675–749), a Syrian monk whose writings became a standard authority for medieval theologians.</note> as follows: **An angel is an intellectual substance, always in motion, free in its choice, incorporeal, serving God, and receiving immortality according to grace, not by nature.** <note>Theologians emphasized that only God is immortal by His own essence; angels are kept immortal by God’s ongoing gift of grace.</note> ¶ **Order** is defined by **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great (d. 604), who organized the traditional nine choirs of angels.</note> as follows: **It is a multitude of heavenly spirits who are similar to one another in some gift of grace, just as they also agree in the gift of their natural endowments.** ¶ However, a **triple** definition of **hierarchy** is found, according to the three types of hierarchy: divine, angelic, and ecclesiastical. ¶ **Divine** hierarchy is defined by **Dionysius** <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, the 5th-century author of "The Celestial Hierarchy," which is the foundation for all medieval angelic study.</note> as follows: **Hierarchy is divine beauty** as it is simple, as it is best, and as it is perfecting. **Angelic** hierarchy is defined by the same author: **Hierarchy is a divine order**, a science, and a <term>deiform</term> <note>original: "deiformis" - meaning "God-like" or "conformed to the shape of God."</note> action—as far as possible—rising proportionally into the likeness of God according to the illuminations divinely bestowed upon it. **Ecclesiastical** <note>Referring to the hierarchy of the Church on Earth.</note> hierarchy is defined in a masterly way: ¶ **Hierarchy is the ordered power of sacred and rational things, holding the proper leadership over its subjects.** ¶ This definition can also apply to the angelic hierarchy. ¶ **Damascene defines** the angel in terms of its **substance** when he says an angel is an "incorporeal substance"; in terms of its **power** <note>original: "virtutem"</note> when he says "intellectual, always in motion, and free in its choice." Here a threefold angelic power is touched upon: namely, the **cognitive** <note>The power of knowing.</note> when he says "intellectual"; the **operative** <note>The power of acting.</note> when he says "always in motion," because it works tirelessly; ¶ and the **imperative** <note>The power of willing or commanding.</note> when he says "free in its choice." ¶ **Operation** is touched upon when he says "serving God." ¶ **Duration** is mentioned when he says "receiving immortality by grace, not by nature." ¶ **Order** is identified regarding its foundation when it is said: "order is a multitude of heavenly spirits," and regarding...

# ¶ The Brief Discourse of Saint Bonaventure

...to its completion when it is said that they are similar to each other by some gift of grace; and as for their disposition, when it is said that they hold the middle place among these things, just as they also agree in the participation of natural gifts. The definition of the **angelic hierarchy** indicates that it proceeds from God in the manner of an image: when it says **hierarchy is a divine order, a science, and an action**. <note>original: "ierarchia est ordo diuinus. scientia. et actio." This definition comes from Pseudo-Dionysius, emphasizing that hierarchy is not just a ranking, but a way of knowing and acting like God.</note> Insofar as it is an **order**—that is, an ordered power—it corresponds to the **Father**; as **science**, to the **Son**; and as **action**, to the **Holy Spirit**, according to memory, intellect, and will. <note>Bonaventure follows the tradition of St. Augustine, who saw the "Image of God" in the human and angelic soul as a trinity of memory, understanding, and will.</note> Secondly, it is described as proceeding in the manner of a likeness when it is said: **conforming to the shape of God as much as possible**, <note>original: "deiforme quantum possibile est similans"</note> both in terms of habit and in terms of the inherent and divine illuminations, rising proportionally into the likeness of God according to the act. In the definition of the **uncreated hierarchy** <note>The "uncreated hierarchy" refers to the Trinity itself—the source and model for all created order.</note> it is shown that there is unity in trinity when it is said that **divine beauty** and its perfection consist of personality and equality. However, to show that plurality does not prejudice unity, it is called **simple**; that unity looks to plurality, it is called **best**; and because unity looks to the perfection of plurality and vice versa, the word **preserving** is added. <note>original: "conseruatiuū"</note>

## ¶ On the Triple Division of the Hierarchy / Chapter Seventeen

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter S featuring floral or foliage patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

It follows next to consider the three divisions of the hierarchy. The first is this: one hierarchy is **supercelestial**, which is the divine; another is **celestial**, which is the angelic; and another is **subcelestial**, which is the ecclesiastical or human. Setting aside the ecclesiastical and divine, the angelic hierarchy is subdivided by a second division into the **highest**, **middle**, and **lowest**; or by other names, into the **epiphania**, **hyperphania**, and **hypophania**. <term>epiphania, hyperphania, hypophania</term> <note>These are Greek-derived terms used by medieval scholars to describe how God's light is revealed: 'hyperphania' (appearing above/the highest), 'epiphania' (appearing upon/the middle), and 'hypophania' (appearing below/the lowest).</note>

<summary>This page defines hierarchy as a reflection of the Trinity (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit) and introduces the three-fold division of all order into Divine, Angelic, and Human systems.</summary>

### Part Three

**Epiphany** <note>original: "Epiphania." While usually referring to the feast of the Magi, here it denotes the "highest manifestation" of the upper hierarchy.</note> is the result of the burning heat of glowing action, the height of higher intellect, and the balanced scale of judgment. **Hyperphany** <note>original: "Hyperphania," meaning "shining above."</note> is a divine illumination that shares in itself, distinguished by a reverence that steps between levels; it teaches the use of mastery and wards off what is contrary. **Hypophany** <note>original: "ypophania," meaning "shining below."</note> is a divine participation that meets the laws of nature, unlocking hidden secrets according to a distinct capacity for them.

¶ The **third** division is this: **In** the supreme hierarchy, which is called epiphany, are the seraphim, cherubim, and thrones. In the middle hierarchy, or hyperphany, are the dominions, virtues, and powers.

¶ **In** the lowest hierarchy, or hypophany, are the principalities, archangels, and angels.

¶ The angels **act** and are acted upon according to the hierarchical order initiated in them by nature and perfected through glory. This glory stabilized the changeability of their free will, enlightened their insight, strengthened their virtue, and ordered their dutifulness.

¶ **Insight** of reason in contemplation either looks toward the goodness to be desired—and these are the **seraphim**;

¶ **Or** toward the truth to be understood—and these are the **cherubim**;

¶ **Or** toward the majesty to be venerated—and these are the **thrones**.

¶ To the thrones belongs reverence.

¶ To the cherubim, wisdom.

¶ To the seraphim, benevolence.

¶ To perfect **virtue** belongs the commanding power which is in the **dominions**; the executive power which is in the **virtues**; and the expediting power which is in the **powers**, whose role is to ward off contrary powers.

¶ To perfect **duty** belongs the act of governing, which the **principalities** perform; directing, which the **archangels** perform; and revealing, which the **angels** perform.

¶ Moreover, an **order** ought to be named after that which receives excellence in its specific gift: namely Raphael, Gabriel, Michael, and Uriel. <note>These four archangels are often cited in medieval tradition as the leaders of the angelic orders, though only the first three are named in the standard Catholic biblical canon.</note>

<summary>This page defines the three levels of the angelic hierarchy—Epiphany, Hyperphany, and Hypophany—and assigns the nine traditional choirs of angels to them based on their specific spiritual capacities for wisdom, power, and service.</summary>

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure
## ¶ On the Guardianship of Humanity by Angels: Chapter Eighteen.

<image-desc>Large decorative initial 'I' marking the start of the chapter text. The letter is white with internal scrollwork patterns, set against a dark square background.</image-desc>

**Now** sixth and lastly regarding the angelic nature, we consider the guardianship of the human beings sent into their governance. To understand this, it should be noted that angels have the task of relieving four specific defects in humans: namely, an inclination toward evil, a difficulty in doing good, a distance from the Lord, and a lukewarmness of mind. To address these four, four names of angels—and no more—have been revealed to humanity.

¶ **Raphael**, which is interpreted as "the medicine of God" <note>original: "medicina dei"</note> or "the healing Lord." He was sent to cure Tobit <note>Tobit is a figure from the Deuterocanonical Book of Tobit who was struck blind by bird droppings.</note> from blindness of the eyes, and to protect Sarah from her shame and the slaying of her husbands <note>Sarah’s first seven husbands were killed by a demon on their wedding nights until Raphael intervened.</note>. This serves as a remedy against two things we have contracted from original sin: ignorance of what ought to be done and the desire for harmful things—the two ways we are made prone to fall into evil.

¶ **Likewise Gabriel**, which means "the strength of God" <note>original: "fortitudo dei"</note>. Through him, we are strengthened and comforted in the observance of God’s commandments, and we are prepared and made capable of fulfilling His counsels. As a sign of the first (the commandments), he was sent to announce the birth of the Precursor <note>John the Baptist, who "ran before" Jesus to prepare His way.</note> to Elizabeth. As a sign of the second (the counsels), he was sent to announce the birth of the Savior to the Virgin Mary. Through John’s example, we are made strong to conquer evil; through Christ’s grace and example, we are made ready and inclined toward what is good.

¶ **Michael**, which is interpreted as "Who is like God?" <note>original: "quis vt deus"</note>. He is said to present souls to the Lord in heaven. For the sake of those souls who must be rescued from the hand of the devil, he committed to battle with the dragon. We solemnly celebrate two of his feast days: the Apparition <note>Referring to his appearance at Mount Gargano.</note> and the Dedication <note>Referring to the dedication of the Basilica of St. Michael in Rome.</note>. This is a sign that he is with us while we live in this world and also when we are led out from our exile <note>A common medieval metaphor for death as leaving the "exile" of Earth for the "homeland" of Heaven.</note>.

¶ **Likewise Uriel**, which is interpreted as "Light of God" <note>original: "lucem dei"</note>...

## ¶ Part Three

<note>original: "Tertia pars"</note> either appearing to God, or the fire of God, or the burning of God <note>original: "deo siue apparens deo vel ignis dei aut incendius dei"</note>. This dual interpretation suggests that through his ministry we are enlightened in the truth and inflamed in charity. ¶ For angels are sent to enlighten our intellect and to inflame our affections—if not by directly creating these effects, then at least by preparing us for them. ¶ Regarding this name, **Uriel**, we read in the third book of Esdras <note>This refers to the book known in modern English Bibles as 2 Esdras or 4 Ezra, where Uriel acts as a visionary guide to the prophet.</note> that this angel was sent for the consolation of the people of God who were being oppressed by foreigners. ¶ Angels also frequently perform a triple action regarding a human being: namely, **purging**, **illuminating**, and **perfecting**. ¶ The first act is directed toward the removal of obstacles. The second act toward the knowledge of truths. ¶ The third toward the love of good things. ¶ Thirdly, they produce a double effect in humans. ¶ One effect regards the good—either preserving, acquiring, or increasing it. The other effect regards evil—either abandoning, avoiding, or diminishing it. ¶ Nevertheless, all angels serve as a "militia" <note>or "army/service"</note> for the sake of the ministry of the human race. ¶ For some are sent to us by an **exterior mission**. ¶ Others are sent to [lower] angels on our behalf by an **interior mission**. These are those supreme ranks that stand above others and never perform an exterior office; nevertheless, they are sent to us or for us according to what the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul in Hebrews 1:14.</note> says: that they are all ministering spirits, sent into ministry for the sake of those who receive the inheritance of salvation. ¶ Furthermore, the angels assigned to the guardianship of humans—from the infusion of the soul into the body until its departure—strive to restrain the efforts of the devil, as **Blessed Jerome** <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), a Father of the Church known for translating the Bible into Latin.</note> says: "Great is the dignity of souls, that each one should have, from the moment of its birth... <note>The text cuts off here and continues on the next page.</note>"

## ¶ On the definitions of the soul ¶ Chapter 19

It remains to look at the third distinction of creature, which is composed of both the spiritual and the corporal, such as the human being. We will look first at what pertains to the soul; second, to the body; and third, to the union of the two. Concerning the human soul, two things are principally to be considered: namely, the definition of the soul and the division of its powers. ¶ The soul is defined by seven distinct definitions.

¶ The first is found in the book *On the Motion of the Heart* <note>original: "de motu cordis," likely referring to the work by Alfred of Sareshel</note>. The soul is an incorporeal, intellectual substance, perceptive of the illuminations which come from the First <note>referring to God as the First Cause</note> through a final relationship.

¶ The second is from Remigius <note>Remigius of Auxerre, a 9th-century scholar</note>: the soul is an incorporeal substance governing the body.

¶ The third is from Aristotle in his book *On the Soul* <note>original: "de anima"</note>. The soul is the **first act** <note>a philosophical term meaning the primary realization or "blueprint" of a living thing</note> of a physical organic body having the potential for life.

¶ The fourth is from Augustine: the soul is a likeness of all things.

¶ The fifth is likewise from Augustine: ¶ The soul is a certain substance partaking of reason, suited to governing the body.

¶ The sixth is taken from Genesis <note>The Latin text "generis" is a common shorthand for the Book of Genesis, specifically referring to the "breath of life" in Genesis 2:7</note>. ¶ The soul is a God-like breath of life.

¶ The seventh is from Seneca: ¶ The soul is an intellectual spirit, existing in itself and in the body, ordained toward blessedness.

¶ Through the first definition, the rational soul is identified as it is a **spirit**; through the second and third, it is identified as it is a **soul** <note>Medieval theologians often distinguished "soul" (the life-force of the body) from "spirit" (the higher intellectual power)</note>. ¶ For the soul is coupled and united to the body as a mover is to the thing it moves—which is the second definition—and as a "perfection" <note>an identifying form</note> is to that which can be perfected, which is the third. ¶ Through the other four definitions which follow, it is defined as both soul and spirit together, such that the fourth is defined by comparison to creatures in general:

<summary>This page marks a transition from angelology to anthropology, listing seven authoritative definitions of the human soul from sources like Aristotle, Augustine, and Seneca. It explains how the soul functions both as the biological life of the body and as a spiritual intellectual substance.</summary>

# Part Three

## On the division of the powers according to the masters into five members. ¶ Chapter 20.

It follows now to examine the division of the powers of the soul. To understand these, a fourfold division must be explored. ¶ One division according to the masters <note>referring to the established teachers of theology and philosophy</note> is into five members: namely, into **sensuality**, **sense**, **imagination**, **reason**, and **intellect**.

¶ **Sensuality**, according to the masters, is that power which gives sensation to the body and moves it; it desires those things which are pleasurable to the body and flees from those that are harmful.

¶ **Sense** is that power of the soul by which a human being knows physical things when they are present <note>original: "presentiales"</note>.

¶ **Imagination** is that power of the soul which beholds physical things that are absent.

¶ **Reason** is that power of the soul by which the soul discerns within itself between good and evil, and between truth and falsehood.

¶ **Intellect** is that power of the soul by which the soul apprehends immaterial and intelligible things <note>things that can be understood by the mind but not perceived by the physical senses</note>: such as the soul itself, an angel, and God.

¶ The sufficiency <note>the logical completeness or grouping</note> of these five members can be understood in this way: ¶ The soul is, as it were, two things (as Augustine says): it is both **soul** and **spirit**. 

¶ It is called "soul" <note>original: "anima"</note> insofar as it completes and gives life to the body. ¶ It is called "spirit" <note>original: "spiritus"</note> insofar as it is considered without the body and endures even when separated from it. According to the way the soul is prepared for the body and the body for the soul—and thus the soul fits and is adapted to the body so that it may grant it both life and sensation—this power of the soul [is] sense...

<header>¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure</header>

...sensuality fits: also the body is fitted to the soul so that it may be perfected through the body, as far as the lower life is concerned, and this power is called *sense*. *Sense*, however, is twofold: namely, the interior, which is called *imagination*, and the exterior, which is commonly called *sense*—such as sight, which occurs through the eye, and hearing through the ear, and so on for the others. *The soul itself*, however, insofar as it is spirit, has a double aspect: *one regarding lower things* and one regarding higher things (according to what Augustine says), because the soul has two parts: namely, one looking toward higher things and the other toward lower things. The masters <note>referring to the established Scholastic theologians</note> call the lower part *reason*, and the higher part *intellect*.

# ¶ On the division of the powers according to the natural philosophers into three members.

<margin>¶ Chapter 21</margin>

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut drop cap 'S' featuring white foliate and floral patterns against a black background within a double-line square frame.</image-desc>

The second division, according to the natural philosophers <note>"naturales": those who studied the physical world, often following Aristotle's biological classifications</note>, is into three members. *For according* to how the soul is compared to its acts, there are three powers of the soul ordered toward a threefold act. *Indeed*, there are three primary acts of the soul: namely, vegetating <note>basic life functions like growth</note>, sensing, and reasoning. *And* according to this, a threefold power of the soul is distinguished: namely, the vegetative, the sensible, and the rational.

*Note*, however, that the vegetative is the soul in plants. ¶ The *sensible* is the soul in brute animals <note>non-human animals</note>. ¶ The *rational* is the soul in humans. *In* man, however, there are not three separate souls, but rather three powers are said to exist in one single soul. The *vegetative* power desires "to be" <note>existence/survival</note>; the sensible desires "to be well" <note>pleasure and well-being</note>; the rational desires "to be in the best way" <note>moral and spiritual perfection</note>.

The *vegetative* is subdivided into the generative and the nutritive. The *generative* is for the preservation of universal being, namely the species <note>procreation</note>. The *nutritive* is for the preservation of singular being, namely the individual. Of the *nutritive*, the act is twofold: namely, to nourish—and this act is always necessary—and to increase <note>growth</note>—and this is only necessary until the completion of the thing...

# Part Three

[...per]fection. <insert>¶</insert> Hence, animals are always being nourished, but they do not always grow <note>The author distinguishes between "nutrition" (maintaining the body) and "augmentation" (growth in size), noting that while growth stops at maturity, nutrition must continue for life</note>. The **sensitive power** is divided into the **apprehensive** and the **motive**. <insert>¶</insert> The **apprehensive** power is further divided into the **particular sense** and the **common sense**, or into the **exterior** and **interior** senses. <insert>¶</insert> The **particular** or **exterior sense** is divided into the five senses: namely, **sight**, **hearing**, **taste**, **smell**, and **touch**. 

Concerning these five senses, three things are necessarily required: namely, the **soundness of the organ**, the **proper state of the medium**, and the **presence of the object**. <insert>¶</insert> The organ of **sight** is the pupils of the eyes. The organ of **hearing** is the ears. <insert>¶</insert> The organs of **taste** are the tongue and the palate. <insert>¶</insert> The organs of **touch** are the nerves of the entire body. <insert>¶</insert> These organs descend from the anterior <note>front</note> part of the brain. <insert>¶</insert> In this part, there is a nerve called the **optic** <note>original: "obticus"</note>, which descends to the pupils and branches into two parts. <insert>¶</insert> Another nerve descends to the nostrils, another to the ears; a certain other one to the tongue and palate. Another, branching out in many ways, is the organ of touch throughout the whole body. 

<insert>¶</insert> The **objects** <note>the things perceived</note> of the senses are these: <insert>¶</insert> The object of **hearing** is sound and its variations. <insert>¶</insert> The object of **touch** are the elemental qualities, such as hot and moist, cold and dry, rare <note>thin/porous</note> and dense, light and heavy, and rough and smooth. <insert>¶</insert> The **media** <note>the substances through which a sensation travels</note> of the senses are air and water. Any <term>diaphanous</term> <note>transparent or translucent</note> and transparent body that is illuminated acts as the medium for **sight**. <insert>¶</insert> Exterior and interior air is the medium for **hearing**. <insert>¶</insert> For the hollow ear contains within its cavity an internal air that is "connatural" to it <note>This reflects the medieval belief that "like perceives like," and that the ear needs internal air to resonate with external air</note>. 

<insert>¶</insert> Vapor carried through the air is the medium for **smell**. <insert>¶</insert> For an odorous thing evaporates a kind of "sensible smoke," by which the air carrying the scent to the sense of smell is changed. <insert>¶</insert> **Salivary fluid** is the medium for **taste**. **Flesh** is the medium for **touch** <note>While the nerves are the organs of touch, the flesh acts as the medium through which the stimulus reaches the nerves</note>.

# The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

The **interior sense** is divided into three parts according to the three parts of the brain: the anterior <note>front</note>, in which resides the **imagination**; the middle, in which resides the **estimative power**; and the posterior <note>rear</note>, in which resides the **memory**. 

Imagination is sometimes called the **common sense**, sometimes **imagination**, and sometimes **fantasy**. It is called the **common sense** <term>sensus communis: the faculty that integrates data from the five individual senses into a single perception</term> insofar as the five particular senses converge within it like lines to a center, and there they are bound together. It is called **imagination** insofar as it represents forms previously received from the particular senses as if they were present even when they are absent. **Fantasy** <note>original: "Fantasia"</note> is its name insofar as it can compose new forms according to the likeness of those things it perceives through the particular senses. 

The **estimative power** <term>estimativa: an instinctual faculty that allows animals to perceive "intentions" like danger or utility, such as a sheep fearing a wolf</term> is 그at power according to which we attribute sagacity or prudence to brute animals—such as in the fox or others—in seeking out delights or fleeing from harmful things. **Memory** is 그at power which preserves or retains the forms of sensible species <note>sensible species: the mental images or "likenesses" of objects perceived by the senses</note>. 

The **sensitive motive power** is the mover that generally moves the members of the body, such as the head, arms, and shoulders, according to motion. The **motive power** of the feet is called the **walking power**, because by it an animal is moved by progressive motion. 

The **rational power** is divided into the **speculative intellect**, which is exercised in the contemplation of truth, and the **practical intellect**, which is exercised in the performance of good. **According** to this dual root of the intellects, they are called the **active life** and the **contemplative life**.

The body-governing power is distinguished into the **animal**, the **vital**, and the **natural**...

### Part Three

...according to these powers, the soul rules the body. ¶ According to physicians, the **animal power** <note>original: "vis animalis"; refers to the power of the "anima" or soul that governs sensory and voluntary movement</note> is located in the brain, the **vital power** is in the heart, and the **natural power** is in the liver <note>original: "epate"</note>. ¶ The animal power is divided into the **cognitive** and the **motive**. ¶ Concerning the cognitive power, it was spoken of above. ¶ The motive power is divided into the **irascible** and the **concupiscible**. ¶ For the movement of the soul <note>original: "animalis"</note> is either away from a harmful evil: and it is called the "movement of flight," which comes from the **irascible** <term>irascibilis: the faculty that reacts to threats and obstacles</term>. ¶ Or it is toward a delightful good, and such movement is from the **concupiscible** <term>concupiscibilis: the faculty that desires pleasure and the fulfillment of basic needs</term>. ¶ The **vital power** is the force that moves air within the animal body through the arteries, from which comes the pulse. ¶ For the heart is of such great heat that unless it were cooled by air drawn in through inspiration and sent out through respiration, the animal would die. ¶ This same power is the motive force for forming the voice in the same way that the irrational voice is formed, such as the voice of birds and other animals. ¶ The **natural power** comprises the **nutritive** and the **generative**: the nutritive exists for the preservation of the individual nature, while the generative exists for the preservation of the universal nature <note>meaning the preservation of the species through reproduction</note>. ¶ Four other virtues serve the nutritive power, namely: the **appetitive**, which desires nourishment; ¶ The **digestive**, which separates what is suitable from what is unsuitable in food; ¶ The **retentive**, which transmits what is suitable in the nourishment to the individual parts of the body so that what has been lost through wear may be restored; ¶ And the **expulsive**, which expels what was unsuitable and harmful in the nourishment. ¶ Furthermore, the **spiritual** power—that is, the power that governs the spirit—is sometimes divided according to its **intellectual** and **affective** nature. ¶ Sometimes it is divided according to its functions <note>original: "officia"</note>, as when **reason** is divided into **higher** and **lower** reason <note>Higher reason focuses on eternal truths; lower reason deals with temporal, worldly affairs</note>. ¶ Sometimes it is divided according to its state, as when it is divided into the **speculative** and the **practical intellect**. ¶ The speculative intellect becomes practical by extension, when it is joined to the will and to action.

# ⸿ The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

**Sometimes** [the cognitive power is divided] according to its perspectives, as when it is divided into **reason**, which looks toward what is lower <note>meaning the physical world and practical matters</note>; and **intellect**, which looks toward what is equal <note>referring to other created spirits or abstract truths</note>; and **intelligence**, which looks toward what is higher <note>meaning God and divine truths</note>. **Sometimes** it is divided according to its acts, as when a division is made into the **inventive** [discovery-based] and the **judicative** [judgment-based]. **To find** and to judge are indeed acts of the cognitive power ordered toward one another. **Sometimes** it is divided according to differences <note>original: "drias," an abbreviation for "differentias"</note>, as when the intellect is divided into the **active** <term>agentem: the active intellect that abstracts universal concepts from sensory data</term> and the **passive** <term>possibilem: the "possible" or passive intellect that receives and stores knowledge</term>. **For** the soul uses the first difference of the intellect to abstract, and the second indeed to receive. **Some-**
**times** it is divided according to various ways of moving: and thus is the division of the **will** or appetite into the **natural** and the **deliberative**. **The first** is the principal division, into the **intellect** and the **affect**, or the cognitive and the affective, or into reason and the will. **Will** is called a power and is thus defined by **Anselm** <note>St. Anselm of Canterbury (1033–1109), a Benedictine monk and philosopher</note>: "Will is an instrument moving through itself." **It is called** an act or movement of a power and is thus defined by **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430)</note> in the book *Eighty-Three Different Questions*: "Will is a movement of the soul, with no one forcing it, toward either admitting something or not admitting it, or toward attaining or not attaining it." **It is called** also the "thing willed," as when someone says, "this is my will." **Six** are the acts pertaining to the free will according to **Damascene** <note>St. John of Damascus (c. 675–749), who codified much of Eastern Christian psychology</note>, namely: **Counsel**, **judgment**, **sentence** [decision], **choice**, **operation**, and **use**. **Free** choice desires—behold the **will**; it **in-**
**quires** and searches—behold **counsel**; it **judges**—behold **judgment**; it **disposes**—behold the **sentence**; it **chooses**—behold **choice** (which is preferring one of two pleasing things); it **makes an impulse**—behold **operation**. It **acts**—behold **use**. **Reason**, however, has five acts: two through which it is directed toward the end of truth, of which the one that precedes knowing is called **reasoning**, which is thus defined by **Augustine**: "**Reasoning** is the inquiry of reason." ⸿ **From here**

->¶ Third part.<-

Innate ability <note>original: "ingenium"</note> is implanted in reasoning. It is defined by **Augustine**: **Innate ability** is that power of the soul, or intention, by which the soul extends and exercises itself toward the understanding of unknown things. **Another** act is the act of knowing itself, which is the beholding of truth. **And** according to this, reason <note>original: "ratio"</note> is defined by Augustine: **Reason** is the gaze of the soul by which it beholds the truth through itself. **Reason** also has three acts by which it is directed toward the end of the good. **The first** is the discernment of good and evil, or of the good and the better. **The second** is the choice of the good or better, once the discernment is made. **The third** is love. **Concerning** these three, Augustine says: **Reason** is the gaze of the soul which discerns good and evil, chooses virtues, and loves God.

### ¶ On the division of the powers according to Augustine / Chapter 24

---

**The** fourth division of the powers is posited by Augustine in the book *On the Spirit and the Soul* <note>A 12th-century treatise often attributed to St. Augustine in the Middle Ages, though now considered pseudo-Augustinian</note>, which provides greater clarity to the things said before. **He** distinguishes the powers of the soul into the **rational**, the **concupiscible** <term>concupiscibilem: the power of the soul that desires or seeks out what is perceived as good or pleasurable</term>, and the **irascible** <term>irascibilem: the power that resists obstacles or recoils from what is perceived as harmful</term>. **Through** the rational power, the soul is capable of knowing what is below it, above it, and beside it. **It** indeed knows God above itself, itself within itself, the angels beside itself, and whatever the circuit of the heavens contains below itself. **From** rationality, every sense of the soul is born; from concupiscibility, every feeling <note>original: "affectus"</note>. **Feeling** is known to be fourfold: when we already rejoice in that which we love, or hope to rejoice in it; or when we already grieve over that which we hate, or fear we must grieve. **Thus**, from the concupiscible power arise joy and hope; from the irascible power, grief and fear. **Indeed**, these four feelings of the soul are the source of all vices and virtues...

# The Hundredfold Speech of Saint Bonaventure

...if certain principles and common matter. **Affection** <note>original: "affectus"; in medieval psychology, this refers to the soul’s disposition or emotional inclination</note> indeed gives a name to the work at all times. **Note** meanwhile that the senses of the soul are called apprehensive or cognitive powers, which arise from rationality. There are **five** steps of progress toward wisdom: sense, imagination, reason, intellect, and intelligence. **Sense** is that power of the soul which receives the bodily forms of bodily things, but only when they are present. **Imagination** is that power of the soul which receives the bodily forms of bodily things, but when they are absent. For **sense** receives forms within matter, whereas imagination receives them outside of matter. And that power, when formed externally, is called sense; when the same is brought into the innermost part, it is called imagination, which arises from sense. **Reason** is that power of the soul which perceives the forms, natures, differences <note>original: "drias," an abbreviation for *differentias*; the specific qualities that distinguish one species from another</note>, properties, and all accidents of bodily things as incorporeal, yet not existing outside of bodily things except by way of reason. It **abstracts** from bodies those things which are founded in bodily things, not by physical action but by mental consideration. **Intellect** is that power of the soul which perceives invisible things, such as angels, demons, souls, and every created spirit. **Intelligence** is the power of the soul which is placed immediately beneath God. It **discerns** the highest truth itself, which is truly unchangeable. **Thus**, the soul perceives bodily things through sense; the likenesses of bodies through imagination; and the natures of bodies through reason. Through **intellect**, it perceives the created spirit; through **intelligence**, the uncreated spirit <note>This refers to God, the only "uncreated" spirit in Christian theology</note>. To **imagination**, which is also called the "common sense" <term>sensus communis: the faculty that binds together the data from the five individual senses</term> and even "fantasy," belong those things which sleepers see in dreams, concerning which **Augustine** speaks. [He says] that there are five kinds of things which sleepers seem to see, namely: the oracle, the vision, the dream...
