<meta>This page continues the discussion on the soul's faculties, moving from the higher intellect and intelligence mentioned on the previous page into the classification of dreams and visions, and finally transitioning into Chapter 24 which explores the nature of the soul itself and the concept of free will.</meta>

<header>Part Three.</header>

...insomnium or phantasm.

¶ An **oracle** <note>original: "Oraculum"</note> occurs when, in sleep, a parent or some other grave person, a priest, or even God, openly announces something that will or will not happen, or something to be done or avoided. A **vision** <note>original: "visio"</note> occurs when one sees something that will come to pass in the exact same manner in which it appeared. A **dream** <note>original: "Sompnium"</note> is veiled in figures and cannot be understood without interpretation. A **night-disturbane** <note>original: "Insomnium"; in medieval dream theory, this refers specifically to non-prophetic dreams caused by daily anxieties or physical needs</note> occurs when that which had fatigued a person while awake forces itself upon the sleeper, such as a concern for food or drink, or certain studies, arts, or illnesses.

¶ Depending on their illnesses, different people experience different dreams. For those of a sanguine temperament see certain things; the choleric see others; the melancholic others; and the phlegmatic still others. The first group sees red and varied things, while the latter see black and white. <note>This refers to the "four humors" of medieval medicine, where a person's temperament and health were determined by the balance of bodily fluids: blood, yellow bile, black bile, and phlegm.</note>

¶ A **phantasm** <note>original: "Fantasma"</note> occurs when someone has barely begun to sleep and thinks they are awake; they seem to behold forms rushing upon them or wandering about in various ways, some joyful and others turbulent. ¶ Within this category is the **Ephialtes** <note>original: "ephialtes"; a Greek term for a nightmare, often associated with the sensation of a weight on the chest, similar to modern sleep paralysis</note> which the common people believe invades sleepers and oppresses those at rest. However, this is nothing other than **vapors** <note>original: "fumositas"; literally "smokiness"</note> ascending to the brain from the stomach or the heart, compressing the animal power.

### ¶ Concerning what the soul is made of, and how "image" and "likeness" differ, and concerning free will. Chapter Twenty-Four.

It can be gathered from the preceding points that the **soul** <note>original: "aia," a common scribal abbreviation for *anima*</note> is a living, intelligent being, making use of freedom. Indeed, the form is a being...

<summary>This page classifies five types of dreams and visions—oracle, vision, dream, night-disturbance, and phantasm—explaining their causes through both spiritual and medical lenses, before beginning a new chapter on the nature of the soul and free will.</summary>
<keywords>dream theory, Oraculum, Somnium, Visio, Insomnium, Fantasma, humors, Ephialtes, nightmare, vapors, Anima, free will, Chapter 24</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the soul (anima) and its relationship to God, specifically defining how the soul reflects the divine through its creation and its faculties of memory, intelligence, and will.</meta>

<header>The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure</header>

[The soul is] not from itself, nor from the divine nature, but brought into being from nothing by God through creation. It is a **living form**, not by an external nature, but of itself—not with a mortal life, but a perpetual one. It is also a **rational form** <note>original: "Forma vero intelligens"</note>, [aware] not only of created things but even of the Creator’s essence, in whose image and likeness it was made.

Now, "**image**" <note>original: "ymago"</note> denotes a conformity in quantity, while "**likeness**" <note>original: "similitudo"</note> denotes an agreement in quality. For "image" refers to a certain configuration, implying a "figure," which is quantity within quality and quality within quantity. <note>This is a technical scholastic distinction: an "image" shares the structural form (like a silhouette), while a "likeness" shares the internal nature or characteristics.</note> "Likeness," on the other hand, is defined as the same quality found in different things.

From this primary difference, a threefold way of distinguishing between "image" and "likeness" is assigned. According to **Hugh** <note>Likely Hugh of Saint Victor (c. 1096–1141), an influential mystical theologian.</note>, "image" regards the figure, while "likeness" regards the nature. Another way of distinguishing them is that "image" exists in our natural faculties, while "likeness" exists in the gifts of grace <note>original: "in gratuitis"</note>. The third way, according to **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), whose work *On the Trinity* established the soul's "image" as a triad of faculties.</note>, is that "image" resides in the power of knowing, and "likeness" in the power of loving.

This last distinction is made by "appropriation" <note>A theological method of attributing certain traits to one person of the Trinity or one part of the soul for clarity, even if they are linked.</note> because in the "image"—which is established through creation—there are two powers on the cognitive side, namely **memory** and **intelligence**, and one on the affective <note>Related to emotions, desires, and the will.</note> side, namely the **will**. Conversely, it is the opposite in the "likeness"—which comes after creation through justification <note>The process by which a person is set in a right relationship with God through faith and grace.</note>—because there are two virtues on the affective side, namely **hope** and **charity**, and one on the cognitive side, namely **faith**.

Therefore, "image" is situated in the cognitive power, and "likeness" in the affective power. "Image" is further defined by **Hilary** <note>Saint Hilary of Poitiers (c. 310–367 AD) in his work *On the Synods*.</note> as follows: "An image is the likeness <note>original: "species"</note> of that thing to which it is modeled, existing in different things." 

Moreover, the soul is a form that exercises freedom...

<summary>This page defines the soul as a created, perpetual, and rational form, distinguishing between the "image of God" (the soul's natural structure) and the "likeness of God" (the soul's state of grace).</summary>
<keywords>soul (anima), image and likeness, Saint Augustine, Hugh of Saint Victor, Hilary of Poitiers, memory, intelligence, will, faith, hope, charity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the soul's nature, transitioning from its likeness to God to the mechanics of its free will and how various Church authorities define the relationship between reason and volition.</meta>

### Part Three
h.i.

[The soul] is always free from coercion. This freedom from coercion is nothing other than the faculty of reason and will, which are the principal powers of the soul. This freedom arises from a preceding deliberation and an attached will, and it is commonly called **free choice** <note>original: "liberum arbitrium." While often translated as "free will," the Latin term "arbitrium" emphasizes the capacity for judgment and decision-making.</note>.

**Concerning which**, we must look at its various definitions and its various names.

**Free choice** is defined by the Philosopher <note>Aristotle (384–322 BC), whom medieval scholars referred to simply as "The Philosopher."</note> thus: **Free choice is a judgment proceeding from the will.**

By **Bernard** <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian monk and mystic.</note> thus in his book *On Free Choice*: **Free choice is a consensus on account of the inalienable freedom of the will and the unswerving judgment of reason.**

By **Augustine** thus: **Free choice is the faculty of reason and will by which good is chosen when grace assists, or evil when grace is absent.**

By **Anselm** <note>Saint Anselm of Canterbury (1033–1109), famous for his "satisfaction theory" of atonement and logical arguments for God's existence.</note> in his book *On Free Choice* thus: **Free choice is the power of preserving righteousness for its own sake.**

For free choice can be considered in terms of its act, and thus it is defined by Bernard; in terms of its act, its object, and its ultimate end, thus by Augustine; and in terms of its act and its object, thus by Anselm.

This power is also named by the **Master of the Sentences** <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160), whose book the *Sentences* was the standard textbook for theology students in the Middle Ages.</note> as freedom from coercion (or necessity), from fault, and from misery.

By **Bernard**, however, it is called freedom of choice, of counsel, and of pleasure.

The Master defines it privatively <note>In scholastic logic, a "privative" definition explains what a thing lacks or what it is free *from* (e.g., freedom *from* sin).</note>; **Bernard** defines it positively. For this freedom can be considered in terms of its *being* (its essence); and thus it is called by the Master "freedom from coercion" in a privative sense.

By **Bernard**, it is called "freedom of choice" in a positive sense.

Likewise, it can be considered in terms of its *well-being* <note>original: "bene esse." A distinction between simple existence and a flourishing or perfected state of existence.</note>, for which a twofold good is required: namely, the **rectifying good** (which puts things in order) and the **delighting good** (which brings peace). Consequently, a twofold evil is excluded: namely, the **deforming evil** (sin) and the **afflicting evil** (suffering). With respect to the deforming evil, it is called...

<summary>This page defines free choice (liberum arbitrium) through the lenses of major medieval authorities, explaining it as a cooperation between reason and will. It distinguishes between defining freedom by what it lacks (privative) versus what it possesses (positive).</summary>
<keywords>free choice, free will, Aristotle, Bernard of Clairvaux, Augustine, Anselm of Canterbury, Peter Lombard, reason, grace, righteousness</keywords>

From the *Lentiloquium* of Saint Bonaventure <note>original: "Lentiloquii sancti Bonaventure." This refers to a "shorter speech" or summary work by the Franciscan theologian Bonaventure (1221–1274).</note>

By the **Master** <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160), known as the "Master of the Sentences," the standard theological textbook author of the Middle Ages.</note>, "freedom from sin" is defined negatively <note>original: *privative*, meaning defined by the absence of a quality (sin) rather than the presence of one.</note>. With respect to the good that sets one right, it is called by **Bernard** <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153).</note> "freedom of counsel" positively. With respect to afflicting evil, it is called by the Master "freedom from misery" negatively. With respect to the good that brings rest, it is called by Bernard "freedom of pleasure" <note>original: *libertas complaciti*, meaning the freedom to find delight in what is good.</note> positively.

### Concerning the condition of the human body. Chapter 25.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' within a square frame featuring a foliate pattern.</image-desc>
It follows now to look at the **composite nature**: that is, human nature as it pertains to the body. For the body of the first man was established and formed from the mud of the earth, subjected to the soul and proportional to it in its own way. I say it was **proportional** in terms of its balanced temperament <term>complexionem: In medieval medicine, "complexion" refers to the balance of the four bodily humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile.</term>, its beautiful and varied internal organization, and the uprightness of its posture.

For although the body and soul are very distant from one another in nature, the lowest part of the spirit and the highest part of the body are unite-able in a wondrous way. For the **animal or rational spirit** possesses the power of giving life, the power of growth <term>vegetandi: The biological functions shared with plants, such as nutrition and reproduction.</term>, and the power of sensation. The body, indeed, possesses a balance of temperament, a variety of organs, and a refinement of "spirits" according to a three-fold difference: for it has the **vital spirit**, the **natural spirit**, and the **animal spirit** <note>In the medieval view, "spirits" were subtle vapors or fluids that carried commands from the soul to the body; the natural spirit resided in the liver, the vital spirit in the heart, and the animal spirit in the brain.</note>.

Therefore, by comparing the balance of the body’s temperament to the life-giving power of the soul, the bond formed through the medium of the **vital spirit** is the most excellent connection. Similarly, by comparing not only the balanced temperament but also the perfection of the physical organization to the powers of growth and sensation, through the mediation of the **natural** and **animal spirits**, there is an optimal proportion and a marvelous connection. Hence, just as earth and fire, although they are very distant from each other, [are joined] by a double me[dium]... <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with a comparison to how the elements are joined by intermediary substances.</note>

## ¶ Part Three

¶ Again, because the soul—ordered toward and capable of blessedness <term>beatitudo: in medieval theology, this refers to the ultimate happiness found in the vision of God.</term>—tends upward, ¶ the body possesses an upright stature and a head raised toward the heavens, so that it might bear witness to the uprightness of the spirit. ¶ The body was also submissible and subject to the soul, such that it was obedient without rebellion, capable of growth <term>vegetabile: referring to the "vegetative" functions of life like nutrition and growth.</term> without decay, and capable of reproduction without lust.

¶ Therefore, the place of the earthly paradise was given to it as a tranquil dwelling. ¶ The tree of life was given to it for continuous vitality <note>original: *vegetationem*.</note>. ¶ The woman was formed from the side of the man for companionship and assistance, and for immaculate reproduction.

¶ And because human comes from human—just as it is in other things propagated through seeds—something must be added concerning the "seminal reason" <term>ratio seminalis: a "seed-like" principle or blueprint embedded in nature by God that allows a specific form to develop over time.</term>. ¶ Regarding this, it should be noted that the terms "cause" and "causal reason" apply to both external and internal principles, according to the force of the name. ¶ However, "seed" and "seminal reason" refer to an internal principle.

¶ Yet they can be distinguished: "cause" and "causal reason" are taken in relation to the uncreated principle <note>The "uncreated principle" refers to God.</note>, while "seed" and "seminal reason" are taken in relation to the created principle. ¶ Nevertheless, a cause and a seminal reason differ: for a cause provides the productive principle, whereas a causal reason speaks of the rule directing that principle in its operation. ¶ Similarly, "seed" and "seminal reason" also differ.

¶ Now, the rule of the uncreated agent <note>God</note> is the exemplary or ideal form <term>forma ydealis: the "Idea" or blueprint of a thing as it exists in the mind of God.</term>. ¶ The rule of the created agent is the natural form. ¶ And thus, causal reasons are ideal or exemplary forms. On the other hand, seminal reasons <note>The text ends here mid-sentence, likely continuing on the following page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the scholastic distinction of "reasons" (causal, primordial, seminal, and natural) and transitions into Chapter 26, which discusses the composite nature of the human being (body and soul) and the gifts and commandments given to humanity at creation.</meta>

<header>¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure</header>

...are natural forms. And according to this, it is clear that 
causal and primordial reasons are the same in reality <note>original: *re*</note>, 
differing only in definition <note>original: *ratione*</note>. They are called "primordial" by 
the priority of what came before. They are called "causal" by the 
position of what follows after. And primordial reasons 
look toward God and the first principle. Causal reasons 
look toward God and the ultimate end, who is the 
cause of all causes. Similarly, natural reasons and 
seminal reasons are the same in reality, but they differ 
in definition: for "seed" refers to *that from which* something comes, 
while "nature" refers to *that by which* it comes. The 
seminal reason is considered insofar as it speaks to the potential 
of nature, so that from something else, something may be made. 
Or, the seminal reason regards the beginning and the 
intrinsic power which moves and works toward the 
production of an effect. The natural reason, however, 
concerns the likeness <note>original: *simulationem*</note> of the producer to the product 
and the habit of the way of acting.

## Chapter 26: On the whole human, composed of body and soul

Now, thirdly, we must look at the whole human being 
constituted of soul and body. Concerning this, it is to 
be held as a matter of doctrine that a twofold sense 
was given to the human: namely, the internal (or that of the mind) 
and the external (or that of the flesh). A twofold movement 
was given: namely, the commanding movement <term>imparativus: the "ordering" act of the will</term> in the 
will, and the executive movement carrying it out in the 
body. A twofold good was given: one visible, the other 
invisible. A twofold commandment was given, namely of 
nature and of discipline. The commandment of nature 
is: "Increase and multiply." The commandment of discipline 
is: "From the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, 
you shall not eat." A fourfold help was given: 
namely, of knowledge and of conscience, of synderesis 
<term>synderesis: the innate spark of conscience in scholastic theology that recognizes basic moral truths.</term> and of grace. And thus, it can be gathered 
from the aforesaid that the creation of the world is like 
a certain book in which the Creator Trinity <note>original: *trinitas fabricatrix*</note> 
shines forth, is represented, and is read, according 
to a threefold level of expression.

# Part Three

...namely, by way of a **trace** <note>original: *vestigij*</note>, by way of an **image** <note>original: *ymaginis*</note>, and by way of a **likeness** <note>original: *similitudinis*</note>. So it is that the nature <note>original: *ratio*</note> of the **trace** is found in all creatures; the nature of the **image** is found in intellectual or rational beings alone; and the nature of the **likeness** is found in godlike <note>original: *deiformibus*</note> beings alone. <insert>¶</insert> For every creature, having God as its beginning <note>original: *principium*</note>, is shaped according to Him through **unity**, **truth**, and **goodness**; and this is the **trace**. 

<insert>¶</insert> A creature that has God as its object <note>as the focus of its mental activity</note> grasps Him through **memory**, **intelligence**, and **will**; this is the **image**. 

<insert>¶</insert> A creature that has God as an infused gift is shaped according to Him through **faith**, **hope**, and **charity**—or through **vision**, **reaching** <term>tensionem: a stretching toward or holding onto God in the afterlife</term>, and **perfect love**; in this is the **likeness**. And because the "image" is an expressed likeness, and this is found in the rational creature according to a threefold degree: <insert>¶</insert> by broadening the term, a threefold image can be distinguished in the rational creature, by which the **Creator Trinity** <note>original: *trinitas fabricatrix*</note> is expressly represented. 

First, there is the **image of creation**, which consists in the three powers [of the soul] and in the unity of its substance; so that **memory** represents the Father, **intelligence** represents the Son, and **will** represents the Holy Spirit. The substance of the soul, since it is one, represents the unity of the divine essence. 

There is also the **image of re-creation** <note>the restoration of the soul through grace</note>, which consists in the three theological virtues and the unity of "grace that makes one pleasing" <term>gratia gratum faciens: the sanctifying grace that restores a person's relationship with God</term>. Thus, **faith** represents the Son, **hope** represents the Father, and **charity** represents the Holy Spirit. The unity of this pleasing grace, therefore, represents the unity of the Divine Essence. 

There is even an **image of glorification** <note>the state of the soul in heaven</note>, which consists in three glorious endowments and the unity of glory. So it is that **clear vision** represents the Son, **secure reaching** <note>original: *secura tentio*</note> represents the Father, and **perfect love** represents the Holy Spirit. The unity of glory, in turn, represents the unity of the Essence. <insert>¶</insert> The first image belongs to souls as they proceed from God by...

The **second** is of those returning to God through **justification** <note>original: *iustificationem*; the theological process by which a person is made righteous or "right" with God</note>. The **third** is of those arriving at God through **glorification**. The first is properly called the **image of nature** <note>original: *ymago nature*</note> and belongs to all angels and all rational souls. The second is called the **likeness of grace** <note>original: *similitudo gratie*</note> and belongs to converted angels and righteous souls, but not to demons or sinful humans. ## ¶ On the union of natures in Christ <margin>¶ Chapter 27</margin>

---

**P**ostquam... <note>The text begins with a large ornamental 'P' for the word "Postquam," meaning "After."</note> After we have seen the **uncreated good**, which is God, and the **created good**, which is the world, we must add something concerning the **good joined from both**, which is Christ. Regarding Him, three things are briefly to be considered: namely, the **association of natures**, the **overflowing of graces**, and the **endurance of sufferings**.

Regarding the **union of natures** in Christ, we must hold that at the end of the ages, when the **fullness of time** had come—after the law of nature and of scripture, and after the patriarchs and prophets to whom and through whom the incarnation was promised—the Son of God deigned to be incarnated for the sake of redeeming humanity. And then, as the angel announced the service of the incarnation to be fulfilled in her, the Virgin Mary believed, desired, and consented. Then the **Holy Spirit** descended upon her to sanctify and make her fruitful; by His power the Virgin conceived the Son of God, whom she bore as a virgin, and after the birth, she remained a virgin. She conceived not only flesh, but indeed **animated flesh** <note>flesh endowed with a soul</note> united to the **Word** <note>original: *verbo*; the Logos or Second Person of the Trinity</note> and subject to no sin.

### Third Part

...not liable to sin but entirely holy and immaculate, by reason of which she is called the **Mother of God** <note>original: *mater dei*</note> and the **Virgin Mary**. The whole **Trinity** <note>The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit</note> brought about this **incarnation** <term>incarnatio: the act of God becoming flesh</term>. Through this incarnation, the taking up of flesh by the divinity was accomplished, as was the union of the divinity with flesh that had been quickened by a rational spirit, according to the powers of growth, sensation, and understanding.

In this incarnation, however, the union was not made in a unity of nature, but of person—not a human person, but a divine one; not the person of the one being taken up <note>the human nature</note>, but of the one taking up <note>the divine Word</note>; not just any person, but only the **person of the Word** <note>original: *psone verbi*; the second person of the Trinity</note>. Indeed, this union was so great that it made God man and man God. Thus, whatever is said of the Son of God by nature is said of the Son of Man by grace, and vice versa—except for those statements in which the union is expressed and a negation is included. For it cannot be said that the human nature "took up" the divine nature or united the divine to itself, but rather that the human was taken up and united. Similarly, it cannot be said that the Son of God "began to be"; for while this is denied of the One taking up <note>the eternal God</note>, it is truly said of the one taken up <note>the human Christ born in time</note>.

## On the perfection of graces in Christ <margin>Chapter 28</margin>

It follows to see the **perfection of the graces** that were in the Lord Jesus Christ. Concerning these, it must be held that in Christ, from his very conception, there was a **fullness of all grace**: both in regard to the grace of his individual person, the grace of his Headship <note>original: *gratiam capitis*; Christ’s role as the "head" or leader of the Church</note>, and the grace of the union.

Thus, through the grace of his individual person, he possessed immunity from all sin, both in act and in possibility; for he neither sinned nor was he able to sin. Regarding the grace of union, he is worthy not only of the happiness of glory, but even of the **adoration of latria** <term>adoratiõe latrie: the highest form of worship, which is offered to God alone, as opposed to honor given to saints</term>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 28 regarding the specific types of grace found in Christ—singular grace, the grace of union, and the grace of the head—and then transitions into Chapter 29, which explores the fullness of Christ’s wisdom and his various ways of knowing.</meta>

¶ **Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure**

...which is the worship of reverence owed to God alone. <note>The text here concludes a point about *latria*, the specific type of worship reserved for God.</note> Through the **grace of the head**, he pours forth movement and perception into all who approach him through right faith and the sacraments of faith, whether they preceded his coming or followed after it. When Christ is considered in himself—insofar as he is a unique individual—the grace that sanctifies and strengthens him is appropriate; we call this the **grace of the singular person**.

In relation to the **Word** <note>original: *verbum*; the Second Person of the Trinity</note> united with him, the **grace of union** belongs to him. By reason of this union, he possessed both a superior nature and an inferior nature—the divine and the human—one being worthy of adoration and the other being the one who adores. In Christ's relationship to his **mystical body** <note>the Church or the community of believers</note>, the **grace of the head** belongs to him. The reason is this: just as a physical head contains the fullness of the senses, is similar in form to the other members of the body, presides over them, and provides the benefit of vital influence to those members connected to it; so too Christ—possessing a superabundance of grace within himself and being like us in nature—is holy and righteous beyond all other saints. To those who approach him, he provides the benefit of grace and the **Spirit**, through whom spiritual perception and movement are made possible: perception, I say, in the form of **knowledge and truth**, and movement in the form of **love and charity** <note>original: *dilectionis et caritatis*</note>.

part of the mind and spirit. And this was threefold: some through **nature**, some through **grace**, and some through **glory**. Hence, he possessed wisdom as God and man, as a **comprehensor** <term>original: *comprehensor*; one who has already attained the full vision of God in heaven</term> and as a **wayfarer** <term>original: *viator*; a pilgrim or person still living on Earth</term>; as one perfectly illuminated through grace and as one rightly formed through nature. ¶ And so, in the universe, there were five ways of knowing or perceiving in Christ.

¶ **Second, through glory:** and in this way, he knew all actual and finite things with an actual knowledge. He knew infinite things, however, through **habitual knowledge** <term>knowledge that is possessed and ready for use, even when not the immediate focus of the mind</term>.

¶ **Third, through grace:** and in this way, he knew all things pertaining to the redemption of the human race far more excellently and better than any of the prophets and angels.

¶ **Fourth, according to an uncorrupted nature**, such as was in Adam. And in this way, he knew those things which pertain to the construction of the **world's machinery** <note>original: *mundanam machinam*; this refers to the physical structure, laws, and ordering of the created universe</note> more excellently and better than Adam himself.

¶ **Fifth, according to sensory experience.** ¶ And in this way, he knew all things that come to the organs of the senses; according to this way, it is said that "he learned obedience from the things which he suffered." <note>A reference to the New Testament, Hebrews 5:8.</note> ¶ Indeed, this fivefold way ought to have been in Christ. ¶ For things have existence in the **eternal art** <note>original: *eterna arte*; a technical term for the divine ideas or blueprints of creation within the mind of God</note>, in the human mind, and in their own kind.

¶ **In the art, he knew** things through the nature of his divinity and through the glory of comprehension. ¶ **In the mind, through a natural habit** or an innate quality, just as the angels knew them. ¶ Through a gracious and infused habit, just as the holy ones of God, illuminated by the Holy Spirit, knew them. ¶ **In their own kind, he knew** by way of the senses, memory, and experience, just as in us a thing is transformed from unknown to known. **In Christ, however**, a thing known according to one way...

# The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

<margin>Chapter 30</margin>
### ¶ On the Fullness of Christ's Merit

It follows that we should consider the fullness of **merit** <term>original: *meritum*; the quality of an action that makes it worthy of a reward from God</term> which Christ held in both his work and its effect. In this regard, it must be held that in Christ the Lord, there was the perfection and fullness of all merit. 

¶ **First**, regarding the person who was meriting: because he was not only a man, but truly God. 

**Second**, regarding the time in which he merited: because it was from the very instant of his conception until the hour of his death. 

¶ **Third**, regarding that by which he merited: on account of his most perfect **habit of charity** <note>In medieval theology, a "habit" is a stable disposition or quality of the soul that allows one to act with ease and love</note> and the most perfect exercise of virtue in his acting, praying, and suffering. 

¶ **Fourth**, regarding the person for whom he merited: because he merited not only for himself, but also for us—indeed, for all the righteous. 

¶ **Fifth**, regarding that which he merited for us: because he merited not only glory, but also grace and pardon. ¶ He merited not only the glory of the spirit, but also the **robe of the flesh** <note>original: *stolam carnis*; this refers to the glorification and immortality of the physical body after the resurrection</note> and the opening of the heavenly gate. 

¶ **Sixth**, regarding that which he merited for himself: because although he did not merit the glorification of his mind (which he already possessed), he nevertheless merited the acceleration of his resurrection, the glorification of his name, and the dignity of judicial power. 

¶ **Seventh**, regarding the manner in which he merited. For merit is described in a threefold way: 
¶ Either making something a "debt" <note>A "debt" here refers to a reward that is justly owed by God</note> where nothing was owed; 
¶ or making something that was already owed even more of a debt; 
¶ or taking what is already owed in one way and making it owed in another way. 
In all these ways, he merited for us. ¶ However, he merited for himself only in the third way, because of the fullness of the Holy Spirit, through which Christ was simultaneously **blessed** <gloss>already enjoying the vision of God</gloss> and in the **state of meriting** <gloss>still living as a mortal capable of earning reward</gloss>. 

¶ Thus it is that all our merits must be founded upon his merit. ¶ Since indeed in Christ there was the ful...

<summary>This page defines the "fullness of merit" in Christ, arguing that His divine-human nature and perfect charity gave His life infinite value from conception to death. It distinguishes between what Christ merited for humanity (grace, pardon, and resurrection) and what He merited for Himself (the timing of His resurrection and His status as Judge).</summary>
<keywords>merit, Christology, charity, grace, resurrection, judicial power, debt, Bonaventure, salvation</keywords>

...the fullness of grace in the affection and the fullness of wisdom in the intellect, it was necessary that in Him there should be the fullness and perfection of **merit** <term>original: *meritum*; the quality of an action that makes it worthy of a reward from God</term> according to every mode of fullness.

## ¶ On the endurance of the passion of the Lord Jesus.
### ¶ Chapter 31.

**I**t follows that we should consider the endurance of the **Passion** <term>original: *passio*; referring specifically to the suffering and crucifixion of Jesus</term> concerning the Lord Savior. It must be known that Christ assumed not only a human nature, but also the **defects** <note>original: *defectus*; these are human frailties or limitations resulting from the Fall, such as hunger or fatigue, which Christ took on voluntarily without taking on sin itself</note> associated with that nature. For He took upon himself bodily penalties such as hunger, thirst, and weariness. He also took on spiritual penalties such as sadness, groaning, fear, and pain. Yet He did not assume all bodily defects, such as the various forms of diseases and illnesses. Nor did He assume all spiritual defects, such as ignorance or the **rebellion of the flesh** against the spirit and vice versa <note>original: *rebellio carnis ad spiritum*; this refers to the internal conflict between lower desires and higher reason, which theologians believed Christ was free from</note>. Nor did He assume these defects in just any manner; for He assumed the necessity of suffering in such a way that He could suffer nothing against His will—neither according to His divine will nor according to His rational will—even though the suffering was against the will of His senses and His flesh.

¶ In the "passible" nature <note>original: *natura passibili*; a nature capable of feeling pain or undergoing change</note> thus assumed, He suffered with a most universal passion regarding His human nature. This is because He suffered not only in all the principal members of His body, but also in every power of His soul, although He could suffer nothing according to His divine nature.

¶ He suffered with a most bitter passion. This was partly due to the **balance of His constitution** <note>original: *complexionis equalitatem*; medieval medicine taught that a perfectly balanced physical body was more sensitive and thus felt pain more acutely</note>, partly due to the liveliness of His senses, and partly due to the continuity of His "hanging" <note>referring to the prolonged agony of the crucifixion</note>. Furthermore, it was because He willed it—as a sufferer on account of His wounds—and because He grieved for our sins as a fellow-sufferer <term>original: *compaciens*; one who suffers with or feels the pain of another</term>.

¶ He suffered with a most shameful passion, on account of...

<summary>This page explains that Christ voluntarily took on human physical and emotional frailties (like hunger and sadness) to suffer for humanity, though He remained free from sickness and internal sin. It argues His suffering was uniquely intense because of His perfect physical sensitivity and His profound compassion for human sin.</summary>

<keywords>Passion of Christ, human nature, bodily penalties, spiritual suffering, balanced constitution, merit, compassion, crucifixion</keywords>

But when the mystery of the Passion was completed, the soul of Christ descended for the liberation of those who had passed away as His members through right faith or through the sacraments of faith <note>This refers to the "Harrowing of Hell," the theological belief that Christ descended to the underworld to rescue the righteous souls of the Old Testament</note>. After forty days He ascended into the heavens, and after ten days had passed, He sent the Holy Spirit.

## ¶ On Sacred Scripture regarding the two Testaments. Chapter 31.

It follows that we should look at the triple benefit of Christ; first, at the sacred doctrine by reason of which He is said to have descended to men "full of truth." We must therefore consider sacred doctrine, or divine scripture, in terms of three things: the diversity of the testaments, the generality of the contents, and the specificity of the precepts. 

Accordingly, Scripture is divided into two testaments: the **Old** and the **New**. The Old Testament contains a multitude of books. It includes the **Legal books**, five in total: namely, Genesis, Leviticus, Exodus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy. It also includes twelve **Historical books**, which are Joshua, Judges, the four books of Kings <note>In the medieval Vulgate Bible, the books of 1 & 2 Samuel and 1 & 2 Kings were titled as the four books of Kings</note>, the two books of **Paralipomenon** <term>original: *palippomenon*; the books of Chronicles</term>, three books of Ezra <note>original: *Esdre. iij.*; this count likely includes the books of Ezra, Nehemiah, and the apocryphal 1 Esdras</note>, the book of Tobit, Judith, Esther, Job, and the two books of Maccabees. 

It also includes five **Sapiential books** <note>original: *sapientiales*; books belonging to the "Wisdom" tradition</note>, which are the **Parables** <term>original: *parabole*; the Book of Proverbs</term>, Ecclesiastes, the Canticle of Canticles <note>Song of Songs</note>, the Book of Wisdom, and Ecclesiasticus <note>also known as the Wisdom of Sirach</note>. Finally, it includes six **Prophetic books**, the first of which is Isaiah...

¶ The difference between the **Mosaic Law** and the **Gospel Law** is assigned in this manner: that the Law of Moses is called the law of figure <note>meaning a "foreshadowing" or symbolic representation</note>, the law of punishment, the literal law, the law that kills <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 3:6, "for the letter kills, but the Spirit gives life"</note>, the law of fear, the law of servitude, and the law of burden. But the **Gospel Law** is called the law of truth, the law of grace, the law of the spirit, the law that gives life, the law of love, the law of liberty, and the law of ease. **In** the Gospel Law, indeed, four most noble conditions are added: namely, instructive teachings, inciting promises, helping sacraments, and perfecting counsels, which are the counsel of pov-

### **¶ Concerning the Precepts of the Decalogue.**

The precepts of the Decalogue <note>The Ten Commandments</note> must be specifically explained, which are called "moral" because, together with faith, they are necessary for the salvation of humanity. For **in faith and morals**, the salvation of humanity is established. Moreover, they were written by the finger of God on two stone tablets, as a sign of twofold love—namely, divine and human. Regarding these, two things are briefly to be seen: namely, their arrangement and the assignment of their number or sufficiency. ¶ They are arranged by Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus (c. 37–100 AD) was a Romano-Jewish historian</note> the historian according to the order of the actual events: five on the first tablet and five on the second. **According to Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine (354–430 AD) was an influential North African bishop and theologian</note>, there are three on the first tablet and seven on the second. ¶ **Origen** <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253 AD) was an early Christian scholar</note>, considering the subject matter of those things which are arranged, distinguishes the first into two: namely, "you shall not have foreign gods before me" and "you shall not make for yourself a carved image." In the first of these, the worship of a statue representing something that does not exist or used in prophecies is forbidden; in the second, it is prohibited...

### Third Part.

...the adoration of a statue having a likeness with any creature is prohibited. ¶ He <note>Referring to an alternative numbering system, likely that of Origen or Josephus</note> does not distinguish between the two final phrases of the second tablet, regarding them as one—namely: "you shall not covet your neighbor’s wife" and "you shall not desire his property." ¶ Saint Augustine, however, considering the end <note>The "end" or purpose/goal</note> toward which the precepts of the Decalogue are ordered, does not distinguish the first two: because in both, according to the intention, idolatry is prohibited. And thus he places only three commandments on the first tablet. ¶ But the precept concerning the coveting of a wife and of another’s possession he distinguishes into two: because a wife is coveted for one end, for the use of pleasure; whereas a possession is coveted for another end, for the use of greed. ¶ And thus he places seven commandments on the second tablet. ¶ Therefore, following Augustine (who places three on the first tablet and seven on the second), let us assign the logic for this number. ¶ In the first tablet, there were three precepts ordering us toward God, the sufficiency of which can be understood according to the "subject," which is threefold within us: namely, the **irascible power** <term>the part of the soul dealing with righteous anger and the strength to stand for truth</term> which is ordered toward the precept of the presentation of truth; the **concupiscible power** <term>the part of the soul dealing with desire and love</term> which is ordered toward the precept of the **Sabbath-rest of the mind** <term>original: "sabbatizatione mentis"; the spiritual peace found in God</term>. ¶ And the **rational power** <term>the part of the soul dealing with intellect and reason</term> which is ordered by the precept concerning the adoration of His majesty. These are the rational, irascible, and concupiscible powers. ¶ This numbering can also be understood according to the "object," which is threefold in divine matters: namely, the **highest majesty** to be revered, in respect to which is the first commandment. ¶ The **highest truth** to be professed, in respect to which is the second. # The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

...of the highest poverty, of the purest chastity, and of the most submissive obedience or humility. ¶ For it deals with seven things in the universe, found in the scriptures of both the New and Old Testaments: namely, the Trinity of God; the creation of the world; the corruption of sin; the incarnation of the Word; the grace of the Holy Spirit; the medicine of the sacraments; and the state of the final judgment. ¶ There are specifically four types of commandments in Scripture: namely, certain **ceremonial** ones, such as "you shall not plow with an ox and a donkey" <note>original: "non arabis in boue et asino"; a reference to Deuteronomy 22:10, often interpreted symbolically in the Middle Ages</note>; certain **sacramental** ones, such as the red heifer <note>A specific purification ritual from Numbers 19</note> and other sacrifices and circumcision; certain **judicial** ones, such as "an eye for an eye and a tooth for a tooth"; and certain **moral** ones, such as the ten commandments of the Decalogue <term>The Ten Commandments; from the Greek "deka logoi" meaning "ten words"</term>.

### ¶ Regarding the commandments of the Decalogue.

The commandments of the Decalogue are to be specifically explained, which are called "moral" because, along with faith, they are necessary for the salvation of humanity. For the salvation of mankind is established in faith and morals. Moreover, they were written by the finger of God on two stone tablets, as a sign of the twofold love—namely, divine and human <note>Referring to the "Greatest Commandment" to love God and love one's neighbor</note>. ¶ Concerning these, two things are to be briefly considered: namely, the order and the assignment of the number or their sufficiency. ¶ They are ordered by Josephus the historian <term>Titus Flavius Josephus (c. 37–100 AD), a Romano-Jewish historian whose works were foundational for medieval biblical history</term> according to the sequence of events as five on the first tablet and five on the second. ¶ According to Augustine, there are three on the first tablet and seven on the second. ¶ Origen <note>An early Christian scholar (c. 184–253 AD) whose system of numbering the commandments differs from the later Roman Catholic tradition</note>, considering the matter of those things which are ordered, distinguishes the first into two: namely, "you shall not have foreign gods before me" and "you shall not make for yourself a carved image." -># Part Three.<-

...[the worship of a statue having a likeness to any creature is forbid]den. <note>original: "prohibetur adoratio statue..."; this completes the thought from the previous page regarding the first commandment</note> ¶ He <note>Referring to the alternative numbering system of Origen or Josephus mentioned previously</note> does not distinguish between the last two phrases of the second tablet: regarding them as one, namely: "you shall not covet your neighbor's wife" and "you shall not desire his property." ¶ Augustine, however, considering the goal toward which the commandments of the Decalogue are ordered, does not distinguish the first two <note>The prohibition of other gods and the prohibition of idols</note>: because in both cases, the intention is to prohibit idolatry. ¶ And thus he places only three commandments on the first tablet. ¶ But the commandment concerning the coveting of a wife and of another’s possession he distinguishes into two: because one covets a wife for the end of pleasure, but one covets property for the end of greed. ¶ And thus he places seven on the second tablet. ¶ Following Augustine, therefore (who puts three on the first tablet and seven on the second), let us explain why this number is appropriate. ¶ On the first tablet, there were three commandments ordering us toward God; the appropriateness of which can be understood in relation to the **subject** <note>The "subject" here refers to the human person</note>, which is threefold within us.

1. The **irascible power**, which is ordered toward the commandment regarding the presentation of truth. <note>original: "potentia irascibilis"; in medieval psychology, this is the "spirited" part of the soul that deals with challenges and upholding what is right</note>

# ¶ From the *Centiloquium* <note>literally "A collection of a hundred sayings"</note> of Saint Bonaventure

...[The] act of the heart which is exercised in the third [commandment] through the affection of love. ¶ No one can properly celebrate the Sabbath—that is, the rest of the mind—unless they strive through continuous rest to turn away from every mortal sin. ¶ On the second tablet, there were seven commandments directing us toward our neighbor, the completeness of which is understood in this way: ¶ We are to be directed toward our neighbor in two ways: first, through doing good <term>beneficence; from the Latin 'beneficentia', the practice of doing good acts</term>, and thus is the first commandment: "Honor your father and your mother." ¶ Second, through innocence <note>remaining blameless by not causing harm</note>, and in this way we can harm our neighbor in three modes: by an act of work <note>physical deeds</note>, an act of the mouth <note>speech</note>, and an act of the heart. ¶ By an act of work, there are three modes: specifically, against the neighbor's own person—and thus it is defined as "You shall not kill." Against a person joined to them <note>specifically a spouse</note>—and thus it is said: "You shall not commit adultery." <note>original: "non mechaberis"</note> ¶ Against their possession—and thus it is said: "You shall not steal." ¶ By an act of the mouth, we can harm our neighbor by testifying falsely—and thus it is said: "You shall not speak false witness against your neighbor." ¶ By an act of the heart, we can harm in two ways: through the affection of carnality <note>lustful desire</note>—and thus it is said: "You shall not covet your neighbor's wife." ¶ And through the affection of greed <note>desire for wealth</note>—and thus it is said: "You shall not desire his property." ¶ These commandments are contained in order in these verses:

> Spurn [false] gods; flee perjuries; keep the sabbath.
> Let there be honor for your father; let there be love for your mother.
> Do not be a killer, an adulterer, a thief, or an unjust witness.
> Beware [taking] the bed of your neighbor or his things.
<note>original Latin mnemonic: "Sperne deos: fugito periuria: sabbata serva. / Sit tibi patris honor: sit tibi matris amor. / Non sis occisor: mechus: fur: testis iniquus. / Vicinique thorum res que caveto suas."</note>

<meta>This page continues the classification of divine laws, moving from the specific commandments of the Decalogue (Ten Commandments) to a broader categorization of all biblical precepts. It transitions from the duty to one's neighbor—the tail end of the previous page's discussion—to the duty toward God and the different types of laws found in the Old and New Testaments.</meta>

### Third Part
i.i.

...[of our neighbor]. With respect to God, there is that in Deuteronomy 6, specifically: "**You shall love** the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your strength." <note>original: "Diliges dominum deum tuum..."</note> With respect to our neighbor, there is a twofold [precept]: one through beneficence <term>from the Latin 'beneficentia', the practice of performing kind or charitable acts</term> from Matthew 7: "**Whatever** you wish men to do to you, do the same to them."

**Another** is regarding innocence <note>the state of being harmless or doing no evil</note>, from Tobit 4: "**What** you hate to be done to yourself by another, see that you never do to another at any time." <note>This is often called the 'Silver Rule,' the negative counterpart to the Golden Rule.</note> A twofold **precept** was given: one for the preservation of the individual, from Genesis 2: "Eat from every tree of paradise"; and the other is for the preservation of the species, from Genesis 1: "**Increase** and multiply."

The **precept** of discipline is twofold: one for the testing of obedience, from Genesis 2: "Of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, you shall not eat." **Another** for the testing of faith, from Genesis 22: "**Take** your only-begotten son Isaac, whom you love, so that you may offer him to me."

The **precepts** of scripture are contained in four kinds, as was said above. **Some** are ceremonial, some sacramental, some judicial, and some moral. **These** four kinds of precepts were in the Old Testament, multiplied in such a number that there were almost no more days in the year nor bones in a person than there were precepts given to God’s people. <note>The author is referencing the Jewish tradition of the 613 Mitzvot (commandments), which are traditionally divided into 365 negative commands (corresponding to the days of the year) and 248 positive commands (corresponding to the perceived number of bones or members in the human body).</note>

The **Gospel Law** <term>Lex evangelica; the teachings of Christ in the New Testament</term>, however, arriving as if shortening and completing [the Law], voided the ceremonial and sacramental ones by fulfilling them. It tempered the judicial ones by removing them, and it completed the **moral** ones by adding soul-saving counsels. <note>In medieval theology, the 'counsels' are suggestions for spiritual perfection, such as voluntary poverty, which go beyond the basic requirements of the commandments.</note>

All the precepts which we read in scripture **can** be brought to three categories or members: so that we may say that of precepts, one is of execution—such as that which we are bound to fulfill by the Lord's will—**another** is of testing [probation], such as was the pre[cept...]

<summary>This page categorizes biblical commandments into types such as moral, ceremonial, and judicial, while contrasting the vast complexity of Old Testament law with the "shortened" and fulfilled law of the Gospel.</summary>
<keywords>precepts, Golden Rule, Tobit, Old Testament Law, Gospel Law, types of commandments, Abraham and Isaac, garden of Eden</keywords>

# The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure
<note>The "Centiloquium" (meaning 'one hundred sayings') is a theological work traditionally attributed to the medieval philosopher and saint, Bonaventure, though its authorship is sometimes debated.</note>

...the [pre]cept of Abraham, that he should sacrifice his son, in which God required not the work itself but the readiness of the will. Another is a precept of instruction, such as when the Lord sometimes healed certain people and commanded those who were healed not to divulge it among the people. For the Lord did not oblige those healed to remain silent <note>He did not make it a strict law they were bound to obey on pain of sin</note>, but was providing instruction: so that we might not seek praise for our own great works.

## Concerning grace considered in itself, as to its definition, division, and operation. Chapter 35.

It follows next to look at the second benefit of Jesus Christ: which is grace; by reason of which He is said to have come "full of grace." <note>A reference to John 1:14.</note> We must consider grace in itself, and in comparison to the habits of virtue. In itself, grace must be considered regarding the diversity of its definition, division, naming, and operation. Grace is defined in many ways according to the many ways in which it operates. It is compared to its principle <note>meaning its source or origin</note>, and thus it is defined by Isidore:

> Grace is a gift of divine mercy through which the outcome of a good will emerges. <note>original: "Gratia est diuine misericordie donum..."</note>

Grace is also compared to its subject <note>the human soul in which it resides</note>, and thus it is described in the gloss upon that verse of the Psalm:

> "That he may make the face cheerful with oil." <note>Psalm 103:15 in the Vulgate, or 104:15 in modern Bibles.</note>

Grace is a certain brightness <term>nitor: a Latin term for luster or spiritual radiance</term> of the soul for the sake of winning over holy love. It is also compared to its opposite, and thus it is defined in the gloss upon the Psalms:

> Grace is justification and the cutting away of sins.

It is compared to its effect, and thus it is the definition of Chrysostom <note>Saint John Chrysostom, a 4th-century Church Father</note>:

> Grace is the health of the mind and the love of the heart.

Fifth, it can be compared to its reward, and thus it is said:

> Grace is the likeness of glory. <note>In medieval theology, grace was seen as the beginning or "seed" of the glory found in heaven.</note>

According to all these comparisons, a magisterial <note>scholarly or authoritative</note> definition is assigned, which is as follows:

> Grace is a form given by God freely without merits; making the one who possesses it pleasing to God, and rendering their work good. <term>gratum faciens: literally 'making pleasing,' a technical theological term for the grace that makes a soul acceptable for salvation</term>

Another description is also found which belongs to grace...

<keywords>Bonaventure, grace, Isidore of Seville, John Chrysostom, justification, divine mercy, theology of will, scholasticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of grace, moving from the general definition provided by Isidore of Seville on the previous page to a detailed classification of grace based on its origins, its effects on the soul, and its role in salvation.</meta>

...given freely. <note>original: "gratis date"</note>

# Part Three.

Grace is a manifestation of the spirit for the benefit of the spirit. <note>This definition is adapted from 1 Corinthians 12:7: "To each is given the manifestation of the Spirit for the common good."</note> Grace is divided in many ways according to the many ways it is compared. 

When compared to its **principle** <note>meaning its source or origin</note> from which it comes, it is divided into the grace of **predestination**, of **calling**, of **justification**, and of **magnification** <term>magnification: here referring to the final glorification or honoring of the soul in heaven</term>. First, God prepares grace; second, He offers it; third, He bestows it; and fourth, He completes it. 

When compared to its **subject** in which it resides <note>the human soul</note>, it is divided into the grace of **knowledge**, of **will**, and of **perfection**, according to the threefold power of the soul: namely, the intellectual, the affective <note>the realm of emotions and desires</note>, and the operational. This division is taken from Saint Bernard in his book *On Free Will*.

When compared to the **opposite** against which it works, it is divided into the grace of **protection**, of **liberation**, and of **salvation**, against a fourfold evil: namely, the struggle of temptation, the misery of persecution, the infection of guilt, and the consequence of damnation. First, grace protects; from the second, it liberates; from the third, it extracts; and from the fourth, it saves. This is taken from Isaiah 31: 

> The Lord of hosts will protect Jerusalem, protecting and delivering, passing over and saving. <note>original: "Proteget dominus exercituum iherusalem protegens & liberans transiens et saluans." (Isaiah 31:5)</note>

When compared to the **effect** for which it exists, it is divided into **prevenient grace** <term>prevenient: from the Latin 'praevenire' (to come before); grace that moves a person before they have even thought to seek God</term> and **subsequent grace**, or **operating** and **cooperating** grace. These are two different names for grace according to the effects it has on the will. It is called "prevenient" because it does not come from our own free choice, but is poured in by God Himself. It is called "subsequent" or "cooperating" insofar as it assists our free choice in performing a good work. 

Furthermore, grace is expressed by various terms according to the different intentions of those naming it. For it is called **grace freely given** <term>gratia gratis data: spiritual gifts like prophecy or healing given for the benefit of others</term> and **grace that makes one pleasing** <term>gratia gratum faciens: the sanctifying grace that makes the individual soul acceptable to God</term>. It is also called **operating** and **cooperating** grace, or **prevenient** and **subsequent** grace.

<summary>This page outlines the systematic theological divisions of grace, explaining how it originates from God, affects the various powers of the human soul, and protects the believer from temptation and damnation.</summary>
<keywords>grace, predestination, justification, free will, Bernard of Clairvaux, prevenient grace, cooperating grace, sanctification, Isaiah 31, spiritual psychology</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholastic categorization of grace. Having previously discussed grace in relation to its origin, its presence in the soul, and its role in protecting against evil, the author now defines the specific types of grace (prevenient, subsequent, and sanctifying) using the authorities of Saint Bernard of Clairvaux and Saint Augustine.</meta>

# The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

Grace is understood in its broadest sense: and thus it encompasses both natural gifts <note>such as life, reason, and the senses</note> and gratuitous gifts <note>supernatural gifts given by God's favor</note>. It is also understood in a less common sense: and thus it encompasses **grace given freely** <term>gratia gratis data: spiritual gifts like healing or prophecy that are given for the benefit of others, regardless of the recipient's personal holiness</term> and **grace making one pleasing** <term>gratia gratum faciens: also known as sanctifying grace, which transforms the soul and makes it pleasing to God</term>.

**And thus the blessed Bernard** <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a highly influential Cistercian monk and mystic</note> says that a certain grace sows the thought: **and this is prevenient grace** <note>original: "gratia preveniens," meaning grace that "comes before" human action</note>. A certain grace perfects the good: and this is **subsequent grace** <note>original: "gratia subsequens," meaning grace that "follows" to assist the human will</note>. For he says in his book *On Free Will* that God works in us to think, to will, and to perform. **The first He does without us.** <note>Thinking the initial good thought is seen as a pure gift from God</note> **The second He does with us. The third He does through us.**

And note that **grace given freely** is commonly said by theologians to be the grace that can be shared by both the good and the evil; it is multiplied in man according to the bounty of the Giver. **But grace making one pleasing** belongs only to the righteous who are endowed with charity <note>supernatural love</note>. And there is only one such grace in one man, for three reasons:

*   First, by reason of the one **effective principle** <note>the source or cause</note> to which it is likened.
*   Second, by reason of the one **perceptive subject** <note>the soul</note> which it brings to life.
*   Third, by reason of the one **ultimate completing end** <note>the goal of heaven</note> to which it gradually directs the person.

For it returns the whole order received to God; it makes the work proceeding from **free will** meritorious <note>deserving of a spiritual reward</note> and leads one to the highest good.

Thirdly, grace is understood strictly, and thus it encompasses both grace making one pleasing and glory <note>the state of the soul in heaven</note>; and then the prevenient is grace itself, and the subsequent is grace <note>original: "subsequens est gratia," though in this context it often implies the fulfillment of grace in glory</note>. And according to this, **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430), the foundational theologian of Western Christianity</note> says in his book *On Grace and Nature*: **"Grace comes before so that we may live piously; it follows after so that we may live with Him."** <note>original: "Gratia prevenit ut pie vivamus, subsequitur ut cum illo vivamus"</note>

It is also understood in the most strict sense, and thus it names only **grace making one pleasing**; and then it is "prevenient" in its effect against evil, and "subsequent" toward the good, or toward the good alone. **So it is called prevenient according to how it informs** <term>inform: in a philosophical sense, to give "form" or essential character to the soul</term>, **and subsequent according to how it moves or advises.** **There is a twofold effect of grace: one at the entrance, the other in progress.** **And thus Augustine explains in the book *On Nature and Grace*.**

<summary>This page defines the theological distinctions between prevenient and subsequent grace, explaining how God initiates good thoughts and works alongside human free will to achieve salvation. It relies on the definitions provided by St. Bernard and St. Augustine to distinguish between general spiritual gifts and the sanctifying grace that justifies the soul.</summary>

<keywords>prevenient grace, subsequent grace, sanctifying grace, Saint Bernard of Clairvaux, Saint Augustine, free will, merit, scholasticism, Centiloquium</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of sanctifying grace, specifically exploring how this single divine gift manifests in various ways within the soul. It transitions from the general nature of grace to its specific "branches": the virtues, gifts of the Spirit, and the beatitudes, beginning a detailed look at the definitions of virtue provided by classical authorities like Aristotle.</meta>

### Part Three

This grace, although it is one, performs many works in the soul. For it makes the soul itself the **bride of Christ**, the **daughter of the eternal Father**, and the **temple of the Holy Spirit**. It cleanses, illuminates, perfects, and gives life to the soul; it reforms, stabilizes, elevates, and assimilates the soul <note>meaning it makes the soul more like God</note>, joining it to God, and through this, makes it acceptable to Him. For this reason, such a gift is rightly called **grace making one pleasing** <term>gratia gratum faciens: the theological term for sanctifying grace that restores the relationship between the human soul and God</term>.

## On grace in comparison to gratuitous habits, such as virtues in general: Chapter 36

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' with floral and scroll motifs.</image-desc>

It follows next to look at grace in comparison to those **spiritual habits** <term>habitus: in medieval philosophy, a stable disposition or "muscle memory" of the soul that makes acting rightly easier</term> into which grace branches out. These habits exist in three distinct categories: there are the **virtues**, the **gifts**, and the **beatitudes**. The first (virtues) pertain to the initial movements of the soul; the second (gifts) to the intermediate movements; and the third (beatitudes) to the perfected movements.

For there are certain primary moral works, such as **believing**; certain intermediate works, such as **understanding** what is believed; and certain final works, such as **seeing** <note>the "beatific vision," or seeing God directly</note> that which was understood. Likewise, the first are for **beginners**; the second for those **making progress**; and the third for the **perfected**. Furthermore, the first serve to **rectify** <note>straighten or correct</note> the powers of the soul; the second to **expedite** <note>unblock or free</note> them; and the third to **perfect** them.

¶ First, let us look briefly at the virtues, regarding which three things must generally be reported: namely, their **definition**, their **distinction**, and their **connection**. 

¶ A manifold definition of virtue is found. For "virtue" is understood in a **common** sense, a **proper** sense, and a **most proper** sense. In its **common** sense, virtue encompasses both moral and natural excellence. According to this, when compared to action, it is defined by "the Philosopher" <note>the standard medieval title for Aristotle</note> in his book *On the Heavens and the Earth* as follows: **"Virtue is the ultimate limit of a thing's power"** <note>original: "Virtus est ultimum potentie de re"</note> in relation to its action. And it is defined by the same author in the seventh book of the *Nicomachean Ethics* as...

<keywords>sanctifying grace, habits, virtues, Aristotle, soul's progression, spiritual growth, scholastic theology</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical classification of grace as it manifests in human habits. Having established that grace branches into virtues, gifts, and beatitudes, the author now provides a series of formal definitions of "virtue" drawn from the greatest authorities of the medieval intellectual world: Aristotle (the Philosopher), Cicero (Tully), and St. Augustine.</meta>

### The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

**V**irtue is the disposition of a perfect thing toward its best state <note>original: "virtus est dispositio perfecti ad optimum"</note>. **W**hen understood more strictly, it encompasses moral virtue, extending to both civic and grace-given virtue. According to this, when compared to action, it is defined by **the Philosopher** <note>The standard medieval title for Aristotle</note> in his *Ethics* in this way: **V**irtue is a habit that perfects the one who possesses it and makes their work good. **C**ompared to its guiding principle, it is defined by the same author in the same place: **V**irtue is a voluntary habit consisting in a **mean** <term>the "golden mean": the virtuous middle ground between two extremes, such as courage being the mean between cowardice and recklessness</term>, determined by right reason as a wise person would determine it. **S**econdly, it is defined by **Tully** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman orator and philosopher</note>: **V**irtue is a habit of a well-ordered mind. **L**ikewise, by Augustine in his book *On the Quantity of the Soul*: **V**irtue is a quality of the soul or of life that follows reason in all things. **W**hen understood even more specifically, it includes grace-given virtue. **A**nd according to this, when compared to its end <note>its final goal or purpose</note>, it is defined by Augustine in his *Soliloquies*: **V**irtue is a habit reaching its goal through right reason. **C**ompared to the subject which it shapes <note>the human soul</note>, it is defined by the same author in *The City of God*: **V**irtue is a **good will**. **R**egarding its own act or completion, it is defined in the book *On the Morals of the Church*: **V**irtue is the **order of love**, or love properly ordered. **C**ompared to its effective principle <note>the source that creates it</note>, it is defined by Augustine thus: **V**irtue is a good quality of the mind by which one lives rightly, which no one uses for evil, and which God works in us without us <note>This famous definition emphasizes that "infused" virtue is a gift from God, not something humans create on their own</note>.

¶ There are, however, **seven virtues** by which the Christian life is governed. **T**hree are **theological**: namely, faith, hope, and charity. **F**our are **cardinal**: namely, prudence, justice, temperance, and fortitude. **T**he sufficiency of these is understood as follows: **T**he soul has two "faces," namely the **superior**, which looks toward the end <note>God, the ultimate goal</note>, and the **inferior**, which looks toward those things that lead to the end <note>earthly means and actions</note>. **T**he superior side must be rectified through the theological virtues in relation to a triple object...

<summary>This page provides a collection of authoritative definitions of virtue from Aristotle, Cicero, and Augustine, ultimately categorizing them into the seven Christian virtues: three theological and four cardinal.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, Aristotle, Cicero, St. Augustine, habit, golden mean, theological virtues, cardinal virtues, faith, hope, charity, prudence, justice, temperance, fortitude</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of virtue, moving from general definitions to the specific structure of the soul. The previous page ended with a definition from Saint Augustine’s "The City of God" regarding how virtue shapes the human subject.</meta>

# A Third Part

...as it exists in things divine, and according to the threefold subject which is within us. **In** things divine, there is supreme Truth, toward which Faith directs us in believing and assenting; supreme majesty or bounty <note>original: "largitas"</note>, toward which Hope lifts us in praising and expecting; and supreme Goodness, with which Charity unites us in embracing and loving.

There is also a threefold subject within us <note>Referring to the medieval psychological model of the soul's three "powers" or appetites</note>: namely, the **rational power**, which is ordered or rectified through Faith; the **irascible power** <term>the faculty of the soul that deals with "spirited" emotions like courage, anger, or the drive to overcome obstacles</term>, which is rectified through Hope; and the **concupiscible power** <term>the faculty of the soul that deals with basic desires, such as the longing for food, pleasure, or love</term>, which is rectified through Charity.

The lower face of the soul, however, must be rectified through the four **cardinal virtues**. For **Prudence** rectifies the rational power; **Fortitude** rectifies the irascible; and **Temperance** rectifies the concupiscible. **Justice** rectifies all powers or virtues in relation to another. **In** relation, I say, to one's neighbor, that which rectifies all powers is called "cardinal justice." **In** relation, however, to one's neighbor, to oneself, and to God, it is not only called cardinal but "general justice"—a general justice encompassing the rectitude of the whole soul.

The sufficiency of the cardinal virtues can also be assigned in another way according to their subjects. For the rational power must be rectified with respect to itself, and thus it needs **Prudence**; and with respect to one's neighbor <note>original: "proximi"</note>, and thus it needs **Justice**. The irascible power must be rectified against adversities, and thus it needs **Fortitude**. The concupiscible power must be rectified regarding its own desires, and thus it needs **Temperance**.

**Virtues**, however, possess their "gracious being" from **grace**, as if from their origin and root. **And** they possess their "meritorious being" from **Charity**, as if from their mover, form, and end. **And** therefore all virtues are connected as far as they are habits; and as meritorious habits, they are equal.

# The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

**Virtue** is the disposition of that which is perfect toward what is best. When understood in its proper sense, it encompasses moral virtue, extending to political and gratuitous <term>grace-given</term> virtue. According to this, in relation to action, it is defined by the Philosopher <note>The medieval title for Aristotle</note> in the *Ethics* as follows: **Virtue** is a habit that perfects the one who possesses it and makes their work good. Compared to the principle that directs it, it is defined by the same author in the same place: **Virtue** is a voluntary habit consisting in a mean, determined by right reason just as the wise person has determined it. According to this, it is defined by Tully <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman orator and philosopher</note>: **Virtue** is a habit of a well-ordered mind.

Likewise, by Augustine in the book *On the Quantity of the Soul*: **Virtue** is a quality of the soul or of life, following reason in all things. Taken more specifically, it encompasses gratuitous virtue. And according to this, in relation to its end <note>The "end" refers to the ultimate goal or purpose of an action</note>, it is defined by Augustine in the *Soliloquies*: **Virtue** is a habit reaching its end by right reason. Compared to the subject which it informs <term>To "inform" means to give essential form or character to the soul</term>, it is defined by him in the book *The City of God*: **Virtue** is a good will. Regarding its proper act or fulfillment, it is defined in the book *On the Morals of the Church*: **Virtue** is the order of love <note>original: "ordo amoris"</note>, or ordered love. Compared to its effective principle <note>The source or cause that produces the virtue</note>, it is defined by Augustine thus: **Virtue** is a good quality of the mind by which one lives rightly, which no one uses for evil, and which God works in us without us.

¶ There are, moreover, seven virtues by which the Christian life is governed. Three are **theological**: namely, faith, hope, and charity. Four are **cardinal**: namely, prudence, justice, temperance, and fortitude. The "sufficiency" <note>The reason why these seven are considered a complete set</note> of these is understood thus: The soul has a **twofold face**; namely, the **superior**, which relates to the end, and the **inferior**, which relates to those things which lead to the end <note>The "superior face" looks toward God, while the "inferior face" manages earthly affairs</note>. The **superior face** must be rectified through the theological virtues in regard to a threefold object...

It introduces the division of the seven virtues into theological and cardinal categories based on the "twofold face" of the human soul.</summary>
