<meta>This page begins the discussion on faith within Bonaventure's "Centiloquium" (The Hundredfold Discourse), specifically focusing on the various ways the term is defined by Church Fathers and philosophers.</meta>

# ¶ The Hundredfold Discourse of Saint Bonaventure

Secondly, regarding the contents of the Creed <note>The "symbolum" or Apostles' Creed</note>, we can consider faith itself in terms of its definition, the various ways the word is used (equivocation), and the realization of its effects. Faith is defined in many ways.

Regarding its proper **category** <note>original: "genus"</note>, it is defined by **Augustine**: Faith is the virtue by which things that are not seen are believed.

Regarding its **ultimate end**, it is defined by the **Apostle** <note>Referring to St. Paul in Hebrews 11:1</note>: Faith is the substance of things to be hoped for, the evidence of things not seen.

By **John of Damascus**: Faith is the foundation <note>original: "ypostaf." for "hypostasis"</note> of those things which are hoped for, and the proof of things that are not seen.

Regarding its **object and first motive**, by **Augustine**: Faith is the illumination of the mind toward the highest truth.

Regarding its **proper act**, by **John of Damascus**: Faith is an unquestioning <note>original: "non inquisitus," meaning a consensus reached without restless or skeptical doubt</note> consensus.

In relation to the **subject**, by **Dionysius**: Faith is the unique foundation of believers, placing them in the truth.

In comparison to its **habit** <note>A "habit" in medieval philosophy is a stable disposition of the soul</note> and its manner of being held, by **Hugh of Saint Victor**: Faith is a certain voluntary certainty regarding absent things, established above mere opinion and below full knowledge <term>knowledge: "scientia," referring to demonstrative certainty gained through sight or reason</term>.

The name "faith," however, is understood in ten ways. Sometimes it is taken for a **promise**, as in 1 Timothy 5: "When they have made void their first faith" <note>Referring to breaking a vow</note>. Sometimes for **fidelity**, as in Jeremiah 7: "Faith has perished and is cut off from the earth." Sometimes for **knowledge**, as in Romans 9 <note>The quote "Everything that is not of faith is sin" is actually found in Romans 14:23</note>: "Everything that is not of faith is sin." Sometimes for the **sacrament of faith**; hence **Augustine** says that for children to have faith is nothing other than to have the sacrament of faith <note>Referring to Baptism</note>. Sometimes for the **acceptance of a conclusion** proven by reason, and thus **Boethius** says: An argument is a reason for a doubtful matter that creates faith <note>meaning conviction or belief</note>. Sometimes for the **knowledge of comprehension**, as in Romans 1: "The justice of God will be revealed from faith to faith," which the Gloss explains as "to the faith of vision" <note>original: "fidem speciei," referring to the direct "sight" of God in heaven</note>, which is called faith because it is most faithful and most certain. Sometimes for the **habit of unformed faith**...

<summary>This page catalogs various authoritative definitions of "faith" from figures like Augustine and Paul, while also beginning a list of the ten different theological and linguistic ways the word "faith" is used in Scripture and philosophy.</summary>

<keywords>faith, definitions of faith, Saint Augustine, John of Damascus, Hugh of Saint Victor, Boethius, theology, medieval philosophy, semantics of faith</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the ten ways the term "faith" is understood, moving from general definitions to the specific distinctions made by the Church Fathers regarding how we believe.</meta>

### Third Part

...[is understood as] in **James** [chapter] two: **Faith** without works is dead. <note>original: "Fides sine operibus mortua est"</note> ¶ Sometimes it stands for the "habit of formed faith," <note>"Formed faith" refers to faith animated by love (charity), as opposed to "unformed faith" which is mere intellectual belief.</note> as in **Romans** [chapter] one: The **just** man lives by faith. ¶ Sometimes it stands for the act of faith, and thus **Augustine** says: Faith is to believe what you do not see. ¶ Sometimes it stands for the object of faith. ¶ Hence it is said in the Creed: This is the Catholic **faith**. ¶ Now, the act of faith, which is "believing," is differentiated:

¶ Through **believing God** <note>original: "credere deum"</note>, **believing God** <note>original: "credere deo"</note>, and **believing in God** <note>original: "credere in deum"</note>. ¶ Believing God [as an object] and believing God [as a witness] can be acts of either unformed or formed faith. ¶ However, they can belong to the same act according to a specific order. ¶ For **believing God** [as an object] looks toward Divine Truth as the object; **believing God** [as a witness] looks to Him as the motive. **Believing in God** looks to Him as the ultimate end. **Anselm** explains this final act in a multifaceted way: **To believe in God** is to love Him by believing, to go toward Him by believing, to cling to Him by believing, and to be incorporated into His members <note>The "members" refers to the Body of Christ, the Church.</note> by believing. ¶ For this reason, in order for faith to tend perfectly toward God, the addition of charity <term>charity: "caritas," the theological virtue of divine love</term> is required, which directs it toward the love of God; and this is the first distinction. ¶ Toward the working of good; this is the second. ¶ Toward the enduring of evil; this is the third. ¶ Toward the love of neighbor; this is the fourth. ¶ To faith is also attributed the working of miracles. ¶ For faith is the first virtue that elevates the intellect above itself, capturing it in obedience to Jesus Christ, and therefore it deserves, by way of fitness <note>original: "de congruo," a theological term for a reward given out of God's kindness rather than a strict legal obligation</note> before God, to obtain a difficult work to which the capacity of the observer does not extend. ¶ And this is what is touched upon in the definition of a miracle: ¶ A **miracle** is a difficult and unusual work, standing above the hope and the power of the one who wonders at it.

## ¶ On the relationship of the Creed to the Articles [of Faith].
### ¶ Chapter Thirty-Eight.

# The Hundred-Fold Dialogue of Saint Bonaventure
<note>original: "Centiloquii sancti Bonauenture." The title refers to a work containing one hundred sections or "sayings" on theological matters.</note>

The following concerns the contents of the Creed <term>Creed: from the Latin "symbolum," a formal statement of Christian beliefs</term>, about which three things should be briefly noted: namely, the distinction of the Creeds, the sufficiency of the things to be believed, and the essence of the articles of faith.

¶ There are three Creeds of the Christian faith: namely, the **Apostles' Creed**; the **Nicene Creed**, which three hundred and eighteen fathers established <note>The Council of Nicaea in 325 AD was attended by approximately 318 bishops.</note>; and the **Athanasian Creed**.

¶ The principal reason for the establishment of the first was the certain tradition of the faith.
¶ The reason for the second was the certain proof or public proclamation of the truth handed down.
¶ The reason for the third was the eradication of burgeoning errors.

¶ The first is called the "Greater" <note>original: "maius"</note>, not because of the number of things contained within it, but because of the greatness of its founders or authors.
¶ And it is recited in a **low voice**, because it was composed at a time when the truth had not yet been made public, or because it is for the security of personal knowledge.
¶ It is also recited at the beginning of the day and at the end—namely, at **Prime** <term>Prime: the first hour of the monastic day, usually at sunrise</term> and at **Compline** <term>Compline: the final prayer service of the day before sleep</term>—appropriately signifying the twofold confession of faith that is necessary: namely, at the beginning of the Church and at its end; or at the beginning of baptismal rebirth and at the finality of bodily death.
¶ As a testimony of a triumphant way of life.
¶ Or in prosperity and adversity.
¶ Or in acting and in suffering.
¶ Or because faith is a **shield** against temptations by day and against temptations by night.

¶ The second [the Nicene Creed], because it was issued for the sake of proclaiming the handed-down truth on Sundays and solemn feasts, is **sung in a loud voice** because of the larger gathering of the people; and this occurs after the Gospel, because it is a brief collection of its [the Gospel’s] teachings, and before Communion, because it is a worthy preparation for it.

¶ The third [the Athanasian Creed] is recited when the night is over, at the first hour of the day, because it was issued at a time when the truth was being shaken by heretics, finally prevailing against them.

¶ The sufficiency of the articles contained in the Apostles' Creed can be assigned in two ways: namely, on the part of the efficient cause—that is, the Apostles—who, for the purpose of establishing...

<summary>This page explains the historical and liturgical reasons for the three Christian Creeds (Apostles', Nicene, and Athanasian), detailing when and how they are recited to protect and proclaim the faith.</summary>
<keywords>Apostles' Creed, Nicene Creed, Athanasian Creed, Liturgy, Prime, Compline, Articles of Faith, Council of Nicaea</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the Creeds, specifically detailing the tradition that the twelve Apostles each contributed a specific phrase (article) to the "Apostles' Creed." It then begins a Scholastic analysis of the "sufficiency" or completeness of these articles, dividing them between those concerning God's divinity and those concerning His humanity.</meta>

### Part Three

...placed the individual particles <note>original: "particulas," referring to the separate clauses or articles of the Creed</note> of the Creed. **Peter** says: "I believe in God the Father almighty, creator of heaven and earth." **Andrew** added: "And in Jesus Christ, his only son, our Lord." Then **John**: "Who was conceived by the Holy Spirit, born of the Virgin Mary." Then **James the Greater**: "Suffered under Pontius Pilate, was crucified, died, and was buried." Then **Thomas**: "He descended into hell, on the third day he rose again from the dead." Then **James the Lesser**: "He ascended into heaven, and sits at the right hand of God the Father almighty." Then **Philip**: "From there he will come to judge the living and the dead." Then **Bartholomew**: "I believe in the Holy Spirit." Then **Matthew**: "The holy catholic church." And the meaning is: I believe in the Holy Spirit who sanctifies the universal church <note>The author clarifies that the Holy Spirit is the active agent who makes the Church holy</note>. Then **Simon the Canaanite**: "The communion of saints, the forgiveness of sins." And the meaning is that the forgiveness of sins occurs in the communion of the living or of the sacraments <note>original: "communionem... sacramentorum." This implies that forgiveness is found through the shared life and rituals of the church community</note>. Then **Jude Thaddeus**: "The resurrection of the body" <note>original: "carnis resurrectionem," literally "the resurrection of the flesh"</note>. And the meaning is: I believe that God will raise up the flesh. Then **Matthias**: "Life everlasting. Amen." <note>This tradition, assigning each of the twelve articles of the Creed to one of the twelve Apostles, was a popular medieval teaching used to emphasize the Creed's apostolic authority.</note>

The "sufficiency" <term>sufficiency: in Scholastic logic, this refers to whether a list is complete and covers all necessary points</term> [of the Creed] can also be assigned from the perspective of the "material cause" <term>material cause: a philosophical term referring to the subject matter or "stuff" being discussed—in this case, the specific articles of faith</term>, namely the articles themselves. All the articles are about God, according to the nature in which He exists or according to the nature which He assumed <note>Referring to the "nature he assumed" through the Incarnation, when God became human</note>. 

And thus some articles pertain to the **divinity**, and some to the **humanity**. Those that pertain to the divinity either regard the divine nature in itself—and thus there is the first article concerning singularity when it is said: "I believe in God" and not in gods—or they regard the three persons of the divine essence. 

And thus, regarding the **Father**, it is to believe in "God the Father almighty." Regarding the **Son**, it is to believe in "Jesus Christ his only Son, our Lord." Regarding the **Holy Spirit**, it is to believe in the "Holy Spirit." Or...

# ¶ **The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure**

<note>original: "Centiloquium," a common title for works divided into one hundred short sections</note>

...regard the operations of the divine essence, one of which is in the conferment of **nature**. And this has its own article: **I** believe in the creator of heaven and earth. **Another** is in the conferment of **grace**, and this is sanctification, having its own article: **The holy** catholic church, the communion of saints, and the forgiveness of sins. **And** the meaning is: **I believe** that the forgiveness of sins occurs in the catholic church, where there is the communion of saints—either in the masculine sense <note>referring to holy people</note> or "of holy things" in the neuter sense, that is, of the sacraments. **Another** is in the conferment of **glory**, and this is the glorious resurrection, having its own article: The resurrection of the **flesh** and life eternal, amen. **There** are therefore seven articles accepted in respect to divinity.

**Seven** are also accepted in respect to the humanity of Christ, according to the seven principal acts which the Lord ordained for our restoration <note>term: "reparation" (restoration); the Scholastic idea that Christ's actions "repaired" the damage done to humanity by the Fall</note>. **The first** was to be incarnated, having its own article: who was conceived of the Holy Spirit. **The second** was to be born, having its own article: born of the **Virgin Mary**. **The third** was to pay the price for us, having its own article: suffered under Pontius Pilate, was crucified, died, and was buried. **The fourth** was to visit the lower regions, having its own article: he descended into hell. **The fifth** was to despoil hell <note>original: "spoliare," referring to the "Harrowing of Hell" where Christ rescues the souls of the righteous</note>, having its own article: on the third day he rose again from the dead. **The sixth** was to transcend the heavens, having its own article: **He ascended** into heaven, sits at the right hand of God the Father almighty. **The seventh** will be to come to judgment, having its own article: **From where** he will come to judge the living and the dead. **And** thus there are seven articles in respect to the humanity of Christ.

An article is **defined** by **Richard** <note>Richard of Saint Victor, a prominent 12th-century theologian</note> thus: **An article** is an indivisible truth binding us to belief. By **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar whose definitions were standard in the Middle Ages</note> it is defined thus: **An article** is the perception of divine truth tending toward itself. The **first** definition does not only apply to the articles...

### ¶ Chapter 39: On Hope.

**I**t follows next to consider hope, regarding which we can examine four things: namely, its definition, its multiple meanings <note>original: *equivocationem*; refers to how one word can have different senses</note>, the excellence of its act, and its distinction from faith. Hope, insofar as it is the name of an emotion <note>Latin: *affectio*</note>, is defined by the philosopher <note>referring to Aristotle</note>: 

> Hope is the expectation <note>original: *suspitio*; literally a "looking up" or anticipation</note> of a future good. 

Insofar as it is the name of a virtue, it is identified by three definitions. The first is this:

> Hope is the virtue by which spiritual and eternal goods are hoped for—that is, they are awaited with confidence.

¶ These spiritual goods can be understood as the forgiveness of sins, the grace of merits, and the glory of rewards; so it may be said there is a certain hope of forgiveness, a certain hope of grace, and a certain hope of glory. The second definition is this:

> Hope is the certain expectation of future happiness <note><term>beatitudo</term>: the supreme state of blessedness and union with God</note> coming from the grace of God and preceding merits. 

The third is from Augustine, as follows:

> Hope is the virtue by which one presumes that they will arrive at that which they believe. 

¶ However, the name "hope" is used in many ways. For sometimes it names an emotion, according to what Boethius says:

> Drive away joys, drive away fear; put hope to flight, and let no grief be present. <note>A famous quote from Boethius’s *The Consolation of Philosophy* regarding the mastery of emotions.</note>

¶ Sometimes it names the habit of the virtue, and in that case, hope does not arise from merits, but rather merits arise from it. ¶ Sometimes it names the act of the virtue, and then hope arises from the consideration of merits. For no one rightly hopes that they will reach happiness unless they serve God or intend to serve Him. ¶ Sometimes it names the state of the virtue, and then hope has its origin from accompanying merits. According to that passage in Romans 5: 

¶ "Testing produces hope"—that is, certainty, according to the Gloss <note>The *Glossa Ordinaria*, the standard medieval biblical commentary</note>. This certainty is reached through good works and, most especially, through the endurance of hardships. Sometimes it names the goal <note>term: *terminus*</note> of the virtue...

<meta>catchword: goal</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the theological virtue of Hope, providing several definitions from classical and patristic sources like Aristotle, Augustine, and Boethius. It distinguishes between hope as a human emotion and hope as a divinely-infused virtue linked to grace and good works.</summary>
<keywords>Hope, Virtue, Aristotle, Augustine, Boethius, Beatitude, Grace, Merits, Scholasticism, Romans 5</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the virtue of Hope, specifically detailing the different ways the term is understood in relation to "merits" (good deeds or spiritual worthiness). It then transitions to a comparison between Faith and Hope before beginning Chapter 40 on Charity.</meta>

...or the object, that is, the thing hoped for—namely, eternal happiness <note>original: *beatitudinem sempiternā*</note>. In this sense, hope arises from grace and preceding merits. Thus, hope as a virtue, according to its first meaning, precedes merits; according to the last meaning, it follows them; and according to the two intermediate meanings, it both follows and precedes them.

¶ Hope also possesses excellence in its act. For, having the **irascible power** <term>irascibilis: the faculty of the soul responsible for vigor, courage, and the pursuit of difficult goals</term> as its subject, it presupposes a habit of virtues over which it presides like a queen. 

¶ For it belongs to this irascible power to detest the evils of fault <note>sin or moral failing</note>, to endure the evils of punishment, to confront terrors, and to be lifted up toward difficult things. 
*   To detest the faults of others, it requires **zealous anger**.
*   To detest one’s own faults, it requires **penance**. 
*   To endure the evils of punishment, it requires **patience**. 
*   To confront terrors, it possesses **fortitude**. 

And then, for the expectation of eternal happiness, it is lifted up through **hope**, which has been so generously promised.

¶ The difference between faith and hope is assigned in this manner: 
1. Faith concerns both good and evil things <note>e.g., believing in both Heaven and Hell</note>, while hope concerns only good things. 
2. Faith concerns things past, present, and future, whereas hope concerns only future things. 
3. Furthermore, faith concerns things belonging to oneself and to others <note>one can believe in the salvation of others</note>, while hope concerns only those things belonging to oneself.

### ¶ Chapter 40: On Charity.

It follows next to consider charity. Regarding this, you ought to consider its definition, its distinction, its perfection, the sufficiency of things to be loved, and the requirements of its order. 

Charity is defined in three ways. First, in relation to its object, it is defined in the *Sentences* by the Master <note>Peter Lombard, whose *Sentences* was the standard textbook of the Middle Ages</note>: 

> Charity is the love by which God is loved for His own sake, and one’s neighbor for the sake of God. 

In relation to its own act, which is to live and to cling <note>to God</note>, it is defined by Augustine as follows:

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of Hope to the third part of the treatise, which focuses on Charity. It picks up from the previous page's comparison of faith and hope, where it was noted that hope concerns only one's own future good, whereas faith can concern others.</meta>

# ¶ Part Three

Charity is the most upright affection of the soul joining us to God; or, charity is the virtue through which we see God because we desire to enjoy Him; or, Charity is the life coupling the lover with the beloved. ¶ When compared to the habit joined to it, it is defined by the Apostle in 1 Timothy 1: "Charity is the end of the commandment from a pure heart, a good conscience, and faith unfeigned." 

¶ A distinction is made between love <note>original: *amorem*</note>, esteem <note>original: *dilectionem*</note>, and charity <note>original: *caritatem*</note>. ¶ For love belongs to both rational and irrational creatures. ¶ Truly, esteem and charity are said to suit only rational beings. ¶ Likewise, a difference is found between esteem and charity. ¶ For esteem <term>dilectio: derived from 'dis-electio,' implying a choice or election from among options</term> belongs to rational beings whether they are just or unjust. Charity, however, belongs only to the just. 

¶ There is, moreover, in charity a perfection and spiritual dignity beyond the other virtues. ¶ For since it is the **form of the virtues** <term>forma virtutum: a scholastic concept meaning charity is the essential "soul" or life-giving force that makes other virtues, like courage or temperance, truly meritorious and directed toward God</term>, it cannot be "unformed" <note>meaning it cannot exist without its spiritual life or grace</note> for three reasons: 

1.  One is on the part of the object. ¶ It has the same thing as its object and its end <note>namely, God</note>. ¶ For it is inseparable from its end, by reason of which it is the perfection regarding merit within the virtue. 
2.  The second is on the part of the subject. ¶ For charity itself is the rule that rectifies the will; once the will is made right, the whole soul lives rightly. 
3.  The third is on the part of the cause. For it possesses a likeness to the Holy Spirit from whom it comes—who is also called Charity or Love—and from whom it is inseparable; and therefore, it can never be "unformed." 

¶ Now, according to Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian for medieval thought</note>, there are four things to be loved in order through charity:
*   One which is above us, namely **God**. 
*   The second which **we are** [ourselves]. 
*   The third which is beside us, namely our **neighbor**. 
*   The fourth which is below us, namely the **human body**. 

¶ The sufficiency of these four is understood in this way: To love from charity is to desire the highest good for the beloved. ¶

<summary>This page defines Charity, distinguishing it from general love and rational esteem, and identifies it as the "form" of all other virtues. It also outlines the Augustinian hierarchy of love: God, self, neighbor, and the body.</summary>

<keywords>Charity, love, esteem, St. Augustine, Holy Spirit, virtue, four objects of love, theological ethics</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the four objects of Charity—God, ourselves, our neighbors, and our bodies—before transitioning to a new section on the Cardinal Virtues, beginning with Prudence.</meta>

Therefore, one loves out of charity by wishing the highest good: either for God himself (the first part); or for oneself (the second); or for one’s fellow <note>original: *sibi simili*, meaning one’s neighbor</note> (the third); or for that which is inherent to oneself <note>referring to the physical body</note> (the fourth). 

God is to be loved above all things. ¶ First and for His own sake. We ourselves are loved second: for God and under God. ¶ Our neighbor is loved third, just as we love ourselves and for the same reason we love ourselves; that is, with both affection and in action, and toward the same end as ourselves—namely, toward grace in the present and toward glory in the future. In this, we love them as ourselves because it is in the pursuit of the good; and we love them for the same reason as ourselves: because it is for the sake of God. ¶ Fourth, we love our own body as a good that is "below" us and our neighbor, being a less principal good.

To carry these out, we are given a single habit <term>habitus: a stable disposition or quality of the soul that inclines a person to act in a certain way</term> of charity, and a double commandment <note>to love God and to love one's neighbor</note> upon which hangs the whole of the New and Old Testaments. ¶ However, Ambrose <note>St. Ambrose, the 4th-century Bishop of Milan</note> assigns another order of loving besides the one mentioned above, suggesting that: ¶ First we should love God, second ourselves, third our parents, then our children and siblings, then those of our household, and finally our enemies.

### ¶ On Prudence

<margin>Chapter 41</margin>
It follows next to look at the **cardinal virtues** <term>cardinal virtues: the four "hinge" virtues—Prudence, Justice, Fortitude, and Temperance—upon which a moral life is built</term>. And first, concerning **Prudence**. ¶ Regarding this, three things are to be briefly examined: namely, its definition, the collection of its types, and the variation of its acts.

Prudence is defined or described by Augustine in his book *On the Customs of the Church*: ¶ "Prudence is the love of those things by which one is helped toward God, sagaciously distinguishing them from those things by which one is hindered." <note>The original Latin *se ligans* in the OCR likely refers to *secernens* (distinguishing) in Augustine's original text.</note> ¶ According to Gregory: ¶ "Prudence is the knowledge of things to be avoided and things to be desired." ¶ Tully <note>the Roman orator Marcus Tullius Cicero</note> says the following in his *Rhetoric*: ¶ "Prudence is the knowledge of good things, bad things, and things that are neither." ¶ From the same author in *On Duties*: ¶ "Prudence is the knowledge of things to be imitated..."

## ¶ Third part.

and the knowledge of things to be [sought]. <note>This sentence continues from the previous page's definition of prudence</note> From the same author in the book *On the Rest of the Mind* thus: ¶ **Prudence** is the knowledge of good and bad things, involving the love of the former and the rejection of the latter. ¶ According to **Tully** <note>original: *Tullius*, referring to the Roman statesman and philosopher Cicero</note> in his works on rhetoric, the parts or species of prudence are three: namely **memory, intelligence, and foresight**. ¶ **Memory** is that through which the mind looks back at those things which have been. **Intelligence** is that through which it perceives those things which are. ¶ **Foresight** <note>original: *providentia*</note> is that through which something in the future is seen before it has actually happened. ¶ **Seneca** <note>the Roman Stoic philosopher</note> seems to agree with **Tully** regarding these three, saying: ¶ **If you are prudent, let your mind be distributed across three times. ¶ Order the present. Foresee the future. And remember the past.** ¶ **According to the teaching** of the philosophers: ¶ **Prudence** is divided into four species: namely foresight, circumspection, caution, and teachability. ¶ **Foresight** is the present awareness of future things, considering the outcome. **Circumspection** is being on guard against opposing vices. ¶ **Caution** is the ability to distinguish vices from virtues, while preferring the appearance of virtues. **Teachability** <term>docilitas: here used as the wisdom required to instruct the unlearned, as well as the readiness to learn</term> is the prudence of instructing the ignorant. According to **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, the influential 5th-century theologian</note> in the book *On the Spirit and the Soul*, prudence has many functions. ¶ **For prudence** knows what it ought to do. ¶ **Prudence** is knowing what one is capable of. ¶ **Prudence** is found in making choices. ¶ **Prudence** is to desire nothing that brings regret, and to wish to do nothing except what is right. ¶ According to **Macrobius** <note>a 5th-century Roman provincial and Neoplatonist philosopher</note> ¶ It belongs to **Prudence** to look down upon this world and all things in it through the contemplation of divine things, and to direct every thought of the mind toward the divine alone. ¶ It belongs to **Prudence** to direct everything one thinks and does according to the standard of reason, and to wish to do nothing except what is right; and in human actions—

...to provide [for actions] <note>The Latin ends in -bus, likely completing a word from the previous page such as "finibus" (ends) or "actibus" (actions).</note> as if by the judgments of reason. ¶ Within **Prudence** are reason, understanding, circumspection, foresight, teachability, and recognition. ¶ Likewise, in a person so purified, prudence is divine—not as if preferring one thing over another in a choice, but simply knowing the truth. ¶ It is also the duty of prudence to weigh future things based on the past and the present. ¶ Wherefore **Boethius** <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose work bridged classical and medieval thought</note> says in the book *On Consolation*: **"It is not enough to look at what is placed before the eyes; prudence measures the outcomes of things."** <note>original: *De consolatione philosophiae*</note> ¶ The duty of circumspection is to hold the middle ground between opposing vices through diligent consideration.

### On temperance.

<margin>¶ Chapter 42.</margin>
There follows the subject of **Temperance**, considered in three respects: namely, its definition, the assignment of its species, and the variation of its acts. ¶ Temperance is described by **Tully** <note>original: *Tulius*, referring to the Roman statesman and philosopher Cicero</note> in his first book on *Rhetoric*: ¶ **Temperance** is the firm and moderate mastery of reason over lust and other improper impulses of the mind. ¶ Also by the same author in the book *On Duties* <note>original: *De officiis*</note>: ¶ **Temperance** is the dominion of reason over lust and other troublesome movements. ¶ From **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo</note> in his book *On the Customs of the Church*: ¶ **Temperance** is love preserving itself whole and uncorrupted for God. ¶ From **Macrobius** <note>A 5th-century Roman Neoplatonist author</note>: ¶ **Temperance** is to desire nothing for which one must repent, to exceed the law of moderation in nothing, and to tame desire under the yoke of reason. ¶ From **Augustine** in the book *On Free Will*: ¶ **Temperance** is an affection restraining and withholding the appetite from those things which are basely desired. ¶ The parts of temperance according to **Tully** in his first book on *Rhetoric* are three: namely, **continence, clemency, and modesty**. ¶ **Continence** is that through which desire... <note>The text breaks off here and continues on the following page</note>

### ¶ Part Three.

k iij.

[... integrity] <note>The word fragment "-tas" likely completes "integritas" (integrity) or "pietas" (piety) from the preceding page.</note> is governed by the guidance of counsel. **C**lemency is the virtue through which the hatred of the mind, which is rashly stirred up against someone, is released through kindness. This is according to **Tully** <note>original: *Tulius*, the common medieval name for the Roman statesman and philosopher Cicero</note>. **A**ccording to **Seneca** <note>A Roman Stoic philosopher</note>, however: **C**lemency is the restraint of the mind when one has the power to take revenge, or the leniency of a superior toward an inferior in determining punishments. **M**odesty, according to Tully, is that through which the shame of integrity <note>or "a sense of honor"</note> procures a pure and stable authority. **A**uthority in this description is called a stable, persevering seriousness <note>original: *gravitas*, a key Roman virtue denoting dignity and depth of character</note>. **A**ccording to the doctrine of the philosophers: The **parts of temperance** are those which rule over foul impulses; these are: modesty, shame, abstinence, chastity, moderation, frugality, sobriety, and purity. **M**odesty is that which causes our appearance, movement, and every occupation to stand beyond deficiency and short of excess. **S**hame <note>original: *verecundia*</note> is to preserve integrity in gesture and word. **A**bstinence is integrity; moderation, frugality, and sobriety restrain the nourishment of food. **A**bstinentia <note>original: *Abstinentia*</note> is not to anticipate the established time for dining. **I**ntegrity <note>original: *honestas*</note> is neither to seek more lavish foods nor to put effort into elaborate preparation. **M**oderation is to call back the excessive appetite for foods by the command of reason. **F**rugality <note>original: *parcitas*</note> is not to exceed the measure of refreshment. **S**obriety is to restrain excess in drink. **I**ts duty is to keep away the evils of drunkenness. **C**hastity is abstinence from all illicit sexual intercourse. **I**t is threefold: namely, conjugal, widowed, and virginal. **V**irginity (according to **Augustine**) is the meditation of perpetual incorruption in corruptible flesh. **P**urity <note>original: *pudicitia*</note> is to tame petulance with the guidance of reason. The **acts of temperance** (according to Augustine) are not to presume in the face of prosperity that which cannot... <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence at the end of the page.</note>

### ¶ On Fortitude.

It **follows** to look at fortitude with respect to its definition, the classification of its types <term>species: the specific sub-categories or forms a virtue takes</term>, and the variation of its acts. **Fortitude** is described thus by **Tully** <note>original: *Tulio*, referring to Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman</note> in the first *Rhetoric*: **Fortitude** is the deliberate undertaking of dangers and the enduring of labors. **Also by the same author** in the second *Rhetoric*: **Fortitude** is the seeking of great things and the contempt for lowly <note>meaning trivial or unworthy</note> things, and the enduring of labors with a spirit of humility. **Also by the same**: Fortitude is the rational confrontation of terrifying things with a constant endurance of them. **According to Aristotle**: Fortitude is the virtue that acts upon difficult passions for the sake of the good. **Augustine**, in *On the Morals of the Church*: Fortitude is love enduring all things easily for the sake of that which is loved. **Fortitude** is also described in another way: **Fortitude** is the firmness of the soul against the troubles of the world. **¶ And** it is taken from **Matthew 9**: *There were four thousand,* etc. <note>original: *Erant quatuor milia*. While the miracle of the four thousand is found in Matthew 15, medieval commentators often linked this number to the four cardinal virtues that "feed" the soul.</note> **¶ The parts** or types of fortitude according to Tully are four, namely: magnificence, confidence, patience, and perseverance. **Macrobius** identifies seven parts of fortitude, namely: magnanimity, trust, security, magnificence, constancy, patience, and firmness. **According to the doctrine of some**: Fortitude is the virtue that restrains the onslaught of adversity. Its parts are whatever achieve this, which are: magnanimity, trust, security, constancy, patience, magni... <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely continuing as "magnificentia" (magnificence) on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page defines the cardinal virtue of Fortitude (Courage) using a synthesis of authorities including Cicero, Aristotle, and Saint Augustine. It categorizes Fortitude as both the strength to endure hardship and the rational courage to face danger for a higher good.</summary>

# ¶ Of justice <margin>¶ Chapter 47</margin>

It follows to look at justice with regard to its definition, the classification of its types, and the progression of its actions. Justice is defined by **Tully** <note>original: *Tullio*, referring to Marcus Tullius Cicero</note> in the first book of his *Rhetoric* as follows: Justice is a habit of the mind which, having preserved the common utility, renders to each their own dignity. ¶ **Macrobius** <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher</note> says: it is the role of justice to preserve for each person what is their own. **Augustine** says in his book *On Free Will*: What should I say justice is, except the virtue by which his own is given to each? On the commentary for Matthew 5, regarding the verse "Blessed are they who hunger and thirst after justice": Justice is giving to each their due—to oneself, to one's neighbor, and to God. **Seneca** <note>A Roman Stoic philosopher</note> asks: what is justice except a silent agreement of nature discovered for the assistance of those in misfortune? <note>original: *malorum*, which can mean "evils" or "the unfortunate"</note>. The same author says that justice is divine law and the bond of human society. **Isidore** <note>St. Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar and encyclopedist</note> says: Justice is the order and equity by which a person is well-ordered with respect to every single thing. **Anselm** <note>St. Anselm of Canterbury, an 11th-century theologian</note> says: Justice is the uprightness of the will <note>original: *rectitudo voluntatis*</note> preserved for its own sake—that is, the uprightness is kept for the sake of the uprightness itself. ¶ It is also defined otherwise in this way: Justice is the virtue that preserves human life and community. **Augustine**, in *On the Morals of the Church*: Justice is love serving only the beloved <note>referring to God</note> and therefore rightly ruling over the other things that are subject to man. ¶ By the **Masters** <note>referring to the Scholastic theologians, using the standard definition from Roman law</note> it is defined thus: Justice is a constant and perpetual will giving to each person their own right. ¶ Justice is divided by some into **severity** and **liberality**. **Severity** is the virtue restraining wrongdoing by the due punishment. **Liberality** is the virtue that bestows benefits. This virtue is called "kindness" <note>original: *benignitas*</note> according to the inward intention, and "beneficence" <note>original: *benificencia*</note> according to the outward effect. And this virtue consists...

...through which diligent service and devoted worship are bestowed upon those joined by blood and to those well-disposed toward their homeland <note>original: *patrie*. **G**ratitude <note>original: *Gratia*</note> is the virtue in which the memory of friendships and the kindnesses of another, and the will to repay them, is contained.

**V**engeance <note>original: *Vindicatio*</note> is the virtue through which force or injury, and indeed everything that is hidden, is repelled by defending or avenging.

**R**everence <note>original: *Observantia*</note> is the virtue by which people of some dignity are deemed worthy of a certain devotion and honor.

**T**ruth <note>original: *Veritas*</note> is the virtue through which things that are, or were before, or will be in the future, are spoken without being changed.

**I**nnocence <note>original: *Innocentia*</note> is the purity of the mind that shrinks from any gathering of injury. **B**y this virtue the gods are pleased <note>original: *dij placentur*. Bonaventure is quoting Cicero's classical definition here, which accounts for the plural 'gods'.</note>.

**F**riendship <note>original: *Amicitia*</note> is a good will toward someone for the sake of the person who is loved, with an equal intention from the one who is loved. **I**ts duty is to want the same things and to reject the same things; to correct another's faults in secret, and to praise them openly.

**C**oncord <note>original: *Concordia*</note> is the virtue that voluntarily joins fellow citizens and compatriots in the same law and way of living together.

**M**ercy <note>original: *Misericordia*</note> is the virtue through which the soul is moved by the calamity of those who suffer. **T**his virtue considers nothing human as foreign to itself; it counts the losses and benefits of others as its own.

**D**iscipline <note>original: *Disciplina*</note> (according to Cyprian <note>Saint Cyprian of Carthage, a 3rd-century bishop</note>) is the orderly correction of behavior and the regular observation of the ways of the fathers who came before.

**A**ccording to Hugh of Saint Victor <note>A famous 12th-century theologian and mystic</note>, discipline is a good and honorable preservation, for which it is not enough to refrain from doing evil, but rather it strives to appear blameless in all things that it does well through every circumstance.

**T**he same author says discipline is the orderly movement of all the limbs and a decent arrangement in every posture and action.

**B**ernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux, a leading 12th-century Cistercian monk</note> says: Discipline bows the neck, lowers the eyebrows, composes the face, binds the eyes, [and restrains] loud laughter <note>original: *cachi...*, likely *cachinnum* (unrestrained laughter). The text cuts off here.</note>.

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

<note>Rubricator's guide letter 's' for missing initial 'S'</note> ...special sufficiency, singular consistency, and general differentiation. ¶ The special sufficiency of the aforementioned habits is understood as follows. ¶ The **gifts of the Holy Spirit** are specifically or principally intended to set in motion the powers of the soul in action <note>original: *expediendas*; meaning to free the soul's powers or make them ready for work</note>. ¶ Now, we must be set in motion concerning the avoidance of evil, and this is the **gift of fear**. ¶ Likewise, regarding the performance of good, and this occurs in two ways: ¶ For it is either a good of necessity, toward which we are set in motion through the **gift of piety**, the guiding rule of which is the **gift of knowledge**. ¶ Or it is a good of supererogation <term>supererogation: performing moral actions that go beyond what is required by duty or command</term>, toward which we are set in motion by the **gift of fortitude**, the rule of which is the **gift of counsel**. It is also necessary for us to find rest in the highest good, both regarding the understanding of truth and regarding the affection for the good. ¶ The first is achieved through the **gift of understanding**; ¶ the second through the **gift of wisdom**, in which there is rest. ¶ For a clearer evidence of these gifts, one must consider the consistency of the actions of each one. ¶ Note first, therefore, that **wisdom**, commonly understood, refers to a general knowledge of things divine and human (according to **Augustine** and **the Philosopher** <note>Aristotle was almost universally referred to as "the Philosopher" in medieval scholarship</note>), who says that the wise person is one who knows all things. ¶ Less commonly, it refers to the knowledge of external things (1 Corinthians 12). ¶ To others, the word "wisdom" means the knowledge of eternal things; and properly speaking, it refers to the knowledge by which God is worshiped through faith, hope, and charity. ¶ In **Job** chapter fifteen <note>original: *Job quindecimo*; while the manuscript cites chapter 15, this definition is famously found in Job 28:28</note>: ¶ **Piety itself is wisdom.** ¶ More properly, it names an experiential knowledge of God, and in this way, it is one of the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit, the act of which consists in tasting the sweetness of God. ¶ This act is indeed partly cognitive and partly affective, so that it begins in knowledge and is completed in affection <note>original: *consummatur*; implies being brought to perfection or fulfillment</note>. ¶ Also

<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of the Seven Gifts of the Holy Spirit. ## ¶ Part Three

...we must find rest in the highest good through the insights of virtue, which constitutes the **gift of understanding**, and through the affection for goodness, which is the **gift of wisdom**. **Knowledge** <term>knowledge: translating *scientia*, which in the medieval university referred to a structured body of certain knowledge rather than just modern physical science</term> is also defined in three ways. First, there is a form of knowledge that resides in the purely speculative intellect, built upon the principles of reason; this is "acquired knowledge," otherwise known as **human philosophy**. ¶ Second, there is a kind of knowledge residing in the intellect when it is influenced by practical results, founded upon the principles of **natural law** <note>original: *iuris naturalis*; the moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason</note>. It directs one toward a right and honorable life; this is the knowledge associated with the virtue of **prudence**. ¶ Third, there is a knowledge that resides in the intellect when it is moved by faith and directs one toward good works. It is founded upon the principles of faith and takes its origin from the spiritual gifts of grace. This is the **gift of knowledge**. Its primary function is to provide guidance in our actions according to the requirements of the rule of faith and the **imitation of Christ**. ¶ Concerning the **gift of understanding**, consider that its action consists in the contemplation not only of the Creator's essence <note>original: *creatricis essencie*</note> ¶ but also in the contemplation of spiritual beings. ¶ A certain knowledge of God as the Truth <note>original: *sub ratione veri*</note> is attained in three ways. First, through **simple assent**, which belongs to faith, causing one to agree to the truth. Second, through the **aid of reason**; this is the **gift of understanding**, whose role is to use reason to grasp what is already believed. ¶ Third, through **simple intuition**, which corresponds to **purity of heart**, whose function is to "see God." However, for the purpose of understanding things believed, a person is not always assisted by reasons derived from earthly circumstances.

### ¶ Concerning the gift of counsel.

Regarding the **gift of counsel**, note that "counsel" <note>original: *consilium*</note> sometimes refers to the actual act of deliberating. At other times, it refers to the final goal or conclusion toward which that deliberation is directed...

<meta>This page continues the systematic breakdown of the Seven Gifts of the Holy Spirit. # The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure,

...the act <note>original: *le actus*; the text likely begins mid-sentence referring to the "habit of counsel"</note>, sometimes signifies a habit that facilitates the act and directs the soul to choose things to be pursued and to avoid things to be shunned. However, a habit that directs one regarding choices that are of necessity belongs to **prudence**, which provides guidance in such matters—though less excellently than the **gift of knowledge**. If, however, the choices involve matters of great weight and difficulty, it belongs to the **gift of counsel**. Indeed, a person can be directed in their choice either according to the rule of **natural law** <note>original: *iuris naturalis*</note>, which is the domain of **prudence**, or according to the rule of **divine law** <note>original: *iuris diuini*</note>, which is the domain of the **gift of counsel**. The purpose of the latter is to direct the soul in choosing what to follow and fleeing what is to be avoided.

Regarding the **gift of fortitude**, it must be held that its act consists in the endurance of suffering and the undertaking of arduous tasks. A person can undertake difficult things or suffer through passions in two ways: either for the sake of preserving justice and honor—and thus it pertains to the **virtue of fortitude**—or for the sake of the perfect **imitation of Christ**, and thus it pertains to the **gift of fortitude**. The first arises from the dictates of natural law. The second arises from the dictates of divine law. Therefore, the act of the gift of fortitude is observed in the prompt and ready endurance of suffering.

Regarding the **gift of piety**, hold that piety is directed toward God—where it is the same as **latria** <term>latria: from the Greek *latreia*, referring to the supreme worship and service due to God alone</term>—and toward one's parents, and in this sense, it is a part of **justice** according to the rule of natural law and the obligation of necessity. It is also directed toward one's neighbor, who is marked with the image of God, and in this sense, it is a gift of the Holy Spirit.

But note that the **virtue of mercy** <note>original: *misericordie virtus*</note> looks upon the neighbor in terms of a shared nature and the necessity of their misery, following the dictate of natural law according to the rule of prudence. **Piety**, however, looks upon the neighbor as the divine image, following the dictate...

<summary>Bonaventure distinguishes between moral virtues (based on natural law) and spiritual gifts (based on divine law), specifically focusing on Counsel, Fortitude, and Piety as means of imitating Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Counsel, Fortitude, Piety, Natural Law, Divine Law, Prudence, Imitation of Christ, Mercy, Latria</keywords>

### Part Three.

...of divine law <note>original: *iurisdiuini*</note> according to the rule of good knowledge. ¶ Therefore, by the **gift of piety**, a person is benevolent toward every creature of God marked with His image. ¶ Such benevolence, according to the circumstances of place and time, sometimes issues forth as a feeling of compassion. ¶ At other times, it issues forth as a beneficial deed. Therefore, the primary act of **piety** is to make a person benevolent toward any neighbor, insofar as that neighbor bears the image of the Creator. ¶ Regarding the **gift of fear**, hold that its general act is to "recoil from what is arduous" <note>In medieval philosophy, "arduous" refers to things that are high, difficult, or overwhelming.</note>. ¶ And all fear can be defined generally in this way: ¶ Fear is a recoiling from something arduous as if from something superior. ¶ If, however, that arduous thing is in the category of good, then it is a recoiling with reverence. ¶ But if it is in the category of evil, then it is a recoiling with flight <note>meaning avoidance or turning away</note>. ¶ Damascene <note>original: *Damascenus*; referring to John of Damascus, an 8th-century theologian</note> says that fear is the "expectation of a future evil." ¶ Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo</note>, in defining fear, says: "Fear is the flight of the mind lest it lose what it possesses." The **gift of fear** looks toward three things: namely, punishment, so that it may flee it, and thus it is **servile fear** <term>servile fear: the dread of punishment, like that of a slave (servus) fearing a master's lash</term>. ¶ Offense, so that it may guard against it, and thus it is **initial fear** <term>initial fear: a "beginning" stage of spiritual fear that looks both at the punishment and the moral offense committed</term>, which has one eye on the punishment and one on the offense. ¶ Supreme Majesty, so that the soul may reverently submit to it; ¶ in this way it is **filial fear** <term>filial fear: the fear of a child (filius) who loves their father and dreads only the loss of that relationship</term>, which has a twofold focus. One is in shunning offense lest one be separated from God. ¶ The other is more primary: in revering God; and this fear remains even in the "blessed" <note>those who have reached heaven</note>. ¶ Furthermore, six different types of fear are distinguished according to the three principles from which they originate. ¶ For fear takes its origin from nature, and thus it is **natural fear**; from lust or desire, and this is either from an excessive love of oneself—and thus it is **human fear**—or from an excessive love of one’s possessions—and thus it is **worldly fear**. ¶ From grace: ¶ this is either from "grace freely given" <note>original: *gratia gratis data*; spiritual gifts given for the benefit of others</note>, and thus it is **servile fear**; or from "grace that makes one pleasing" <note>original: *gratia gratum faciēte*; sanctifying grace that heals the soul</note>...

<summary>This page defines the gifts of Piety and Fear, explaining Piety as benevolence toward others as God's image-bearers, and categorizing Fear into six types—including natural, worldly, servile, and filial fear—based on their origins in nature, desire, or grace.</summary>

# The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

...or according to its beginning, and thus it is **initial fear** <term>initial fear: a transitional state where one begins to turn from sin partly out of fear of punishment and partly out of love for God</term>. Or it is from perfected grace, and thus it is **filial fear** <term>filial fear: the reverent dread of a child (filius) toward a parent, fearing only to cause offense or be separated from their love</term>. And this is called **amicable** or **friendly** fear because it fears a disagreement of will. It is **Chaste** because it fears separation from the embrace of the Spouse <note>In mystical theology, the "Spouse" refers to Christ.</note>. It is **filial** because it fears the indignation of the Father. **It is Holy because it fears defilement in all things.**

**Regarding the gifts of the Holy Spirit, note a general distinction.** For a certain gift is from the Holy Spirit but never with the Holy Spirit <note>original: *a spiritu sancto et nunquam cum spiritu sancto*</note>. Such is **servile fear**, in which the will to sin remains strong if one hoped for impunity <note>meaning the person only avoids sin to avoid the "lash" of punishment, not because they dislike the sin itself</note>. A certain gift is from the Holy Spirit and never without the Holy Spirit; such is the gift of charity and of "grace making one pleasing" <term>gratia gratum faciens: sanctifying grace that restores a person's relationship with God</term>. ¶ A certain gift is from the Holy Spirit and can be found both with the Holy Spirit and without the Holy Spirit, such as the gift of knowledge and the gift of prophecy and other "graces freely given" <term>gratia gratis data: spiritual abilities, like healing or prophecy, given for the benefit of the Church rather than for the personal holiness of the recipient</term>.

## Regarding the beatitudes and the active life with works of mercy; and regarding the contemplative life and prayer; and regarding the twelve fruits and the five senses. Chapter forty-six.

The seven beatitudes <note>The "blessings" listed by Jesus in the Gospel of Matthew</note> which the Savior enumerates in the Sermon on the Mount are: **poverty of spirit**, **meekness**, **mourning**, **hunger for justice**, **the feeling of mercy**, **purity of heart**, and **peace**. 

Concerning the beatitudes, we must look at their number, their sufficiency <note>The logical completeness of the list</note>, and the excellence of the twofold life <note>The "Active Life" of work and the "Contemplative Life" of prayer</note>, as well as the consequence of their twofold use. There are seven beatitudes in number; their reasoning and sufficiency are understood as follows: The beatitudes are for the purpose of perfecting the powers of the soul. **Toward** complete perfection, however, three things...

### ¶ Third part.

...chiefly required, namely: a perfect retreat from evil, progress in good, and a perfect state in the best.

¶ Moreover, evil proceeds either from the swelling of **pride**—from which we distance ourselves through **poverty of spirit**; or from the bitterness of **malice**—from which we distance ourselves through **meekness**; or from the weariness of **concupiscence** <term>concupiscence: a strong desire for worldly or sensual pleasures that distract from spiritual goals</term>—from which we distance ourselves through **mourning**.

¶ Furthermore, perfect progress or advancement in good is according to divine imitation. ¶ For "all the ways of the Lord are mercy and truth" <note>original: *misericordia et veritas*; a reference to Psalm 25:10</note>, which is here put in place of **justice**. ¶ We imitate the just God through a **hunger for justice**; we are conformed to the merciful God through the **affection of mercy**.

¶ The perfect state in the "best" is achieved through clear knowledge in the truth—and thus there is **purity of heart** for seeing God. ¶ And it is achieved through tranquil affection in the good—and thus there is **peace of mind** for enjoying God.

¶ The habits of the **virtues** dispose one to the exercise of the **active life** <term>active life: the outward practice of Christian duties and charity in the world</term>. ¶ The habits of the **gifts** lead toward the leisure of the **contemplative life** <term>contemplative life: a state of prayerful stillness and internal focus on God</term>. ¶ The habits of the **beatitudes** work toward the perfection of both lives.

¶ Regarding these two lives—signified by **Leah and Rachel**, by **Peter and John**, and by **Martha and Mary** <note>These are traditional biblical pairings used to represent the Active life (Leah, Peter, Martha) and the Contemplative life (Rachel, John, Mary).</note>—**Bede** <note>Bede the Venerable (c. 672–735), an influential English monk and scholar.</note> says: 

¶ "The **active life** reaches out with bodily bread to the hungry or the bread of doctrine to the ignorant; it corrects those who wander; it recalls the proud to humility; it dispenses what is profitable for individuals. ¶ The **contemplative life** retains the love of God and neighbor, but rests from outward work; it clings to the desire for the Creator alone; and, having trampled all cares, it burns to see the face of the Creator; and it desires to be mingled with the heavenly citizens who rejoice in the incorruption of the Lord’s supper."

¶ However, there is a threefold property in each life according to which they mutually surpass one another. The first property of the **active life** is diligence regarding public utilities and necessities...

## The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

...with respect to the rational power. The **second** is charity toward one’s neighbor with compassion, with respect to the concupiscible <term>concupiscible: the part of the soul associated with basic desires, such as the longing for food or love</term> power. **The third** is the fruitfulness of good works in action, with the perfection of patience, with respect to the irascible <term>irascible: the part of the soul that governs one's "fighting spirit," dealing with anger, courage, and the willpower to overcome difficulties</term> power. **In these**, the contemplative life is surpassed by the active life.

The **first** quality of the contemplative life is the contemplation of the beauty of the highest majesty with delight, with respect to the rational power. The **second** is rest with security, with respect to the irascible power. The **third** is purity of heart with goodness, with respect to the concupiscible power. **In these**, the contemplative life surpasses the active life.

**To the active life** belong the corporal and spiritual works of mercy. The **corporal** works are seven, contained in this verse. **Whence the verse:** *I visit, I give drink, I feed, I ransom, I clothe, I welcome, I bury.* <note>original Latin: *Visito poto cibo redimo tego colligo condo*. These are the traditional Seven Corporal Works of Mercy based on Matthew 25.</note>

**Whose** sufficiency <note>sufficiency: a scholastic term meaning the logical completeness of a list</note> is understood in this way: **Man** is an animated substance. Through this, the vegetative aspect is touched upon, which requires the restoration of "hot and dry" through food, and of "cold and moist" through drink. <note>Bonaventure is using medieval medical theory here, where the body's health depends on balancing these four qualities.</note> He is also a sensible being, and for this, a double covering is necessary because of harmful external things: namely, a distant covering, which is shelter, and a nearby covering, which is clothing.

**Rational:** and thus he is a free substance, born to have freedom from prison or from infirmity. Thus, against confinement in prison or in bed, there is visiting; or against servitude, there is ransom. Finally, man is mortal, and thus there is death; because of which, or after which, he requires burial.

*The spiritual [works] are also seven, contained in this verse: Instruct, correct, console, forgive, endure, pray.* <note>original Latin: *Instrue, castiga, solare, remitte, fer, ora*</note>.

**Whose** sufficiency is understood in this way: A spiritual **defect** in one's neighbor, if it cannot be relieved except through God, then prayer must be employed. **If it can** be relieved by man, and it is a matter of knowledge: as...

### ¶ Part Three

...ignorance of things that should be known: then there is instruction. If it is ignorance of things that should be done: then there is counsel <term>counsel: providing wise advice or guidance to someone facing a difficult decision</term>. If indeed it is a matter of action: either it is a fault committed against you, and thus there is forgiveness; or against others, and thus there is correction. If it is suffering: either one is to be relieved by word, and thus there is consolation; or by deed, and thus there is strengthening <term>strengthening: providing physical or moral support to someone in a state of weakness or trial</term>.

¶ To the contemplative life belongs prayer. Whence, regarding the contemplative life, we must consider its definition, the distinction of its degrees, and the prayer contained under it. Contemplation is defined thus: Contemplation is a true and certain intuition <term>intuition: a direct, clear spiritual "seeing" or gazing upon a truth without the need for complex reasoning</term> regarding any thing whatever. According to Richard <note>original: *A richardo*. This refers to Richard of Saint Victor (died 1173), a famous mystical theologian known for his detailed mapping of the spiritual life.</note> thus: Contemplation is a free insight of the mind into the spectacles of wisdom, suspended with wonder. <note>original Latin: *Contemplatio est libera mentis perspicatia in sapientie spectacula cum admiratione suspensa.*</note> Or thus: Contemplation is a keen and free intuition of the soul into things to be perceived, spread out everywhere. Bernard <note>Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian monk and one of the most influential spiritual writers of the Middle Ages.</note> posits six degrees of contemplation which are: leisure <term>leisure: *vacatio*, the state of being still and free from worldly distractions</term>, intuition, wonder, love, delight, and longing <term>longing: *hanelatio*, literally a "panting" or "breathlessness" caused by intense desire for God</term>. Augustine, in his book *On the Quantity of the Soul*, posits seven degrees or acts. The first is called animation <note>giving life to the body</note>; the second, sense; the third, art; the fourth, virtue; the fifth, tranquility; the sixth, approach; the seventh, contemplation.

¶ Regarding prayer, it must be considered with respect to its definition in general, and with respect to the assignment of the petitions of the Lord's Prayer in particular. Prayer is described thus by Augustine: Prayer is a pious affection of the mind directed toward God. According to John of Damascus <note>A 7th-century Syrian monk and scholar whose definitions became standard in medieval theology.</note> thus: Prayer is the ascent of the intellect into God. That is: Prayer is the expression of desire directed toward God for the sake of obtaining good or removing evil, formed through the internal and external word. And scholastically <note>original: *magistraliter*, referring to the way the "Masters" or university professors of the time taught it.</note> thus: Prayer is the showing of our will to the one from whom we hope to obtain that which...

# From the Lentiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

with respect to the rational faculty <term>rational faculty: the part of the soul responsible for reasoning and understanding</term>. **Second** is charity toward one's neighbor with compassion, with respect to the desiring faculty <term>desiring faculty: also called the 'concupiscible' power, this is the part of the soul that seeks out what is perceived as good or pleasurable</term>. **Third** is the fruitfulness of good works in acting with the perfection of patience, with respect to the spirited faculty <term>spirited faculty: also called the 'irascible' power, this is the part of the soul that deals with obstacles, courage, and anger</term>. **In these things**, the contemplative life is exceeded by the active life.

## ¶ Part Three

If there is **i**gnorance of things that ought to be known, then there is instruction. **I**f there is ignorance of things that ought to be done, then there is counsel. **I**f, however, it concerns an action: it is either a fault committed against you, and thus there is remission <note>meaning forgiveness or the letting go of a debt or offense</note>; or it is against others, and thus there is correction. **I**f there is suffering <note>original Latin: *pena*</note>, one is either to be relieved by word, and thus there is consolation; or by deed, and thus there is confirmation <note>meaning strengthening or providing support</note>. ¶ **P**rayer belongs to the contemplative life. Regarding the contemplative life, we must look at its definition, the distinction of its degrees, and the prayer contained within it. **C**ontemplation is defined as follows:

# The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

...we desire. **G**regory <note>Saint Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), a Pope and Doctor of the Church known for his writings on the spiritual life</note> says: To pray truly is to sound out bitter groans in compunction <note>original Latin: *compunctione*; a "pricking" of the heart or deep remorse for one's sins</note> rather than resonating with composed words. ¶ **N**ow we must descend to the prayer which the Lord composed <note>the *Oratio Dominica* or the "Our Father"</note>, in which the entirety of all things to be asked for is contained under a sevenfold set of petitions. This covers both those things which pertain to the reward of our homeland <note>Heaven, the final destination of the soul</note> and those things which pertain to the merit of the way <note>the earthly pilgrimage or "the way" toward God</note>.

**T**hose things which pertain to the reward of the homeland are:
1.  The understanding of truth, or the effective vision of the True, which is asked for in the words: **hallowed be thy name** <note>original: *sanctificetur nomen tuum*</note>. That is, that the knowledge of your name may be given to holy and pure persons.
2.  **R**everence for majesty, or the secure holding of the highest heights which makes us kings and through which the kingdom is possessed; this is asked for in: **thy kingdom come** <note>original: *adueniat regnum tuum*</note>.
3.  **H**armony of the will, or the perfect enjoyment of the highest Good, which is asked for in: **thy will be done, on earth as it is in heaven** <note>original: *fiat voluntas tua sicut in celo et in terra*</note>.

**T**hose things which pertain to the merit or the passage of the way <note>our journey through this life</note> either regard the bestowing of good or the removal of evil. **T**he bestowing of good is asked for in the **daily bread**, or the "supersubstantial" bread <term>supersubstantial: *supersubstantiali*, a term used by St. Jerome in his Latin translation of the Bible to describe bread that is "above all substances" or spiritual/Eucharistic bread</term>. In this, we ask for whatever is necessary for the preservation of the present life, both according to the spirit and according to the body.

**T**he removal of evil is asked for in the three following petitions. **F**or evil is threefold. One kind pertains to the past, another to the present, and another to the future. **O**r, one is the evil of fault or transgression; another is the evil of struggle or temptation; and another is the evil of punishment <note>original: *pene*</note> or tribulation.

**T**he first is asked to be removed in the **forgiveness of debts** <note>addressing past sins</note>. The second is removed in the **victory over temptations** <note>addressing present struggles</note>. The third is removed in the **liberation from the oppression of adversaries** <note>addressing future tribulations</note>.

¶ **A** twofold use follows the perfection of the beatitudes, namely of the five spiritual senses...

# ¶ Part Three
<note>The text continues from a list of spiritual attributes</note> ...of such things. And the twelve fruits of the spirit. The **spiritual senses** are sight, hearing, taste, smell, and touch. 

By **Sight**, the supreme beauty of the bridegroom Christ is seen under the aspect of splendor. By **Hearing**, the supreme harmony of Christ is heard under the aspect of the Word. **Taste** allows the supreme sweetness of Christ to be tasted under the aspect of wisdom <note>original: *sapientiae*; derived from *sapere*, meaning "to taste," implying a knowledge that is felt or experienced</note>, which encompasses both the Word and the splendor. By **Smell**, the supreme fragrance of Christ is perceived under the aspect of the Word inspired within the heart. By **Touch**, the supreme sweetness of Christ is embraced and felt under the aspect of the Word made flesh, dwelling among men. 

The **Fruits** <note>original: *fructus*; referring to the "Fruits of the Holy Spirit" described in Galatians 5:22-23</note> of the spirit are: **Charity**, **joy**, **peace**, **patience**, **long-suffering**, **goodness**, **kindness**, **faith**, **gentleness**, **modesty**, **continence**, and **chastity**. 

However, neither these senses nor these fruits are <term>habits</term> <note>in medieval theology, a "habit" is a stable disposition or quality of the soul</note> in themselves. Rather, "fruits" refers to the delights of the heart in tasting the goodness of the bridegroom. The **Senses** refer to the intellectual perceptions involved in contemplating the truth of the bridegroom. Among the prophets, this contemplation occurred through revelation in a threefold vision: namely, the bodily, the imaginary (or spiritual), and the intellectual. **In** other just men, it is found through <term>speculation</term> <note>original: *speculationem*; here meaning a focused meditation or "looking into" divine things as if in a mirror</note>, which begins with the physical senses and proceeds to the imagination; from the imagination to reason; from reason to the intellect; from the intellect to understanding; and from understanding to wisdom or "ecstatic knowledge" <note>original: *noticiam excessiuam*; a knowledge that "goes beyond" or transcends normal human boundaries</note>. In this way, one stands before the true Solomon <note>a traditional title for Christ, representing the ultimate King of Wisdom and Peace</note>.

# The Breviloquium of Saint Bonaventure 
<note>The OCR reads "Lentiloquii," which is a common transcription error for *Breviloquii*, the title of Bonaventure's famous "brief summary" of theology.</note>

...specifically. In general, we must consider the sacraments regarding their institution, definition, repetition, sufficiency, and preeminence. The institution of the sacraments was fitting for God and beneficial for us. It was fitting for divine **mercy** to provide us with remedies not only for the forgiveness of guilt but also for punishment. It was fitting for divine **justice**, which requires from us a certain humility, which we display through the observance of sensible <note>original: *sensibilium*; meaning things that can be perceived by the physical senses</note> signs. It was fitting for divine **wisdom**, which decreed that remedies should be found in those very physical things through which human nature happened to perish. 

It was beneficial for us because we were blind in our **reason** <note>original: *rationalem*; referring to the rational faculty of the soul</note>, therefore sensible sacraments were given for our instruction. Because we were prideful in our **spirited nature** <note>original: *irascibilem*; the "irascible" faculty, associated with courage, anger, and ambition</note>, therefore bodily sacraments were given for our humility. Because we were prone to evil in our **desires** <note>original: *concupiscibilem*; the "concupiscible" faculty, associated with physical cravings</note>, therefore many sacraments were given for our spiritual exercise. 

¶ A **Sacrament** is defined in the *Sentences* <note>referring to Peter Lombard’s *Sentences*, the standard theological textbook of the medieval university</note> as follows: "A sacrament is a visible form of invisible grace, which bears the likeness of that grace and exists as its cause." Hugh of Saint Victor <note>An influential 12th-century theologian at the Abbey of Saint Victor in Paris</note> defines it thus: "A sacrament is a material element, set before the eyes, representing by its likeness, signifying by its institution, and conferring grace by its signification." 

¶ The sacraments are ordered in this way: **Baptism**, **Confirmation**, **Eucharist**, **Penance**, and **Extreme Unction** <note>now commonly known as the Anointing of the Sick</note>, **Holy Orders**, and **Matrimony**. This is because the first five concern the individual person, while Holy Orders and Matrimony concern the Church. The order is clear: for **Baptism** is for the healing of wounds. **Confirmation** is for the preservation of the state of healing. The **Eucharist** is for the nourishment <note>original: *pmocionem* / *promotionem*; implying advancement or strengthening</note> of that which is held and preserved. **Penance** <note>original: *Pnia* / *Poenitentia*</note> is for the restoration of healing that has been lost.

<summary>This page introduces the theology of the Sacraments, explaining why God chose physical signs to deliver grace and how the seven sacraments function together to heal and strengthen the soul and the Church.</summary>
<keywords>Sacraments, Bonaventure, Peter Lombard, Hugh of Saint Victor, Baptism, Eucharist, Grace, Scholasticism</keywords>
