### Part Three

...[and finally,] **Last Rites** <term>Extrema unctio: literally 'extreme unction' or the anointing of the sick</term> is for the purging of venial <note>minor or pardonable sins</note> sins with respect to guilt and for the remission of a portion of the penalty owed. **Holy Orders** <term>Ordo: the sacrament of ordination for clergy</term> regards the spiritual generation of persons, while **Marriage** is for the corporal <note>physical</note> generation of persons. And both types of generation are necessary to the Church so that it does not fail in either grace or nature.

¶ There are four sacraments, however, which can be repeated, or rather, received frequently: namely, the Eucharist, Penance <note>confession</note>, Last Rites, and Marriage. **Baptism**, however, and **Confirmation**, and **Holy Orders** are not repeated because in them three indelible characters <note>original: *caracteres*; permanent spiritual marks or seals on the soul</note> are imprinted; by these, according to the various states of faith, people are distinguished from other people. For according to the state of faith being born, there is the baptismal character, by which the faithful are distinguished from the unfaithful. According to the state of faith being strengthened, there is the character of Confirmation, which distinguishes the strong from the weak. According to the state of faith being multiplied, there is the character of Holy Orders, by which the clergy are distinguished from the laity.

¶ The sufficiency of the sacraments is understood in this way: the Sacraments are medicines against disease. A **disease**, moreover, is twofold: namely, the disease of guilt and the disease of punishment. The **disease** of guilt is twofold: namely, original guilt, against which is Baptism; and actual guilt, which is also doubled. For one type is actual mortal <note>original: *mortalis*; serious sins that "kill" the life of grace in the soul</note> guilt, against which is Penance; another is actual venial guilt, against which is Last Rites. The **disease** of punishment is fourfold: namely, ignorance, against which is Holy Orders; malice, against which is the Eucharist; infirmity <note>spiritual weakness</note>, against which is Confirmation; and concupiscentia <note>disordered desire or lust</note>, against which is Marriage.

¶ The Sacraments are also aids because they exist for the assistance of the seven virtues. **In** aid of faith is Baptism. **In** aid of hope is Last Rites. **In** aid...

<summary>This page explains the specific purposes of the seven sacraments, distinguishing between those that leave a permanent spiritual mark (Baptism, Confirmation, Holy Orders) and those that can be repeated. It uses the metaphor of "medicine" to show how each sacrament heals specific spiritual diseases like guilt, ignorance, and malice.</summary>

<keywords>Sacraments, Last Rites, Holy Orders, Marriage, Indelible Character, Seven Virtues, Penance, Eucharist, Original Sin, Mortal Sin, Venial Sin</keywords>

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure
<note>original: *Centiloquium* — a title meaning "one hundred sayings" or "a collection of one hundred parts"</note>

...the **Eucharist** serves charity; **Holy Orders** assists prudence; **Matrimony** assists temperance; **Confirmation** assists fortitude; and **Penance** <note>original: *p[oeniten]tia* — the sacrament of confession and repentance</note> assists justice. These sacraments are also the armaments of the **Church Militant** <term>Ecclesia Militans: the Church on earth, viewed as being in a spiritual struggle against sin and evil</term>, which in the Song of Songs, chapter 4 <note>specifically Song of Solomon 6:4</note>, is called "terrible as an army set in battle array."

Indeed, the Church is "terrible" because it is strong and fortified through the sacrament of **Confirmation**. It is an "army" because it is joined and united through the sacrament of the **Eucharist**. It is "set in array" through the sacrament of **Holy Orders**. From this army, some are withdrawn through death; to restore these, there is **Matrimony** <note>which provides new generations of faithful</note>. Some are struck down through guilt; to raise these up, there is **Penance**. Some enter this army to go into battle, and this is through **Baptism**. Some leave it and are crowned <note>in heaven</note>, and this is through **Last Rites** <term>Extrema unctio: the anointing of the sick or dying</term>.

¶ Now, one sacrament is said to excel another in five ways: 
1. Because of its **signification**: thus Matrimony excels because it signifies the union of the two natures in Christ <note>the divine and human natures</note>. 
2. Because of what it **contains**: and thus the Eucharist exceeds others <note>because it contains the actual body of Christ</note>. 
3. Because of its **efficacy** <note>effectiveness in securing salvation</note>: and thus Baptism excels. 
4. Because of its **dispensing dignity**: and thus Confirmation, which is administered by bishops <note>original: *pontificibus*</note> alone. 
5. Because of the **greater and nobler rank** into which it places a person: and thus it is called a "Greater Order" <term>Ordo maior: referring to the higher ranks of the clergy</term>. 

Whence the common verse: 
*Baptism is greater in effect; the Eucharist in its essence;* 
*Marriage in its great sign; Confirmation in its minister;* 
*While Holy Orders is said to excel by its noble rank.* 
<note>original Latin mnemonic: "Maioꝛ in effectu baptismus corpus in esse / Cõiugium signo maioꝛi crisma ministro. / Nobilioꝛ gradu precellere dicitur ordo."</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the seven sacraments from the previous section. Having established the general purpose of sacraments as remedies for spiritual "diseases" and as the armaments of the Church, Bonaventure now turns specifically to the first sacrament, Baptism, providing various definitions from established theological authorities.</meta>

### Part Three

¶ Regarding what the name of the element signifies and sanctifies by the power of the word: it is defined by Hugh <note>Hugh of Saint Victor (c. 1096–1141), a prominent mystical theologian</note> as: **Baptism** is water sanctified for the washing away of sins. According to what is the act or use of the element, it is defined by the Master in the *Sentences* <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1096–1160), whose "Sentences" was the standard textbook of medieval theology</note>: **Baptism** is the outward washing of the body performed under the prescribed form of words. However, according to what is accepted as the sacrament and the "reality of the sacrament" <term>res sacramenti: the underlying spiritual reality or effect that the visible sign points toward</term>, which is the character <term>caracter: an indelible spiritual mark or "seal" impressed upon the soul</term>, it is defined by [John] Damascene: **Baptism** is the beginning and protection of spiritual life, and the illumination of the mind.

And here a fourfold condition of the character is touched upon: because it prepares one for the grace of God, it is called the "beginning of spiritual life"; because it distinguishes the Lord's flock, it is called a "seal"; and because it perseveres indelibly, it is called a "protection"; because it spiritually disposes one toward faith, it is called the "illumination of the mind."

According to a third <note>though listed as third, this is the fourth definition provided</note> way it is accepted as a sacrament, it is defined by Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential 5th-6th century Christian Neoplatonist</note>: **Baptism** is the sacrament of rebirth as it first exists within the soul, spiritually shaping natural impulses <note>original: *animales... motus* — here referring to the movements of the "anima" or soul, rather than "animalistic" in a modern sense</note> toward the performance of divine words and actions, and bringing about the spiritual ascent <term>anagoge: a mystical or spiritual interpretation that leads the mind from earthly things to heavenly ones</term> toward heavenly rest.

In this definition, the "reality of the sacrament"—which is the grace that gives life to the soul—is addressed when it is said "as it first exists within the soul." The enabling of the soul's powers through virtue is also touched upon: first, regarding the active life, when he says "shaping natural impulses"; second, regarding the contemplative life, when he says "and the spiritual ascent toward heavenly rest."

Concerning its significance: **Note** that in baptism, just as in the other sacraments, there is something that is only the sacrament or signifying sign, such as the visible element <note>water</note>; there is something that is only the reality signified, such as healing grace; and there is something that is both the sacrament and the reality signified, such as the character <note>the spiritual mark mentioned above</note>. The **Form** used when the child is immersed is this: **I** baptize you in the name of the Fa[ther...] <note>The text cuts off here, referring to the Trinitarian formula: "In the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit."</note>

<summary>This page provides a systematic theological definition of Baptism, drawing on authorities like Hugh of Saint Victor, Peter Lombard, John Damascene, and Pseudo-Dionysius to explain its physical, legal, and spiritual dimensions.</summary>
<keywords>Baptism, Hugh of Saint Victor, Peter Lombard, John Damascene, Pseudo-Dionysius, sacramental character, grace, spiritual rebirth, anagoge</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific discussion of the Sacrament of Baptism, picking up mid-sentence from the previous page's explanation of the trinitarian formula required for a valid sacrament.</meta>

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

¶ [of the Fa]ther and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit. Amen. ¶ This form must be expressed without the omission of a vowel, without the interruption of a word, without reversing the aforementioned order, and without changing the spoken name. ¶ Furthermore, the effect that is received in baptism when a person approaches it humbly is the impression of the character <term>character: an indelible spiritual mark or "seal" impressed upon the soul</term>, the infusion of grace, the restoration of innocence, and the opening of heaven. This grace, I say, is infused for regenerating, rectifying, and purifying the person from all guilt—both original <note>The sin inherited from Adam</note> and actual <note>Sins committed by the individual's own will</note>, whether mortal or venial. ¶ It absolves, nonetheless, from eternal punishment and from all temporal satisfactory punishment <note>The "debt" or penance usually required to balance the scales of justice for sins committed after baptism</note>. Moreover, a threefold baptism is distinguished, namely: **of water**, **of spirit**, and **of blood**. In the baptism **of spirit** <note>original: *flaminis*, literally "of the breath/wind"</note>, which occurs through **penitence** and the graces of the **Holy Spirit**, we are purified from guilt. In the baptism **of water** <note>original: *fluminis*, literally "of the river"</note>, we are purified from both guilt and from satisfactory punishment. In the baptism **of blood** <note>original: *sanguinis*</note>, we are purified from all misery. However, the baptisms of blood and spirit are not "sacraments" in the strict sense, since they lack the qualities that belong to a sacrament: namely, representation through likeness, significance through institution <note>The formal establishing of the ritual by Christ</note>, and sanctification through the pleasure of the word. ¶ Regarding the integrity of the sacrament, the word and the element <note>The physical matter, such as water</note> suffice. Regarding the solemnity of the ritual, the catechism and exorcism are provided as preparations for both infants and adults; however, in adults, one's own faith is required, whereas for infants, the faith of another <note>meaning the faith of the Church or the sponsors</note> is sufficient. ¶ Regarding its effectiveness, the right intention of the one baptizing is required, and regarding the benefit for the one being baptized, the laying aside of pretense <note>original: *fictionis*, meaning the absence of an internal obstacle or "faking" the conversion</note> is required.

### ¶ **Concerning Confirmation**

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the sacraments from the previous page, which concluded by noting that baptismal variants like "baptism of blood" lack the formal significance of the sacrament of water. Here, the author transitions into the significance and ritual "form" of Confirmation, followed by a brief mention of the Eucharist.</meta>

### Part Three

...in terms of its significance. The **Form** to be observed in confirmation is this: "I sign you with the sign of the cross and I confirm you with the chrism of salvation, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit. Amen." **In** this form, a twofold action is expressed, because in this sacrament grace is given to strengthen faith, both for confirmation within the heart and for free confession with the mouth. **One** action concerns the free confession of the Crucified One, as if it were always to be carried upon the forehead <note>A reference to the public nature of the faith, marked by the sign of the cross on the brow</note>. The other concerns firm stability in the heart.

The **Material** in this sacrament is chrism <term>chrism: a consecrated mixture of oil and balsam used in Christian initiation</term> made from olive oil and balsam. **Oil**, because of its brightness, signifies the inner faith of a good conscience; **balsam**, because of its fragrance, signifies a worthy and praiseworthy confession in public view. This corresponds to the twofold action expressed in the form.

**This** sacrament, however, is administered by bishops alone; if others presume to do so, they accomplish nothing. **Because** the fullness of grace is given in both confirmation and in holy orders, these two sacraments are administered only by those who possess the fullness of authority <note>The author argues that only bishops, as successors to the apostles, hold the "fullness" of ecclesiastical power required to bestow the "fullness" of grace</term>.

The **Grace** conferred in confirmation is "grace that makes one pleasing" <term>sanctifying grace: or *gratia gratum faciens*, the supernatural gift that makes the soul holy and pleasing to God</term>. **Grace** that makes one pleasing is spoken of in two ways: it either makes one pleasing who was not previously pleasing—such as the grace of baptism and penance—or it makes one who is already pleasing even more so. This is the grace given in confirmation, which expands and strengthens the grace received previously.

**In** this sacrament, there is one aspect that is only a sign, such as the audible form <note>The spoken words</note> and the visible material <note>The chrism</note>. There is another that is only the spiritual reality <note>original: *res*</note>, such as the strengthening and confirming grace. And there is another that is both a reality and a sign, such as the **character** <term>an indelible spiritual seal impressed upon the soul that marks a person as a soldier of Christ</term>.

# The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

...of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit. Amen. <note>original: "tris et filij ⁊ spiritus sancti. amen"</note> **This** form must be expressed without the omission of a vowel, without the interruption of a word, without reversing the aforementioned order, and without changing the designated name. **The** effect received in baptism by a person approaching it humbly is the imprinting of a character <term>character: an indelible spiritual mark or "seal" placed upon the soul</term>, the infusion of grace, the restoration of innocence, and the opening of heaven. The grace that is infused is regenerating, rectifying, and purifying from all guilt, both original and actual <note>Actual sin refers to sins committed by one's own will, distinguished from original sin inherited from Adam</note>, whether mortal or venial. It furthermore absolves the person from eternal punishment and from all temporal punishment of satisfaction <note>satisfaction: the penance or "debt" remaining to be paid for sin</note>. 

**Baptism** is distinguished into three types: namely, of water, of the spirit, and of blood <note>original: "fluminis, flaminis, ⁊ sanguinis"</note>. In the baptism of the spirit—which occurs through penance and the grace of the Holy Spirit—we are purified from guilt. In the baptism of water, we are purified from guilt and from the punishment of satisfaction. In the baptism of blood <note>A reference to martyrdom</note>, we are purified from all misery. However, the baptisms of blood and of the spirit are not "sacraments" in the strict sense, since they lack the qualities proper to a sacrament: namely, representation through likeness, significance by institution, and sanctification by the utterance of the word. 

**Regarding** the integrity <note>The essential requirements</note> of the sacrament, the word and the element <note>water</note> are sufficient. Regarding the solemnity of the rite, the catechism and exorcism are provided as preparations for both infants and adults; however, in adults, one's own faith is required, while in infants, the faith of another <note>specifically, the faith of the Church or godparents</note> suffices. Regarding its utility, the right intention of the baptizer is required, and on the part of the baptized, the laying aside of pretense <note>Latin: "fictionis depositio"; refers to the recipient being sincere and not creating a spiritual obstacle to grace</note>.

Next, we must consider confirmation with respect to its form, its material, its administration, the conferring of grace, and...

### Part Three

As for its meaning, the **Form** to be observed in confirmation is this: "I sign you with the sign of the cross, and I confirm you with the chrism of salvation, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit. Amen." <note>original: "signo te signo crucis confirmo te crismate salutis In nomine patris. & filij & spiritus sancti amen"</note> **In** this form, a twofold act is expressed, because in this sacrament grace is given for the strengthening of faith, both in regard to confirmation in the heart and in regard to free confession with the mouth. **One** act regards the free confession of the Crucified—which should be carried, as it were, always on the forehead <note>This refers to the practice of the bishop tracing the cross on the recipient's forehead.</note>—while the other regards firm stability in the heart. The **Matter** in this sacrament is chrism <term>chrism: a consecrated mixture of olive oil and balsam</term> made from olive oil and balsam. **Oil**, because of its brightness, signifies interior faith arising from a good conscience. **Balsam**, because of its fragrance, signifies a worthy and laudable confession in public view; and this corresponds to the twofold act expressed in the form. **This** sacrament, however, is dispensed by bishops alone; so that if others presume to perform it, they accomplish nothing. **Because** the fullness of grace is given in both confirmation and in holy orders, for this reason these two sacraments are dispensed only by those who bear the fullness of authority <note>In medieval theology, bishops were understood to possess the "fullness of the priesthood," allowing them to confer sacraments that "perfect" or "order" the faithful.</note>. The **Grace** that is conferred in confirmation is "grace making one pleasing" <term>grace making one pleasing (gratia gratum faciens): a theological term for sanctifying grace that restores or enhances the soul's relationship with God</term>. **Now**, "grace making pleasing" is spoken of in two ways: either making one pleasing from a state of being not pleasing—and such is the grace of baptism and penance—or making one *more* pleasing from a state of already being pleasing. This latter grace is given in confirmation, which expands and strengthens the grace previously received. **In** this sacrament, one thing is only a sign, such as the audible form <note>The spoken words</note> and the visible matter; another is only the reality, such as the strengthening and confirming grace; and another is both the reality and the sign, such as the character <term>character: an indelible spiritual mark or "seal" imprinted on the soul, which cannot be repeated</term>.

# The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

Next, we must consider the Eucharist: specifically regarding its integrity, its meaning, its representation, its consumption, and its operation. ¶ Concerning the integrity and necessity of this sacrament, there are four requirements: namely, priestly **Holy Orders**; the **Intention** to consecrate; the appropriate **Matter**; and the prescribed **Form** of words. ¶ The **Matter** of this sacrament is wheaten bread and wine from the vine. ¶ For this food is, among all foods and all drinks, the most suitable, the most common, the most effective, and the most healthy. ¶ Therefore, the Lord compared himself to a grain of wheat and to the wood of the vine; and for this reason, he performed the consecration and instituted it to be performed in these two species, to show that his body contained under this sacrament as food is not just any food, but a suitable food: a common food, an effective food, and a healthy food. ¶ **Water** is mixed with the wine, which signifies the people <note>original: "f[ignifi]cat p[o]p[u]l[u]m"</note>, and through its conjunction with the wine, it signifies the unity of the members with the Head <note>Christ is the "Head" and the faithful are the "members" of his body, the Church.</note>. ¶ Through the wine, however, the union of the members with one another is signified. ¶ The **Form** spoken over the bread is this: "This is my body" <note>original: "hoc est corpus meum"</note>. ¶ Over the wine, it is: "This is the chalice of my blood" <note>original: "Hic est calix sanguinis mei"</note>. ¶ The proposition of this form is unique. ¶ For it is an **operative word**; and it is operative not through a gradual process, but instantaneously. It is operative not upon some new or non-existent thing, but its endpoint is a being that is actually complete and unchanged, and terminated toward that which is a sign of another—that is, the mystical body of Christ. All of this is done by the power of the uncreated Christ, with the minister added as an agent who performs nothing upon the matter itself. ¶ Because, therefore, this speech is **convertive** and **operative**, it works upon the **substance** of the bread. Therefore, the pronoun demonstrates the substance of the bread under the **accidents** <term>accidents: the physical qualities of a thing (like color or taste) that remain even if its inner essence changes</term> which can be perceived by the eyes, and it touches upon that which is converted through the form, just as it is into that which it is converted through the predicate. ¶ Because the endpoint of this operation is a being in act and not one newly created, therefore the verb of "being" <note>The verb "is" in "This is my body."</note> is placed there, rather than a verb of "becoming," because it happens suddenly.

<summary>This page defines the four essentials for the Eucharist (priestly orders, intention, matter, and form) and explains why bread and wine are the chosen elements. It describes the words of consecration as "operative," meaning they instantly transform the substance of the bread into Christ's body.</summary>
<keywords>Eucharist, bread and wine, transubstantiation, sacramental matter, sacramental form, holy orders, Saint Bonaventure</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the Eucharistic consecration, moving from the physical transformation (transubstantiation) to the symbolic meanings of the ritual actions performed during the Mass.</meta>

### Part Three

...conversion such that the "being made" and the "having been made" occur at the exact same moment. Therefore, among philosophers, the word is considered instantaneous; it is not proposed as something in the future, but rather it simply *is* <note>original: "est"</note> and will not [merely] become. ¶ Because this operation results in the **true body** as a sign of the **mystical body** <note>The "mystical body" refers to the Church, the community of believers.</note> in which there is a diversity of functions, it is called a "body" and not "flesh." This is because a body is heterogeneous <term>heterogeneous|composed of different parts or organs, like a body has hands, eyes, and feet</term> rather than homogeneous. Flesh, however, is homogeneous <term>homogeneous|consisting of the same substance throughout</term>. ¶ Because this operation occurs by the power of the uncreated Word <note>The "uncreated Word" refers to the second person of the Trinity, Christ.</note> joined to the speech, and as it is joined, the priest says "mine"—speaking not in the person of the priest, but as Christ, namely, the Word made flesh. ¶ Since the minister [the priest] performs no physical work upon the material elements nor changes them by his own power, nothing more is required beyond the "form" <note>In medieval theology, the "form" refers to the specific ritual words spoken to make the sacrament valid.</note> of the words. ¶ Thus, even if the minister neither touches nor breaks the bread, provided he intends to consecrate and speaks the words, the sacrament is completed. ¶ Regarding its meaning, it is held that in this sacrament there is one thing which is only a **sign**, such as the visible species <term>species|the outward appearances of bread and wine, such as taste and color, which remain even after the substance changes</term> of bread and wine. There is another which is only the **reality**, such as the mystical body of Christ [the Church]. Finally, there is that which is both **reality and sign**, namely the true body of Christ, which is the reality signified by the bread (made from many different grains) and the blood signified by the wine (pressed from many different clusters of grapes), and it is itself a sign of the mystical body. ¶ For just as a body has different parts with different functions, it signifies the various works performed within the Church. ¶ In this sacrament, a representation of the Lord’s Passion is made. ¶ For this reason, the signs of the cross are made seven times to express the Passion of the Lord. ¶ The first time, three signs are made because of the triple betrayal of Christ: namely, by God [the Father], by Judas, and by the Jews <note>The "betrayal" or "handing over" by God refers to the theological belief that the Father gave up His Son for the salvation of humanity.</note>. ¶ The second time, five signs are made because of the five types of persons involved in the betrayal: the person betrayed [Christ]; the person of the betrayer [Judas]; and the persons of the buyers, who were three: namely the high priests, the scribes, and the Pharisees. ¶ The third time, two signs are made because of the conversion of the two elements, namely the bread and the wine. ¶ The fourth time, five are made because of...

# ¶ The Lenten Discourses of Saint Bonaventure

five wounds <note>The text refers back to the ritual signs of the cross made during the Mass representing the five wounds of Christ.</note> ¶ On the fifth occasion, two [crosses] are made on account of the bonds with which the body was bound, and on account of the scourges with which it was lashed even unto blood. ¶ On the sixth occasion, three are made on account of the crucifixion carried out by the tongues of the Jews when they cried out three times, "Crucify him!" ¶ On the seventh occasion, three crosses are made with the body <note>The "body" here refers to the consecrated Host</note> over the chalice on account of the three sufferings, namely: anticipation of suffering, the suffering itself, and compassion <note>original: "propassiōis passiōis ⁊ cōpassiōis." For some approach ill-disposed <note>meaning they are in a state of sin or lack faith</note>, and these eat sacramentally [only]. ¶ Some approach well-disposed, and these eat both spiritually and sacramentally. ¶ Second, from the manner of receiving. For some receive with the "mouth of the flesh," and these eat sacramentally; some receive with the "mouth of the heart," and these eat spiritually. ¶ Therefore, there is a double mouth and a double eating. ¶ Third, from the double reality of this sacrament: for indeed there are two essences, namely the true body of Christ and the grace in the **mystical body** <note>The Church community</note> which creates unity; one of these can be received without the other. ¶ To eat sacramentally, however, is to eat the sacrament as a sacrament, for which four things are required: the existence of the food, its natural suitability for being food—namely, that it is consumed by a human and not by a mouse <note>This addresses a common medieval theological question about whether an animal could technically consume the sacrament.</note>. ¶ [Also] the intention, and the regulation of that intention through faith. ¶ Spiritual eating has two aspects: first, its own kind of mastication <term>mastication|the act of chewing or processing; here used metaphorically for mental digestion</term>, which consists in the contemplation of the food—namely, the flesh of Christ offered for us as the price of redemption and as the food of refreshment. ¶ It also involves incorporation <note>The process of becoming part of the body</note> which arises from such contemplation; while the one contemplating is moved by the love of charity and is joined to Him whom he contemplates, and thus ina... <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "inauguratur" or "incorporatur".</note>

### Part Three

is embodied, and while it is incorporated, the person is restored and made more **assimilated** <note>meaning to become more like Christ</note> to Him. Moreover, the spiritual operation or efficacy of this sacrament is to unite—not for the first time, but to more deeply unite those already united through grace. For to one who approaches worthily, it renders them more fervent, like a **glowing coal**, and stronger, like good food. And in order to have this efficacy in oneself, first, it is necessary for the one consuming this food to distinguish it from others through faith. Second, to prepare a lodging <note>a "hospicium," referring to the soul as a guest-house for God</note> through self-examination. Third, to receive Him into the prepared lodging with honor and devotion. Such a person is said to approach worthily.
Note regarding the breaking of the host. A verse:
> The host is divided into
> parts. The dipped part marks the blessed. The fully dry part denotes the living; the preserved
> part denotes the buried.

The **breaking** <note>term: Fraction; the ritual breaking of the consecrated bread during Mass</note> represents the Passion <note>original: "passio," meaning Christ's suffering</note>. The three broken parts represent the three orders of the just. Some reign in heaven; these are represented by the dipped part <note>original: "tinctam," referring to the practice of intinction where the bread is dipped in the wine</note>. Some wait securely in their graves; these are represented by the part which was anciently preserved <note>referring to the Reserved Sacrament</note>. Some fight in the world; these are represented by the dry part of the host.

## On Penance <margin>Chapter II</margin>

It follows to look at **penance** <note>the sacrament of confession and repentance</note> according to its various definitions, its various names, its integration, its justification, and its significance. Penance, according to its primary act, is defined thus: Penance is the first virtue destroying the **"old man"** <note>a biblical reference to the sinful nature of a person before grace</note>. **Ambrose** says: Penance is the grief and bitterness of the soul for the sins which anyone has committed. **Augustine** says: Penance is a certain vengeance of one willing [to be punished], always punishing in oneself what one grieves to have lost. It is also defined according to its annexed act thus: **Ambrose** says: Penance is to lament past evils and not to commit again things to be lamented. **Gregory** says: Penance is to weep for past evil deeds and not to commit things to be wept for. The same Gregory says: Penance is the virtue or grace by which omitted evils are corrected with amendment...

## ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

<note>original: "Centiloquii," meaning "one hundred sayings" or a concise compendium</note>

...with a purpose, we lament and hate [past sins] and no longer wish to commit things to be lamented. It is also defined according to its effect by John of Damascus <note>A 7th-century Syrian monk and influential theologian</note> thus: ¶ Penance is a conversion from that which is contrary to nature to that which is according to nature. Likewise, on Matthew 4, "Do penance": Penance is the correction of an evil mind. ¶ Augustine, in Book V of his *Responses*: Penance is a most excellent thing that, being perfect, recalls all defects back to perfection. ¶ Penance is named by various terms according to the different ways it works against the stain of sin. For it expels the stain of actual sin which deforms the soul and inclines its power toward evil, or weakens it away from God; it wounds nature, and is committed by the act of free will, and is perpetrated with delight and lust. ¶ To erase the fault as something deforming belongs to **reforming grace**; as something weakening, it belongs to **enabling virtue**; as something wounding, it belongs to the **healing sacrament**; as something committed by the will, it belongs to a similar **act of the will**; and as something perpetrated with delight and lust, it belongs to **contrary suffering** <note>original: "passione," meaning a counter-balancing hardship or penance that offsets the previous pleasure of sin</note>. Therefore, penance is rightly called grace, virtue, sacrament, act, and passion. ¶ The integral parts of penance are **contrition of the heart**, **confession of the mouth**, and **satisfaction of works** <note>term: Satisfaction; the performance of some penance to amend for the injury caused by sin</note>. Contrition of the heart is sorrow voluntarily taken up for sins. ¶ Confession of the mouth, performed in the proper way, ought to have sixteen conditions, which are summarized in these verses:

¶ Let confession be simple, humble, pure, and faithful;
¶ Let it be frequent, naked <note>meaning direct and without covering up or making excuses</note>, discreet, willing, and ashamed;
¶ Complete, secret, tearful, and swift;
¶ Strong, accusing, and ready to obey.

¶ According to the Masters <note>referring to the Scholastic "Masters of Theology"</note>, there are four cases in which confession must be repeated. The first of these is because of the **enormity of the crime**, for which the person confessing must be referred to a superior <note>e.g., a Bishop or the Pope, who holds the authority to forgive particularly grave sins</note>; the confession may be heard again by him if he wishes. ¶ The second is because of the **ignorance of the priest**, which then...

## ¶ The Third Part

**T**here are indeed two [defects] on the part of the person confessing, the first of which is on account of the malice of deception: such as if he approached with the will to sin, or if he knowingly reserved <note>meaning: deliberately hid or failed to mention</note> some sin in his conscience. **T**he second is on account of contempt for satisfaction <note>term: satisfaction (satisfactio). The voluntary performance of penance to repair the harm caused by sin</note>: such as when he neglects and despises making satisfaction. **I**n these last two cases, if he goes to another priest—or even to the same one after a period of time has passed and he has forgotten—the man must confess his evils again. Thus, on the part of the priest, there is a double defect, namely because he cannot absolve or because he does not know how. **O**n the part of the one confessing, there is also a double defect, namely because he despises the priest’s absolution or his "binding" <note>original: ligationem. The act of a priest refusing absolution or imposing a penalty</note>. **T**here are two other cases about which there is controversy among the teachers <note>term: doctors (doctores). Refers to established theological authorities or scholars like Thomas Aquinas or Bonaventure himself</note>. **O**ne is concerning the forgetting of some sin; the other is concerning a relapse [into sin]. However, it can be said that in these two cases, to confess all sins again is a matter of counsel <note>meaning: a recommendation for spiritual health</note>, not a command.

**T**here are five cases in which the person confessing is to be sent to the bishop, or the bishop is to be consulted regarding him. **T**he first is concerning "solemn penance," which must be imposed by the bishop himself, and through him the penitent is reconciled <note>Solemn penance was a public and formal process for notorious or exceptionally grave sins</note>. **T**he second is concerning excommunicated persons whom the [parish] priest cannot at any time absolve. **T**he third is when the priest discovers an irregularity <note>term: irregularity (irregularitas). A legal impediment in Canon Law that prevents someone from being ordained or performing priestly duties, often resulting from a serious crime like homicide</note> has been committed, on account of which he must send the confessing person to the bishop, so that the bishop may absolve him if he can, or send him with his own letters to the Pope. **T**he fourth is concerning arsonists <note>original: incendariis</note>. **T**he fifth is if there is a custom in a particular diocese that certain crimes—such as homicide and sacrilege—are reserved to the bishop; however, this case is not universal.

**T**o make satisfaction, as Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD)</note> says, is to shut out the causes of sins and not to yield to their suggestions. **A**nselm <note>Saint Anselm of Canterbury (1033–1109 AD)</note> indeed says: To make satisfaction is to pay back what is owed to God.
¶ **T**hree parts of satisfaction are commonly assigned.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the sacrament of Penance. ## The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

**F**asting, almsgiving, and prayer: Fasting is directed against the desires of the flesh. Almsgiving is against the greed of the eyes. Prayer is against the pride of life <note>These three categories represent the "triple concupiscence" mentioned in 1 John 2:16, often cited by medieval theologians as the roots of all sin</note>. Likewise, fasting sets us in order regarding ourselves, almsgiving regarding our neighbors, and prayer regarding God. Furthermore, through prayer, we are reconciled to God, for whom—according to Augustine—we create "two wings" through fasting and almsgiving.

**F**our things are required for justification <note>term: justification (iustificatio). The theological process by which a person is moved from a state of sin to a state of grace or righteousness</note>: two from God's side, namely the infusion of grace and the expulsion of guilt; and two from the sinner's side, namely the movement of free will in harmony with the incoming grace, and contrition of heart, agreeing with the grace that expels guilt. However, these four are preceded by attrition <note>term: attrition (attritio). An imperfect sorrow for sin, often motivated by fear of punishment, which serves as the starting point for full repentance</note>, which is the preparation for justification; it is a movement of faith and sorrow that remains incomplete for a long time. 

**T**hese four requirements are touched upon in that verse of the Psalm: *“You have shaken the earth and disturbed it; heal its breakings, for it is shaken”* <note>original: "Commouisti terraz et conturbasti eam sana contriciones eius quia cōmota est." From Psalm 60:2 (Psalm 59 in the Latin Vulgate)</note>. Indeed, grace arriving in the soul moves the reason (insofar as it is the power or capacity of free will) and disturbs the will through contrition; afterward, it heals the soul through the expulsion of sin. A double reason is given for these: namely, the shaking of the reason and the disturbance of the mind that preceded it; hence the addition, "because it is shaken." Thus, "shaking" (*commotio*) is named from the prefix *con-*, meaning "together," because then all the soul's powers are pricked together and moved toward the good.

**I**n the sacrament of penance, there is something that is merely a sign, such as outward penance according to the institution of the Church. In this, the priest's absolution serves as the formal part, while the humiliation of the one confessing—whether in the act of contrition or in the spoken word of accusation—[serves as the material part].

### On Extreme Unction.

¶ Chapter 52.

The subject of Extreme Unction <note>term: Extreme Unction (vnctio extrema). Now commonly called the Anointing of the Sick, this is the sacrament administered to those near death for spiritual and physical strengthening</note> follows, regarding which seven things are briefly to be considered: namely, what is the matter; what is the form; who is the dispenser; who is the recipient; what is its efficacy; what is the order of confirmation; and the suitability of its meaning. 

¶ **T**he matter of this sacrament is simple oil, but consecrated by a bishop. ¶ **T**he form is the vocal expression of the prayers contained in the rituals of this sacrament. The administration of this sacrament belongs to priests by virtue of their office; and this is confirmed by authority and reason. ¶ **A**uthority: **James, last chapter.** <note>original: "Jaco. vltio." referring to the fifth and final chapter of the Epistle of James</note> ¶ **"L**et him who is sick bring in the priests." <note>James 5:14: "Is any among you sick? Let him call for the elders [priests] of the church..."</note> ¶ **R**eason also confirms this because, since the matter of this sacrament is oil consecrated by a bishop, it ought to be handled by consecrated hands. ¶ **A**nd so, since it is a medicine for those in danger, it does not belong to bishops alone <note>Bishops were often distant, but the dying needed immediate access to the sacrament, so the duty fell to local priests</note>. 

¶ **H**owever, the administration of the consecrated oil does not extend to laypeople. ¶ **F**urthermore, only the sick who possess the use of reason ought to receive this sacrament: namely, those who were capable of committing venial sins <note>term: venial sins (peccata venialia). Lesser sins that do not destroy the state of grace but weaken the soul’s inclination toward good</note>, or have indeed sinned, and are at the point of death. ¶ **A**nd so it is clear that extreme unction should not be administered to infants, nor to those who are naturally foolish <note>A historical term for those born with severe intellectual disabilities who cannot "use reason" to understand the sacrament</note>, nor to those entering a just war. For the sacraments of the Church do not follow the turns of fortune <note>i.e., the risk of death in battle</note> but restore nature. Nor is it for those condemned to death by a legal sentence, because such people, however much they are condemned, are "infamous" <note>term: infamy (infamia). A legal status in the Middle Ages where a person lost their civil and religious standing due to a crime</note> in the face of the Church. ¶ **N**or is it for those who are only moderately sick, because they can still repeat venial sins. ¶ **T**his sacrament is both final and ef[fective]...

...[ef]ficaciously ordained against venial <note>term: venial sins (venialia). In Catholic theology, these are "lesser" sins that do not fully separate a person from God's grace but weaken the soul</note> sins, both regarding the remission of guilt and regarding the dismissal of part of the punishment. It is also consequently effective for recovering the health of the body, if it should be expedient for the sick person. And because venial sins are then efficaciously cured when they are so deleted that they are no longer repeated, this medicine <note>The author refers to the sacrament as a "medicine" (medicina) for the soul and body</note> ought to be applied when one despairs of the sick person’s life. If, however, it happens that he is restored to health, the medicine for venial sins can and ought to be repeated because of repeated venial sins. 

And because venial sins are especially contracted through the organs of the senses, therefore the parts serving the three powers are anointed. For there is a triple power according to which the soul rules the body and also contracts a just disease <note>original: "morbum" — here referring to the infirmity or "sickness" of sin</note>, namely: the **sensitive**, the **generative**, and the **progressive** [powers]. To the sensitive [power] serve the mouth, eyes, nostrils, ears, and hands; to the generative, the loins; to the progressive, the feet. 

And according to this, the sick person is to be anointed in seven parts of the body <note>The text lists six locations following this, though traditionally ears and eyes are paired or counted separately to reach seven</note>, namely: in the lips, in the eyes, in the hands, in the nostrils, in the loins, and in the feet. From the aforesaid it is gathered that in this sacrament and the sacrament of Confirmation there is a **sevenfold variety**, namely: in efficacy, in matter, in form, in the recipient, in the giver, in the place, and in the time. 

Note, however, that the anointing which is done with chrism <note>term: chrism (crisma). A consecrated mixture of olive oil and balsam used for high sacraments like Baptism, Confirmation, and Holy Orders</note> is called the "principal anointing" for a fourfold reason: 
1. By reason of the **place**, because it is on the forehead; 
2. By reason of the **minister**, namely [it is performed] by a bishop; 
3. By reason of the **element**, because it is from oil mixed with balsam; 
4. By reason of the **effect**, because of the abundance of grace, for there the Paraclete <note>term: Paraclete (paraclitus). From the Greek meaning 'advocate' or 'helper,' referring to the Holy Spirit</note> is given in great abundance. 

Near this, note that those baptized are anointed with chrism on the crown of the head; those to be confirmed, on the forehead; pontiffs <note>i.e., Bishops</note> on the head; and kings, as it is said, on the shoulders. In the sacrament of Extreme Unction, according to the requirement of sacramental signification, there is something that is only a **sign**, such as the exterior anointing; another which is only the **reality** <note>original: "res" — the actual spiritual grace produced by the ritual</note>, such as the [healing] of the venial disease with...

### On Holy Orders. Chapter 53.

**I**t follows now to look at the sacrament of Holy Orders <term>sacramentum ordinis (Holy Orders): the sacrament through which men are ordained for spiritual ministry in the Church</term> in terms of: the preparation of its meaning, the definitions of the orders, the impression of the character <term>character: a permanent spiritual mark or "seal" believed to be left on the soul by certain sacraments</term>, its proper dispensation, its correct reception, its significance, and the culmination of the state. Those who are to be admitted to the ecclesiastical orders are first designated with a divine seal that is both distinctive and instructive. For they are tonsured <term>tonsura (tonsure): the practice of shaving the hair from the scalp as a sign of religious devotion or clerical status</term> on the head, which is the most noble member of the body compared to the others; and because the tonsure, made both above and below, has the shape of a crown <note>original: "corone"</note>, which is a sign of royal dignity. This implies that those who are so distinguished from others are being taken into a noble office, namely into the royal priesthood <note>original: "regale sacerdotium"; a reference to 1 Peter 2:9</note>. This sign, however, is not only a sign of distinction but also of instruction. In such a tonsure, there is the removal of superfluity <note>the cutting of excess hair symbolizes stripping away ego and distraction</note>, by which the clergy <note>original: "clerici"</note> are instructed on what must be avoided: namely, the excess of earthly things. There is also the baring of the crown of the head, by which they are instructed on what should be loved: namely, the happiness of heavenly things. There is the imprint of a circular shape, by which they are instructed on how they ought to behave. And because the circular shape is the simplest, most capacious, and most beautiful, it suggests that those so "crowned" ought to be simplified in intention, expanded in love, and beautified in purity. "Order" is defined thus: Order is a seal <note>original: "signaculum"</note> through which spiritual power is handed over to the ordained person. For since there are seven orders, namely: the order of porter <term>ianitor: the doorkeeper who guarded the church entrance</term>, lector <term>lector: the reader of sacred texts</term>, exorcist <term>exorcista: one charged with performing rites to drive out evil spirits</term>, acolyte <term>acolitus: an assistant to the higher clergy</term>, subdeacon <term>subdyaconus</term>, deacon <term>dyaconus</term>, and priest <note>original: "sacerdotum"</note>. In or-

# ¶ The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure
<meta>catchword: so that</meta>

...the priestly order, power is given for consecrating the true body of Christ and for absolving the mystical body of Christ <term>mystical body of Christ: a theological term for the Church and the community of believers</term>. And this order, being the most noble, must have others serving it in respect to this twofold power. ¶ In the consecrating of the true body of Christ, the subdeacons and deacons serve the priest. ¶ Thus, the power and office of the subdeacon consists in receiving the hosts <note>original: "hostias"; the bread intended for the Eucharist</note> from the people. ¶ The power and office of the deacon is to offer those same hosts, received from the subdeacon, to the priest. ¶ In absolving the mystical body of Christ, however, the four minor orders serve under the priest. ¶ Thus, the power and office of the doorkeepers <note>original: "ianitorum"; also known as the order of porter</note> is to admit people to the sacred place. ¶ It is the duty of the lectors to provide instruction. ¶ It is the duty of the exorcists to provide assistance. ¶ It is the duty of the acolytes to demonstrate examples <note>the acolyte's life should serve as a visible example of holy conduct to the laity</note>. ¶ The Lord Himself made use of this sevenfold office. ¶ For He was the doorkeeper when, having made a whip, He cast the merchants out of the temple. ¶ He was the lector when He read in the synagogue, having taken up the book, as noted in *Luke 10* <note>The author likely refers to the event in Luke 4:16-20</note>. ¶ He was the exorcist when He touched the tongue of the deaf and mute man with His saliva, as noted in *Matthew 7* <note>A similar account is found in Mark 7:33</note>. ¶ He showed Himself to be the acolyte when *John 6* <note>original: "iohannes vi"; the quote "I am the light of the world" is actually from John 8:12</note> says: "I am the light of the world." ¶ He was the subdeacon when He washed the feet of the disciples and girded Himself with a linen cloth, according to *John 13*. ¶ He was the deacon when, after the supper, He distributed His body and blood to the disciples. ¶ He was the priest while He offered Himself on the altar of the cross, being at once both the priest and the victim <note>original: "hostia"; the sacrificial offering</note>. ¶ *And also when, at the supper, He changed the bread into His body and the wine into His blood.* ¶ The character <term>character: a permanent spiritual mark or "seal" believed to be left on the soul by the sacrament</term> (since it is a distinctive seal impressed to perpetually configure one to Christ) is imprinted in every order—not the same one, but different ones appropriate to each order. This is not strictly necessary [for validity], as one could be received improperly while another is skipped over, so that

### Third Part

...when one has arrived by degrees at the order of the priest, in which the full status of the office resides. ¶ Although there are seven degrees, there is only one sacrament of orders, just as we call it a single letter <note>original: "epistolam"; referring to a written message or epistle</note> even though it may have many parts. In any of the orders, however, a character <term>character: a spiritual and indelible mark imprinted on the soul that distinguishes the recipient</term> is imprinted by that sign which has the greatest similarity to the thing signified; and in this, the principal power <term>principalis potestas: the specific spiritual authority granted by that level of ordination</term> which the order concerns is signified as being handed over to the one being ordained. To signify this, two external things come together: namely, the handing over of a certain instrument <note>original: "traditio alicuius instrumenti"; the physical act of giving the tools of the office, such as a chalice or book</note> and the vocal expression of the word, so that the gift is signified in both a visible and an audible sign at the same time. These two elements are essential and intrinsic to the sacrament. This sacrament ought to be dispensed with great discretion and solemnity; and because of this, it is not to be given by just anyone, nor to just anyone, nor in just any place or at any time, but under the required signs regarding both sight and hearing, as has been said, with solemnity nonetheless observed regarding the time, place, office, and person. A multifaceted condition is required so that the sacrament of orders may be worthily and properly received: namely, on the part of **origin**: that he is not the son of a priest, nor illegitimate, nor even a public penitent <note>someone undergoing formal, public penance for a grave sin</note>. ¶ On the part of the **body**: that he is not physically disabled, possessed by a demon, or epileptic. ¶ On the part of **action**: that he is not a murderer or otherwise a notorious sinner. ¶ On the part of **condition**: that he is not a slave. ¶ On the part of **way of life**: that he is not a courtier <note>original: "curialis"; one involved in secular legal or administrative court business</note>. ¶ On the part of **marriage**: that he is not a bigamist <note>original: "bigamus"; in canon law, this usually referred to a man who had been married twice, even if the first wife had died</note>. ¶ On the part of **sex**: that it is not a woman, who cannot be ordained either by law or in fact. ¶ On the part of the **senses**: that he is not a small child in whom the judgment of reason does not yet flourish. In this sacrament, there is something that is only...

->The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure<-

...sign, such as the external action in the handing over of some instrument <note>original: "tradicione alicuius instrumenti"; referring to the "traditio instrumentorum," a rite where the tools of the office are handed to the candidate</note> and the vocal expression of the word. **Another** aspect is that which is only the spiritual reality <term>res: the actual grace or power conferred by the sacrament, as opposed to the outward sign</term>, such as the conferring of spiritual power. **Another** is that which is both the reality and the sign, such as the interior imprinting of the character <term>caracteris: an indelible spiritual mark on the soul</term>. **Gather** from what has been said above that there are seven orders and no more. There are four minor orders which are called "non-sacred" because after receiving them, a cleric is still able to marry. **Three are major, namely:** the subdiaconate, the diaconate, and the priesthood, in which the full status of the orders resides. **These** are called sacred because after receiving them, it is not lawful for those ordained to serve carnal works <note>original: "carnali opi deservire"; a reference to the requirement of celibacy for major orders</note>, since they are totally dedicated to divine worship. **And so it is clear that** the role of psalmist <note>original: "psalmistatus"; the office of leading the singing of psalms</note> is not an order but an office annexed to an order. **Nor** is the episcopate <term>episcopatus: the office of a bishop</term> an order, but rather a name of dignity and of office. **Gather** into one the names of both the orders and the offices and dignities so that you may arrive at the single hierarchy of the ecclesiastical hierarchy; and so proceed: psalmist, doorkeeper, lector, exorcist, acolyte, subdeacon, deacon, priest, **Archpriest, Bishop,** archbishop, patriarch, and supreme pontiff <note>original: "pontifex summus"; referring to the Pope</note>.

### On Matrimony

**I**t follows to look at the sacrament of matrimony regarding its definition, its institution, its contraction <note>original: "contractionem"; how the marriage bond is legally and spiritually formed</note>, its division, its significance, the good that comes from it, and the impediments that prohibit it. **Matrimony** is defined thus: **Matrimony** is the marital union of a man and a woman between legitimate persons, maintaining an inseparable way of life. **Matrimony** was instituted by God **as a duty, as a remedy, and as a sacrament or sign. As a duty, insofar as...**

### ¶ The Third Part

...for the procreation of offspring, having been instituted even in the state of [unfallen] nature. **As** a remedy against the impulse of lust in the state of fallen nature. **As** a sacrament or sign of the indissoluble union of the two natures in Christ—namely, the divine and the human. **¶ Concerning** the contracting of marriage, note that it is initiated through betrothal for the future <term>sponsalia de futuro: a promise to marry at a later date</term>; it is ratified by mutual consent between legitimate persons expressed through words of the present <term>verba de presenti: the legal exchange of vows that creates a binding marriage immediately</term>; and it is consummated by the subsequent carnal union. **¶ Concerning** betrothals, note that those contracted before the seventh year <note>original: "ante septennium"; in medieval law, seven was the age of reason and the minimum age for a binding betrothal</note> are not legally valid, but after the seventh year they hold. **And** these are contracted in four ways: namely, by a simple promise; by the giving of faith or the interposition of an oath; by the giving of betrothal gifts; and by the placing of a ring. **However**, those thus contracted are dissolved in eight ways.

**¶ The first** is by a subsequent solemn vow, as when one of the betrothed enters a religious order. **¶ The second** is by a subsequent marriage, as when one contracts a marriage with another person through words of the present. **The third** is by subsequent affinity <term>affinitate: a legal impediment to marriage created by previous sexual relations with a relative of the betrothed</term>, as when one has carnal knowledge of another blood relative of the betrothed, whether this can be proven by reputation or by fact. **¶ The fourth** is by the onset of an incurable illness, such as leprosy, or the mutilation of some limb. **¶ The fifth** is when one absents themselves, such as when one goes into a distant region without the permission of the other. **¶ Sextus** is when one commits fornication. **¶ The seventh** is when both desire it, namely when both consent and mutually release each other. **¶ The eighth** is at the request of a minor, as when a betrothal was contracted between two people of whom one is of the required age and the other is not. **By the first two** ways, betrothals are dissolved by the very fact <term>ipso facto: automatically, by the act itself without needing a court ruling</term>. **¶ But by the following** ways, they are dissolved by the judgment of the Church. **¶ There is, moreover,** a twofold effect of contracted betrothals: one is an obligation for...

# ¶ The Short Treatise of Saint Bonaventure

...promise. <note>original: "promissionem"</note> For this reason, such persons are to be compelled to contract the marriage unless wife-killing <note>original: "uxoricidium"</note> is feared, or if one of the aforementioned cases occurs. Another effect is the justice of public honesty <term>publicae honestatis iustitia: a legal impediment preventing marriage with the close relatives of someone to whom one was previously betrothed</term>, which means that no relative of the groom can contract a marriage with the bride, or vice versa. Those joined matrimonially through the Church are separated in three ways: either simply as to the bond, when it is shown that due to a legitimate impediment there was no marriage between them; or they are separated for a time to perform penance, as when they contracted the marriage against a prohibition of the Church; or they are separated simply as to time <note>that is, a separation of bed and board rather than a dissolution of the bond</note> as a punishment for some crime committed against the substance of marriage, such as when one of the spouses commits adultery. Or, if the innocent party petitions with proof of the adultery, they are released by the Church from mutual cohabitation with the spouse and from the rendering of the marital debt <term>debiti reddicione: the moral and legal obligation for spouses to provide sexual intimacy to one another</term>.

There are, however, seven cases in which an adulteress can raise a defense against one seeking a divorce <note>original: "divorcium"; in this context, it refers to a legal separation of cohabitation rather than a total dissolution of the bond</note>:

**¶ The first** is if he [the husband] is convicted of fornicating himself. **The second** is if he prostituted her. **The third** is when she believed her husband had died in war and married another. **¶ The fourth** is if she was known <note>meaning carnally known or had sex</note> by another in secret whom she believed to be her husband. **¶ The fifth** is if she was overcome by force <note>original: "vi oppressa"; referring to rape or coercion</note>. **¶ The sixth** is when, after the adultery, he reconciled her to himself and sought the marital debt. **¶ The seventh** is a rarer case: as when an unbeliever repudiated his wife and she married another; if he [the husband] then comes to the Christian faith, he ought to receive her back notwithstanding that she committed adultery.

**¶ In a ratified marriage** <term>matrimonio ratificato: a marriage that is legally valid and sacramental but not yet physically consummated</term>, there is something that is only a **sign**, such as the outward consent expressed through words or signs. **¶ Another** thing is only the **reality**, such as the union of God and the soul through grace, which is nevertheless separable through the transgression of mortal sin, just as that marriage is separable by entry into a religious order <note>meaning one party can choose to leave the unconsummated marriage to join a monastery or convent</note>. **¶ Another** thing is both the **reality and the sign**, such as the interior consent of the minds. **¶ In a consummated marriage** there is only...

## Part Three

The external sign of consent is only the sign; the reality <note>original: "res"</note> is the inseparable union of natures in Christ. **The reality** is the sign of the mutual consent of souls. ¶ **Three** are the goods of marriage, namely: the good of faith, the good of offspring, and the good of the sacrament. **Faith** is that a man knows his own wife and no other, and this is a moral good falling under the category of the honorable <term>honesti: in medieval philosophy, this refers to something that is virtuous and worthy of choice for its own sake</term>. **The good** of offspring falls under the category of the beneficial <note>original: "conferentis"</note>: for offspring contribute to the preservation of the species and to the perfection of the number [of the elect]. **The good** of the sacrament falls under the category of the pleasurable. **So great** is the union there that for the sake of his wife, a man shall leave father and mother. And so it is clear that there are three factors in making a choice (according to the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note>), namely: the honorable, the beneficial, and the pleasurable. **In** this triple category, the goods of marriage are distributed, and these goods are three in number because three things are chiefly required for a marriage to be complete: institution, use, and fruit. **By reason** of the institution, there is the good of the sacrament; by reason of the required use, there is the good of faith; by reason of the beneficial fruit, there is the good of offspring.
¶ **On the impediments to marriage in general, according to Chapter 55**

<image-desc>A decorative drop cap letter 'C' in a square frame initiates the next section.</image-desc>

**C**oncerning the impediments to marriage, which occur frequently, special attention must be paid. Regarding these, it must be held that some hinder out of propriety <term>ex congruitate: based on what is fitting or suitable rather than strict legal necessity</term>, such as a prohibition by the Church or the forbidden times for women <note>likely referring to liturgical seasons when weddings were prohibited</note>. **And** these are said to hinder a marriage from being contracted, but they do not dissolve <note>original: "dirimere"</note> one already contracted. **Some** things hinder by necessity, because the sacrament of marriage cannot exist alongside them. Therefore, they hinder a marriage from being contracted and also dissolve one already contracted. **These** are twelve in number, and they are contained in these verses:

**Error, condition, vow, kinship, crime ¶ Difference of worship, force, holy orders, bond, [public] honesty. ¶ If you are related by marriage, or if by chance you are unable to copulate. ¶ These forbid marriages from being joined, and void those already joined.**
<note>This mnemonic verse lists the twelve legal bars to a valid marriage: 1. Identity error, 2. Condition (slavery), 3. Vows of chastity, 4. Blood relations, 5. Criminal acts, 6. Religious difference, 7. Duress, 8. Clerical orders, 9. Prior marriage bond, 10. Public decency, 11. Affinity through marriage, and 12. Impotence.</note>

# ¶ The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

...revoke [the marriage]. ¶ **There are three impediments regarding the efficient cause:** <note>In medieval philosophy, the "efficient cause" is the primary source of change or the agent that brings something into being—in this case, the consent that creates a marriage.</note> which is the consent in which reason and will concur. ¶ Opposed to the act of reason is **error**; opposed to the act of the will is **force** or **violence**. ¶ Opposed to the act of both is the **condition of servitude** <note>original: "condicio servitutis"</note>, which hinders marriage partly due to a lack of freedom and partly due to error. ¶ **Three are regarding the material cause** <note>The "material cause" refers to the subjects involved—the people being joined.</note> ¶ For marriage is a union voluntarily entered into between persons not previously joined. Thus, those joined in proximity [of relation] are unfit for marriage. Some are joined by **consanguinity** <term>consanguinitas: kinship by blood or common descent</term>; some by **affinity** <term>affinitas: relationship created by marriage, such as in-laws</term>; some by **public legal honesty** <note>original: "publicae iustitiae honestas"; a legal impediment that arises from a valid betrothal, preventing one from marrying the close relatives of the person they were engaged to.</note>, which is contracted with the betrothal through words of the present tense; and thus there are three impediments: namely, kinship, affinity, and [public] honesty. ¶ **There are three regarding the formal cause** <note>The "formal cause" is the internal structure or essence of the thing—here, the "bond" and the rights over the body.</note> because marriage is a bond in which there is the mutual power of husband and wife. This power, however, is hindered when one grants the power of their body to another while their own [spouse] is still living, and thus it is a **binding tie** <note>original: "ligamen"; the existence of a prior, living spouse.</note>; or because one has dedicated themselves to God [in the priesthood], and thus it is **holy orders**; or because one has surrendered themselves to God and a prelate [in a religious order], and thus it is a **vow**. ¶ **The final three are regarding the final cause.** <note>The "final cause" is the purpose or goal for which marriage exists.</note> For marriage exists for the procreation of offspring for the worship of God, or at least as a remedy for the storm [of passion]. Against the purpose of procreation is the remedy [needed for] **impotence** in sexual union; against the worship of God which is faith, there is **disparity of cult** <note>original: "cultus disparitas"; marriage between a baptized Christian and a non-baptized person.</note>; against the worship of God regarding morals, there is **enormity of crime** <note>original: "criminis enormitas"; a legal category referring to grave sins, such as a person murdering their spouse in order to marry another.</note>. ¶ **Regarding the impediment of error**, it must be noted that error can concern the person or the qualities of the person. Error concerning the person removes consent and hinders marriage; for example, if one believes they are consenting to Mary, of whom they are thinking in their inner mind, but actually consents to Margaret, who is presented to their outer senses. ¶ If, however, there is an error regarding the qualities of the person

### Part Three

...marriage is not hindered because of this. For example, if someone believes they are taking a wealthy and noble woman whom they later discover to be low-born and poor. ¶ Regarding the properties of the person, however, the **condition of servitude** <term>condicio servitutis: the legal status of being unfree or enslaved</term> is an exception, which hinders a marriage from being contracted and dissolves one already contracted. Regarding this, note that there can be a triple **error of condition** <note>original: "error condicionis"</note>. Namely, an error of an equal condition, such as when a male servant believes he is taking a free woman but instead takes a female servant; such an error does not hinder [the marriage]. Or an error of a better condition, such as when he believes he is taking a female servant and takes a free woman; nor does this error hinder [the marriage]. Or an error of a worse condition, such as when a man is free and believes he is taking a free woman, but instead takes a female servant; such an error hinders the contracting [of marriage] and dissolves one already contracted, unless he wishes to consent to her freely <note>The law allowed the marriage to stand if the free person discovered the servitude and chose to stay anyway.</note>. ¶ Regarding a **vow**, note the definition of **Hugh** <note>Likely Hugh of Saint Victor, a foundational 12th-century theologian.</note>. ¶ A vow is the witnessing of a promise made spontaneously, which ought to be made to God and concerning those things that pertain to God.

¶ Alternatively, it is defined thus: ¶ A vow is the conception of a greater good formed with deliberation. ¶ Furthermore, a vow is twofold: namely, **simple** and **solemn**. ¶ A **simple vow** <term>votum simplex: a private promise to God, such as a vow of chastity, that does not involve formal entry into a religious order</term> is one in which authority has not been formally transferred [to a superior]. ¶ It hinders a marriage from being contracted but does not dissolve one already contracted. ¶ A **solemn vow** <term>votum solemnne: a public, formal commitment, such as taking holy orders or joining a monastery</term> is that which is solemnized by the receiving of Holy Orders, the making of a religious profession, or the taking up of the habit of the professed, along with other things that show a person is professed; this hinders the contracting of marriage and dissolves one already contracted. ¶ Regarding **crime**, note that marriage is denied in seven types of crimes. ¶ The first is **adultery** with the killing of a woman or wife—specifically [when involving] the giving of a pledge <note>original: "fidei dacione"; this refers to a promise to marry the partner in crime once the current spouse is murdered.</note>...

# ¶ The Hundred Sayings of Saint Bonaventure

...with his own adulterous wife after her death; or with a *de facto* <note>original: "de facto"; refers to an illegal attempt at marriage while a previous spouse is still alive</note> contract. The **Second** is incest. The third is the abduction of another's betrothed. The fourth concerns him who laid traps for a marriage [or] who lifted his own child from the sacred font <note>original: "de sacroscte leuauit"; this refers to a parent acting as a godparent to their own child, which created "spiritual kinship" and was a legal impediment.</note>. For adultery combined with uxoricide <term>uxoricide: the act of killing one's wife</term>, or with a promise of marriage or a *de facto* contract with respect to that person, prevents a marriage from being contracted and dissolves one already contracted. Likewise, regarding the natural power of sexual union: if it is temporary, it does not hinder; if it is perpetual, it hinders and dissolves [the bond] if it precedes the marriage. If, however, it follows the marriage, it does not dissolve it. Coercion by this means, or fear, does not dissolve nor hinder. **Just fear** is that which can affect a steady man, when it is likely that gre[at]...

[danger] will be inflicted by the one who threatens, and such coercion takes away consent. And according to the laws, it prevents a marriage and dissolves one already contracted. Regarding the legal tie <note>original: "ligamen"</note>, note that however much a divorce <note>original: "diuorcium"; in medieval canon law, this usually refers to "divorce from bed and board" (legal separation) rather than the dissolution of the marriage bond itself, which was considered indissoluble.</note> is granted between spouses on account of the crime of adultery—as far as the marriage bed and living together are concerned—the persons thus separated remain unable to marry others. This is because between them, as long as both shall live, there remains an indissoluble bond and tie. Regarding public legal decency <term>public legal decency: "honestas publicae iustitiae," a legal impediment arising from a valid betrothal (engagement) that prevents a person from later marrying the close relatives of their betrothed</term>, note that if one contracts a betrothal with a girl after her seventh year and he dies before the marriage is consummated, the blood relatives of the deceased groom are held back by such "decency" from contracting a marriage with the widow; and this decency prevents a marriage from being contracted and dissolves one already contracted.

## ¶ Concerning the impediments of affinity and kinship in particular, and first concerning affinity ¶ Chapter 56

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'D' featuring a stylized flower within a square frame.</image-desc>

Regarding the two impediments that remain, we must look at them specifically: namely, affinity <term>affinity: the relationship created by sexual union (inside or outside of marriage) between one person and the blood relatives of their partner</term> and kinship <note>original: "congnacione"; here referring to "cognation," which includes both legal and spiritual ties.</note>. And first regarding affinity: about which, first, a triple difference is to be noted; second, a triple note is to be noted. It differs, therefore, first between being an affine and a blood relative. For blood relationship <note>original: "consanguinitas"</note> comes from the propagation of the flesh; affinity, however, comes from carnal union, presupposing a blood relationship, which is defined thus: "Affinity is the proximity of persons proceeding from carnal union, lacking all parentage"—meaning, of itself. Whence, if anyone "knows" <note>A common historical euphemism for sexual intercourse.</note> a woman, he is made an affine to all the blood relatives of the woman, and vice versa. Second, it differs between the kind <note>original: "genus"</note> of affinity and the degree <note>original: "gradus"</note>. For the kind is considered according to the multiplication of the union of the flesh. The de[gree]—

<meta>This page continues the specific discussion regarding the impediment of affinity (relationship by marriage or sexual union). The previous page ended with a partial sentence explaining that one type of relationship is by blood, and this page picks up by contrasting it with the other type. It explains the "ancient laws" versus the "modern laws" (reforms of the 13th century) concerning how marriage ties extend to relatives.</meta>

# The Centiloquium of Saint Bonaventure

...[the second] is on the part of blood and propagation. For this reason, it was said in ancient times: *A bride changes her kind, but her offspring change the degree.* <note>original: "Mutat nupta genus sed generata gradum." This was a legal maxim explaining that a woman entering a family through marriage created a new "type" of affinity, while her children moved the relationship further away by "degrees" of distance on the family tree.</note> For there were formerly three kinds <note>original: "genera"</note> of affinity. The first was from consanguinity <term>consanguinity: relationship by blood</term> and the first carnal union. For example: if your brother married my [relative], she was related to you by affinity in the first kind. The second kind was from affinity and a second carnal union: for example, if after your brother died, my [relative] married Martin; Martin was then related to you by affinity in the second kind. The third kind was from his affinity and a third carnal union: for instance, if Martin, after my [relative] died, married Guillota; that Guillota was related to you by affinity in the third kind.

And thus the bride changed the kind; but the offspring, or the one propagated, changed the degree. For if my [relative], after your brother died, had fathered a daughter with Martin, that daughter would have been related to you by affinity in the second degree—similar to a niece—and not in the second kind. 

Today, however, the second and third kinds have ceased, except for the first; and a daughter born from your kinswoman <note>original: "agnata"; a relative through the male line</note> by another man is said to have no relation to you. And if perhaps there is a difference whether a woman related by affinity bears children by a blood relative or a stranger: for if she bears a child by a blood relative, the child born relates to you by a degree of consanguinity. If, however, it is from a stranger, the child born relates to you by a degree of affinity, as was said according to the ancient laws. 

According to modern laws, however, that offspring is not related to you. Having seen these things, three small notes should be made, the first of which is that in the first kind of affinity, it is not permitted to marry up to the fourth degree inclusive, just as is said concerning consanguinity. If, however, you wish to recognize the fourth degree, go back to the person through whom the affinity was contracted; in whatever degree you are distant from that person, you are in that same degree distant [from the relative].

<summary>This page discusses the complex medieval legal definitions of affinity, explaining how the "ancient" three-fold classification of relationships was simplified in the "modern" era to focus only on the first kind of affinity.</summary>
